# Geography fact cards: review list

2074 new flashcards across 97 subsections, drafted from the information packages in `course-outlines/packages/geography/` and stored as `factCards` in `content/geography/<subsection>.json`. In the app they appear after each subsection's key terms, in both flashcards and tests.

A separate review pass checked every card against its package and found 222 problems: 6 that would have taught a false fact, 98 misleading, 5 that broke a package trap, and 113 weak (mostly a front whose count didn't match the back). 220 cards were corrected and 2 cut; the list below is the corrected set.

Each card shows its front, its back, and the package must-cover item(s) it comes from. To change or remove a card, edit the `factCards` list in the content file, then run `python3 scripts/build_notes.py geography`. Delete this file once review is done.

## Aerial photographs

### Aerial photograph interpretation (24)

- **Vertical or oblique photograph: how to tell (the 2-mark question)** — Oblique: the sides as well as the roofs of buildings are visible, foreground features look larger than background ones, and depth or a horizon shows. Vertical: only roofs are visible, the scale looks even and the photo looks like a map. _(T4, T1, T2)_
- **Scale on aerial photographs: oblique vs vertical** — Oblique: no single scale, so measure distances on the OS map instead. Vertical: scale = photo distance ÷ ground distance read from the map. Illustrative: 5 cm on the photo and 2 cm on a 1:50 000 map (1 km, or 100 000 cm) give 1:20 000. _(M6, T2)_
- **Nine-area grid: method for locating a feature** — Draw the grid lightly on the photo in pencil, or fold it into thirds in your head; find the feature; state its zone, across-term first, such as left foreground. The same method answers true or false zone statements and matches cropped sections to zones. _(L4, L2)_
- **Matching a street map or satellite image to the photo: three steps** — Find two features common to both, such as the river, a bridge or a large church, and turn the second source mentally to match; identify each feature by a clue (spire, flat roof beside a car park, pitch markings, shopfronts); give its zone in accepted notation. _(L5)_
- **Photo recognition clues: church, graveyard, school, shopping centre** — Church: a spire or tower, a cross-shaped plan and a graveyard beside it. Graveyard: rows of small headstones. School: a large building with a playground, pitches and a car park. Shopping centre: a very large flat roof and an extensive car park. _(L6)_
- **Photo recognition clues: factory, housing estate, railway, waste ground** — Factory or warehouse: a large flat or sawtooth roof, yards and lorries, no gardens. Housing estate: rows of similar houses with gardens, often in cul-de-sacs. Railway: gentle curves, no cars, bridges over roads. Waste ground: rough, unused land. _(L6)_
- **Camera direction: how to work it out with the OS map** — Identify a foreground feature and a background feature on the photo and find both on the OS map; the direction from the foreground one to the background one is the way the camera was pointing. Give an eight-point compass term; check with a third feature. _(M2)_
- **Grid reference of a feature seen on the photo: method** — Find the feature on the photo, then locate it on the OS map from its position relative to roads, the river and junctions (buildings are often not drawn at 1:50 000, but churches have symbols); then give a four- or six-figure grid reference. _(M3)_
- **Naming a road or river seen on the photo: method** — Trace the road or river on the photograph, find the same route on the OS map, and read its number (N, R or M) or its name from the map. _(M4)_
- **Photo vs OS map: what each shows that the other does not** — Photo only: individual buildings, land-use type, crops, building height and age, traffic and recent developments. Map only: names, road numbers, heights and contours, the grid, labelled antiquities, boundaries, and features hidden by trees or beyond the frame. _(M5)_
- **Sketch map: frame size and setting-up rules** — Draw a frame with four ruled sides, the same shape as the photo. Half the length and half the breadth means halving each side, a quarter of the area (illustrative: 22 cm by 16 cm becomes 11 cm by 8 cm). Never trace; grid photo and frame lightly in pencil. _(S1, S2)_
- **Sketch map: how to draw lines, areas and single buildings** — Show only the features asked for, in roughly the right position and shape: rivers (a broad double line), roads and railways as lines; housing, industry and farmland as closed outlined shapes; single buildings as small blocks or symbols. _(S3)_
- **Sketch map: labelling and position rules** — Label every feature on or beside it, or in a key, and give the sketch a title; a feature drawn but not labelled scores nothing. A feature asked for in a zone, such as a car park in the centre foreground, must be drawn in that zone. _(S4)_
- **Urban functions: photo evidence for transport, tourism, market and defensive functions** — Transport: a bridge, railway station, converging roads or a harbour. Tourism: hotels, caravan parks, marinas, beaches or historic sites. Market: a square or market house. Defensive (historic): a castle or town walls. _(U6)_
- **Describe and explain a town function: the answer formula** — Name the function; give the photo evidence; give its location in accepted notation; explain why the town performs it, using its site, situation, size or hinterland. Give several pieces of evidence for each function. _(U7)_
- **Evidence of a town's history on an aerial photograph** — A castle, tower house or abbey ruin; an old church and graveyard; town walls or gates; a market square, fair green or market house; old stone bridges, quays and mills; a railway station (19th-century growth); modern estates on the edge (recent growth). _(H1)_
- **Unplanned town: the street evidence, and the planned-town trap** — Narrow, winding, irregular streets, often of medieval origin, show unplanned growth. Wide, straight streets, a regular grid, a central square or diamond and uniform terraces show a planned town, so wide streets never show an unplanned one. _(H2)_
- **Causes of traffic congestion visible on a photo** — Narrow streets in the old core; on-street parking narrowing the road; routes converging on one bridge or junction; through-traffic passing the centre; delivery vehicles; limited off-street parking; schools and shopping centres creating peak traffic. _(R1)_
- **Traffic management measures you can see on a photo** — Roundabouts; traffic lights; pedestrian crossings; one-way streets (parked cars all face one way); double yellow lines; bus and cycle lanes; multi-storey car parks; pedestrianised streets; bypasses and ring roads; bollards and ramps. _(R2)_
- **Traffic answers: formula for a measure, a problem and a solution** — Measure: name it, locate it in accepted notation, explain how it reduces congestion or improves safety. Problem: name it, locate it, explain why it causes congestion. Solution, such as a bypass or a car park on waste ground: justify it with photo evidence. _(R3, R4)_
- **Land-use change from the photo and map: method** — Find the location and identify its present use on the photo; infer the earlier use from the map or from clues such as new estates with fields beyond, cleared sites or new roads across field patterns; state the change, e.g. agricultural to residential. _(C1)_
- **Site suitability for a proposed development: reasons to give** — Give three reasons, each with photo evidence: available flat land; access to main roads; nearness to the population it serves; space for parking; a site away from a flood-prone river bank; for industry, a site away from housing. _(C3)_
- **Time of year: evidence on an aerial photograph** — Deciduous trees in full leaf (summer) or bare (winter); ripening crops, golden fields or stubble (late summer to autumn); ploughed fields (autumn or spring); silage or hay bales (summer); cattle outdoors (not winter, when Irish cattle are housed). _(D1)_
- **Time of day from shadows in Ireland: the rules** — Shadows point away from the sun. The midday sun is due south, so midday shadows point north and are shortest; morning shadows point west, evening shadows east; long shadows mean a low sun. Use the camera direction to turn left or right into compass points. _(D2)_

## Economic activities

### 1. Case study of a developed economy: Ireland (35)

- **Ireland from protection to free trade, 1932 to 1973** — Protection: the Control of Manufactures Acts (1932, 1934) and high tariffs shielded Irish firms, leaving 1950s unemployment and emigration. Opening: IDA 1949; Export Profits Tax Relief 1956; Whitaker's First Programme 1958; Anglo-Irish Free Trade Agreement 1965; EEC 1973. _(E3)_
- **How Ireland built a skilled, open economy after 1966: four dated steps** — Free second-level education (announced 1966 by Donogh O'Malley, introduced 1967) built an educated workforce; the EU Single Market began in 1993; the euro came in 1999 (notes and coins 2002); corporation tax on trading profits was 12.5% from 2003. _(E3)_
- **Celtic Tiger, mid-1990s to 2007: causes and effects** — Driven first by exports and FDI, especially US technology and pharmaceutical firms, then by a property and construction boom. Unemployment fell to about 4–5% by the mid-2000s; net immigration replaced emigration, with large inflows from new EU states after 2004. _(E3)_
- **Irish banking crash: the sequence of events, 2007 to 2013** — Property bubble burst 2007–2008; bank guarantee 30 September 2008; Anglo Irish Bank nationalised January 2009; NAMA set up later in 2009 to take over property loans; EU–IMF programme of €67.5 billion in external loans agreed November 2010, exited December 2013. _(E4)_
- **Why the Irish government built up such a large debt after 2008** — The state borrowed heavily to rescue the banks (bank guarantee 2008, Anglo Irish Bank nationalised 2009, EU–IMF loans 2010) while its tax base collapsed with the property crash, so government debt peaked at about 120% of GDP in 2012. _(E4)_
- **Five benefits of high employment to an economy** — More income-tax and VAT revenue; lower welfare spending; consumer spending that supports jobs in other sectors; less emigration, so skilled people stay; more government money to invest in infrastructure and services. _(E5)_
- **Irish agriculture: key figures and where tillage is concentrated** — Census of Agriculture 2020: about 135,000 farms averaging about 33 hectares; about 7 million cattle (2020s). Mild winters and reliable rain favour grass-based livestock. Barley is the largest tillage crop; tillage is concentrated in Wexford, Carlow, Kildare, Meath and Cork. _(P1)_
- **Ireland's large cattle herd: benefits and environmental problems** — Benefits: exports (about 90% of beef and dairy output), food-processing jobs, rural incomes; dairying grew after milk quotas ended on 1 April 2015. Problems: methane makes agriculture Ireland's largest source of greenhouse gases, about 38% (EPA, 2022 data); nitrate and phosphate runoff; ammonia. _(P2)_
- **Name an Irish food product and an Irish drink, with their makers** — Food: Kerrygold butter (Ornua, launched 1962); Tayto crisps (first made 1954 by Joe 'Spud' Murphy). Drink: Guinness (brewed at St James's Gate, Dublin, since 1759); Jameson whiskey (Irish Distillers, Midleton, Co. Cork); Baileys Irish Cream (launched 1974). _(P3)_
- **Irish fishing: natural advantages and value to the economy** — Advantages: a long, indented coastline with sheltered harbours; a wide continental shelf; plankton-rich waters and the North Atlantic Drift; pelagic stocks, mackerel being the most valuable. Value: exports, jobs in remote coastal communities, fish processing. _(P4)_
- **Name Ireland's six State Fishery Harbour Centres** — Killybegs (Co. Donegal), the largest, landing mainly pelagic fish; Castletownbere (Co. Cork); Dingle (Co. Kerry); Rossaveel (Co. Galway); Dunmore East (Co. Waterford); Howth (Co. Dublin). _(P4)_
- **Ireland's main manufacturing sectors: a location and firm for each** — Pharmaceuticals and chemicals, the largest goods exports: Cork Harbour (Pfizer, Janssen, Novartis at Ringaskiddy). Medical devices: Galway (Boston Scientific 1994, Medtronic). ICT: Intel, Leixlip (1989); Apple, Cork (1980). Food and drink: Kerry Group (Tralee) and Glanbia (Kilkenny). _(S2)_
- **Challenges facing industry in Ireland: name seven** — Dependence on a few US multinationals; the 15% minimum tax for very large groups (2024); US tariffs on EU goods (2025); Brexit; housing costs that deter staff; grid and water limits (EirGrid will not connect new Dublin data centres until about 2028); lower-cost rivals. _(S4)_
- **Silicon Docks, Dublin: where it is, named firms and effects** — Grand Canal Dock, south of the Liffey. Google has had its European headquarters in Dublin since 2003, now on Barrow Street; Meta and LinkedIn also have large offices in the area. Effects: high-paid jobs, rising rents, congestion. _(F2)_
- **Why service and footloose firms cluster in the Dublin region** — Graduates from Trinity College Dublin, UCD, DCU, TU Dublin and Maynooth University; EU-wide recruitment; Dublin Airport (over 30 million passengers, 2023) and fibre links; low tax and incentives; agglomeration, as firms share skills and suppliers. _(F2, F1)_
- **Shannon: duty-free shop, Free Zone and aircraft leasing** — Brendan O'Regan opened the world's first duty-free airport shop at Shannon in 1947; the Shannon Free Zone (1959) gave firms tax relief and duty-free imports. Tony Ryan founded GPA at Shannon in 1975; Ireland now manages about half the world's leased aircraft. _(F4)_
- **Silicon Valley, California: origins, named firms and problems** — Stanford University encouraged spin-off firms and opened its Research Park in 1951; Hewlett-Packard began in a Palo Alto garage in 1939; Intel (founded 1968) is headquartered in Santa Clara; Apple is at Cupertino, Google at Mountain View. Problems: high house prices, congestion, homelessness. _(F5)_
- **IFSC, Dublin: when, where and why it was set up** — Set up in 1987 by the Charles Haughey government on the derelict Custom House Docks, north of the Liffey. Reasons: a 10% tax rate for approved firms, government incentives, an educated English-speaking workforce, EU access, the time zone, and regeneration of the docks. _(B2)_
- **IFSC: growth, effects and the Brexit opportunity** — Irish-domiciled funds hold over €3 trillion (2020s). Effects: high-value jobs; regeneration of the north docklands, served by the Luas Red Line (2004). After the 2016 Brexit vote, Bank of America and Barclays moved their main EU operations to Dublin. _(B2, B3)_
- **Factors that influence the development of tourism in an area** — Physical attractions (climate, coast, scenery); cultural and historic attractions (heritage, music, festivals, sport); access (airports, ports, low-cost airlines); accommodation and services; marketing; cost and exchange rates; safety and political stability. _(T1)_
- **Irish tourism: its importance and the COVID-19 collapse** — About 11 million overseas visits and about a quarter of a million jobs (2019); main markets Britain, the USA, Germany and France. COVID-19 travel restrictions collapsed visits in 2020–2021, causing job losses, closed hotels and lost revenue in remote areas. _(T2)_
- **Irish tourism agencies and marketing brands** — Fáilte Ireland (2003) develops tourism in the Republic; Tourism Ireland, created under the Good Friday Agreement (1998), markets the whole island. The Wild Atlantic Way (2014) runs about 2,500 km from Inishowen to Kinsale; Ireland's Ancient East followed in 2015. _(T2)_
- **Why Ireland's east coast attracts the most visitors** — Dublin Airport and Dublin Port are the main entry points; Dublin, the capital, has the most attractions, hotels and events; city breaks suit short trips; good motorway and rail links radiate from Dublin. _(T3)_
- **Irish tourist attractions: four in the east and two in the west** — East: the Guinness Storehouse (2000), the most visited fee-paying attraction; the Book of Kells, Trinity College Dublin; Brú na Bóinne (Newgrange), UNESCO 1993; Glendalough, Co. Wicklow. West: the Cliffs of Moher, Co. Clare; Kylemore Abbey, Connemara. _(T6)_
- **Co. Kerry as a mass-tourism region: main attractions** — Killarney National Park (1932), Ireland's first, from the Muckross estate; the Lakes of Killarney, Torc Waterfall, Gap of Dunloe; Carrauntoohil (1,038 m), Ireland's highest mountain; the Ring of Kerry (about 180 km); Skellig Michael (UNESCO 1996). _(T4)_
- **Co. Kerry tourism: positive and negative impacts** — Positive: jobs and income in a peripheral region; rural services kept open; heritage protected. Negative: seasonal jobs; coach congestion on the narrow Ring of Kerry roads; erosion on Carrauntoohil paths and litter; holiday homes raising house prices for locals. _(T4)_
- **Spain's mass tourism: causes of its growth** — From the 1960s the Franco regime promoted tourism ('Spain is different') to earn foreign currency; jet aircraft and package holidays, later cheap flights; sun and beaches; low costs; nearness to northern Europe. About 84 million tourists in 2019; about 94 million in 2024. _(T5)_
- **Benidorm and other mass-tourism resorts in Spain** — Benidorm, Costa Blanca: a fishing village transformed in the 1950s–60s by mayor Pedro Zaragoza's high-rise plan; the Gran Hotel Bali (186 m, 2002) was then Europe's tallest hotel. Costa del Sol: Torremolinos, Marbella. Balearics: Mallorca (Magaluf), Ibiza. _(T5)_
- **Spain's mass tourism: four problems** — Concrete ribbon development and high-rise overbuilding on coasts; water shortages (Catalonia's drought emergency, early 2024); low-paid seasonal jobs; rents raised by short-term tourist lets, prompting 2024 protests in Barcelona, Mallorca and the Canary Islands. _(T5)_
- **Spain's mass tourism: economic effects and two responses** — Effects: jobs, foreign earnings and infrastructure; tourism gives over a tenth of Spain's GDP (2020s). Responses: the Balearic and Catalan governments charge tourist taxes; Barcelona plans to end licences for about 10,000 tourist apartments by 2028. _(T5)_
- **Evidence of industrial decline in a region: give six types** — Plant closures; derelict sites and buildings; high unemployment; emigration or out-migration; falling population in industrial towns; social problems. _(D1)_
- **Causes of industrial decline in Ireland** — The end of protection exposed small, inefficient firms (Anglo-Irish Free Trade Agreement 1965, EEC 1973); competition from low-wage countries; recessions (1980s, 2008); new technology replacing labour; EU reform such as the sugar regime; firms moving production abroad. _(D2)_
- **Cork's 1980s closures and the effects of decline** — Dunlop's tyre factory closed in 1983; Ford's Marina plant and Verolme Cork Dockyard, Rushbrooke, closed in 1984. Effects: job losses in Cork as national unemployment reached about 17% in the mid-1980s; heavy emigration; derelict docklands; lost skills and spending in local towns. _(D3, D4)_
- **Irish industrial closures since 2001: name five with dates** — Irish Steel, Haulbowline (2001), leaving a contaminated site; Irish Sugar's Carlow (2005) and Mallow (2006) factories after EU sugar reform; Waterford Crystal, Kilbarry (January 2009); Dell manufacturing, Limerick (2009, about 1,900 jobs); peat power stations (2020). _(D3)_
- **Responses to industrial decline in Ireland** — IDA and Enterprise Ireland replacement investment; regeneration of the Dublin Docklands (Docklands Development Authority 1997, IFSC 1987) and Cork Docklands; heritage reuse, like the House of Waterford Crystal on the Mall (2010); retraining; the EU Just Transition Fund. _(D5)_

### The mobility of modern economic activities and factors in their location (22)

- **Aughinish Alumina: what it makes, where, when and two problems** — Europe's largest alumina refinery, opened 1983 on Aughinish Island near Askeaton, Co. Limerick, on the Shannon Estuary. It refines imported bauxite into alumina for smelters elsewhere (not aluminium). Problems: red mud stored on site; owned by Russia's Rusal since 2007. _(C1)_
- **Aughinish Alumina: why it is located on the Shannon Estuary** — Bulk-reducing: two to three tonnes of bauxite make one tonne of alumina, so it sits at the port where bauxite arrives from Guinea and Brazil; deep, sheltered water for large bulk carriers; a flat island site away from towns; energy supply; government support. _(C1, L1)_
- **Intel, Leixlip: why a footloose firm chose this site** — Opened 1989. Skilled graduates, with Maynooth University nearby; IDA support and low corporation tax; access to the EU market from an English-speaking base; beside the M4 and close to Dublin Airport; a large greenfield site with electricity and water. _(C2)_
- **Intel, Leixlip: upgrading as an alternative to closure** — A plant survives by moving up the value chain: Fab 34 (2023) makes advanced chips using extreme ultraviolet lithography. The huge fixed investment makes moving unlikely, but Intel's global job cuts in 2024–2025 still affected Leixlip. _(B3, C2)_
- **Cork Harbour pharmaceutical cluster: location factors** — Deep-water berths at Ringaskiddy; large flat sites zoned for industry, with water supply and waste facilities; graduates from UCC and Munster Technological University (2021); IDA support and tax; clustering of Pfizer, Janssen and Novartis. _(C3, L10)_
- **Galway medical-technology cluster: how it grew** — DEC's Galway plant closed in 1993, leaving skilled engineers; Boston Scientific opened in 1994 and Medtronic followed at Parkmore; University of Galway research and a network of suppliers reinforced the cluster. _(C4, B4)_
- **Kerry Group and Killybegs: industries located at the raw material** — Dairy processing is bulk-reducing: Kerry Group grew from the North Kerry Milk Products co-operative at Listowel (1972), near the milk supply, and is based in Tralee. Killybegs, the largest fishing port, processes pelagic fish such as mackerel and herring. _(C5)_
- **Guinness, St James's Gate: why brewing is located near the market** — Founded in 1759 by Arthur Guinness on a 9,000-year lease. Brewing is bulk-gaining because water is added, so the brewery sits near its Dublin market. It still brews there today, an example of industrial inertia. _(C5, L11)_
- **Data centres in Ireland: location factors and the grid problem** — A cool, mild climate cuts cooling costs; fibre links to the USA and Europe; clusters of technology firms; tax. The problem is grid capacity: in 2022 EirGrid said it would not connect new Dublin data centres until about 2028, and in 2023 they used about 21% of metered electricity (CSO). _(C6, L2)_
- **Corrib gas: how local opposition delayed a project** — Discovered in 1996, with a terminal at Bellanaboy, Co. Mayo. The Shell to Sea campaign opposed it and the Rossport Five were jailed in 2005. Opposition and planning changes meant gas did not flow until December 2015. _(L13)_
- **Freight transport modes: advantages, and one problem of air transport** — Road: door-to-door flexibility over short and medium distances. Rail: heavy loads on fixed routes. Sea: cheapest for bulk over long distances. Air: fastest, suiting light, high-value goods such as medicines and microchips, but costly and a source of CO2 emissions. _(L5)_
- **Government policy as a location factor in Ireland** — IDA Ireland (1949; independent 1969) offers grants, serviced sites and advance factories; corporation tax 12.5% from 2003, 15% for very large groups from 2024; Enterprise Ireland (1998); Údarás na Gaeltachta (1980); regional policy; planning; EU state-aid rules. _(L7)_
- **How the importance of location factors has changed over time** — 19th century: raw materials and power, so industry clustered on coalfields. 20th century: markets and labour, as electricity grids freed most industry. Today: footloose activities chase skills, tax, connectivity and quality of life. _(L14, L2)_
- **Apple, Cork: a location shift through the product life cycle** — Apple opened at Hollyhill, Cork, in 1980 to make computers. As its manufacturing moved to contract manufacturers in Asia, the Cork site shifted mainly to operations, support and services; it is now Apple's European headquarters, with about 6,000 staff (2020s). _(P2)_
- **How a host country can hold on to mobile firms: two responses** — Attract firms early in new life cycles (pharmaceuticals, medical technology, software); invest in education and research so jobs are harder to move, e.g. Science Foundation Ireland (2000; merged into Research Ireland in 2024) and university graduates. _(P3)_
- **Broadband and the future location of jobs in Ireland** — Teleservices and remote work can locate anywhere with broadband, including peripheral areas, though low-skilled work moves abroad easily. National Broadband Plan contract 2019; Connected Hubs 2021; Our Rural Future 2021. Regional broadband gaps decide which areas gain. _(U2, U4)_
- **E-commerce: three effects on where activities locate** — Warehouses and delivery depots locate near motorways and airports; town-centre shops come under pressure; bank branches close as business moves online. _(U3)_
- **OS map or photo: how to write each reason for locating an activity** — Name the factor; give the evidence (a grid reference on the map, or the photo location in accepted notation such as left background); explain why it matters to the activity. Develop each reason in three to five SRPs, and use the same structure for a reason against. _(S1)_
- **OS map: evidence that a site suits a factory or multinational** — Flat land on the edge of a town; a national primary road or motorway, railway, port or airport; a town labour force and services such as schools, a hospital and housing; a water supply; an existing industrial estate. _(S2)_
- **OS map: evidence that a site suits a fish processing plant** — A pier, harbour or fishing port on the coast; roads to markets; a water supply; a labour force in a nearby town; low-lying land. _(S2)_
- **OS map: evidence that a site suits a hotel** — Coast and beach, scenery, lakes or mountains; antiquities; a golf course and water sports; nearby town services; road access; tourist information. _(S2)_
- **OS map: reasons an activity might not locate at a site** — Steep or high land; a floodplain; a protected or scenic area where planning conflict is likely; poor roads; remoteness from labour and services; impact on nearby residents. _(S2)_

## Economic development

### 1. Measuring economic development (24)

- **Economic growth: four benefits for a country** — More jobs; higher incomes; more tax revenue to fund services and infrastructure; less emigration. _(G6)_
- **GNP or GDP per head: four advantages as a measure** — It is simple, giving one comparable figure; data exist for almost every country, collected in a standard way; it lets change over time be tracked; it is closely linked to a government's ability to fund health, education and other services. _(G7)_
- **GNP per head: three things the average hides or leaves out** — It hides inequality between rich and poor and between regions; it ignores the informal economy and subsistence farming, which are large in developing countries; it ignores unpaid work such as caring, housework and volunteering. _(G8)_
- **GNP per head: four ways it misleads about well-being** — Counts harmful spending such as pollution clean-up, disaster repair and crime as growth; ignores environmental damage and resource depletion; exchange rates distort dollar comparisons unless PPP is used; says nothing about health, education or freedom. _(G8)_
- **Ireland: why GDP overstates prosperity, and how GNI* corrects it** — Multinationals send their profits abroad, so GNP is well below GDP; Irish GDP per head is second in the EU after Luxembourg (Eurostat, early 2020s). The CSO's GNI*, published from 2017, strips this out: in the early 2020s it was a little over half of GDP. _(G9)_
- **Leprechaun economics: what happened to Irish GDP for 2015** — Irish GDP growth for 2015, first published in July 2016, was about 26%, mainly because multinationals moved intellectual property (patents) and aircraft-leasing assets to Ireland, not because people were richer. US economist Paul Krugman named it. _(G9)_
- **HDI: who created it and when it was first published** — The UNDP first published it in the Human Development Report in 1990. Mahbub ul Haq, a Pakistani economist, created it working with Amartya Sen, an Indian economist who won the Nobel Prize in Economics in 1998. _(H1)_
- **HDI: the three dimensions and their four indicators** — A long and healthy life: life expectancy at birth. Knowledge: mean years of schooling of adults aged 25 and over, and expected years of schooling for children starting school. A decent standard of living: GNI per head in PPP dollars. _(H2)_
- **HDI: how the value is calculated and the four categories** — Each dimension becomes an index from 0 to 1 and the three are combined into one value between 0 and 1; closer to 1 is more developed. Very high 0.800 and above; high 0.700 to 0.799; medium 0.550 to 0.699; low below 0.550. _(H3, H4)_
- **HDI rankings: top and bottom in the 2020 and 2023/24 reports** — Human Development Report 2020 (2019 data): Norway 1st (0.957), Ireland and Switzerland joint 2nd (0.955), Niger last of 189 (0.394). Report 2023/24 (2022 data): Switzerland 1st (0.967), Ireland in the top ten, Somalia last of 193 (0.380). _(H5)_
- **HDI: where it is highest, and one example of each category** — Highest in Europe (especially Scandinavia and Switzerland), North America, Australia and Japan. 2023/24 report: very high, Switzerland; high, Brazil (about 0.76); medium, India (134th, 0.644); low, mostly sub-Saharan Africa, e.g. Chad, Niger. _(H5)_
- **HDI: four advantages as a measure of development** — It is broader than income alone, combining health, education and income; it focuses on people and outcomes; it can be tracked over time to show progress or decline; it helps governments and aid agencies target spending on schooling or health. _(H6)_
- **HDI: four disadvantages as a measure of development** — As a national average it hides inequality between rich and poor, men and women, and regions; it leaves out freedom, human rights, gender equality and the environment; data are poor or late in some developing countries; equal weighting is a judgement. _(H7)_
- **Three newer UNDP measures introduced in 2010** — The inequality-adjusted HDI (IHDI), the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and the Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI), the last developed with Oxford University's Oxford Poverty and Human Development Initiative. They answer some criticisms of the HDI. _(H8)_
- **GNP vs HDI: name countries that rank very differently on each** — Equatorial Guinea's oil wealth gives it a far higher GNI per head than its HDI rank suggests; oil-rich Gulf states generally rank higher on income than on HDI. Cuba ranks higher on HDI than on income because of strong public health care and education. _(H9, H6)_
- **Development indicators: social and economic examples** — Social: life expectancy; infant mortality; literacy rate; doctors per 1,000 people; access to safe water and sanitation; daily calorie intake. Economic: share of workers in the primary sector; unemployment rate; energy use per head; internet access. _(O1, O2)_
- **Why life expectancy is lower in some countries: five reasons** — Scarce doctors, hospitals and clinics; lack of vaccination, clean water and sanitation; infectious disease such as malaria and HIV/AIDS; war and conflict; poverty and poor nutrition. In rich countries, diet, smoking, alcohol and obesity shorten some lives. _(O4)_
- **Life expectancy at birth: three figures to quote (early 2020s)** — Japan about 84 years; Ireland about 82 to 83 years, among the highest in the EU; below 60 years in several sub-Saharan African countries, such as Chad, Nigeria and Lesotho. _(O4)_
- **Malaria and HIV/AIDS: their effect on life expectancy** — Malaria caused about 600,000 deaths in 2022, over nine-tenths of them in Africa (WHO World Malaria Report 2023). HIV/AIDS lowered life expectancy in Botswana, Zimbabwe and South Africa in the 1990s and 2000s; it has since recovered with antiretroviral drugs. _(O4)_
- **Why years of schooling differ between countries: six reasons** — Government spending on schools and teachers; poverty forcing children to work or stay at home; distance to schools in rural areas; fees and the cost of uniforms and books; cultural attitudes to girls' education and early marriage; conflict closing schools. _(O5)_
- **Why an educated workforce benefits an economy: four reasons** — It attracts foreign investment in high-tech and service industries; higher productivity and wages raise tax revenue; it brings more innovation and new firms; educated women tend to have healthier, smaller families. _(O6)_
- **Ireland: how education has helped its economy** — Free secondary education came in 1967. Ireland has one of the EU's highest shares of 25 to 34-year-olds with third-level education (early 2020s, Eurostat), supplying graduates to multinationals in pharmaceuticals, medical devices and information technology. _(O6)_
- **High unemployment: effects, remedies and Ireland's rates** — Effects: lost income and poverty; youth emigration; lower tax revenue and higher welfare costs. Remedies: attract investment; retraining; support small firms; infrastructure spending. Ireland: about 15% in 2012 after the 2008 crash, below 5% by 2022 (CSO). _(O7, G6)_
- **Skill: what a bar chart of development data needs** — A title; labelled axes with units; a suitable scale; accurate bars; a key. To find a difference, subtract the lower value from the higher and state the unit: illustrative life expectancies of 80 and 60 years differ by 20 years. _(O8)_

### 2. Spatial variations in development (25)

- **Brandt Line: who drew it, where it runs, and its weakness** — From the 1980 Brandt Report, by a commission under former West German Chancellor Willy Brandt. It runs along about 30°N, dipping to put Australia and New Zealand in the North. A simplification: Singapore, South Korea and the Gulf states were placed in the South but are now rich. _(W2)_
- **Global pattern: where HDI is low, and how unequal wealth is** — Low HDI is concentrated in sub-Saharan Africa, plus Afghanistan, Yemen and Haiti, the poorest country in the Americas. The richest 10% of the world's people own about 76% of global wealth and the poorest half about 2% (World Inequality Report 2022). _(W3, W4)_
- **EU: where GDP per head is highest and lowest, and where the core is** — Luxembourg highest, Ireland second, Bulgaria lowest (Eurostat, early 2020s). The richer core is the north-west Blue Banana; the periphery lies in the east and south. Romania and Bulgaria contain many of the EU's less developed regions. _(E1, E2)_
- **Ireland's regional divide: richest and poorest regions, and why** — The Dublin region has the highest disposable income per person (CSO): capital and port functions, multinational and financial jobs, universities, airport. Border and Midland regions are among the lowest: remoteness, weak infrastructure, farming, out-migration. _(E3)_
- **Irish regional policy: three milestones since 2000** — Objective 1 status for the BMW region, 2000 to 2006; three regional assemblies from 2015 (Northern and Western, Southern, Eastern and Midland); Project Ireland 2040 (February 2018): the National Planning Framework plus the National Development Plan. _(E4)_
- **National Planning Framework: its two growth targets** — The Northern and Western and Southern regions together should grow as much as the Eastern and Midland region; half of future population growth should go to the five cities, Dublin, Cork, Limerick, Galway and Waterford. _(E4)_
- **How transport investment can reduce regional income differences** — Better access attracts firms to regional towns, cuts costs and commuting times and widens the job market; it also eases congestion. Examples: motorways and Metrolink (Swords via Dublin Airport to the city centre). The National Broadband Plan (2019) does the same for digital access. _(E4)_
- **Climate as a factor: the Sahel, and why climate does not decide development** — Repeated droughts since the 1970s in the Sahel (Mali, Niger, Chad, Burkina Faso) limit farming and cause famine; drought, worsened by civil war, contributed to the 1984 to 1985 Ethiopian famine (Live Aid, 1985). Yet Singapore is tropical and one of the world's richest countries. _(P1)_
- **Disease, relief and soils: how they affect development** — Malaria and other tropical diseases cut life expectancy, workers' productivity and children's schooling. Mountains, deserts and poor soils limit farming and raise transport costs; fertile lowlands and river plains support dense populations and farm surpluses. _(P2, P3)_
- **Landlocked countries: why they lag, with named examples** — They depend on neighbours' roads and ports, which raises trade costs, while coastal countries with good harbours trade more easily. Examples: Chad, Niger, Mali, Burkina Faso, Malawi, Uganda, Afghanistan and Bolivia. _(P4)_
- **Natural resources: one success and two resource-curse examples** — Norway: North Sea oil since the early 1970s; its sovereign wealth fund is among the world's largest. Nigeria: Niger Delta oil dominates exports, with spills and conflict. DR Congo: about 70% of world cobalt (early 2020s), yet one of the poorest countries. _(P5)_
- **Haiti 2010: how a natural hazard set development back** — The earthquake of 12 January 2010, magnitude 7.0, struck near Port-au-Prince and killed over 100,000 people (estimates vary). Hazards destroy infrastructure and set poor countries back for years; rich countries recover faster. _(P6)_
- **Social factors: population growth, education, health and gender** — Fast population growth spreads school and health spending thinly (Niger: about 6 to 7 children per woman); an educated workforce attracts investment, while low female literacy holds development back; disease and HIV/AIDS shrink the workforce; educating girls means smaller, healthier families. _(S1, S2, S3, S4)_
- **Cultural factors: four that affect development, with examples** — Early marriage cuts girls' schooling and raises fertility; caste in India and land concentration in Latin America limit opportunity; many languages in colonial borders hinder unity, though English helps India's IT sector; South Korea prizes education. _(C1, C2, C3, C5)_
- **Political stability vs war: three named examples** — Stability: Botswana, independent in 1966, has held regular multi-party elections ever since. War destroys infrastructure and deters investors: Syria's civil war from 2011 made millions refugees; South Sudan, independent July 2011, at war from December 2013. _(L1)_
- **Corruption and government policy: named examples** — Corruption diverts public money and aid: the Corruption Perceptions Index puts Denmark near the top and Somalia and South Sudan near the bottom (2020s). Good policy helped South Korea, China and Ireland; mismanagement harmed Zimbabwe and Venezuela. _(L2, L4)_
- **Why a country's HDI falls: three causes, with examples** — War, economic collapse or disease. Syria's HDI fell in the civil war that began in 2011, reversing decades of progress; Zimbabwe and Venezuela suffered economic collapse; HIV/AIDS sharply cut life expectancy in Botswana in the 1990s and 2000s. _(T1, T6, T4)_
- **South Korea: starting point and how industry was planned** — The Korean War (1950 to 1953) left the South among the world's poorest countries. From the 1960s, under Park Chung-hee, the government planned export-led industrialisation: textiles first, then steel, shipbuilding, cars and electronics. _(T2)_
- **South Korea: what drove growth, and the milestones since** — Chaebol such as Samsung, Hyundai and LG, and heavy investment in education, not natural resources. Joined the OECD in 1996; hit by the 1997 Asian financial crisis, then recovered; reclassified as a developed country by UNCTAD in 2021; now very high HDI. _(T2)_
- **China: three reforms that opened its economy** — Economic reforms from 1978 under Deng Xiaoping (opening up); Special Economic Zones from 1980, the first at Shenzhen beside Hong Kong; membership of the World Trade Organization in 2001. _(T3)_
- **China: results of growth, and the inequality it brought** — Second-largest economy since 2010 (overtaking Japan) but upper-middle income; close to 800 million out of extreme poverty since 1978 (World Bank, 2022). Coastal provinces are far richer than the western interior and drew hundreds of millions of rural migrants. _(T3)_
- **Botswana: how it rose from poverty after 1966** — Independent in 1966, one of the world's poorest. Diamonds (Orapa, 1967), mined by Debswana (government and De Beers), paid for roads, schools and clinics; stable democracy and comparatively low corruption helped. Now upper-middle income, but diamond-dependent. _(T4, P5)_
- **Ireland's rise: from 1950s emigration to EEC membership** — Net emigration was about 400,000 in 1951 to 1961. T. K. Whitaker's Economic Development (1958) led to the First Programme for Economic Expansion: free trade, grants and tax breaks for foreign firms. Free secondary education, 1967; EEC entry, 1 January 1973. _(T5)_
- **Ireland: Celtic Tiger, crash and bailout dates** — Celtic Tiger boom, mid-1990s to 2007. Property crash and banking crisis from 2008; EU–IMF bailout of €85 billion agreed in November 2010; Ireland exited the programme in December 2013. _(T5, L6)_
- **Zimbabwe and Venezuela: how their economies collapsed** — Zimbabwe: land seizures from 2000 cut farm output; hyperinflation peaked in 2008; the Zimbabwe dollar was abandoned in 2009. Venezuela: oil-dependent; after the 2014 oil price collapse the economy shrank by well over half and over 7 million emigrated (2024). _(T6)_

### 3. Case study of a developing economy (32)

- **The colonial economy: four typical features** — Colonies sent cheap raw materials (cotton, sugar, rubber, minerals) to the colonial power and bought its manufactured goods; competing local industries were destroyed; plantations and mines used forced or slave labour; export cash crops replaced food crops. _(K3)_
- **Colonialism: four lasting political and social legacies** — Artificial borders split some ethnic groups and grouped rivals together; 'divide and rule' and weak institutions handed over at independence led to conflict and coups; education only for a small elite; land owned by a few. _(K4, K5, K6)_
- **India: five dates in British colonial rule** — East India Company chartered 31 December 1600; Battle of Plassey 1757 (Robert Clive) began Company rule in Bengal; Rebellion of 1857; Government of India Act 1858 transferred rule to the Crown (the Raj); independence and Partition, 15 August 1947. _(I1)_
- **India: how colonial rule destroyed its textile industry** — Cheap machine-made Lancashire cloth destroyed India's handloom textiles (Bengal cottons, Dhaka muslin), while raw cotton was shipped to the mills. Angus Maddison estimated India's share of world GDP at about a quarter around 1700 and about 4% by 1950. _(I2)_
- **India: five cash crops grown under British rule** — Cotton, exported to Lancashire; jute, processed in mills along the Hooghly near Calcutta; indigo, a blue dye; tea from Assam plantations from the 1830s–40s; opium, exported to China. They replaced food crops. _(I3)_
- **India: land revenue and famines under British rule** — The Permanent Settlement of Bengal (1793, Lord Cornwallis) took heavy land revenue through zamindars, leaving peasants in debt. Famines: the Great Famine of 1876–78; the Bengal famine of 1943 (about 2 to 3 million deaths), while grain was still exported. _(I4)_
- **India: why the railways were built, and the first line** — Mainly to carry raw materials to the ports of Bombay and Calcutta and to move troops, though Indians came to use them heavily. First passenger train: Bombay to Thane, 16 April 1853. Calcutta was the capital until 1911, then Delhi. _(I6)_
- **India: social and political legacies at independence** — Literacy about 18% (1951 census) and life expectancy around 32 years; Partition displaced more than 10 million and killed hundreds of thousands; Kashmir disputed with Pakistan since 1947; a civil service, courts, parliament and English, now an asset for IT. _(I7)_
- **India: state-led development after 1947** — First Five-Year Plan (1951) under Jawaharlal Nehru; import substitution behind high tariffs; state steel plants at Bhilai, Rourkela and Durgapur; the Licence Raj; slow growth (the Hindu rate of growth). The Green Revolution made India self-sufficient in food. _(I9, I10)_
- **India's 1991 crisis: what happened and the reforms that followed** — Reserves fell to a few weeks of imports, so India pledged gold and borrowed from the IMF. Narasimha Rao and Manmohan Singh then removed most licences, cut tariffs, welcomed foreign investment, privatised some state firms and devalued the rupee. _(I11)_
- **India: four results of opening up to the global economy** — Fast growth, with services over half of GDP; an IT and outsourcing industry in Bengaluru (TCS, Infosys); multinationals encouraged by Make in India (2014); among the world's five largest economies (early 2020s) and the largest recipient of remittances (2020s). _(I12)_
- **India: four problems that remain after liberalisation** — About nine-tenths of workers in the informal sector; about 55% in agriculture (Census 2011), with low productivity; literacy about 94% in Kerala, about 62% in Bihar (Census 2011); HDI 0.644, 134th of 193, medium (2023/24 report). _(I13)_
- **Brazil: three dates in Portuguese colonisation** — The Treaty of Tordesillas (1494) gave the east of South America to Portugal; Pedro Álvares Cabral reached Brazil on 22 April 1500; independence came on 7 September 1822 under Dom Pedro I. _(B1)_
- **Brazil: the colonial export cycles in order** — Brazilwood, a red dye wood (the country's name); sugar in Pernambuco and Bahia (North-East); gold in Minas Gerais; coffee in São Paulo state (19th century); rubber at Manaus until about 1912, undercut by plantations in British South-East Asia. _(B2)_
- **Brazil: slavery and its legacy** — About 4 to 5 million enslaved Africans were taken to Brazil, more than any other country in the Americas. The Lei Áurea (Golden Law) ended slavery on 13 May 1888, the last in the Americas. Afro-Brazilians are over-represented among the poor and in favelas. _(B3)_
- **Brazil: effects of colonisation on indigenous people and land** — Indigenous peoples were killed by disease, war and enslavement and lost their land: about 1.7 million today, under 1% of the population (Census 2022). Latifundia from Portuguese land grants left land with a few families; the MST (1984) campaigns for land. _(B4, B5)_
- **Brazil: how colonialism left regional inequality** — Sugar's decline left the North-East poor; wealth and industry moved to the South-East (São Paulo, Rio de Janeiro, Minas Gerais), drawing migrants to favelas. SUDENE (1959) was set up to develop the North-East. Coffee dominated exports after independence. _(B6, B7)_
- **Brazil: import substitution and the Brazilian miracle** — Getúlio Vargas (1930–45, 1951–54) began state-led industry; Petrobras, 1953; Juscelino Kubitschek (1956–61): fifty years of progress in five, a car industry, Brasília (inaugurated 21 April 1960); the Brazilian miracle, about 1968–73, under military rule. _(B8)_
- **Brazil: debt crisis and stabilisation** — Heavy foreign borrowing led to the 1980s debt crisis, the lost decade, with hyperinflation and IMF programmes. The Plano Real (1994) ended hyperinflation and introduced the real as the currency. _(B9)_
- **Brazil: privatisation and trade blocs when opening up** — Vale, the iron-ore miner with Carajás mines in Pará, was privatised in 1997. Mercosur was founded in 1991 by the Treaty of Asunción with Argentina, Paraguay and Uruguay. Brazil is in BRICS: Jim O'Neill of Goldman Sachs coined BRIC in 2001; first summit 2009. _(B10)_
- **Brazil: commodity boom and a high-tech export** — Soya, iron ore, oil, beef and coffee, especially to China, Brazil's largest trading partner since 2009. Brazil is the world's largest producer and exporter of soya beans and largest coffee producer (2020s). Embraer (São José dos Campos) exports aircraft. _(B10)_
- **Brazil: Bolsa Família and the limits of poverty reduction** — Bolsa Família (2003, President Lula da Silva) pays poor families if children attend school and are vaccinated; Brazil left the FAO World Hunger Map in 2014. But inequality is among the world's highest by the Gini coefficient; HDI about 0.76 (2023/24 report). _(B11)_
- **Brazil: four costs of adjusting to the global economy** — Amazon deforestation, driven mainly by cattle ranching and also soya; dependence on commodity prices, bringing recession in 2015–16 when prices fell; favelas; the North-East still the poorest region. _(B12)_
- **Sudan: five dates in its colonial history** — Egyptian conquest 1821; Mahdist state 1885–98 (the Mahdi's forces took Khartoum and killed General Gordon in 1885); Battle of Omdurman, 2 September 1898 (Kitchener); Anglo-Egyptian Condominium 1899–1956, in practice British rule; independence 1 January 1956. _(U1)_
- **Gezira Scheme: location, size, purpose and effect** — Between the Blue and White Niles south of Khartoum, watered by the Sennar Dam on the Blue Nile (1925); nearly 900,000 hectares. Tenant farmers grew long-staple cotton for Lancashire mills under strict control, so Sudan depended on one crop's world price. _(U2)_
- **Sudan: how British rule divided the country** — Investment went to the Nile valley around Khartoum and Gezira; the south and Darfur were neglected. The Southern Policy (1920s) and Closed District Ordinance (1922) kept the Arab, Muslim north and the African, largely Christian and animist south apart; power then went to a northern elite. _(U3, U4, U5)_
- **Sudan: the two civil wars and South Sudan's independence** — First civil war 1955–72, ended by the Addis Ababa Agreement; second 1983–2005, about 2 million deaths, ended by the Comprehensive Peace Agreement (January 2005). About 99% voted for independence in January 2011; South Sudan became independent on 9 July 2011. _(U6)_
- **Darfur conflict: start date and two key facts** — It began in 2003. The UN estimated about 300,000 deaths, and the International Criminal Court issued an arrest warrant for President al-Bashir in 2009. _(U6)_
- **Sudan after independence: four features of its economy before oil** — Continued dependence on Gezira cotton; state-led development attempts; heavy borrowing leading to debt and IMF programmes; famine in the 1980s caused by drought and war. _(U7)_
- **Sudan's oil: when exports began and why the boom ended** — Exports began in 1999 by pipeline to Port Sudan, with China the main buyer and investor; GDP grew fast in the 2000s. Most oil lay in the south, so South Sudan's independence in 2011 took about three-quarters of output, causing inflation and protests. _(U8)_
- **Sudan: isolation, debt and other primary exports** — US listing as a state sponsor of terrorism (1993–2020) cut it off from Western banks and investment; one of the world's largest debt burdens, reaching the HIPC decision point in 2021. Exports: gum arabic (world's largest producer), gold, livestock, sesame. _(U9, U10)_
- **Sudan today: political turmoil since 2019** — Omar al-Bashir (in power 1989–2019) was removed in April 2019 after mass protests; military coup October 2021; war between the SAF and RSF since 15 April 2023; more than 10 million displaced (2024); famine confirmed in Zamzam camp, North Darfur, August 2024. _(U11)_

### 4. Global issues and a justice perspective (25)

- **Global extreme poverty: figure, trend since 1990 and setbacks** — About 700 million people (under $2.15 a day, 2017 prices) in 2022, mostly in sub-Saharan Africa. The share fell from over a third in 1990 to under a tenth by 2019, especially in China; COVID-19 in 2020 caused the first rise in about a generation (World Bank). _(J2, V1, V2)_
- **A justice perspective: what it asks, and its human-rights basis** — It asks whether the distribution of wealth and power is fair, who decides and who benefits, treating development as a right, not charity. The Universal Declaration of Human Rights (10 December 1948) includes rights to food, health, education and decent work. _(J1, J3)_
- **MDGs and SDGs: number of goals, dates and progress** — MDGs: eight goals, 2000 to 2015; halving the extreme-poverty share (from 1990) was met by 2010, five years early, largely thanks to China. SDGs: 17 goals adopted in September 2015 for 2030, e.g. no poverty, zero hunger, climate action; the world is off track. _(J4)_
- **Unfair trade: three ways poor exporters lose out** — They depend on a few commodities (coffee, cocoa, cotton) with low, unstable prices and worsening terms of trade; growers get a small share of the final price, most going to rich-country brands and retailers; tariff escalation discourages local processing. _(T1, T2, T3)_
- **Rich-country farm subsidies: the cotton example** — US cotton subsidies (and the EU's Common Agricultural Policy in the past) let surpluses be dumped abroad, undercutting poor farmers such as the Cotton-4: Benin, Burkina Faso, Chad and Mali. The WTO ruled for Brazil against US cotton subsidies (2004–05). _(T4)_
- **Two trade responses: the Doha Round and Everything But Arms** — The WTO Doha Development Round, launched in 2001, has stalled without agreement. The EU's Everything But Arms scheme (2001) gives the least developed countries duty-free, quota-free access to the EU for all products except arms and ammunition. _(T5)_
- **Fair trade: five ways it helps producers** — A minimum price covering sustainable production (or the market price plus premium when that is higher); a premium paid to co-operatives for schools, clinics and clean water; long-term contracts; decent conditions and no child labour; environmental standards. _(T6)_
- **Fair trade: first label, products and Irish links** — Max Havelaar, the first label, began in the Netherlands in 1988 for Mexican coffee. Products include coffee, cocoa and bananas. Fairtrade Ireland licenses the Mark; towns and schools win Fairtrade status; Fairtrade Fortnight runs late February to March. _(T6, T7)_
- **Fair trade: four limits** — It is a tiny share of world trade; certified products can cost consumers more; certification is expensive for small producers; it does not change the trade rules that cause unfair trade. _(T8)_
- **The debt crisis: how it arose, in order** — 1970s oil price rises gave oil exporters surplus money, which banks lent to developing countries (petrodollars). Interest rates then soared and commodity prices fell. In August 1982 Mexico said it could not pay, starting the Latin American debt crisis. _(D1)_
- **Debt: effects on poor countries, and why it is unjust** — Repayments exceeded health and education spending: a lost decade in the 1980s. Structural adjustment cut public spending, added school and health fees and removed food subsidies, hurting the poorest. Odious debts were borrowed by dictators who stole or wasted the money, yet the people repay them. _(D2, D3, D5)_
- **Debt relief: campaigns, initiatives and results** — Jubilee 2000 campaigned for cancellation; the IMF and World Bank launched HIPC in 1996; the Multilateral Debt Relief Initiative followed the Gleneagles G8 summit (July 2005), backed by Make Poverty History. Over 30 countries, most in Africa, got relief. _(D4)_
- **Name six types of aid** — Emergency (humanitarian) aid after disasters; long-term development aid; bilateral, government to government; multilateral, through the UN, World Bank or EU; NGO (voluntary) aid; tied aid, which must be spent on the donor's goods or services. _(A1)_
- **The UN aid target: what it is and who meets it** — Set in 1970: rich countries should give 0.7% of gross national income as official development aid. Norway, Sweden, Luxembourg and Denmark have reached or exceeded it in recent years; Ireland has not yet (2020s). _(A2)_
- **How aid helps development, and what makes it work best** — It saves lives in emergencies; funds schools, clinics, wells and vaccination; builds roads; trains teachers, nurses and farmers; supports women's groups and small businesses. It works best untied, targeted at the poorest, locally owned and transparent. _(A3, A5)_
- **How aid can hinder development: five ways** — Tied aid benefits donor firms and may bring unsuitable technology; corrupt elites can steal it; food aid can undercut local farmers; it can create dependency, and loans add to debt; donors' political interests, not need, can decide who gets it. _(A4)_
- **Irish Aid: five facts** — Ireland's official aid programme, in the Department of Foreign Affairs; bilateral since 1974; untied; partners include Ethiopia, Malawi, Mozambique, Tanzania, Uganda, Zambia and Vietnam; A Better World (2019) puts the furthest behind first. _(A6)_
- **Three Irish NGOs: when each was founded, and by whom or why** — Concern Worldwide, 1968, in response to the famine in Biafra during Nigeria's civil war; Trócaire, 1973, by the Irish Catholic bishops, known for its Lenten box and campaigns on human rights and climate; GOAL, 1977, by John O'Shea. _(A7)_
- **Multinationals in low-wage countries: benefits vs exploitation** — They locate labour-intensive work (clothing, electronics assembly, mining) there. Benefits: jobs, wages above farm work, new skills. Exploitation: very low wages, long hours, unsafe factories, banned trade unions and child labour. _(W1)_
- **Rana Plaza, 2013: what happened** — An eight-storey building of garment factories in Savar, near Dhaka, Bangladesh, collapsed on 24 April 2013, killing about 1,130 people and injuring about 2,500. Cracks had appeared the day before, but workers were ordered back in. _(W2)_
- **Rana Plaza: the brands involved and the response** — Its factories supplied Western brands, including Primark (founded in Dublin in 1969 as Penneys), which paid compensation. In May 2013 brands and unions signed the Accord on Fire and Building Safety in Bangladesh, with independent factory inspections. _(W2)_
- **Child labour: global figure and the cobalt example** — About 160 million children were in child labour in 2020 (ILO and UNICEF), about 70% in agriculture. The DR Congo produces about 70% of the world's cobalt, used in batteries; Amnesty International (2016) documented child labour in its hand-dug mines. _(W3)_
- **Four responses to the exploitation of workers** — Ethical consumer campaigns; company codes of conduct; trade unions pressing for safer, better-paid work; the EU Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive (2024), making very large companies address human-rights and environmental harm in supply chains. _(W4)_
- **Climate justice: who emits least but suffers most** — Bangladesh, a low-lying delta, faces cyclones, floods and sea-level rise; Tuvalu, Kiribati and the Maldives are threatened by rising seas; sub-Saharan Africa produced only a small share of historical emissions yet its dry regions suffer drought. _(C1)_
- **Climate justice: two responses with names and dates** — The Loss and Damage Fund, agreed at COP27 in Sharm el-Sheikh, Egypt (2022), made operational at COP28 in Dubai (2023); the Mary Robinson Foundation – Climate Justice (2010), set up by the former President of Ireland (1990–97). _(C2)_

## Economic environment

### 1. Renewable and non-renewable resources in the economy (24)

- **Peat and nuclear energy: why neither is renewable** — Peat: a bog grows only about 1 mm a year, far slower than it is cut, so it is used up faster than it forms. Nuclear: uranium is finite, so nuclear is a low-carbon alternative to fossil fuels but not renewable. _(R6, R7)_
- **Ireland's energy import dependence: how it has changed** — Ireland imported close to 90% of its energy before Corrib gas came ashore in 2015; the share fell after Corrib began and has risen again as Corrib's output declines; in the early 2020s Ireland still imported about three-quarters (SEAI). _(I1)_
- **Ireland's primary energy mix in the early 2020s** — Oil just under half, the largest single source (all imported, used mainly for transport and home heating, refined at Whitegate, Co. Cork, the only refinery); natural gas about a third; renewables a little over a tenth; coal and peat small and falling (SEAI). _(I1, I2)_
- **Kinsale Head and Corrib gas fields: key dates** — Kinsale Head, off Co. Cork: discovered 1971, produced from 1978 as Ireland's first commercial gas field, ended 2020 when depleted. Corrib, off north-west Co. Mayo: discovered 1996, first gas December 2015, processed at Bellanaboy. _(I3)_
- **Natural gas in Ireland: where it comes from now** — For a time after 2015 Corrib supplied over half of Ireland's gas, but its output is declining; most gas now comes from Britain through interconnector pipelines from Moffat, Scotland. Gas is the largest single source of Irish electricity. _(I3, I11)_
- **Coal in Ireland: native coalfields and Moneypoint** — Arigna, Co. Roscommon (mining ended 1990) and Castlecomer, Co. Kilkenny are long closed. Moneypoint, Co. Clare, on the Shannon Estuary: ESB station opened 1985, Ireland's largest (about 900 MW), burned imported coal shipped to its deep-water jetty until 2025. _(I4)_
- **Peat power in Ireland: how it ended** — Bord na Móna, the State peat company, was founded in 1946. Peat stations at Lanesborough (Lough Ree) and Shannonbridge (West Offaly) closed in December 2020; all peat harvesting ended in January 2021; Edenderry, Co. Offaly, switched to biomass. _(I5)_
- **Cutaway bogs: two examples of reuse** — Bellacorick, Co. Mayo: the peat station closed in 2003 and was replaced by wind farms on the cutaway bog, including Oweninny. Lough Boora Discovery Park, Co. Offaly: parkland, wetlands and walking and cycling trails. _(I5, I7)_
- **Hydroelectric power in Ireland: name the stations and their rivers** — Ardnacrusha on the Shannon near Limerick (Siemens, 1929; the ESB was set up in 1927 to run it); Poulaphouca on the Liffey; Inniscarra and Carrigadrohid on the Lee; Cathaleen's Fall on the Erne; Turlough Hill, Co. Wicklow (1974), pumped storage. _(I6)_
- **Wind energy in Ireland: its share and why it suits Ireland** — Wind, Ireland's main renewable, supplied about a third of electricity in the early 2020s and all renewables about two-fifths (but only a little over a tenth of primary energy). Strong, frequent Atlantic winds make western coasts and uplands the best sites. _(I7, R8)_
- **Name three Irish wind farms, onshore and offshore** — Bellacorick, Co. Mayo (1992), Ireland's first commercial wind farm; Galway Wind Park, Connemara (completed 2017), one of the largest onshore; Arklow Bank Wind Park, off Co. Wicklow (2004), the first offshore, with seven turbines. _(I7)_
- **Solar, wave, geothermal and biomass in Ireland: one fact each** — Solar: rapid growth since the early 2020s (solar farms, rooftop panels, SEAI home grants); wave: Atlantic Marine Energy Test Site off Belmullet, Co. Mayo; geothermal: only low-temperature ground-source heat pumps; biomass: Edenderry power station, Co. Offaly, switched from peat to biomass. _(I8)_
- **Ireland's 2020 EU renewable energy target: was it met?** — No. The target was 16% of gross final energy consumption from renewables by 2020; Ireland reached only about 13.5%, one of the furthest from target of any EU member state. _(I9)_
- **Ireland's climate and energy targets for 2030 and 2050** — Climate Action Plan: 80% of electricity from renewables and at least 5 GW of offshore wind by 2030. Climate Action and Low Carbon Development (Amendment) Act 2021: cut emissions by 51% by 2030 (compared with 2018) and reach net zero by 2050. _(I9)_
- **Why Ireland lagged behind other EU countries on renewables** — Heavy reliance on cars and road freight; oil home heating in a dispersed rural settlement pattern; slow planning and grid connection for wind; no large hydro potential left; a late start on offshore wind; fast economic and population growth raising demand. _(I10)_
- **Ireland's electricity interconnectors: name two** — East-West Interconnector, Co. Dublin to north Wales, opened 2012; Celtic Interconnector, Co. Cork to Brittany, France, under construction and due in the late 2020s to link Ireland to the EU mainland grid. _(I11)_
- **Energy use by sector in Ireland: order and a fast-growing user** — Transport is the largest energy-using sector (SEAI), then heat for homes and businesses, then electricity generation for industry and services. Data centres used about a fifth of metered electricity in 2023 (CSO), most in the Dublin region. _(I12)_
- **European energy supply: imports and the main suppliers** — The EU imports close to three-fifths of its energy (early 2020s, Eurostat). The North Sea (UK and Norway) is the main oil and gas province; Norway, outside the EU, pipes gas to it. Core Germany and France consume most; peripheral Ireland imports. _(E1, E6)_
- **Russia, Ukraine and REPowerEU: what happened in 2022** — Before 2022 Russia supplied about two-fifths of the EU's imported gas. Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022 and gas and electricity prices soared. REPowerEU (May 2022): save energy, import more LNG (much from the USA), speed up renewables. _(E2)_
- **France and Poland: contrasting electricity mixes** — France: nuclear power supplies about two-thirds of its electricity. Poland: coal generates well over half of its electricity (early 2020s). _(E3)_
- **Germany's Energiewende: three key facts** — A long-term switch to renewables. After Fukushima (March 2011) Germany phased out nuclear; its last three reactors closed on 15 April 2023. It still burns lignite from open-cast mines in the Rhineland; a 2020 law ends coal power by 2038 at the latest. _(E3)_
- **Denmark, Norway and Iceland: how each makes its electricity** — Denmark: wind about half, an early leader and home of turbine maker Vestas. Norway: hydro about 90% (mountains, high rainfall, glacial lakes). Iceland: geothermal and hydro almost all; about 9 in 10 homes heated geothermally. _(E3)_
- **EU renewable energy targets: 2020 and 2030** — The 20% target for 2020 was achieved, at about 22%; the revised Renewable Energy Directive (2023) requires at least 42.5% by 2030; renewables supplied about a quarter of EU gross final energy consumption in 2023. _(E4)_
- **EU energy consumption: by fuel and by sector** — Fossil fuels still supply most of the EU's energy, and oil is the largest single source, mainly for transport. Transport, households and industry each take roughly a quarter to a third of final energy; services take less. _(E5)_

### 2. Burning fossil fuels and using alternative energy sources (25)

- **World fossil fuels: where oil, gas and coal are produced** — Middle East (e.g. Saudi Arabia, Iran, Iraq): about half of proven oil reserves; Venezuela: the most of any one country; USA: top oil producer since the late 2010s (fracking); Russia and Qatar (LNG): leading gas exporters; China: about half the world's coal. _(P2, P4)_
- **World fossil fuels: share of energy and who consumes them** — Fossil fuels give about four-fifths of world primary energy. The USA, Canada and Australia use many times more energy per person than India or most of sub-Saharan Africa; demand grows fastest in Asia; China emits most CO2 in total, the USA far more per person. _(P1, P3)_
- **Burning fossil fuels: name five pollutants released** — Carbon dioxide, the main greenhouse gas; sulphur dioxide, mainly from coal and heavy oil; nitrogen oxides, mainly from vehicles and power stations; smoke and fine particles (soot); carbon monoxide. _(F1, F2)_
- **Acid rain: how it forms, step by step** — Coal-fired power stations, heavy industry and vehicles emit SO2 and NOx; these dissolve in water vapour to form sulphuric and nitric acids; the acids fall as wet deposition in rain or as dry deposition of acidic particles and gases. _(F3)_
- **Acid rain in Europe: where it came from and where it fell** — Emissions from Britain and Germany were carried by the prevailing south-westerly winds to Norway and Sweden. In the Black Triangle (former East Germany, Poland, Czechia) lignite-burning power stations caused severe forest death in the 1970s and 1980s. _(F3)_
- **Acid rain: five effects to describe** — Forests lose needles and leaves as nutrients are leached (Waldsterben, Black Forest, 1980s); thousands of Scandinavian lakes acidified as aluminium from the soil killed fish; limestone and marble buildings dissolve; metal corrodes; respiratory illness. _(F4)_
- **Acid rain: four responses that cut emissions** — The 1979 Convention on Long-Range Transboundary Air Pollution (UNECE, Geneva); EU limits on large power stations; flue-gas desulphurisation, fitted at Moneypoint in the late 2000s; catalytic converters, compulsory on new EU petrol cars from 1993. _(F5)_
- **Acid rain: other responses and the outcome** — Low-sulphur fuels; switching from coal to gas and renewables; Sweden and Norway have limed acidified lakes since the 1970s and 1980s. European SO2 emissions have fallen by well over three-quarters since 1990 (EEA), and many lakes are recovering. _(F5)_
- **Smog: how it forms and its health effects** — Homes burning coal and turf release smoke and fine particles; in calm, cold winter weather an anticyclone creates a temperature inversion that traps the smoke near the ground as smog, causing bronchitis, asthma, heart and lung disease and extra deaths. _(F6)_
- **London 1952 and Dublin 1990: smoke-control laws and their causes** — London's Great Smog (December 1952, about 4,000 deaths at the time) led to the UK Clean Air Act 1956 and smokeless zones. Severe winter smogs in the early 1980s led Dublin to ban the sale of smoky (bituminous) coal from 1 September 1990. _(F7)_
- **Dublin smoky-coal ban: results and extension** — Black smoke fell by about 70%; a Lancet study (2002) linked the ban to about 350 fewer deaths a year from respiratory and heart disease. Extended to Cork in 1995, then to other large towns as low-smoke zones; 2022 rules restrict smoky fuels and wet wood. _(F7)_
- **Wind energy in Ireland: an advantage, drawbacks and examples** — Strong, frequent Atlantic winds make wind Ireland's main renewable, about a third of electricity in the early 2020s (Galway Wind Park onshore, Arklow Bank offshore). But output varies, and residents object to noise, shadow flicker and visual impact. _(A1)_
- **Derrybrien wind farm, Co. Galway: what went wrong** — On 16 October 2003, during construction of a wind farm on bog, a peat slide of about 450,000 m³ occurred. In 2019 the EU Court of Justice fined Ireland for the lack of a proper environmental impact assessment. _(A1, A10)_
- **Hydroelectric power: methods, an advantage and a drawback in Ireland** — Dams and reservoirs; run-of-river schemes; pumped storage, as at Turlough Hill, Co. Wicklow (1974), whose output can be raised quickly at peak times. Ireland has little potential left for large new schemes; Norway gets about 90% of its electricity from hydro. _(A2)_
- **Solar energy: two types and its limit in Ireland** — Photovoltaic panels make electricity and solar thermal panels heat water. Solar has grown rapidly in Ireland since the early 2020s (solar farms on farmland, rooftop panels, SEAI grants), but cloud cover and short winter days limit output. _(A3)_
- **Wave and tidal energy: name three sites and their technology** — Wave: Atlantic Marine Energy Test Site off Belmullet, Co. Mayo. Tidal barrage: La Rance estuary, Brittany, France (1966). Tidal stream: SeaGen, Strangford Lough, Co. Down (2008), first commercial-scale turbine, since removed. Tides are predictable but costly. _(A4)_
- **Biomass and biofuels: sources, an Irish example and one drawback** — Wood chip, energy crops (willow, miscanthus) and biogas from slurry and food waste; biofuels blended into Irish road fuels by law; Edenderry, Co. Offaly, converted from peat. Carbon-neutral in principle, but energy crops compete with food for land. _(A6)_
- **Nuclear power: how it works, advantages and disadvantages** — Fission (splitting uranium atoms) releases heat to make steam for turbines; France gets about two-thirds of its electricity this way. For: low carbon, reliable, large output. Against: radioactive waste, accident risk, very high cost, decommissioning. _(A7)_
- **Chernobyl and Fukushima: what happened** — Chernobyl, Ukraine (then USSR), 26 April 1986: a reactor exploded and fallout spread across Europe, including Ireland; a 30 km exclusion zone remains. Fukushima Daiichi, Japan, 11 March 2011: an earthquake and tsunami cut cooling, causing meltdowns. _(A7)_
- **Why Ireland has no nuclear power station: five reasons** — Banned by the Electricity Regulation Act 1999; public protests stopped the ESB's 1970s plan for Carnsore Point, Co. Wexford; a small grid; high cost and waste disposal; fears raised by Chernobyl and by Sellafield's discharges into the Irish Sea. _(A8)_
- **Renewable energy: six general advantages** — Inexhaustible; little or no CO2, SO2 or NOx; a smaller fossil-fuel import bill and better energy security; jobs, often in rural areas; free fuel once built; help to meet EU and national targets and avoid penalties. _(A9)_
- **Renewable energy: five general disadvantages** — Intermittent, so backup or storage is needed; high initial cost; visual impact, noise and local objections; remote sites need grid connections; habitat damage, such as birds killed by turbines, valleys flooded by dams and peat slides at wind farms on bog. _(A10)_
- **Renewable energy in Ireland: economic reasons it is crucial** — Ireland imported about three-quarters of its energy in the early 2020s at a cost of billions of euro a year, so renewables cut the import bill and price shocks; interconnectors allow exports; rural jobs and income; green power attracts multinationals. _(A11)_
- **Renewable energy in Ireland: environmental and target reasons** — Renewables help meet the Climate Action Plan target of 80% renewable electricity by 2030 and avoid the cost of buying compliance when EU targets are missed; cutting CO2, SO2 and NOx gives cleaner air and water. _(A11)_
- **Siting a wind farm on an OS map: five factors to check** — A high, exposed upland or coastal site with strong winds; away from houses (noise, shadow flicker); near an access road for construction; close to the grid (a transmission line); not in a protected area or on deep peat, ideally on land of low farming value. _(A12)_

### 3. Environmental pollution (25)

- **Greenhouse gases: name each gas and its main sources** — CO2: burning fossil fuels, deforestation and cement. Methane: cattle and sheep, rice paddies, landfill and gas leaks. Nitrous oxide: nitrogen fertilisers and slurry. CFCs and other fluorinated gases: fridges, air conditioning and aerosols. _(W3)_
- **Global warming evidence: how CO2 levels have changed** — About 280 ppm before the Industrial Revolution; passed 400 ppm in the mid-2010s; over 420 ppm by the mid-2020s. Measured at Mauna Loa, Hawaii, since 1958. _(W4)_
- **Global warming evidence: temperature, ice and sea level** — Global surface temperature up about 1.1 to 1.2 °C since the late 1800s (IPCC); 2024 the warmest year on record and the first above 1.5 °C (Copernicus); Arctic summer sea ice down about 13% per decade since 1979; sea level up about 20 cm, 1901 to 2018. _(W4)_
- **Global warming: global effects, with named places** — Sea-level rise threatens Bangladesh (Ganges-Brahmaputra delta), the Maldives, Tuvalu and Kiribati; worse heatwaves, floods and droughts (the Sahel); coral bleaching on the Great Barrier Reef since the late 1990s; retreating glaciers threaten water supplies. _(W5)_
- **Global warming: effects on Ireland** — Average temperature up about 0.9 °C since 1900 (EPA, Met Éireann); wetter winters and more flooding; risk of summer drought; stronger storms and coastal erosion (Storm Éowyn, January 2025, record damage and power cuts); changes to farming and marine species. _(W6)_
- **Ireland's greenhouse gas emissions: the main sources** — Agriculture over a third, the largest share (methane and nitrous oxide from cattle, slurry and fertiliser); transport next, about a fifth (EPA, early 2020s); then energy. Ireland's emissions per person are high for an EU country. _(W7)_
- **Global climate agreements: Rio, Kyoto and Paris** — UNFCCC: Earth Summit, Rio de Janeiro, 1992. Kyoto Protocol (1997, in force 2005): binding cuts for developed countries only; the USA never ratified it. Paris Agreement (2015): warming well below 2 °C, aiming for 1.5 °C; each country sets its own plan. _(W8)_
- **EU responses to global warming: ETS and the Green Deal** — EU Emissions Trading System (2005): power stations and heavy industry must hold an allowance for each tonne of CO2. European Green Deal (2019): climate neutral by 2050; Fit for 55: cut net emissions by at least 55% by 2030 compared with 1990. _(W9)_
- **Ireland's responses to global warming: law, tax and plan** — Climate Act 2021: 51% cut by 2030 (vs 2018), net zero by 2050; sectoral ceilings (2022): agriculture to cut 25% by 2030; carbon tax since 2010, rising towards €100 a tonne by 2030; Climate Action Plan: retrofit 500,000 homes to B2 by 2030. _(W9)_
- **Reducing emissions: four things individuals and communities can do** — Improve energy efficiency and insulate homes; use public transport, cycling and walking instead of the car; reduce food waste; switch to renewable heating. _(W10)_
- **Agricultural water pollution in Ireland: causes** — Nitrogen and phosphorus from slurry, manure and chemical fertilisers wash off fields into rivers and lakes, especially after heavy rain or when spread on wet or frozen ground; silage effluent, and sewage from towns and septic tanks, add more. _(N1)_
- **Eutrophication: the sequence from fertiliser to fish kill** — Nutrients feed rapid growth of algae (algal blooms); when the algae die, bacteria decomposing them use up the dissolved oxygen; fish and other life suffocate; the water turns green and smells, and some blue-green algae are toxic. _(N2)_
- **Agricultural water pollution: four effects** — Loss of fish and other wildlife; damage to angling and tourism; drinking water needs costly treatment and nitrates contaminate groundwater; algal mats in estuaries and coastal waters. _(N3)_
- **Irish water quality: EPA findings and the dairy expansion** — EPA (2016 to 2021): only about half of rivers, lakes and estuaries in good or high ecological status; agriculture the main pressure, then urban wastewater. After milk quotas ended on 1 April 2015 the dairy herd grew, raising nitrogen in the south and east. _(N4)_
- **Lough Neagh algal bloom, 2023: what happened and why** — Summer 2023: a huge toxic blue-green algal bloom covered much of Lough Neagh, Northern Ireland, the island's largest lake. Causes: farm runoff, sewage, septic tanks, warm weather and invasive zebra mussels clearing the water. A major drinking-water source. _(N5)_
- **Agricultural water pollution: how it is being reduced** — Nitrates Directive (EU, 1991) and Nitrates Action Programme: winter closed periods for spreading, nitrogen limits per hectare, slurry storage, buffer strips. Water Framework Directive (EU, 2000): good status for all waters. Upgraded urban wastewater treatment. _(N6)_
