- **Marine oil pollution: four causes, with a drilling example** — Tanker accidents; drilling blow-outs, such as Deepwater Horizon (BP), Gulf of Mexico, 20 April 2010, which killed 11 workers and spilled about 4.9 million barrels; illegal tank washing at sea; pipeline leaks. _(O1, O4)_
- **Exxon Valdez, 1989: where, when and how much oil** — The tanker ran aground on Bligh Reef, Prince William Sound, Alaska, on 24 March 1989, spilling about 11 million US gallons (about 37,000 tonnes) of crude oil and oiling about 2,000 km of coastline. _(O2)_
- **Exxon Valdez: effects on wildlife and the legal response** — Hundreds of thousands of seabirds and thousands of sea otters were killed, and the herring fishery collapsed. The US Oil Pollution Act 1990 then required double-hulled tankers in US waters. _(O2)_
- **Prestige oil spill, 2002: what happened and the effects** — The single-hulled tanker broke in two and sank off Galicia, Spain, in November 2002, spilling tens of thousands of tonnes of heavy fuel oil on coasts of Spain, Portugal and France; Galician fishing shut for months; the EU sped up its single-hull phase-out. _(O3)_
- **Oil spills: effects, clean-up methods and prevention** — Effects: oiled seabirds and mammals, smothered shorelines, fishing bans, lost tourism. Clean-up: booms contain the oil, skimmers remove it, dispersants break it up. Prevention: double-hulled tankers and routing ships away from coasts. _(O5)_
- **Plastic and waste pollution: causes and effects** — Causes: single-use packaging, litter, illegal dumping, landfill; plastic takes hundreds of years to break down. Effects: wildlife entanglement and ingestion; the Great Pacific Garbage Patch; microplastics in food chains; landfill leachate and methane. _(L1, L2)_
- **Irish responses to plastic and waste pollution** — Plastic bag levy (March 2002, 15 cent; 22 cent from 2007) cut bag use by over 90% almost at once; Deposit Return Scheme (Re-turn) since 1 February 2024 for drinks bottles and cans; the waste hierarchy; Poolbeg Waste-to-Energy incinerator, Dublin, since 2017. _(L3)_
- **Ozone depletion: cause, discovery and effects** — CFCs from aerosols, fridges and air conditioning destroy stratospheric ozone, which absorbs ultraviolet radiation. The Antarctic ozone hole was reported in 1985 (British Antarctic Survey). More UV causes skin cancer and cataracts and harms plankton and crops. _(Z1)_
- **Montreal Protocol: what it did and the outcome** — Agreed in 1987, it phased out CFCs, and every UN member state has ratified it. The Antarctic ozone layer should recover to 1980 levels around the 2060s (UN, 2023). It is often called the most successful global environmental agreement. _(Z2)_

### 4. Sustainable economic development (24)

- **Sustainable development: who defined it, its pillars and global framework** — Defined in the Brundtland Report, Our Common Future (1987); it balances environmental protection, economic growth and social fairness. The later Rio Earth Summit (1992) produced Agenda 21; the UN Sustainable Development Goals (2015) set 17 goals for 2030. _(S1, S2, S4)_
- **The EPA: when and how it was set up** — Set up under the Environmental Protection Agency Act 1992 and began work in 1993; an independent public body with its headquarters at Johnstown Castle, Co. Wexford. _(E1)_
- **EPA licensing: who needs a licence and what it covers** — Large industries (pharmaceutical and chemical plants, power stations, intensive pig and poultry farms, cement works, mines) and waste facilities; the licence sets emission limits, and one licence covers air, water, waste and noise. _(E2)_
- **EPA monitoring and reporting: four roles** — Monitors rivers, lakes, estuaries, groundwater and bathing waters; runs the national air quality network and greenhouse gas inventory; publishes a State of the Environment report every four years; radiological protection, including radon, since 2014. _(E3, E5)_
- **EPA enforcement and the limits of its control** — Inspections; the Office of Environmental Enforcement (2003) prosecutes polluters; oversight of local authorities. Limits: it cannot inspect everywhere, diffuse pollution from thousands of farms is hard to police, and local authorities share responsibility. _(E4, E6)_
- **Environmental impact assessment: legal basis and projects covered** — The EU Environmental Impact Assessment Directive (1985), applied in Irish planning law, requires one for projects above set sizes: motorways, power stations, wind farms, mines and quarries, large intensive farms, pipelines, ports and airports. _(I2)_
- **EIAR: what the report must contain** — A description of the project; the existing environment (baseline); likely effects on people, wildlife, soil, water, air, climate, landscape, heritage, traffic and noise; alternatives considered; mitigation measures; a non-technical summary for the public. _(I3)_
- **Environmental impact assessment: the three-step process** — First the developer, often with consultants, prepares the EIAR; then the public and bodies such as the National Parks and Wildlife Service make submissions; finally the planning authority or An Coimisiún Pleanála (An Bord Pleanála until 2025) decides. _(I4)_
- **Why environmental impact studies are necessary: four reasons** — They prevent damage before it happens, which is cheaper than clean-up; they make developers consider alternatives; they give the public a say; they are legally required, as the Derrybrien case shows. _(I5)_
- **Derrybrien, Co. Galway: the cost of no proper EIA** — A peat slide occurred on 16 October 2003 during wind-farm construction on bog. In 2019 the EU Court of Justice fined Ireland a lump sum of €5 million plus €15,000 a day because no proper environmental impact assessment had been carried out. _(I5)_
- **Overfishing: six causes** — Modern technology (sonar, satellite navigation, factory ships, huge nets); too many boats chasing the same stock; subsidies that keep fleets fishing; young fish caught before they breed; discards; illegal fishing outside the quota system. _(F2)_
- **Grand Banks cod collapse, Newfoundland: what happened and the effects** — Catches rose sharply with factory trawlers from the 1960s and the stock collapsed; Canada banned northern cod fishing on 2 July 1992; about 30,000 people lost their jobs, the largest single lay-off in Canadian history; recovery has been slow and partial. _(F3)_
- **North Sea herring: collapse and recovery** — Overfished in the 1960s and 1970s; the fishery was closed from 1977 to the early 1980s; the stock recovered after the fishery reopened under strict quotas. _(F4)_
- **Common Fisheries Policy: five conservation measures** — CFP (1983): total allowable catches divided into national quotas; minimum mesh and landing sizes; closed areas and seasons; vessel decommissioning; marine protected areas, with aquaculture as an alternative to wild catch. _(F5)_
- **CFP 2013 reform and monitoring in Irish waters** — The 2013 reform: fish at maximum sustainable yield and a landing obligation ending discards, phased in 2015 to 2019. Monitoring by satellite, the Sea-Fisheries Protection Authority (2007) and Naval Service patrols within the 200-nautical-mile zone. _(F5)_
- **Irish fishing: most valuable catch and main port** — Mackerel is the most valuable Irish catch, landed mainly at Killybegs, Co. Donegal, Ireland's largest fishing port and base of the pelagic fleet. Conservation limits cause hardship for fishing communities that depend on the stocks. _(F6)_
- **Mining past and present: Avoca compared with Tara Mines** — Avoca, Co. Wicklow: centuries of copper mining left acid mine drainage that still pollutes the Avoca River. Tara Mines, Navan, Co. Meath, Europe's largest zinc mine: EPA licence, lined tailings facility, water monitoring, restoration plans. _(M1, M2)_
- **Mine rehabilitation: Lisheen and the rules for new mines** — Lisheen, Co. Tipperary, a zinc and lead mine, closed in 2015 and its site was rehabilitated. A new mine needs an EIAR and a closure and restoration plan backed by a financial bond before it opens. _(M3)_
- **Peatlands: from industrial harvesting to restoration** — Bord na Móna (1946) harvesting removed raised bogs, released stored carbon and destroyed habitats. Harvesting ended in January 2021; cutaway bogs are rewetted or reused (Lough Boora parkland, Bellacorick wind farms); Midland workers need a just transition. _(P1, P2)_
- **Aral Sea: what was done and what happened to the sea** — From the 1960s the Amu Darya and Syr Darya, which feed the Aral Sea (Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan), were diverted to irrigate cotton in the Soviet Union; the sea shrank to a fraction of its size, split into separate lakes and grew saltier. _(A1)_
- **Aral Sea: effects on people and the partial recovery** — Fishing collapsed; ports such as Moynaq (Uzbekistan) were stranded far from the water; dust storms spread salt and pesticides, harming health. The Kok-Aral dam (Kazakhstan, 2005) raised the small northern sea, and fishing there has partly revived. _(A1)_
- **Sustainable farming in Ireland: name three schemes** — REPS (Rural Environment Protection Scheme, 1994), now ACRES; Origin Green, Bord Bia's sustainability programme (2012); BurrenLIFE (2005 to 2010), which paid Burren farmers to manage limestone grassland by winter grazing, now the Burren Programme. _(O2)_
- **The polluter pays: four examples in Ireland** — The Irish carbon tax (2010); the plastic bag levy (March 2002); the landfill levy on waste sent to landfill; the EU Emissions Trading System (2005), under which companies pay for the emissions they release. _(O6)_
- **Sustainable development: four obstacles** — Cost: clean technology, retrofitting and rehabilitation are expensive; short-term economic pressure favours quick profit; jobs in traditional industries such as peat and fishing are at risk; global cooperation is needed because pollution crosses borders. _(FP2)_

### 5. Conflicts between economic interests and environmental interests (24)

- **Corrib gas, Co. Mayo: the economic case** — About 80 km off north-west Co. Mayo; discovered 1996; gas first flowed December 2015, processed at Bellanaboy; for a time over half of Ireland's gas. Arguments for: energy security, a domestic supply, revenue for the State and companies, construction jobs. _(N1)_
- **Corrib gas: local objections and the Shell to Sea campaign** — The plan piped high-pressure, unprocessed gas onshore near homes at Rossport, a safety risk for residents; Shell to Sea (from 2005) argued it should be processed at sea; the pipeline route crossed Sruwaddacon Bay, a protected estuary (SAC). _(N1)_
- **Corrib gas: protests and outcome** — The Rossport Five were jailed in June 2005 for defying a court order not to obstruct pipeline work, freed after 94 days; policing was heavy and the community divided; the route moved away from homes, with a tunnel under Sruwaddacon Bay; Shell sold out in 2018. _(N1)_
- **Irish fish stocks: the conflict over EU quotas** — Fishermen and coastal communities such as Killybegs need catches for jobs; the CFP (1983) conserves stocks by dividing EU total allowable catches into national quotas. Irish fishermen argue Ireland's share of its own rich waters is too small, with much taken by other EU fleets. _(N2)_
- **Irish fish stocks: Brexit, decommissioning and the mackerel dispute** — The Brexit deal (December 2020) cut Irish quotas and a scheme paid owners to scrap vessels. From 2010 Iceland and the Faroes set themselves large mackerel quotas; the EU, backed by Irish fishermen, imposed trade measures on the Faroes over herring and mackerel in 2013; the dispute eased in 2014. _(N2)_
- **Wild salmon drift-net ban: the two sides** — Ireland banned drift-net fishing for wild salmon at sea from 2007 because returning salmon had fallen sharply. Commercial salmon fishermen were compensated; anglers and conservation groups backed the ban, as wild salmon sustain angling tourism. _(N2)_
- **Mullaghmore, the Burren: what was planned and why the site matters** — In the early 1990s the OPW began building an interpretative centre and car park near Mullaghmore, Co. Clare, a hill of folded, terraced limestone in the Burren National Park, with rare plants such as spring gentian and mountain avens. _(N3)_
- **Mullaghmore: the two sides of the conflict** — Supporters: tourism jobs and visitor spending in a poor rural area, and better visitor management. The Burren Action Group: traffic, sewage and visitor pressure would damage a fragile, unspoilt landscape, and any centre should be in nearby villages. _(N3)_
- **Mullaghmore: legal challenge and outcome** — In 1993 the Supreme Court ruled that the State had to obtain planning permission like any other developer, and work stopped. In 2000 An Bord Pleanála refused permission, and the partly built site was cleared and restored. _(N3)_
- **Skellig Michael, Co. Kerry: the conflict and how it is managed** — UNESCO World Heritage monastic site (1996) run by the OPW; Star Wars filming (from 2014) boosted demand. Boat operators want more access; conservationists fear for the site and its seabirds. Access is by licensed boats in summer, about 180 visitors a day. _(N4)_
- **M3 and the Hill of Tara: the economic case for the road** — The M3 (Clonee to north of Kells) relieves congestion on the N3 for fast-growing Meath commuter towns such as Navan and Kells and cuts journey times to Dublin. It runs through the Tara–Skryne valley, about 2 km from the Hill of Tara. _(N5)_
- **M3 and the Hill of Tara: objections and outcome** — Campaign groups, archaeologists and protesters camped on the route said it would destroy an archaeological landscape. In 2007 a prehistoric enclosure at Lismullin was declared a national monument, excavated and recorded, then built over. The M3 opened in 2010. _(N5)_
- **Turf cutting on protected raised bogs: the conflict and outcome** — Rural families: a traditional right and cheap fuel. Conservation: rare raised bogs that store carbon. Under the Habitats Directive (1992), cutting was banned on 53 raised-bog SACs from 2011; compensation: about €1,500 a year for 15 years; cutters protested. _(N6)_
- **Wind farms: the two sides of the conflict** — For: renewable targets (at least 5 GW offshore wind by 2030), landowner income, community funds. Against: visual impact, noise, shadow flicker, property values, birds, peat stability (the 2003 Derrybrien slide, Co. Galway); offshore, fishing grounds. _(N7)_
- **Dairy expansion since 2015: economic vs environmental interests** — After milk quotas ended on 1 April 2015 the herd grew strongly, raising farm incomes and food exports; however, nitrates harm water quality, agriculture produces over a third of Irish emissions, and sectoral ceilings (2022) require a 25% cut by 2030. _(N8)_
- **Data centres in Ireland: the conflict over electricity** — For: investment, multinational jobs, the tech economy. Against: they used about a fifth of metered electricity in 2023 (CSO), mostly in the Dublin region, straining the grid and climate targets; in 2022 EirGrid paused new Dublin connections until about 2028. _(N9)_
- **Amazon rainforest: the economic drivers of clearing** — Cattle ranching (most cleared land becomes pasture); soya in the south; logging; the Trans-Amazonian Highway (begun 1970) with fishbone clearings, as in Rondônia; the Carajás iron-ore mine, Pará (Vale); the Belo Monte dam on the Xingu (2019). _(G1)_
- **Amazon rainforest: environmental and global interests** — About 60% of the world's largest tropical rainforest is in Brazil. It holds biodiversity, stores carbon, recycles rainfall and is home to indigenous peoples such as the Kayapó. Clearing adds to climate change, yet Brazil wants development. _(G1)_
- **Brazilian Amazon deforestation: the PRODES trend** — About 27,800 km² cleared in 2004; cut by over 80% to about 4,600 km² by 2012; rose again to about 13,000 km² in 2021; fell sharply from 2023. _(G1)_
- **Amazon rainforest: four protection measures** — Forest Code: landowners must keep 80% of their Amazon land as forest (often not enforced); soy moratorium (2006): traders refuse soya from newly cleared land; Amazon Fund (2008): Norway and Germany pay for reduced clearing; indigenous reserves. _(G1)_
- **Amazon rainforest: Chico Mendes and the policy swings** — Chico Mendes, a rubber tapper and union leader who campaigned to protect the forest, was murdered at Xapuri, Acre, on 22 December 1988. Clearing fell under President Lula (2003 to 2010, again from 2023) and rose under Bolsonaro (2019 to 2022). _(G1)_
- **Lignite mining in Germany: Hambach Forest and Lützerath** — RWE's Hambach open-cast mine, North Rhine-Westphalia, expanded into the ancient Hambach Forest; protesters occupied tree houses for years; in October 2018 a court halted clearing and the rest was spared. Lützerath was cleared in January 2023 for the Garzweiler mine. Coal power ends by 2038. _(G2)_
- **Three Gorges Dam, China: benefits vs costs** — Benefits: clean electricity from the world's largest power station, flood control downstream, better navigation. Costs: over one million people relocated; farmland, towns and archaeological sites flooded; sediment trapped; river ecology changed. _(G3)_
- **Map question: explaining conflict over a development site** — Identify what the site needs (e.g. high open ground for a wind farm); read contours, settlements, roads, rivers and protected areas; name the groups for (developers, landowners) and against (residents, environmental groups, tourism firms), with reasons. _(M1)_

## Economic eu

### 1. EU trading patterns (22)

- **EU trade: its world share and the dates of its two big steps** — About 13% of world goods trade in 2024, second to China in goods, and the largest trader counting goods and services. The customs union was completed on 1 July 1968; the single market began on 1 January 1993, set up by the Single European Act (1986). _(B1, B2, B3)_
- **Single market: which non-tariff barriers it removed** — Border checks; different national product standards; national preference in public contracts. Common standards such as the CE mark replaced them, on top of the four freedoms of goods, services, capital and people. _(B3)_
- **Which non-EU countries are in the single market, and how** — Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein through the European Economic Area (EEA). Switzerland is in neither the EU nor the EEA but trades through bilateral agreements. _(B4)_
- **Intra-EU trade: its scale in 2024** — Intra-EU goods exports were about €4.0 trillion against about €2.6 trillion of extra-EU goods exports, so about three-fifths (about 60%) of members' goods exports stayed inside the EU. _(I1)_
- **Intra-EU trade: biggest and smallest traders, and who depends most** — Germany is by far the largest (about €845 billion of intra-EU goods exports, 2024); Cyprus the smallest (about €1 billion). Intra-EU trade ran from about 30% (Cyprus) to about 81% (Czechia) of goods trade, as eastern members sit in Germany's supply chains. _(I2, I6)_
- **What is traded within the EU, with a complementary-trade example** — Mainly manufactured goods (machinery, vehicles and parts, chemicals and pharmaceuticals), then food, plus services such as finance and tourism. Complementary trade: greenhouse vegetables from Almería, Spain, go north; manufactures go south and east. _(I3, I5)_
- **EU car supply chains: the eastern example** — Since the 2004 enlargement car plants in Slovakia, Czechia, Hungary and Poland supply the German-centred industry. Volkswagen took over Škoda in stages from 1991, fully owning it since 2000. Slovakia makes more cars per head than any other country. _(I4)_
- **Gateway ports and the Rotterdam effect** — Rotterdam (Europe's largest port) and Antwerp (second) at the Rhine-Meuse-Scheldt delta handle goods entering and leaving the single market, so the Netherlands and Belgium show huge trade totals; goods recorded as Netherlands trade may go elsewhere. _(I7)_
- **Internal trade factors: barrier removal and the euro** — The customs union, single market and Schengen border-free travel cut costs and delays. The euro, used by 21 countries from 1 January 2026 (Bulgaria the newest), removes exchange costs and exchange-rate risk and makes prices easy to compare. _(IF1, IF2)_
- **Internal trade factors: transport links and a wealthy market** — Short distances; dense motorways and rail; the Rhine, navigable to Basel; the Channel Tunnel (1994); the Øresund Bridge, Copenhagen to Malmö (2000); EU-funded TEN-T networks. About 450 million people (2024) with high incomes and similar tastes. _(IF3, IF4)_
- **How EU enlargement changed internal trade** — Ten countries joined in 2004, Bulgaria and Romania in 2007 and Croatia in 2013. Each widened the single market, opening new markets and lower-cost production sites in the east, such as car assembly linked to Germany. _(IF5, I4)_
- **How Brexit changed EU–UK trade** — The UK left on 31 January 2020; transition ended 31 December 2020. The Trade and Cooperation Agreement (from 1 January 2021) has zero tariffs and quotas on qualifying goods, but customs declarations, checks and rules-of-origin paperwork make trade costlier. _(IF6)_
- **EU's top five export and import partners for goods (2024)** — Exports: USA about 21%, UK about 13%, China about 8%, Switzerland about 7–8%, Turkey about 4%. Imports: China about a fifth (20–21%), USA about 13–14%, UK about 7%, Switzerland about 6%, Turkey about 4%. _(E2, E3)_
- **EU balance of trade in goods (2024)** — Extra-EU exports about €2.58 trillion and imports about €2.43 trillion, a surplus of about €150 billion. The EU had a surplus with the USA and a large deficit with China. _(E4)_
- **What the EU exports outside the EU (2024)** — Machinery and vehicles about €1.0 trillion (about 39% of extra-EU exports); chemicals including pharmaceuticals about €560 billion (about 22%); also food, drink and services. High-tech, high-value industry explains this pattern. _(E5, EF2)_
- **What the EU imports, and why it depends on energy imports** — Energy, mainly oil and gas, about 15% of imports by value in 2024 (about 23% in 2022), because the EU lacks fuels and some raw materials; also raw materials, electronics from China and Asia, and tropical foods such as coffee, cocoa and bananas. _(E6, EF3)_
- **Russia and EU energy imports since 2022** — Russia invaded Ukraine on 24 February 2022; sanctions and REPowerEU (May 2022) cut Russian gas from about 45% of EU gas imports (2021) to about 19% (2024). The USA supplied almost half of EU LNG in 2024. The EU plans to end Russian gas by the end of 2027. _(E7)_
- **EU trade agreements: four named examples with dates** — CETA with Canada, provisionally applied from September 2017; EU–Japan agreement in force from February 2019; EU–South Korea from 2011; EU–Mercosur talks concluded December 2024, strongly opposed by Irish farm organisations over South American beef. _(E8)_
- **EU trade deals with developing countries, and a justice criticism** — Lomé Convention (1975) with ACP countries; Cotonou Agreement (2000); Everything But Arms (2001), duty-free and quota-free for the least developed countries. Critics say tariff escalation and past subsidised farm exports harmed developing-world producers. _(E9)_
- **Two recent EU trade disputes acting as one bloc** — The EU–USA deal of July 2025 capped US tariffs on most EU goods, including pharmaceuticals, at 15%; steel and aluminium stayed at 50%. EU definitive duties on Chinese-made electric cars from October 2024 reached up to about 35%. _(E10)_
- **Factors shaping the EU's external trade: name five** — Market size gives bargaining power; high-tech industry makes manufactures the main exports; lack of energy forces fuel imports; colonial links (Africa, Caribbean, Pacific) shaped preferential deals; sanctions, tariffs and Brexit redirect trade. _(EF1, EF2, EF3, EF4, EF6)_
- **Skill: reading a trade pie chart or table** — Identify the largest partner (biggest slice); calculate a missing share by subtracting the others from 100 (illustrative: 25% and 15% leave 60%); compare exports and imports with one partner: more sent than received is a surplus, less is a deficit. _(S1)_

### 2. Irish trading patterns with the EU (19)

- **Irish trade before 1973: main market, main exports and the key agreement** — In 1960 about three-quarters (74%) of Irish exports went to the UK, mainly live cattle and other farm produce. Trade with the UK was governed by the Anglo-Irish Free Trade Agreement (1965); protectionism was dismantled from the late 1950s. _(P1)_
- **Ireland joining the EEC: date, partners and referendum result** — Ireland joined on 1 January 1973, together with the UK and Denmark, after a referendum on 10 May 1972 returned about 83% Yes. _(P2)_
- **Irish export markets since 1973: how they diversified** — The UK's share fell from about three-quarters in 1960 to a small fraction today (Great Britain took under a tenth of goods exports in February 2024), while the rest of the EU and the USA became far more important. _(C1)_
- **Irish exports since 1973: how the products changed** — From mainly farm goods and live animals to high-value manufactures: pharmaceuticals and chemicals, medical devices, and computers and electronics, which grew strongly in the 1990s and 2000s. Processed food and drink replaced live animals, and services grew. _(C2)_
- **Ireland's goods trade totals and surplus (2024)** — Goods exports about €224 billion, a record, up about 14% on 2023; imports about €135 billion; a surplus of about €89 billion (CSO). The surplus is large mainly because foreign multinationals export most of what they make in Ireland. _(T1, C3)_
- **Ireland's main goods export markets (2024)** — The EU 27 is the largest bloc, about €88 billion (about 40%); the USA is the largest single country, about €73 billion (about one-third). Main EU markets: the Netherlands about €23 billion, Germany about €20 billion, Belgium about €17 billion, then France. _(T2)_
- **Ireland's main sources of goods imports (2024)** — The EU 27 is the largest bloc, about €50 billion (about 37%); then Asia about €31 billion, the USA about €23 billion and the UK about €22 billion. Asia, especially China, is a growing source. _(T3)_
- **Ireland's main goods exports (2024)** — Medical and pharmaceutical products are the largest group, about €100 billion (about 45% of goods exports); then organic chemicals, medical devices, and food and drink. _(T4)_
- **Ireland's main goods imports, and why fuels are one** — Fuels, because Ireland imports most of its energy; machinery and transport equipment, including aircraft for the leasing industry; chemicals; consumer goods; food. _(T5)_
- **The 2025 Irish export surge: what happened and the risk it shows** — Pharmaceuticals were rushed to the USA before threatened tariffs: goods exports about €260 billion (up about 16%), pharma about €139 billion (53%), the USA about 43% (CSO, preliminary). It shows reliance on a few firms, one sector and one market. _(T6, X1)_
- **Ireland's services exports: the three main types** — Software and computer services from multinationals such as Google, Meta and Microsoft, with European headquarters in Dublin; financial services at the IFSC, Dublin Docklands (opened 1987); aircraft leasing. Much is sold into EU markets. _(V1)_
- **Irish agri-food exports (2024): value, markets and largest category** — About 90% of beef and dairy output is exported. Food, drink and horticulture exports were a record €17 billion (Bord Bia): about 35% (€5.9 billion) to the EU, €5.2 billion to international markets, about a third to the UK. Dairy (€6.3 billion) leads. _(A1, A2)_
- **Irish food exports: the UK's role, meat markets and named brands** — The UK is still the largest single market for Irish food; meat and livestock went about 44% to the EU and 42% to the UK (2024). Brands: Kerrygold butter (marketed by Ornua, formerly the Irish Dairy Board) in Germany and other EU markets, and Baileys. _(A3, A4)_
- **Why Ireland trades heavily with the EU: single market, euro and CAP** — Tariff-free access to about 450 million consumers (2024); the euro (adopted 1999, notes 2002) removes exchange-rate risk, while sterling's fall after the June 2016 referendum hurt food exporters to the UK; CAP and common standards open EU food markets. _(F1, F3, F4)_
- **Foreign direct investment as a reason for Irish trade with the EU** — Multinationals locate in Ireland to supply the EU from inside: Intel at Leixlip (since 1989), Apple in Cork (since 1980), Pfizer. They come for an English-speaking, educated workforce, 12.5% corporation tax (15% for the largest from 2024) and IDA Ireland. _(F2)_
- **How Irish goods reach the EU: ports, air freight and the periphery** — As an island on the periphery, Ireland sends most trade by sea: Dublin Port (the largest), Rosslare Europort (direct ferries to the continent), Cork and Shannon Foynes. Light, high-value pharmaceuticals and electronics go by air; distance adds costs. _(F5, X2)_
- **Brexit: dates, and the new costs for Irish trade with Great Britain** — The UK left on 31 January 2020; transition ended 31 December 2020; the Trade and Cooperation Agreement applied from 1 January 2021. Trade with Great Britain now crosses an EU external border, with customs declarations and checks on food and animals. _(B1, B2)_
- **Brexit: the growth of direct ferries from Rosslare** — DFDS began a direct Rosslare–Dunkirk freight ferry on 2 January 2021, six times a week, to avoid the UK landbridge; by April 2021 there were about 36 direct weekly sailings to the continent. Rosslare also sails direct to Cherbourg, Saint-Malo, Roscoff and Bilbao. _(B2)_
- **Brexit: three effects on the pattern of Irish trade** — Imports from Great Britain fell as some importers switched to EU suppliers; cross-border trade with Northern Ireland grew, as the Windsor Framework (February 2023) keeps it in the single market for goods; the rest of the EU's share of Irish trade rose. _(B3, B4)_

### 3. How the EU influences the Irish economy (26)

- **EU membership for Ireland: advantages, costs and budget position** — Advantages: single market access, FDI, CAP payments, structural funds, equality laws, free movement. Costs: lost control of fishing and farm prices; lower-cost competitors; net contributor since 2014, after over €40 billion net received from 1973. _(O1, O2)_
- **CAP: launch date, five aims and share of the EU budget** — Launched in 1962. Treaty of Rome aims: raise farm productivity; a fair standard of living for farmers; stable markets; secure food supplies; reasonable prices for consumers. It still takes about a third of the EU budget (2021–27). _(A1)_
- **The early CAP (1962–1992): how it worked and what went wrong** — Intervention prices above world levels, intervention buying of surpluses, export subsidies and import levies. Results: overproduction (butter mountains and wine lakes in the 1970s and 1980s), high cost, and surpluses dumped on world markets. _(A2)_
- **CAP: positive effects on Irish farming since 1973** — Prices and incomes rose sharply in the 1970s golden age; a guaranteed market for beef and dairy; modernisation (machinery, fertiliser, silage, drainage, farm buildings); food quality standards; environmental schemes; support for rural communities. _(A3, A9)_
- **Milk quotas: dates, and what happened after abolition** — In force from 1984 to 1 April 2015, they held back dairying for 30 years. Milk output then rose from about 5.5 billion litres (2014) to about 8.3 billion (2020); over 2013–2023 cows rose about 41% and milk about 56% (EPA), especially in Munster. _(A4)_
- **CAP reforms in order, from MacSharry to the Basic Payment Scheme** — MacSharry 1992 (Ray MacSharry, from Sligo): direct payments in place of price support, plus set-aside. Agenda 2000: rural development became the second pillar. Fischler 2003: decoupling; Single Farm Payment 2005; Basic Payment Scheme 2015. _(A5)_
- **Ireland's CAP Strategic Plan 2023–2027: key figures and schemes** — About €9.8 billion (EU and national), about 45% for environment and climate; BISS about €3.6 billion; eco-scheme about €1.5 billion (25% of direct payments); convergence to at least 85% of the national average; ACRES; young farmer and ANC payments. _(A6)_
- **CAP rural development: two named Irish schemes** — REPS (Rural Environment Protection Scheme), 1994 to 2009, an agri-environment scheme; LEADER, from 1991, funding local rural enterprise such as farm diversification, rural tourism and crafts. Grants also support forestry and young farmers. _(A7)_
- **Irish farms in the Census of Agriculture 2020** — 135,037 farms, down about 3% on 2010; average size about 33 hectares; on 33% of farms the holder was 65 or over and only 7% were under 35. Farm types: dairy, cattle (beef) rearing, sheep, tillage and mixed; specialist beef is the most common. _(A8)_
- **CAP: five negative effects on Ireland** — Payments favoured bigger farmers; subsidy dependence, bureaucracy and inspections; intensification harmed water (the 1991 Nitrates Directive caps organic nitrogen per hectare) and raised emissions; fewer, older farmers; Mercosur fears for beef. _(A10, A11)_
- **CFP: when agreed, its main principle, and why Ireland lost out** — Agreed in full in 1983, it makes EU waters a common pond, open to all fleets beyond coastal limits, and conserves stocks. Ireland had to share its rich waters with larger fleets from Spain, France and the Netherlands; Spain and Portugal joined in 1986. _(B1)_
- **How the CFP controls fishing: five tools, and the Hague Preferences** — TACs set each December by fisheries ministers, split into national quotas by fixed shares; a 12-nautical-mile band mainly for local boats; days-at-sea limits; mesh sizes; closed areas and seasons. The Hague Preferences (1976) protect Ireland when TACs fall. _(B2, B3)_
- **Ireland's fishing ports: the largest and the other harbour centres** — Killybegs, Co. Donegal, is the largest by volume and value, landing pelagic mackerel, herring and blue whiting. Other Fishery Harbour Centres: Castletownbere, Ros an Mhíl, Dingle, Howth and Dunmore East. BIM is the state seafood development agency. _(B4)_
- **CFP: four positive effects on Ireland** — EU funding for boats, harbours and processing plants, now through the European Maritime, Fisheries and Aquaculture Fund; conservation to protect stocks long-term; access to EU seafood markets; the Hague Preferences protect Ireland's share when quotas are cut. _(B5)_
- **CFP: negative effects on Ireland, and the 2013 reform** — Ireland's quota share is small relative to its rich waters; overfishing and stock collapses; decommissioning and lost coastal jobs; discards, until the 2013 reform's landing obligation (phased in 2015–2019) and maximum sustainable yield goal. _(B6, B7)_
- **Brexit and Irish fishing: what Ireland lost** — The Trade and Cooperation Agreement (2021) moved quota to the UK: Ireland lost about 26% of its mackerel quota, and the Seafood Task Force valued its annual quota loss at about €43 million by 2026. Irish ministers said Ireland's losses were about double other EU fleets' in proportion. _(B8)_
- **Brexit Adjustment Reserve: how it helped Irish fishing** — Ireland received about €1 billion, the largest share of the EU-wide reserve. It funded a €25.6 million pelagic support scheme and a voluntary permanent cessation (decommissioning) scheme in 2022–23. _(B8, C8)_
- **ERDF: when it began, its purposes, and the 1988–89 reform** — Set up in 1975 to cut regional inequality by funding infrastructure, enterprise, research and innovation in poorer regions. The Structural Funds (ERDF, ESF, farm guidance fund) doubled after the 1988–89 reform to prepare poorer regions for the single market. _(C1, C2)_
- **Objective 1 and the Cohesion Fund: Ireland's qualification** — All of Ireland was one Objective 1 region, 1989–1999 (income below 75% of the EU average). The Cohesion Fund (1993/94, Maastricht) funded transport and environment in Ireland, Spain, Portugal and Greece (below 90%); Ireland stopped qualifying in the 2000s. _(C3, C4)_
- **Ireland's EU regional status in 2000–2006 and 2021–2027** — 2000–06: the BMW region kept Objective 1 status; Southern and Eastern was in transition. 2021–27: Northern and Western became a transition region as its relative income fell; two ERDF programmes, about €853 million (€396 million EU), run by the assemblies. _(C5, C7)_
- **What EU regional funds built in Ireland, and their mixed results** — Inter-urban motorways and bypasses, public transport, water treatment, ports, airports, broadband, research centres. They opened up the periphery, drew FDI and fed the Celtic Tiger, but the east–west gap persists and funds now go mainly to eastern Europe. _(C6, C9)_
- **Three other EU funds for Irish regions** — PEACE PLUS (2021–27) and INTERREG support the border region; the Just Transition Fund (2021–27) targets the Midlands peat region after Bord na Móna ended peat harvesting for electricity. _(C8)_
- **European Social Fund: origin and what it funded in Ireland** — Set up by the Treaty of Rome (1957), the oldest structural fund. In Ireland it co-financed FÁS training (1988–2013; then SOLAS and 16 Education and Training Boards), Youthreach (from 1989), Post-Leaving Cert courses and apprenticeships. _(D1, D2)_
- **ESF+ EIST programme 2021–2027: value and priorities** — About €1.08 billion (about €508 million EU, €573 million national), launched March 2023: about €500 million for social inclusion, €280 million for upskilling and lifelong learning, over €180 million for jobs for young and long-term unemployed people. _(D3)_
- **Globalisation Adjustment Fund: the Dell Limerick example** — Set up in 2007. After Dell announced in January 2009 that it was moving manufacturing from Limerick to Łódź, Poland, the EU approved about €14.8 million in 2009 to retrain about 2,400 Dell and supplier workers. Waterford Crystal workers were also helped. _(D4)_
- **EU social laws that changed Irish workplaces** — Civil service marriage bar ended 1973; Anti-Discrimination (Pay) Act 1974 and Employment Equality Act 1977 followed EEC directives; Organisation of Working Time Act 1997: average 48-hour maximum week; also maternity leave and part-time workers' rights. _(D5)_

## Economic globalisation

### 1. One multinational company (MNC) (18)

- **Typical structure and organisation of a multinational company** — Headquarters, R&D and top management stay in the home country (the core); branch plants, regional headquarters and sales offices are abroad. Decisions are made at headquarters, and branch plants compete with each other inside the company for investment. _(M2)_
- **MNC production stages: what they are and how they are spread** — Design and R&D; basic processing of raw materials and components; assembly and testing; distribution and sales. Each stage is placed wherever it is cheapest or most efficient, which spreads sourcing, processing and markets across the world. _(M3)_
- **Why multinationals choose Ireland: give eight advantages** — Low corporation tax (12.5%; 15% for very large groups from 2024); a young, educated, English-speaking workforce; EU single market access, and since Brexit the only mainly English-speaking euro country; IDA grants; ports, airports and broadband; clusters; universities; stability. _(M6)_
- **IDA Ireland: role, size and why FDI concentrates around Dublin** — Set up in 1949 to attract and keep foreign direct investment. Client firms employ about 300,000 people in about 1,800 companies (2023–24), concentrated in Dublin and the Mid-East: the largest labour pool, universities, Dublin Port and Airport, services and existing clusters. _(M5)_
- **Intel: founding, founders, headquarters and products** — Founded in 1968 in California by Robert Noyce and Gordon Moore; headquarters Santa Clara, in Silicon Valley. It made the first commercial microprocessor, the Intel 4004, in 1971, and makes the x86 processors in most personal computers (Pentium, Core; Xeon for servers). _(I1)_
- **Intel in Leixlip, Co. Kildare: key facts** — Came in 1989; Ireland's largest private-sector investment (over €30 billion by 2023), with several fabs and several thousand staff. Fab 34 (2023) was Europe's first high-volume EUV chip fab. In 2024 Intel sold 49% of the Fab 34 venture to Apollo for about $11 billion. _(I3)_
- **Why Intel chose Leixlip: local site factors** — On the M4 west of Dublin, within reach of Dublin Airport and Port; a large, flat greenfield site; Maynooth University nearby, with Trinity College Dublin and University College Dublin in the city; a large Dublin labour pool; water and electricity, used in huge amounts. _(I4)_
- **Intel microprocessor: sourcing of raw materials and components** — Silicon wafers (silicon refined from quartz sand) bought from specialist wafer makers; chemicals, specialty gases and metals; huge volumes of ultra-pure water and electricity; machines, especially EUV lithography machines from ASML of Veldhoven, Netherlands, the only maker. _(I6)_
- **Intel microprocessor: location of basic processing units** — Wafers are processed in fabs at Leixlip, Hillsboro (Oregon), Chandler (Arizona) and Kiryat Gat (Israel), then flown to Penang and Kulim (Malaysia), Ho Chi Minh City (Vietnam) and Chengdu (China) to be cut, packaged and tested. Chips are light and valuable, so they go by air. _(I7, I2)_
- **Intel microprocessor: location of its markets** — Computer makers such as Lenovo (China), HP and Dell (USA); makers of servers for data centres and cloud computing; governments and businesses worldwide. Markets are in North America, Europe and Asia, with China one of the largest. _(I8)_
- **Difficulties facing Intel: give seven** — Competition from AMD, TSMC, Samsung and Nvidia; Apple switched Macs to its own M1 chip from 2020; very costly new fabs; falling profits and about 15,000 global job cuts (2024); a narrowing Irish tax advantage; high energy costs; reliance on Asian supply chains (2021 chip shortage). _(I9)_
- **Intel: benefits of its Leixlip plant to Ireland** — Direct jobs on the Leixlip campus and spin-off jobs with local suppliers and services; corporation tax paid to the State; exports; skills development and research links with universities. _(I10)_
- **Apple: founding, founders, headquarters and products** — Founded in 1976 by Steve Jobs and Steve Wozniak; headquarters Apple Park, Cupertino, California (opened 2017). Products: iPhone (2007), iPad (2010), Mac, Apple Watch (2015) and services such as the App Store and iCloud. Design, software and management stay in California. _(A1, A3)_
- **Apple in Cork: what the Hollyhill campus does** — Opened in 1980 as Apple's first European manufacturing plant; now its European headquarters with about 6,000 staff. It runs operations and supply chain, finance and multilingual AppleCare support, and assembles some built-to-order iMacs. It does not make iPhones. _(A2)_
- **iPhone: sourcing of components and raw materials** — Chips designed by Apple and made by TSMC in Taiwan; screens from Samsung and LG (South Korea); memory from other Asian suppliers; aluminium, lithium and cobalt for batteries (most cobalt comes from the Democratic Republic of the Congo), rare earth metals and gold. _(A4)_
- **iPhone: basic processing units and markets** — Apple outsources assembly to Foxconn, a Taiwanese contract manufacturer, at Zhengzhou ('iPhone City') and Shenzhen in China; a growing share is assembled near Chennai and Bengaluru, India (Foxconn, Tata). Largest markets: the Americas, Europe and China. _(A3, A5, A6)_
- **Apple and the EU state-aid tax case: the timeline** — 2016: the European Commission ruled Ireland had given Apple illegal tax benefits worth €13 billion. 2020: the EU General Court overturned this. September 2024: the EU Court of Justice reinstated it; Ireland received about €14 billion with interest. Unpaid tax, not a fine. _(A7)_
- **Apple: other difficulties and issues it faces** — Dependence on China for assembly amid US–China trade tensions; competition from Samsung and Chinese phone makers; criticism of supplier working conditions; the Athenry data centre (about €850 million), announced 2015, abandoned 2018 after planning delays. _(A7)_

### 2. The MNC and change (19)

- **Why modern economic activities are mobile: give five reasons** — Light, high-value goods (chips, medicines, software) are cheap to fly; containerisation; ICT lets headquarters run branches anywhere; free movement of capital and lower trade barriers (WTO, EU single market); governments compete with grants and low taxes. _(B2)_
- **What drives a multinational to move production to another country** — Lower labour and land costs; tax advantages and grants; getting inside a trade bloc to avoid tariffs; nearness to growing markets; skills; political and economic stability. Moves go both ways: into Ireland (Intel 1989, Dell 1990) and out of it (Dell's manufacturing, 2009). _(B3)_
- **Mobility of economic activity: four examples beyond Dell** — Nissan's car plant at Sunderland, north-east England (1986), inside the EEC market; call centres and IT services moving to Bengaluru, India (Infosys, Wipro); Apple shifting iPhone assembly to India; Intel's planned Magdeburg fab cancelled in 2025. _(B4)_
- **Effects of mobility on receiving and losing regions** — Receiving regions gain jobs, skills and exports. Losing regions suffer unemployment, the loss of spin-off jobs and emigration. Branch-plant economies are vulnerable because decisions about their plants are taken abroad. _(B5)_
- **Corporate strategies that open branch plants** — Expansion into new markets; locating inside a trade bloc; following tax incentives and grants; mergers and takeovers; joint ventures to share huge costs; government subsidies, such as the US CHIPS and Science Act (2022, about $50 billion) and the EU Chips Act (2023). _(C2)_
- **Corporate strategies that close branch plants** — Rationalisation into fewer, larger plants; relocation to cheaper countries; outsourcing to contract manufacturers; falling demand for a mature product; mergers that leave duplicate plants; reshoring production to the home country for security of supply. _(C3)_
- **Dell: the company, its business model and its role in Limerick** — Founded in 1984 by Michael Dell in Austin, Texas (headquarters Round Rock). It sold direct, building computers to order and selling by phone and, from the mid-1990s, online. Dell came to Raheen, Limerick, in 1990: its European manufacturing base and a top Irish exporter. _(C4)_
- **Dell, 2009: the move from Limerick and the reasons for it** — In January 2009 Dell announced its Limerick manufacturing would move to Łódź, Poland. Reasons: lower wages and costs; Polish government aid, approved by the EU; nearness to growing Central and Eastern European markets; falling computer prices; the 2008–09 recession. _(C4)_
- **Dell, 2009: effects on Limerick and what Dell kept in Ireland** — About 1,900 direct jobs were lost, plus jobs at local suppliers, along with one of Ireland's largest exporters. The EU Globalisation Adjustment Fund paid for retraining. Dell kept sales and support offices, and buying EMC in 2016 gave it a large site at Ovens, Co. Cork. _(C4)_
- **Intel's corporate strategy, 2023 to 2025** — Over €30 billion invested in Leixlip by 2023; 49% of the Fab 34 venture sold to Apollo for about $11 billion (2024) to raise cash; about 15,000 global job cuts (August 2024) as profits fell; Magdeburg fab cancelled (2025); US government stake of about 10% (2025). _(C5)_
- **Product life cycle: how production location changes at each stage** — Introduction and growth: in the core, near headquarters, R&D, skilled staff and rich early customers. Maturity: the standardised product gets cheaper, so production moves to low-cost places (once Ireland, now Eastern Europe and Asia). Decline: closure or upgrading. _(P2, P1)_
- **Product life cycle applied to Dell in Limerick** — The personal computer became a mature, standardised product with falling prices, so in 2009 Dell moved assembly from Limerick to cheaper Łódź, Poland, while keeping sales and support in Ireland: the mature-stage pattern. _(P3)_
- **Product life cycle applied to Intel** — Each chip generation lasts a few years. Processes developed at Hillsboro, Oregon, are copied exactly into fabs such as Leixlip (Copy Exactly!); older fabs are upgraded or closed. Leixlip survives by winning new generations (Fab 34). Intel missed the smartphone and AI cycles. _(P4)_
- **Information technology: five impacts on an MNC's operations** — Computer-aided design and automation in fabs; global communication lets one headquarters run plants on several continents; real-time stock control and just-in-time supply; online sales (Dell's direct model); demand shifting from PCs to smartphones, cloud and AI. _(T1)_
- **Teleservices: why Ireland attracted them and the threats they face** — An English-speaking, multilingual workforce (e.g. AppleCare support in Cork), a time zone between the USA and Europe, good broadband and IDA support. Risks: routine work moving to cheaper India (Bengaluru) and the Philippines (Manila); automation and AI chatbots. _(T2)_
- **Ireland's information technology sector: three key facts** — Google's European headquarters is at Barrow Street, Grand Canal Dock, Dublin (Silicon Docks); Meta (Facebook) has its international headquarters in Dublin; Ireland is one of the world's largest exporters of computer services. _(T3)_
- **Data centres in Ireland: why they came and their electricity demand** — Server buildings for cloud computing (Amazon Web Services, Microsoft, Google), drawn by the cool climate, connectivity and firms already here. Their share of metered electricity rose from 5% (2015), so EirGrid restricted new Dublin connections from 2022; by 2023 the share was about 21%. _(T4)_
- **E-commerce: named firms and effects on economic activity** — Amazon (1994, Jeff Bezos, Seattle); Alibaba (1999, Hangzhou). Effects: online-only firms; decline of some high-street shops; growth of warehouses and delivery services; more parcels from Asia. Dell was an early online seller; lockdowns from March 2020 sped up online buying. _(T5)_
- **Future trends for economic activities, and risks for Ireland** — Remote and hybrid working after COVID-19; artificial intelligence and automation cutting routine jobs; reshoring and friend-shoring of strategic industries such as chips; risks for Ireland from the 15% global minimum tax and from US tariffs. _(T6)_

### 3. Patterns in world trade (19)

- **Persistent trade deficit: four disadvantages** — Money flows out of the country to pay for the extra imports; the country must borrow or sell assets to pay for them; cheaper imports replace home industries and jobs; the currency may weaken. _(V4)_
- **Exports and many export partners: the advantages** — Exports bring jobs, tax revenue, foreign earnings and growth. Many partners mean less dependence on one market, so a recession, tariff or crisis in one country, such as the 2025 US tariffs, does less damage. _(V4, V5)_
- **Barriers to trade, and the GATT and WTO dates** — Barriers: tariffs, quotas, subsidies to home producers and regulations; trade blocs remove them between members. GATT was signed in 1947. The WTO, set up on 1 January 1995 and based in Geneva, now has over 160 members; it sets trade rules and settles disputes. _(V6)_
- **Why world trade has grown faster than output since 1950** — Falling trade barriers (GATT, WTO); containerisation, from Malcolm McLean's Ideal X (1956), and bigger ships; air freight and ICT; MNCs trading components between their own plants; China and Eastern Europe entering world trade. _(G1)_
- **Global trade linkages: chokepoints and disruptions to know** — The Suez (1869) and Panama (1914) canals link the triad. The Ever Given blocked Suez for about six days (March 2021); Red Sea attacks from late 2023 sent ships round the Cape of Good Hope; the 2008–09 financial crisis cut trade sharply in 2009; COVID-19 caused the 2021 chip shortage. _(G2)_
- **The trading triad: three ways it dominates the world economy** — North America, Europe and the Pacific Rim produce most of the world's manufactured goods, hold the headquarters of most MNCs, and carry most world trade and foreign direct investment among themselves. _(T1)_
- **The USA in world trade: key facts** — The largest economy and largest importer, with a goods deficit every year since the mid-1970s. NAFTA (1 January 1994) was replaced by USMCA (1 July 2020); Mexico overtook China as top source of US imports in 2023. Tariffs on China from 2018; wider tariffs in 2025. _(T2)_
- **The EU as a triad area: key facts** — The single market began on 1 January 1993; 27 member states since 2020. The largest trading bloc by value, trading strongly with the USA and China. The euro came in 1999 (notes and coins 2002). In July 2025 the EU and USA agreed a 15% US tariff on most EU goods. _(T3)_
- **The Pacific Rim: members and trade organisations** — Mainly East and South-East Asia plus Australia: Japan, China, South Korea, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore and the ASEAN states, not India. ASEAN 1967; APEC 1989; RCEP (signed November 2020, in force 2022) has 15 members with about 30% of world population and GDP. _(T4)_
- **Japan's role in world trade: key facts** — Post-war growth on car and electronics exports (Toyota, Honda, Sony, Nissan); few raw materials, so it imports most oil, coal and iron ore. Toyota uses just-in-time production. Firms built plants inside the EEC, e.g. Nissan, Sunderland (1986). Slower growth since the 1990s. _(T5)_
- **China: from closed economy to workshop of the world** — Deng Xiaoping's reforms from 1978; Special Economic Zones from 1980, including Shenzhen (over 17 million people by 2020); WTO entry on 11 December 2001; largest goods exporter since 2009, with a huge surplus; Belt and Road Initiative 2013; US trade war from 2018. _(T6)_
- **South Korea: how it became a newly industrialised country** — War-damaged (1950–53) and among the poorest countries in the early 1960s, it grew by export-led industry under Park Chung-hee (1961–79). Chaebol such as Samsung (1938), Hyundai, LG and SK lead in ships (Ulsan), cars, memory chips and phones. OECD member from 1996. _(T7)_
- **Old international division of labour: colonial examples** — Colonies exported raw materials and food; Europe made goods and sold them back. Sudan's Gezira Scheme (from 1925) grew cotton for Lancashire mills; India sent raw cotton and tea to Britain; the Gold Coast (Ghana) exported cocoa, still mostly shipped unprocessed. _(D2)_
- **New international division of labour: what changed, with examples** — From the 1960s–70s MNCs moved assembly to NICs and low-wage countries; the core kept headquarters, R&D, design, finance and marketing. Apple: 'Designed by Apple in California. Assembled in China.' Intel: R&D in Oregon, fabs at Leixlip, assembly in Malaysia and Vietnam. _(D3)_
- **The changing division of labour, and Ireland's place in it** — Services are now divided too (IT and call centres in Bengaluru); China is moving into high-value products; cheap assembly is spreading to Vietnam, Bangladesh and India. Ireland went from exporting food to the UK to hosting high-value MNC manufacturing and services. _(D4, D5)_
- **Ireland's main goods and services exports** — Goods (over €200 billion in 2024, a record): pharmaceuticals and chemicals (largest), medical devices (Galway), chips and electronics, food and drink (beef, dairy, whiskey). Services: computer services, financial services, aircraft leasing, tourism. _(I1)_
- **Irish export destinations, and why the USA dominates** — The USA takes about a third of Irish goods exports, then Belgium, Germany, the Netherlands, the UK and China. US pharmaceutical and technology MNCs export from Irish plants back to the USA. Risks: dependence on a few US firms and exposure to US tariffs (2025). _(I2, I4)_
- **Why Irish exports to the UK changed after Brexit** — The UK left the EU on 31 January 2020 (transition ended 31 December 2020), bringing customs checks and taking the UK out of the single market; a weaker pound made Irish goods dearer; Irish firms diversified into other markets. _(I3)_
- **Trade data questions: how to calculate and present the figures** — Balance of trade = exports minus imports; a negative result is a deficit. Difference: subtract the smaller value from the larger. Total: add every partner's value. State units. Use a bar chart to compare countries and a line graph to show change over time. _(V2)_

### 4. How the global economy is organised (22)

- **Why basic processing units are spread across many countries** — Cheap labour for labour-intensive assembly; nearness to raw materials or markets; getting inside trade blocs; tax breaks, grants and export processing zones; falling transport and communication costs; spreading risk, so one disaster or strike cannot stop all production. _(B2)_
- **Nike: how a manufacturer without factories spreads production** — Founded as Blue Ribbon Sports (1964) by Phil Knight and Bill Bowerman; renamed Nike in 1971. Design and marketing stay in Beaverton, Oregon; contract factories in Vietnam (the largest), China and Indonesia make its shoes. 1990s sweatshop criticism led to factory monitoring. _(B3)_
- **Export processing zones: three named examples with dates** — Shannon Free Zone, Co. Clare (1959), one of the world's first; Shenzhen, China, a Special Economic Zone from 1980 (over 17 million people by 2020); maquiladoras along Mexico's border with the USA, such as at Tijuana and Ciudad Juárez, from 1965. _(B4)_
- **Disadvantages of a spread-out production system** — Long supply chains are easily disrupted (2021 chip shortage, Ever Given, Red Sea attacks); branch plants close when costs change; some plants pay low wages with poor conditions; long-distance transport harms the environment. Responses: reshoring and friend-shoring. _(B5)_
- **How the global core developed** — Britain industrialised first (late 18th century), then western Europe and the USA; colonies supplied raw materials and markets, and profits were reinvested at home; cumulative causation (Myrdal, 1957) drew in more investment and people; backwash drained the periphery. _(C2)_
- **How the global periphery developed, or failed to** — Colonial economies were built to export one or two raw materials; railways ran from mines and plantations to ports, not between regions; colonisers drew the borders; after independence, falling commodity prices, debt and conflict held them back. _(C3)_
- **Global core, semi-periphery and periphery: named examples** — Core: the USA, western Europe's Blue Banana and Japan. Semi-periphery: South Korea earlier; China, India, Brazil and Mexico now. Periphery: most of sub-Saharan Africa, parts of Latin America and South Asia. Ireland became core-like through MNC investment. _(C1, C4)_
- **Sub-Saharan Africa: evidence of exclusion from world manufacturing** — Mostly unprocessed raw-material exports: Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana grow over half the world's cocoa, mostly sold as beans; oil is most of Nigeria's exports and copper most of Zambia's; the Democratic Republic of the Congo mines most of the world's cobalt but exports it raw. _(X1)_
- **Why sub-Saharan Africa is excluded: infrastructure and physical reasons** — Poor roads and ports and unreliable electricity; 16 landlocked countries, such as Chad, Niger, Mali and South Sudan, facing high transport costs to reach ports; climate hazards such as drought disrupting farming and production. _(X2)_
- **Why sub-Saharan Africa is excluded: political and human reasons** — Political instability, conflict and corruption scare off investors; low levels of education and skills, and poor health, limit the workforce; small, poor home markets give firms little reason to produce locally; heavy debt absorbs money for development. _(X2)_
- **Why sub-Saharan Africa is excluded: trade and historical reasons** — A colonial legacy of economies built to export raw materials; tariff escalation, as coffee beans enter rich markets at low tariffs while roasted coffee faces higher ones, so processing at home does not pay; competition from established Asian manufacturers. _(X2)_
- **Sudan: a named example of a country excluded from manufacturing** — Civil war between the Sudanese Armed Forces and the Rapid Support Forces since April 2023 has caused one of the world's largest displacement crises. South Sudan's independence in 2011 took most of the oil fields, Sudan's main export base. _(X3)_
- **Attempts to reduce Africa's exclusion from world trade** — Debt relief through the Heavily Indebted Poor Countries initiative (World Bank and IMF, 1996); the African Continental Free Trade Area, trading from 1 January 2021; Chinese and other foreign investment in infrastructure; fair trade. _(X4)_
- **Globalisation's effects on world trade: give seven** — About 80% of world trade is linked to MNC production networks (UNCTAD, 2013), much of it intra-firm; more trade in components; fast-growing services and digital trade; new powers led by China; lower barriers (WTO, trade blocs); containerisation; e-commerce. _(W2)_
- **Globalisation: winners and losers** — Winners: consumers (cheaper goods); NICs and China (jobs and growth). Losers: core manufacturing regions (lost jobs); excluded regions (falling further behind); low-wage assembly workers in unsafe conditions (Rana Plaza). Transport also adds environmental costs. _(W3)_
- **Reaction against globalisation: recent examples** — The US–China trade war (from 2018), US tariffs (2025) and Brexit raised barriers between major economies; governments are reshoring strategic industries such as chips; supply-chain worries grew after COVID-19. _(W4)_
- **India before 1991, and the 1991 reforms** — Five-year plans (from 1951), protection and the Licence Raj (permits to produce or expand) held back growth. After a 1991 balance-of-payments crisis, P. V. Narasimha Rao and Manmohan Singh removed most licences, cut tariffs and opened India to foreign investment. _(E1)_
- **India: positive impacts of globalisation on IT and services** — Bengaluru became the 'Silicon Valley of India'; IT and business-process outsourcing grew, led by Tata Consultancy Services (1968), Infosys (1981) and Wipro; Texas Instruments opened a Bengaluru design centre in 1985. Services now give over half of India's GDP. _(E1)_
- **India: positive impacts of globalisation on industry and living standards** — Maruti Suzuki, a joint venture with Japan's Suzuki, made its first car in 1983; India is the 'pharmacy of the world' for generic medicines; Make in India (2014) and iPhone assembly near Chennai; a growing middle class; hundreds of millions lifted out of extreme poverty. _(E1)_
- **India: negative impacts of globalisation** — Inequality between cities and rural areas and between states; small farmers exposed to world prices (2020–21 protests against three farm laws, repealed November 2021); most workers still informal; MNC accidents such as the Bhopal gas leak at Union Carbide's plant (1984). _(E1)_
- **China since 1978: positive and negative impacts of globalisation** — Reforms from 1978, SEZs from 1980 and WTO entry (2001) made China the largest goods exporter (since 2009) and lifted about 800 million people out of extreme poverty (World Bank). Costs: inequality between coast and interior, pollution, dependence on exports. _(E2)_
- **Bangladesh: garment exports, Rana Plaza and the Accord** — Garments are most of its exports; it is the world's second-largest garment exporter after China, with jobs especially for women. On 24 April 2013 Rana Plaza at Savar, near Dhaka, collapsed, killing over 1,100 garment workers; the 2013 Accord on Fire and Building Safety brought factory inspections. _(E3)_

## Glaciation

### 1. Glacial processes, patterns and associated landforms (17)

- **Ireland's two glacial cold stages: names and extent of the ice** — The older Munsterian ice covered almost all of the island. The more recent Midlandian ice did not cover the far south; the Southern Irish End Moraine marks its southern limit. The Midlandian glaciation ended about 10,000 years ago. _(G1, D2)_
- **Glacier snout: what makes it advance or retreat** — It advances when more ice arrives from the accumulation zone than melts, e.g. after heavier snowfall or cooler summers. It retreats when melting exceeds the ice arriving, e.g. with less snowfall or warmer summers, even though the ice still flows forward. _(G5)_
- **Skill: how to work out the average rate of snout movement** — Average rate = distance moved ÷ number of years, given in metres per year. Illustrative example with invented figures: a snout that retreats 600 m over 20 years moves 600 ÷ 20 = 30 m per year. _(G7)_
- **Corrie and tarn: how they form, in stages** — First, nivation and freeze-thaw enlarge a snow-filled mountain hollow; then the snow turns to ice and the rotating glacier plucks and abrades the back wall and floor, leaving a steep back wall and rock lip; finally, meltwater held behind the lip forms a tarn. _(L1)_
- **Name examples of corries, pyramidal peaks and an arête** — Corries: Coumshingaun, Comeragh Mountains, Co. Waterford; the Devil's Punchbowl, Mangerton, Co. Kerry. Pyramidal peaks: Carrauntoohil, Macgillycuddy's Reeks, Co. Kerry; the Matterhorn. Arête: Striding Edge, Helvellyn, Lake District, England. _(L1, L2, L3)_
- **U-shaped valley: how it forms from a river valley, with an example** — A river first cuts a V-shaped valley with interlocking spurs. A glacier then deepens and widens it by plucking and abrasion and cuts off the spurs as truncated spurs, leaving a steep-sided, flat-floored trough, e.g. Glendalough and Glenmalure, Co. Wicklow. _(L4)_
- **Hanging valley and waterfall: how they form, with an example** — A tributary glacier is smaller than the main one and erodes less deeply, so when the ice melts its valley is left high above the main valley floor and its stream falls over the edge as a waterfall, e.g. Poulanass Waterfall, Glendalough, Co. Wicklow. _(L5)_
- **Ribbon lake: how it forms, with an Irish example** — Plucking and abrasion overdeepen one section of a glacial valley floor more than the rest, leaving a rock basin; when the ice melts, the basin fills with water as a long, narrow lake, e.g. the Upper Lake, Glendalough, Co. Wicklow. _(L6)_
- **Fjord: how it forms, with an Irish and a foreign example** — A glacier overdeepens a coastal valley so that its floor lies well below sea level; when the ice melts, the sea floods the valley, leaving a steep-sided inlet, e.g. Killary Harbour on the Co. Galway–Co. Mayo border, and Sognefjord, Norway. _(L7)_
- **Roche moutonnée: how each side forms, with an example** — Ice flows over a bedrock outcrop: abrasion smooths and polishes the upstream (stoss) side, while plucking tears blocks from the downstream (lee) side, leaving a steep, rough face. Example: Lembert Dome, Yosemite, California, USA. _(L8, E1, E2)_
- **Drumlin: how it forms, in stages** — First, ice deposits or overrides a mass of till on its bed; then the moving ice moulds it into a streamlined hill parallel to the ice flow, with a blunt up-ice end and a tapered down-ice end; drumlins lie in aligned swarms, a basket of eggs landscape. _(D3)_
- **Name an Irish drumlin belt and two places with drowned drumlins** — The drumlin belt across Co. Cavan, Co. Monaghan and Co. Leitrim. Drowned drumlins, islands formed where the sea flooded the low ground between drumlins: Clew Bay, Co. Mayo, and Strangford Lough, Co. Down. _(D3)_
- **Esker: how it forms, with an Irish example** — Meltwater flowing in a tunnel within or beneath the glacier deposits sorted sand and gravel on the tunnel floor as its velocity falls; when the ice melts, a long, winding ridge is left, e.g. Esker Riada across the Midlands, the ancient Slí Mhór routeway. _(F2)_
- **Outwash plain: how it forms, with an Irish example and its uses** — Meltwater spreads sorted sand and gravel beyond the terminal moraine onto flat ground, building a flat plain. Example: the Curragh, Co. Kildare, well-drained grassland used for horse training and an army camp. _(F3)_
- **Glacial till vs fluvio-glacial deposits: how they differ** — Till is dropped directly by ice, so it is unsorted, unstratified and angular, ranging from clay to boulders. Fluvio-glacial material is laid down by meltwater, which sorts it by size and lays it in stratified layers. _(F1, T2)_
- **Classify glacial landforms as erosion, deposition or fluvio-glacial** — Glacial erosion: corrie, arête, pyramidal peak, U-shaped valley, hanging valley, ribbon lake, fjord, roche moutonnée, striations. Glacial deposition: till, moraines, drumlins, erratics. Fluvio-glacial: eskers, outwash plains, kames, kettle holes. _(F1)_
- **Name an Irish example of glacial erratics** — Granite erratics carried by ice from Connemara rest on the limestone of the Burren, Co. Clare; their rock type is unlike the bedrock beneath them. _(D4)_

### 2. Isostasy (10)

- **Isostatic vs eustatic change: what is the difference?** — Isostatic change: the land moves, a local change in its height relative to the sea, e.g. rebound after an ice sheet melts. Eustatic change: the sea moves, a worldwide change in sea level, e.g. falling as water is locked in ice sheets and rising as it melts. _(I6, I3, I4)_
- **Isostatic rebound: why it continues today, and three places where it does** — Rebound lags far behind melting, so land can still be rising long after the ice has gone. It continues in Scandinavia around the Gulf of Bothnia, around Hudson Bay in Canada, and in Scotland. _(I4)_
- **Denudation and deposition: how they change the load on the crust** — Erosion removes weight from the land, so the crust rises. Heavy deposition adds weight, so the crust sinks; a delta is one place where a large load builds up. _(I5)_
- **Landscapes temporarily out of balance: the two situations** — Where uplift is faster than denudation, features such as raised beaches are left above the present sea. Where sea level rises faster than the land, the sea floods valleys cut when it stood lower, forming rias, fjords and drowned drumlins. Either way the features don't match present conditions. _(I8, I1)_
- **Raised beaches: five features found on them, and two examples** — An old cliff line inland of the sea; a wave-cut notch at the former sea level; sea caves and stacks above the present tide reach; beach shingle; a flat former wave-cut platform. Examples: Malin Head, Inishowen, Co. Donegal; the Isle of Jura, Scotland. _(C1)_
- **Submergent coast: three features, with an example of each** — Rias (drowned river valleys), e.g. Bantry Bay; fjords (drowned glacial troughs), e.g. Killary Harbour on the Co. Galway–Co. Mayo border; drowned drumlins, e.g. Clew Bay, Co. Mayo, and Strangford Lough, Co. Down. _(C2)_
- **Name four rias in the south-west and the landscape they drowned** — Bantry Bay, the Kenmare River, Dingle Bay and Cork Harbour are drowned river valleys of the Munster ridge-and-valley landscape, flooded as the sea rose relative to the land. _(C2)_
- **North vs south-west of Ireland: how isostasy shaped each coast** — The north had thicker ice, so it was depressed more and has rebounded more: its coast shows raised beaches, as in Donegal. The south-west shows the opposite pattern: a drowned coast with rias, as in Cork and Kerry. _(C3)_
- **Fall in base level: effect on a river, and three landforms it leaves** — The land rises relative to the sea, giving the river renewed erosive power (rejuvenation); it cuts down again, working upstream from the mouth. Landforms: a knickpoint (often with a waterfall), incised meanders and river terraces. _(R1, R2, I7)_
- **Rise in base level: effect on a river's lower course** — When the sea rises relative to the land, the lower course of the river is drowned and becomes an estuary or a ria. _(R3)_

## Graph skills

### Figure interpretation (24)

- **Reading any figure: what to check before reading a value** — Read the title, both axis labels, the key or legend, the units (%, thousands, €m, mm, °C, per 1,000), the years shown and the source line. Most lost marks come from a misread unit, year or column. _(R1)_
- **Reading a value from a bar chart, line graph or table: the method** — Run a ruler from the top of the bar or point across to the vertical axis, estimate between gridlines and quote the value with its unit. In a table, find the row and column headings and read the cell where they meet; don't confuse a totals row or the wrong year's column with the value asked for. _(R2, R4)_
- **Short data questions: how to find 'twice as much' and 'above the average line'** — Twice as much: double the smaller value and look for the value that matches it. Above the average line: count the bars or points whose tops lie above it. Highest or lowest: scan every value, not just the first and last. _(R3)_
- **Infographics and pictograms: how to read them without losing marks** — Read each panel's own label and unit: headline figures are often totals, while small charts and panels may show percentages. In a pictogram, check the key for what one icon stands for; a part-icon is a fraction of it. _(R5, G8)_
- **Describing a trend in an answer: what to include** — State the overall direction; give the start and end values with years and units; name the period of fastest change and any peak, trough or reversal; quantify the change (difference and/or percentage change); point out anomalies. _(T2)_
- **Comparing two series on one graph: what to say** — Which series is higher; when the lines cross; whether the gap widens or narrows; the difference in a given year, for example net migration = immigration minus emigration. _(T3)_
- **Evaluating a figure (Higher): weaknesses to look for** — An axis that does not start at zero or has uneven intervals; dates far apart or unevenly spaced; a small sample; a source that is unreliable or out of date; percentages hiding small totals; treating correlation as proof of cause. _(T5)_
- **Percentage change: the formula and the common mistake** — Percentage change = (new value − old value) ÷ old value × 100; a positive result is an increase and a negative result a decrease. Always divide by the old (earlier) value, never the new one. _(S6)_
- **Percentage points vs percentage change: how to answer when both values are percentages** — Subtract to give the change in percentage points, and add the percentage change if unsure. Illustrative: 20% rising to 90% is +70 percentage points but a 350% percentage change, (90 − 20) ÷ 20 × 100. _(S7)_
- **Percentage of a total, rate per 1,000 and density: the three formulas** — Percentage of a total = part ÷ total × 100; rate per 1,000 = number ÷ population × 1,000 (e.g. birth rate = births ÷ population × 1,000); density = number ÷ area, given as people per km². _(S5, S9)_
- **Calculating a missing value X: the method** — Parts of a whole add to 100% (pie chart, percentage table) or to a stated total (stacked bar, total row), so X = total minus the sum of the known parts. To find a number from a percentage: number = percentage ÷ 100 × total. _(S8)_
- **Calculation questions: rules on rounding, units and working** — Round to the precision the data uses, or one decimal place; keep the unit and the multiplier (thousands, millions) in every answer; for a difference, say which is larger; show the working, which can earn marks if the answer slips. _(S10, S1)_
- **Drawing a graph: rules for the paper, title and axes** — Use graph paper, a pencil and a ruler, and fill most of the space. The title says what the data is, where and when, in the question's wording. Categories or time go on the x-axis and values on the y-axis; label both with units. _(D1, D2, D3)_
- **Drawing a graph: rules for the scale, plotting and key** — The scale starts at zero, is evenly divided and fits the largest value (e.g. steps of 5 or 10). Plot every value accurately; bars of equal width with equal gaps; join points with ruled straight lines; a key if more than one shading is used. _(D4, D5, D6)_
- **Line graph: how to space the time axis** — Space the years in proportion to the time between them: for data for 1990, 2000, 2010 and 2015, the last gap is half the others. Plot each point at its correct year and join the points with ruled lines; a steeper line means faster change. _(D4, G5)_
- **'Draw a suitable graph': which graph suits which data** — One value for separate categories: bar chart. Change over time: line graph. Shares of a whole adding to 100%: pie chart or divided bar. Two related variables for the same places: scatter graph. Three components adding to 100%: triangular graph. _(D7)_
- **Compound bar chart: how to read one component** — Take the top of its segment minus the bottom of its segment; the top of the whole bar is the total. In a percentage compound bar every bar reaches 100%, so the segments show shares rather than totals. _(G3)_
- **Pie chart: calculating the angles and drawing it** — Degrees = percentage × 3.6 (360° ÷ 100), or value ÷ total × 360 for raw numbers; angles must total 360°. Draw a circle with a compass and a radius to 12 o'clock, measure each angle clockwise with a protractor, largest first, and label each segment. _(G6)_
- **Population pyramid: how to draw the axes and bars** — Age groups in 5-year bands on the central vertical axis, 0–4 at the bottom and the oldest at the top; males on the left, females on the right; the horizontal axis shows % of the population (or number), scaled outward from zero on both sides. _(G9)_
- **Population pyramid: what four features of the shape tell you** — A wide base means a high birth rate; a narrow top means low life expectancy; a narrowing base means a falling birth rate; bulges and indentations suggest baby booms, migration, war or famine. _(G9)_
- **Climate graph: the calculations examiners ask for** — Annual temperature range = maximum minus minimum mean monthly temperature; mean annual temperature = sum of the 12 monthly means ÷ 12; total annual rainfall = sum of the 12 bars. Also read the wettest and driest months. _(G10)_
- **Scatter graph: drawing rules and how to describe it** — Put the independent variable on the x-axis; plot each place as a point and do not join the points; draw a line of best fit by eye. Describe the correlation (positive, negative or none; strong or weak) and any outliers. Correlation does not prove cause. _(G11)_
- **Triangular graph: how to read and plot a point** — Read each component along the gridlines parallel to the side where its 0 is, following the direction its numbers run. To plot: find the first value on its scale, follow its gridline in to where it meets the second value's gridline, and check with the third. The three always total 100%. _(G12)_
- **Choropleth map: rules for the classes and its weaknesses** — Classes must not overlap and must cover every value (e.g. 0–49, 50–99, 100–149, 150+); darker = higher; a key is essential. But it implies the value is even across each area, hides variation inside it and makes boundaries look like sudden changes. _(G13)_

### Census of population data (18)

- **Irish census: who runs it, how often and on what night** — Run by the Central Statistics Office (CSO), and taking part is required by law. Normally held every five years on a Sunday night in April; traditionally in years ending in 1 and 6, but since the 2022 census the next is 2027. The first census of the Irish Free State was in 1926. _(C1, C2)_
- **Which two Irish censuses were postponed, and why?** — The 2001 census was postponed to 2002 because of foot-and-mouth disease; the 2021 census was postponed to 2022 because of COVID-19. The next census is planned for 2027. _(C2, C3)_
