- **Census 2011, 2016 and 2022: the population totals** — Census 2011: about 4.59 million. Census 2016 (24 April 2016): about 4.76 million. Census 2022 (3 April 2022): 5,149,139, about 5.15 million, a rise of about 387,000 (about 8%) on 2016. _(C3)_
- **Census 2022: what it asked for the first time** — It asked for the first time about working from home and about renewable energy sources at the home, such as solar panels, and included an optional time capsule page whose contents will be released after 100 years. _(C7)_
- **How the census forms are delivered and collected** — Enumerators deliver a household form to every dwelling before census night and collect it afterwards; one form per household, with a page for each person; hotels, hospitals and prisons are covered separately; vacant dwellings are recorded. _(C5)_
- **Census confidentiality: the 100-year rule and the oldest returns online** — Information is used only for statistics, and personal forms are kept closed for 100 years before becoming historical records. The 1901 and 1911 returns are free online from the National Archives of Ireland and are used for family history. _(C6)_
- **Census data: six ways governments and planners use it** — Schools (number of young children); healthcare and nursing homes (size of the older population); housing (household numbers and vacancy); transport (commuting flows). Also pensions and welfare, and targeting resources at disadvantaged areas. _(U1)_
- **Census data and Dáil constituencies: the rules** — Constituencies are reviewed after each census by the Electoral Commission (An Coimisiún Toghcháin). The Constitution requires one TD for every 20,000 to 30,000 people and a revision at least once every 12 years. _(U2)_
- **Census data: three uses outside government** — Businesses use it to choose where to locate shops and services; researchers and students use it, including to track the Irish language; old returns are used for historical and genealogical research. _(U3)_
- **Limitations of census data: name seven** — It is a snapshot of one night, out of date between censuses, with a long wait for detailed results; homeless people and people avoiding the count may be missed; self-reported answers may be inaccurate; it is very expensive; area boundaries change over time. _(U4)_
- **Net migration from two censuses: how to estimate it** — Population change = natural increase + net migration, so net migration = total change minus natural increase (births minus deaths from civil registration). Illustrative Rivertown ED: change +630, natural increase 170, so net migration +460. _(D5)_
- **Age structure: the three groups and what a high dependency ratio means** — Young dependents 0–14, working age 15–64, old dependents 65 and over. A high young dependency ratio means more schools are needed; a high old one means more pensions and healthcare. Divide by the 15–64 group, not the total. _(D2)_
- **Sex structure: why the balance of males and females varies** — Women outnumber men in the older age groups because of longer life expectancy. Local imbalances arise from migration, for example young women moving to cities for work. _(D3)_
- **Ireland's long-term population trend: three census figures** — The whole island had about 8.2 million people in 1841. After a steep fall following the Great Famine, the State was at its lowest in the 1961 census, about 2.8 million. Census 2022 was the first to pass 5 million since the 1851 census. _(D7, C3)_
- **Ireland's population today: three features shown by the census** — Growth driven by both natural increase and immigration; an ageing population, with a growing share aged 65 and over; rising cultural diversity, with many non-Irish citizens. _(D7)_
- **Why Irish census figures do not include Northern Ireland** — Irish census figures cover the State, not the island. Northern Ireland has its own census, run by NISRA; its latest, on 21 March 2021, counted about 1.9 million. The Republic's latest was 2022, so the two must not be merged. _(C3)_
- **Local-area census study: the eight steps** — Choose the area (ED, Small Area, town or county); find its tables for two or more censuses on the CSO website; record population, age, sex, households, nationality, commuting; calculate changes and ratios; graph them; describe; explain; note limitations. _(L1)_
- **Local population trends: typical explanations** — New housing estates and commuter growth near cities and motorways bring rising population, young families and high young dependency; rural decline in remote areas brings a falling, ageing population; also immigration, or a major employer closing or opening. _(L2)_

## Mapwork

### Map interpretation (25)

- **1:50 000 OS map: the scale conversions to know** — 1 cm on the map = 500 m (0.5 km) on the ground; 2 cm = 1 km; each grid square is 2 cm × 2 cm, which is 1 km × 1 km = 1 km². Give distances in km and areas in km². _(O2)_
- **Grid references: the order and the rules that lose marks** — Start with the sub-zone letter; read eastings (numbered along the top and bottom) first, then northings (up the sides): along the corridor, then up the stairs. A four-figure reference uses the bottom-left (south-west) corner of the square. _(G1, G2, G3)_
- **Six-figure grid reference: how to give one, and when to use it** — Read the easting to the west of the point and estimate tenths east of it, then the northing below and tenths north of it; give the centre of the symbol. Use it for points (a building, junction, spot height); use four figures for areas. _(G4, G3)_
- **Latitude and longitude: how to write a position, and Ireland's range** — Each degree has 60 minutes; write latitude first with N or S, then longitude with E or W, e.g. Dublin about 53°20′N, 6°15′W. Ireland lies between about 51.5°N and 55.5°N and about 5.5°W and 10.5°W. _(L3, L7)_
- **Straight-line distance on the OS map: the method** — Measure in cm with a ruler and multiply by 0.5 to get km, or mark a paper edge and read it against the scale bar; answer in km to one decimal place. From two grid references: distance = √(ΔE² + ΔN²). _(D1, D2)_
- **Curved distance along a road, railway or river: the method** — Mark the start on the straight edge of a strip of paper, pivot it at each bend and mark as you go (or lay string along the route), then straighten it and measure against the scale bar. Start and stop exactly at the points named. _(D3)_
- **Area on the OS map: regular and irregular shapes** — Rectangular area: km east–west × km north–south, each square 1 km². Irregular feature (lake, bay, land above a contour): count full squares, count squares more than half covered as full, ignore those less than half covered; answer in km², approximately. _(D4, D5, D6)_
- **Direction on the OS map: the method** — North is the top of the map. Draw a line from the start to the destination, imagine a compass centred on the start and read the direction of travel; 'from A to B' is read at A. A river flows from high ground to low ground, shown by spot heights and contours. _(R1, R2, R3)_
- **Finding the height of a point on an OS map** — Read the spot height or triangulation pillar beside it, or the nearest numbered contour, and count contours up or down at 10 m each (the Discovery Series interval). The highest point on the map is the highest spot height or pillar. _(H1, H2)_
- **Contour patterns: concave vs convex, and valley vs spur** — Concave: contours close near the top, so steep above and gentle below; convex is the reverse. Students often swap them. In a valley the contour Vs point upstream, towards higher ground; on a spur they point downhill. _(H4, H5)_
- **Gradient: the method, with an illustrative example** — Find each point's height and subtract for the VI; measure the distance and convert to metres for the HE; divide HE by VI and write 1 in x. Illustrative: summit 512 m to bridge 52 m gives VI 460 m; HE 4 600 m; gradient 1 in 10. _(H6)_
- **Elevation profile questions: how to identify each labelled point** — Match the point to the map by its distance along the line and its height. A river is a dip with a blue line on the map; read a road's number, the forestry type or land use, a walk or the townland from the map there; read slope type from the curve. _(X3)_
- **Short legend questions: road letters and forestry types** — M = motorway; N = national road (national primary and secondary routes); R = regional road. Coniferous plantation has pointed tree symbols; deciduous woodland rounded ones; mixed woodland both. Use the legend's terms. _(S2, S3)_
- **Half-scale sketch map: how to draw the frame** — Halve the length and breadth of the extract (each 2 cm square becomes 1 cm) and draw a frame with four ruled sides; add light pencil grid lines at the same eastings and northings and label the edge numbers. Illustrative: 28 cm × 16 cm becomes 14 cm × 8 cm. _(K1, K2)_
- **Sketch map: the mistakes that lose marks** — Tracing the map; adding features not asked for; placing a feature in the wrong part of the frame; leaving a feature unlabelled (it earns nothing); not following a contour round every part asked for; no title or north arrow. _(K4, K5, K6)_
- **Name and describe five drainage patterns as seen on a map** — Dendritic: tree-like, tributaries meet at acute angles; trellised: tributaries meet at right angles; radial: streams flow out from a central hill; centripetal: streams flow in to a lake or lowland; deranged: no clear pattern, many small lakes and marshes. _(P1)_
- **Glacial landforms: how to recognise them on an OS map** — Corrie: armchair hollow, contours close on three sides; tarn: round lake in a corrie; arête: narrow ridge, close contours on both sides; U-shaped valley: flat floor, steep sides; ribbon lake: long narrow lake on its floor; drumlins: small oval contour rings. _(P2)_
- **Fluvial landforms: how to recognise them on an OS map** — Meander: river swinging in loops; oxbow lake: crescent lake beside a meander; floodplain: wide, flat, no contours, often marshy; braiding: river split into channels round islands; confluence: where a tributary joins; waterfall: contours crowded across a river. _(P2)_
- **Coastal and karst landforms: how to recognise them on an OS map** — Beach: sand or shingle shading along the shore; cliff: cliff symbol or contours crowded at the coast; headland: land projecting into the sea; bay: curved inlet; sand dunes: dune shading; swallow hole: a stream ending abruptly where it goes underground. _(P2)_
- **Rural settlement patterns: why each forms where it does** — Nucleated settlement clusters at a route focus or service, such as a crossroads, bridge or church; linear settlement forms along a road that gives access to services; dispersed settlement reflects farming and land consolidation. Give a grid reference for each. _(N1)_
- **Absence of settlement: reasons with their map evidence** — High land above about 200–300 m (cold, wet, exposed, thin soils); steep slopes (crowded contours); poorly drained land (marsh or bog symbol, floodplain liable to flooding); forestry plantations; lakes. Give a four-figure grid reference. _(N3, N4)_
- **Historic settlement: map evidence for three phases** — Prehistoric: megalithic tombs, standing stones, fulachta fia, promontory forts, crannógs. Early Christian: ringforts, souterrains, churches, holy wells, round towers, high crosses. Norman: mottes, castles, town walls, abbeys and friaries. _(A1)_
- **Why a town developed at its location: reasons and map evidence** — Bridging point (bridge, converging roads); route focus (N and R roads and a railway meet); defence (castle, town walls, hill, river bend); river or coastal site (pier, harbour, mills); flat lowland (wide contours); fertile farmland; an early church or abbey. _(U1)_
- **Site suitability for a development: reasons to give with map evidence** — Give a six-figure reference. Flat land (widely spaced contours); access (N road, motorway junction, railway, port); near a town for labour and markets; space to expand; off the floodplain, away from scenic areas. Wind farms need exposed high ground. _(E1)_
- **Tourism potential from the OS map: what to cite** — Accessibility (road access, parking); services (hostels, hotels, camping sites, tourist information); attractions (antiquities, viewpoints, walks, cycle routes, beaches, lakes, golf courses). Evidence each with a grid reference and explain it. _(E3)_

### 2. Textual sources (11)

- **Primary or secondary? How to classify questionnaires, old maps and the census** — A questionnaire the student designs and carries out is primary; a published survey is secondary; old maps and the census are secondary. Secondary does not mean less useful: it gives background, past data and comparisons fieldwork cannot. _(T1)_
- **Name six present-day and five past secondary sources** — Present: textbooks; newspapers; official reports; development plans; tourist brochures; websites. Past: historical maps (old OS editions, town plans, estate maps); old photographs and postcards; old newspapers; earlier census returns; parish records. _(T2, T3)_
- **Who produces official data in Ireland? Name five bodies and what each supplies** — CSO: census and official statistics; Met Éireann: weather and climate data; EPA: water and air quality reports; Tailte Éireann: national mapping and OS maps (Ordnance Survey Ireland joined it in 2023); local authorities: development plans. _(T4)_
- **How to spot bias in a source** — Ask who wrote it and why: a developer's brochure, a campaign group's leaflet, a political leaflet or an advertisement may be one-sided. Bias shows in one-sided arguments, emotive language and selective figures. _(E2)_
- **Reliability checklist for a secondary source: six checks** — Author (official body, expert or anonymous); date (current, or past for comparison); purpose (inform, persuade or sell); evidence (sources cited, data given); scale (national figures may not fit the local area); agreement with other sources. _(E3, U4)_
- **Limitations of secondary data: name five** — It may be out of date; it was collected for another purpose; it may not cover the exact study area; definitions may differ between sources or dates; online sources may be unverified. _(E4)_
- **Fact vs opinion: identify each in a passage (illustrative)** — Illustrative newsletter: 'Traffic on Main Street has doubled since the new industrial estate opened' is a fact, checkable with traffic counts or council data; 'the relief road is a disaster for the town' is an opinion, shown by the emotive word 'disaster'. _(E1)_
- **Comparing an old map with a present-day map: the five steps** — Match scale and orientation; find fixed points on both (bridge, church, castle, main street); list what has appeared, disappeared and stayed; describe the change (e.g. a new bypass, loss of a mill, a straightened river); explain each change. _(C3, C1)_
- **First Ordnance Survey of Ireland: its scale and date, and the limits of old sources** — It mapped the whole country at six inches to one mile in the 1830s and 1840s. Old maps may use different scales, symbols and place-name spellings and can be inaccurate; old photographs and accounts show a single moment, a chosen viewpoint or one writer's view. _(C2, C5)_
- **Sketch map of a historical map (Higher, 20 marks): the method** — Rule a frame of the same shape. Old maps usually have no grid, so divide map and frame into a light pencil grid of equal sections; place and label only the features asked (e.g. shoreline, two streets drawn meeting, a named building); add title and north arrow. _(C4)_
- **Stimulus text or cartoon questions: how to answer them** — Read the question first; underline key terms and figures in the text; answer using the text's evidence plus your own knowledge, in correct geographical terms. For a cartoon, identify the message, the issue, who is shown and the viewpoint. _(X1, X2, U2)_

### 3. The Geographical Investigation (20%) (17)

- **Geographical Investigation: weighting, topic list and deadline** — Worth 20% of the total mark (written exam 80%) and compulsory at Higher and Ordinary level. The topic comes from the SEC's annual list issued in Leaving Certificate Year 1; the report is due at the end of term one in Year 2, on a date the SEC sets. _(R1, R2, R3)_
- **Geographical Investigation: rules on group work, sources and authenticity** — A class may collect data together, but each student writes a full report of their own; group reports are not accepted. Sources are about 60% primary and 40% secondary. The geography teacher and school principal verify it is the candidate's own work. _(R4, R5, R6)_
- **Investigation hypothesis: the rules, with an illustrative example** — A precise, testable statement, not a question or topic title; narrow enough to test in a day or two; no vague words such as 'better'; the place named. Illustrative: 'The velocity of the River Dallow increases downstream.' _(I2, I5)_
- **Investigation objectives: how many, and what each must state** — Three to five specific, measurable tasks that together test the hypothesis, each naming what will be measured and where; each later gets its own results. Illustrative: measure width, average depth and velocity at five sites 500 m apart. _(I3, I4)_
- **Investigation planning: tasks to complete before fieldwork** — Study OS maps and aerial photographs; choose and justify sites (e.g. evenly spaced along a river); choose the date and time; gather equipment; divide tasks in the group; arrange landowner permission; prepare a recording sheet with columns for repeat readings. _(P2, P3)_
- **Questionnaire design for an investigation: the rules** — Keep it short; mostly closed questions (tick boxes, yes/no, rating scales) for easy analysis, plus a few open ones; neutral, unbiased wording; no unnecessary personal questions; pilot it first; plan who, where, when and how many to ask. _(P4)_
- **Fieldwork safety and ethics: precautions to state** — Complete a risk assessment; never work alone; supervision near water or traffic; suitable clothing and footwear; check the weather and river level; respect property and the environment; questionnaires anonymous and voluntary. _(P5)_
- **River study instruments: what each one measures** — Measuring tape or ranging poles: channel width; metre stick: depth at regular intervals across the channel; float and stopwatch, or a flow meter: velocity; clinometer: slope angle; callipers or ruler: pebble size; roundness chart: pebble shape. _(C2)_
- **Measuring velocity with a float: the method and its limitation** — Time a float over a measured distance, such as 10 m, repeat the timing and average it; velocity = distance ÷ time. A surface float overestimates average velocity, so the results are estimates, not exact values. _(C2, C5)_
- **Discharge calculation: an illustrative worked example** — Illustrative site 5: width 6.0 m × average depth 0.40 m = cross-sectional area 2.4 m²; a float takes 10 s over 10 m, so velocity is 1.0 m/s; discharge = 2.4 × 1.0 = 2.4 m³/s. _(W4)_
- **Accuracy in data collection: four good practices** — Take repeat readings and average them; use the same method and the same people at every site; record the units; note the conditions, such as the weather, time and river level. _(C5)_
- **Problems encountered: what the report must say, with an example** — For each problem, state the problem, its effect on the data and the solution. Illustrative: at site 3 the float snagged on reeds twice, so those runs were discarded and three clean timings taken. _(C6)_
- **Investigation results: how to present each finding** — Go objective by objective: state the finding in words with its key figures; support it with a titled, labelled illustration with units, referred to in the text; describe trends with figures; note anomalies; keep results separate from conclusions. _(W2, W3, W5)_
- **Investigation conclusion: three things it must do** — State whether the hypothesis is accepted, rejected or partly accepted, using each objective's evidence; explain the pattern geographically; compare with established theory (Bradshaw model for rivers, Burgess for urban land use) and explain differences. _(V2, V3)_
- **Bradshaw model: what changes downstream** — Downstream, a river's discharge, channel width, depth and velocity tend to increase, while gradient and load particle size decrease. _(V3)_
- **Investigation evaluation: limitations and improvements to give** — Limits: one day's data, few sites, a small sample, measurement error, the weather on the day, a float measuring surface velocity only. Improvements: more sites, repeat visits in other seasons, a flow meter, larger samples, a longer stretch of river. _(V4, V5)_
- **Investigation report: what loses marks** — Vague or untestable hypotheses; methods listed without explanation; results without figures; unlabelled or irrelevant illustrations; conclusions not linked to results; generic evaluations ('it went well'); writing outside the booklet's space; copied material. _(M3, R7)_

## Option atmosphere ocean

### 1. The atmosphere and oceans as linked systems (25)

- **Atmosphere and oceans: the four things they exchange as one linked system** — Heat (the ocean warms or cools the air above it); water (evaporation and precipitation); gases (the ocean absorbs and releases carbon dioxide and oxygen); momentum (wind drives surface currents, and currents shift pressure and wind patterns). _(L1)_
- **Dry air: the four main gases and their share by volume** — Nitrogen about 78%; oxygen about 21%; argon about 0.9%; carbon dioxide about 0.04%. Traces of neon, helium, methane, ozone and other gases make up the rest. _(A1)_
- **Name three variable components of the atmosphere** — Water vapour, from almost 0% over deserts and the poles to about 4% in hot, humid tropical air; aerosols (dust, sea salt, smoke, volcanic ash, pollen) that act as condensation nuclei and scatter sunlight; ozone, concentrated in the stratosphere. _(A2)_
- **Atmospheric carbon dioxide: key figures and where it is measured** — About 280 ppm before the Industrial Revolution and over 420 ppm by the mid-2020s, because of human activity. Measured continuously at Mauna Loa, Hawaii, since 1958: the Keeling Curve, begun by Charles David Keeling. _(A3)_
- **Troposphere: height, temperature trend and why weather happens there** — Up to about 8 km over the poles and 16 to 18 km over the equator. Temperature falls with height because the air is heated from below by the ground. It holds most of the atmosphere's mass and nearly all its water vapour, so nearly all weather occurs here. _(S1)_
- **Stratosphere: height, temperature trend and key features** — From the tropopause to about 50 km. Temperature rises with height because ozone absorbs ultraviolet radiation; the ozone layer is at about 15 to 35 km. Very dry and stable, so few clouds form, but cumulonimbus anvils spread out at its base. _(S2)_
- **Mesosphere and thermosphere: temperature trend and one feature of each** — Mesosphere (about 50 to 80-85 km): temperature falls to about −90 °C, the coldest point; most meteors burn up here. Thermosphere (above about 85 km): temperature rises sharply as its few molecules absorb high-energy solar radiation; auroras occur here. _(S3, S4)_
- **Oceans: key figures for extent and depth** — Cover about 71% of the earth's surface and hold about 97% of its water; average depth about 3.7 km. Deepest point: the Challenger Deep, Mariana Trench, western Pacific, about 11,000 m. The Pacific covers about a third of the earth's surface. _(O1)_
- **Seawater salinity: average figure and where the salts come from** — About 35 parts per thousand (35 g of salt per kg) on average, mostly sodium chloride, with magnesium, sulphate, calcium and potassium. The salts come from rocks weathered and carried by rivers and from volcanic gases and vents. _(O2)_
- **Salinity: why it varies, with named seas** — Higher where evaporation exceeds precipitation: subtropical oceans, the Red Sea (about 40 parts per thousand) and the Mediterranean. Lower where rain is heavy, large rivers enter or ice melts: the Baltic is brackish, well under 10 parts per thousand. _(O3)_
- **Ocean temperature layers: the three layers from the surface down** — A warm, well-mixed surface layer heated by the sun and stirred by wind; the thermocline, where temperature falls rapidly with depth; the cold (about 0 to 4 °C), dark deep ocean, the largest part by volume. Polar waters change little with depth. _(O4)_
- **Seawater density: what makes water sink and why it matters** — Water is denser when colder and saltier. When sea ice forms it leaves its salt behind, so the water beneath becomes saltier and denser and sinks; these density differences drive the deep ocean circulation. _(O5, O3)_
- **The ocean as a store of heat and carbon: key figures and one effect** — It has taken up over 90% of the extra heat from global warming (IPCC) and roughly a quarter of human carbon dioxide emissions. This slows warming but acidifies the water: surface pH has fallen from about 8.2 to about 8.1, harming corals and shellfish. _(L2, O6)_
- **Measuring temperature: how three thermometers work** — Liquid-in-glass: mercury or alcohol expands up a narrow tube. Six's maximum-minimum (invented by James Six): metal indexes are pushed to the highest and lowest points, read daily and reset with a magnet. Electronic: a sensor's resistance changes with temperature. _(M2)_
- **Measuring pressure: mercury barometer, barograph and sea-level correction** — Mercury barometer (Torricelli, 1643): the atmosphere's weight holds up a mercury column that rises as pressure rises. Barograph: an aneroid drives a pen on a rotating drum, giving a continuous trace. Readings are corrected to mean sea level for comparison. _(M3)_
- **Wind: how speed and direction are measured and the standard height** — A cup anemometer's three or four cups spin faster as wind rises; turns are counted electrically and converted to speed. A wind vane's large tail is pushed downwind, so its pointer faces where the wind comes from. Standard height: 10 m above open ground. _(M4, M5)_
- **Wet-and-dry bulb hygrometer: steps to find relative humidity** — Read the dry bulb; read the wet bulb, whose wet muslin is cooled by evaporation so it reads lower; subtract to get the difference; look up relative humidity in tables using the dry bulb reading and the difference. Equal readings mean saturated air. _(M6)_
- **Rain gauge and Campbell–Stokes sunshine recorder: how each works** — Rain gauge: a standard funnel, rim raised above open ground to avoid splash, drains into a measuring cylinder read in mm daily. Campbell–Stokes: a glass sphere focuses the sun's rays to burn a trace on a card; the burn length gives hours of sunshine. _(M7)_
- **Valentia Observatory, Co. Kerry: two reasons it matters** — It launches weather balloons carrying radiosondes, and tracking the balloons gives upper winds; it has one of the longest weather records in Ireland, from the 19th century, used to show climate trends. _(M8, I6)_
- **Measuring the oceans: four methods besides Argo floats** — Moored buoys of the Irish Marine Data Buoy Observation Network (Marine Institute with Met Éireann) record pressure, temperature, wind and waves; drifting buoys follow currents; altimeters and tide gauges measure sea level; research ships lower CTD instruments. _(M10)_
- **Ocean data: how they help explain the ocean–atmosphere link** — Buoy and Argo data on sea temperature and salinity track El Niño, ocean heat content and the Atlantic circulation, improving seasonal forecasts; the TAO array of moored buoys across the equatorial Pacific gives warning of El Niño. _(I7, M10)_
- **Weather records: four rules for accurate readings** — Site instruments correctly (open ground, standard height, thermometers shaded, away from buildings); calibrate them regularly; read them at the same times each day; keep long records at one site, as urban growth can bias readings upward. _(M11)_
- **Weather measurements: importance for forecasts and warnings** — Observations are the initial conditions for computer forecast models, so more and better data give better forecasts; they support storm, rain, snow, heat, storm-surge and flood warnings; a falling barograph trace warns of an approaching depression. _(I1, I2)_
- **Weather measurements: four economic uses** — Aviation (wind, visibility, icing, altimeter settings); shipping and fishing (sea area forecasts, wave heights from buoys); farming (timing silage, spraying and harvesting, frost warnings); energy (siting wind farms, predicting electricity and gas demand). _(I3, I4, I5)_
- **Climate records: why long measurement records matter** — Long, consistent records such as Mauna Loa's carbon dioxide record and Valentia's weather record show global warming, sea-level rise and ocean warming; they are evidence for governments and the IPCC (set up in 1988 by the WMO and UN Environment Programme). _(I6)_

### 2. Solar energy and its redistribution (19)

- **Natural greenhouse effect: how it works and the temperatures it explains** — The sun's short-wave radiation passes through water vapour, carbon dioxide and methane; the cooler earth emits long-wave radiation, which these gases absorb and re-radiate back down, keeping the surface at about 15 °C, not about −18 °C. _(R1, B4)_
- **Solar energy: four ways it is transformed** — Absorbed radiation becomes sensible heat; evaporation stores latent heat, released on condensation to power storms; heat differences create pressure differences, giving the kinetic energy of wind, waves and currents; photosynthesis makes chemical energy. _(R3)_
- **Heat budget: what happens to 100 units of incoming solar radiation** — About 30 units are reflected back to space, mainly by clouds, so the earth's albedo is about 30%; about 20 are absorbed by the atmosphere and clouds (ozone, water vapour, dust); about 50 are absorbed by the land and ocean surface. _(B1)_
- **Albedo: approximate values for named surfaces** — Fresh snow about 80–90%; thick cloud up to about 80%; sea ice about 50–70%; desert sand about 30–40%; grassland about 25%; forest about 10–20%; open ocean under 10% when the sun is high. _(B2)_
- **Three ways the earth's surface loses energy** — Long-wave radiation; latent heat transfer by evaporation, the largest non-radiative loss, especially from oceans; sensible heat transfer by conduction to the air touching the ground, which then rises by convection. _(B3)_
- **Heat budget by latitude: where the surplus and deficit lie and what follows** — Between the equator and about 35–40° N and S more energy is received than radiated away (surplus); poleward of that more is lost (deficit). The tropics do not keep heating nor the poles keep cooling, so energy must be moved poleward. _(B5, B6)_
- **Uneven insolation: how the angle of the sun's rays matters** — At the equator the rays strike nearly vertically, concentrating energy on a small area; towards the poles the same beam strikes at a low angle, spreading over a larger area and passing through more atmosphere, so more is absorbed, scattered and reflected. _(U1, U2)_
- **Earth's tilt: how it causes seasons, with the key dates** — The axis is tilted about 23.5°, so the overhead sun migrates: over the Tropic of Cancer about 21 June, the Tropic of Capricorn about 21 December, and the equator at the equinoxes (about 21 March and 23 September), changing day length. _(U3)_
- **Uneven insolation: effects of day length, cloud and snow** — Long summer days at high latitudes partly offset the low sun; cloud over the equatorial belt and mid-latitude west coasts reflects much insolation, so clear subtropical deserts, not the equator, are hottest; polar snow reflects most of what arrives. _(U4, U5, U6, T8)_
- **Continentality: compare the annual temperature ranges of Ireland and Verkhoyansk** — Ireland about 10 °C (January 4–7 °C, July 14–16 °C); Verkhoyansk, north-east Siberia, over 60 °C (January about −45 °C, July about +15 °C). Land heats and cools quickly; the sea slowly, as water has a high specific heat, mixes, is transparent and evaporates. _(T2)_
- **North Atlantic Drift and Labrador Current: evidence that currents move heat** — The North Atlantic Drift keeps Norway's coast ice-free north of the Arctic Circle (Hammerfest) and Murmansk an ice-free port. Labrador, Canada, at Ireland's latitude, has freezing winters and sea ice because the cold Labrador Current flows past it. _(T3, D8)_
- **Altitude and temperature: rate of fall and a named example** — Temperature falls by about 6.5 °C per 1,000 m, so mountains are cooler than lowlands at the same latitude: Mount Kilimanjaro, Tanzania, about 5,900 m high and only about 3° south of the equator, is snow-capped. _(T4)_
- **Prevailing winds and aspect: how each affects temperature** — Onshore westerlies bring ocean warmth in winter and coolness in summer to mid-latitude west coasts; winds from cold interiors, such as polar continental air reaching Ireland, bring cold spells; south-facing slopes in the northern hemisphere are warmer. _(T5, T6)_
- **Temperature extremes: the world and northern hemisphere records** — Highest (WMO): 56.7 °C at Furnace Creek, Death Valley, California, 10 July 1913. Lowest: −89.2 °C at Vostok Station, Antarctica, 21 July 1983. Northern hemisphere lowest: about −68 °C at Verkhoyansk and Oymyakon. Arctic highest: 38 °C at Verkhoyansk, 20 June 2020. _(T8)_
- **Hadley cell: how it moves heat, step by step** — Intense equatorial heating makes air rise (low pressure, the ITCZ), cool and release latent heat as convectional rain; at height it flows poleward, sinks at about 30° (subtropical high, deserts) and returns as the trade winds. Named after George Hadley, 1735. _(D2)_
- **Ferrel and polar cells: how they move heat poleward** — Ferrel cell (30–60°): surface westerlies carry warm air poleward to meet cold polar air at the polar front (about 60°). Polar cell: air sinks at the poles and flows out as polar easterlies. Depressions along the polar front do most mid-latitude transfer. _(D3)_
- **Latent heat and hurricanes: how they carry heat poleward** — Water evaporated over warm tropical oceans carries latent heat, released where it condenses, as in a depression over Ireland. Hurricanes carry tropical heat and moisture poleward: ex-hurricane Ophelia reached Ireland on 16 October 2017. _(D4, D5)_
- **Ocean currents: name four warm and four cold currents and where each kind flows** — Warm, on the western sides of ocean basins: Gulf Stream, Kuroshio, Brazil, East Australian. Cold, on the eastern sides: Canary, Benguela, Humboldt (Peru), California. Off Europe the Gulf Stream becomes the North Atlantic Drift. _(D6)_
- **Three changes that could alter how heat is redistributed** — Melting Arctic ice lowers albedo, so more heat is absorbed; a weaker Atlantic circulation could change Europe's climate; El Niño shifts heat across the Pacific. _(D9)_

### 3. Exchanges of water between oceans and atmosphere (24)

- **Hydrological cycle: share of the earth's water in each main store** — Oceans about 97%; ice sheets and glaciers about 2%, most of it in Antarctica; groundwater under 1%; the atmosphere only about 0.001%. Only about 2.5–3% of all water is fresh, and most of that is frozen. _(H2)_
- **Ocean–atmosphere water exchange: key shares and residence time** — About 85–90% of evaporation is from the oceans and about three-quarters of precipitation falls on them; wind carries the surplus over land and rivers return it. Vapour stays about 9 days in the atmosphere, but water stays thousands of years in the ocean. _(H4, H5)_
- **Why relative humidity is usually highest at dawn and lowest in mid-afternoon** — Warm air can hold about 7% more water vapour for each 1 °C rise. Cooling air raises its relative humidity without adding water and warming lowers it, so humidity peaks in the cool of dawn and falls in the afternoon heat. _(U2)_
- **Evaporation: five factors that increase its rate** — Higher temperature and strong sunshine (more energy); dry air with low relative humidity; wind, which replaces moist air with drier air; a large supply of water such as oceans, lakes or wet ground; low air pressure. _(E2)_
- **Four ways air is cooled to its dew point** — Adiabatic cooling of rising air, the main cause of cloud; contact cooling when air touches ground that has lost heat at night; advection cooling when warm, moist air moves over a cold surface; mixing of air masses of different temperature. _(C2)_
- **Adiabatic lapse rates: the dry, saturated and environmental figures** — Unsaturated rising air cools at the dry adiabatic rate, about 10 °C per 1,000 m; once condensation begins, released latent heat slows cooling to the saturated rate, about 5–7 °C per 1,000 m. The environmental lapse rate averages about 6.5 °C per 1,000 m. _(C3, C4)_
- **Stable and unstable air: what happens and the weather each gives** — Unstable: rising air stays warmer than its surroundings and keeps rising, giving towering cumulus, showers and thunderstorms. Stable: rising air turns cooler than its surroundings and sinks back, giving layer cloud or clear skies. An inversion traps fog. _(C4)_
- **Radiation fog: conditions and how it forms** — On clear, calm nights, usually under anticyclones in autumn and winter, the ground loses heat and cools the air touching it to dew point; fog gathers in valleys and hollows and burns off in the morning sun. Cloud or wind would prevent it. _(C5, C2)_
- **Advection fog: two named examples and why they form** — The Grand Banks off Newfoundland, Canada, among the foggiest places on earth, where warm moist air from over the Gulf Stream crosses the cold Labrador Current; San Francisco's summer fogs, where moist Pacific air crosses the cold California Current. _(C5)_
- **Cloud names: who devised them and what the Latin words mean** — Luke Howard, an English pharmacist and amateur meteorologist, in 1803: cirrus (wispy, a curl of hair), cumulus (a heap), stratus (a layer), nimbus (rain-bearing). Alto- means middle level. The WMO sets ten main cloud types. _(K2)_
- **High clouds: three types with their appearance and weather** — Above about 6,000 m, of ice crystals: cirrus (wisps, mares' tails; fair, but may herald a warm front); cirrostratus (a milky veil with a halo round the sun or moon; rain likely within hours); cirrocumulus (small ripples, a mackerel sky). _(K3)_
- **Middle and low clouds: five types, their appearance and weather** — Altocumulus (patches in rolls); altostratus (sun as if through frosted glass, thickening ahead of a warm front); stratus (drizzle, hill fog); stratocumulus (commonest over Ireland, dry or light drizzle); nimbostratus (long, steady rain or snow at warm fronts). _(K3)_
- **Cumulonimbus: appearance, weather and how hail forms in it** — A towering cloud with an anvil top spreading at the tropopause, giving heavy showers, thunder, lightning and squalls. Hail forms when strong updrafts carry frozen drops up and down repeatedly, adding layers of ice until they are too heavy. _(K3, P4)_
- **Convectional rainfall: the four stages** — First the ground is heated strongly, warming the air above; the air expands, rises and cools adiabatically; at dew point cumulus and then cumulonimbus form; finally heavy showers, often with thunder, fall in the afternoon. Daily in the equatorial belt. _(P5)_
- **Relief rainfall: the stages on each side of a mountain barrier** — Moist sea air is forced to rise over the mountains; it cools, condenses and rains heavily on the windward slope; on the leeward side it descends, is compressed and warms, so clouds evaporate, leaving a rain shadow. Ireland's western mountains get this rain. _(P5)_
- **Relief rainfall in India: name the mountains, wet coasts and rain shadow** — The Western Ghats force the Arabian Sea branch of the monsoon to rise, giving heavy rain on the Konkan and Malabar coasts, with the Deccan in rain shadow. Mawsynram and Cherrapunji in the Khasi Hills, Meghalaya, are among the wettest places on earth. _(P5)_
- **Frontal rainfall: what happens at the warm front and the cold front** — At the warm front, warm, moist tropical air rises gently over cold polar air, giving long, steady rain; at the cold front the warm air is undercut, rising sharply to give short, heavy showers. This is Ireland's main source of rain. _(P5)_
- **World rainfall: why the equatorial belt is wet and subtropical belts dry** — Equatorial (0–10°): over 2,000 mm (Manaus about 2,300 mm) from low pressure, convergence at the ITCZ and convection. Subtropical high (20–30°): sinking, warming, stable air and clear skies give hot deserts, worst on west coasts beside cold currents. _(D1)_
- **World rainfall: the pattern in the tropics, mid-latitudes and polar regions** — About 10–20°: rain in summer when the ITCZ moves over, dry winters. About 40–60°: rain from depressions in the westerlies, wettest on west coasts such as Ireland's. Polar: very little, as cold air holds little vapour; Antarctica is the driest continent. _(D1)_
- **Cold ocean currents: effect on coastal rainfall, with two deserts** — Cold currents chill the air above them and make it stable, so rain is rare though fog is common: the Atacama, Chile, is the driest non-polar desert, and the Namib gets much of its moisture as fog from the cold Benguela Current. Warm currents add moisture. _(D2, D1)_
- **Ireland's rainfall: key figures for east and west** — About 750 mm a year in the driest parts of the east, around Dublin; about 1,000–1,250 mm across much of the west; over 2,000 mm on many western mountains. Wet days: about 150 a year on east and south-east coasts, up to about 225 in parts of the west. _(D4)_
- **Ireland's rainfall: four reasons the west is wetter than the east** — Prevailing south-westerly winds come off the Atlantic; Atlantic depressions reach the west first; the western mountains force relief uplift; the east lies in their rain shadow. _(D4)_
- **Sahel: rainfall figures and why drought risk is high** — Roughly 200–600 mm a year, falling in one short summer wet season when the ITCZ moves north, and varying greatly from year to year. Rainfall is least reliable where totals are lowest, so drought risk is greatest there. _(D5)_
- **Distinctive weather regimes: four produced by ocean–atmosphere exchanges** — The equatorial daily cycle of morning heating and afternoon storms; the Indian monsoon's reversal of moist sea air and dry land air; fog deserts on cold-current coasts; Ireland's cool, cloudy, wet regime fed by Atlantic moisture and the North Atlantic Drift. _(D6)_

### Atmospheric and oceanic circulation (28)

- **Coriolis effect and friction: how they shape surface winds** — The Coriolis effect deflects moving air to the right in the northern hemisphere and to the left in the southern; it is zero at the equator, strongest at the poles, and changes direction, not speed. Near the ground, friction slows the wind and weakens it, so winds cross the isobars into lows. _(F2, F4, F5)_
- **Pressure belts: the four belts and the weather each brings** — Equatorial low (ITCZ, doldrums): calm, rising air, heavy rain; subtropical highs at about 30° (horse latitudes): sinking, dry air, deserts; subpolar lows at about 60°: the polar front, where depressions form; polar highs: cold, sinking air. _(G1)_
- **Jet streams: height, speed and effect on Ireland's weather** — At about 9–12 km, near the tropopause, often over 150 km/h and faster in winter. The polar front jet meanders in Rossby waves and steers depressions: a strong, straight jet brings stormy winters; a slow, looping jet can block weather systems for weeks. _(G5)_
- **North Atlantic Oscillation: what it is and its two phases** — The see-saw of pressure between the Icelandic Low and the Azores High. Positive phase (large difference): strong westerlies, mild, wet, stormy Irish winters. Negative phase (small difference): weaker westerlies, more blocking highs, colder, drier winters. _(G6)_
- **Tropical maritime and polar maritime air masses: source and weather** — Tropical maritime: from the Atlantic near the Azores; warm, moist and mild, with cloud and drizzle. Polar maritime: from the North Atlantic near Greenland and Iceland; cool, moist and showery; Ireland's most frequent air mass. _(G7)_
- **Arctic maritime and the two continental air masses: source and weather** — Arctic maritime: from the Arctic; cold, with winter snow showers in the north and west. Polar continental: from Russia and Scandinavia in winter; very cold, dry, east-coast snow. Tropical continental: from North Africa in summer; hot, dry, heatwaves. _(G7)_
- **North Atlantic gyre: trace the currents in order** — The North Equatorial Current flows west with the trade winds and turns north as the Gulf Stream; the westerlies carry it north-east as the North Atlantic Drift to Ireland; part returns south as the cold Canary Current. The calm Sargasso Sea lies at the centre. _(O2)_
- **North Atlantic circulation: four effects on weather and climate** — Mild Irish winters and ice-free ports such as Hammerfest and Murmansk; warm sea feeds depressions with moisture and energy; fog on the Grand Banks where the Gulf Stream meets the Labrador Current, which carries icebergs south (the Titanic, 15 April 1912). _(O3)_
- **Upwelling: effects on fishing and coastal climate, with named coasts** — Plankton thrive in the nutrient-rich water, supporting fisheries off Peru, south-west Africa, California and north-west Africa; the Peruvian anchoveta is the world's largest single-species fishery. The cold water chills the air, giving coastal fog and aridity. _(O5)_
- **Thermohaline circulation: the conveyor belt in stages** — Water made cold and salty (by evaporation and sea-ice formation) in the Greenland, Norwegian and Labrador Seas and off Antarctica sinks; it flows slowly south through the Atlantic to the Indian and Pacific Oceans, rises and returns as warm surface currents. A circuit takes about 1,000 years. _(O6)_
- **Depression formation: the first three stages** — Cold polar maritime air from the north meets warm, moist tropical maritime air from the south-west at the polar front; a wave forms, often under a diverging jet stream; warm air rises over the cold, pressure falls and air spirals in anticlockwise. _(D2)_
- **Depression formation: the last three stages** — A warm front forms ahead and a cold front behind, with the warm sector between, and the low deepens as latent heat is released; the faster cold front catches the warm front, lifting the warm sector (occlusion); the low then fills and dies within about a week. _(D2)_
- **Depression passing Ireland: weather on approach and at the warm front** — Approach: pressure falls; cirrus, then cirrostratus with a halo; the wind backs and strengthens. Warm front: cloud thickens and lowers to altostratus and nimbostratus; long, steady rain for several hours; pressure keeps falling; temperature rises slightly. _(D3)_
- **Depression passing Ireland: weather in the warm sector and at the cold front** — Warm sector: drizzle or dry, low stratus, mild, humid; wind veers south-westerly. Cold front: cumulonimbus, short heavy showers, hail, thunder, squalls; temperature falls sharply; wind veers westerly or north-westerly; pressure rises. Then clearer, colder air. _(D3)_
- **Depressions: five impacts on Ireland's weather** — Changeable weather with rain in every month; a wetter west, as depressions arrive there first; storms in autumn and winter, when temperature contrasts and the jet stream are strongest; coastal storm surges; floods when depressions follow in quick succession. _(D4)_
- **Anticyclone formation: how sinking air clears the sky, and two origins** — Air converging aloft sinks, is compressed and warms, so relative humidity falls and clouds evaporate. Dynamic (warm) highs come from sinking air in the general circulation (the Azores High); thermal (cold) highs from intense winter cooling (the Siberian High). _(A2)_
- **Anticyclone: typical summer weather in Ireland** — Clear skies, strong sunshine, high daytime temperatures and light winds; cool nights with dew and sometimes morning mist; occasional heat-triggered thunderstorms at the edges; sea breezes on coasts. Prolonged highs bring heatwaves and drought. _(A3)_
- **Anticyclone: typical winter weather in Ireland** — Clear nights let the ground lose heat fast, giving hard frost and freezing fog in valleys that may last all day; cold, crisp, sunny days; or anticyclonic gloom when stratocumulus is trapped under the inversion; smog in towns where pollution is trapped. _(A4)_
- **Blocking anticyclone: effect, with the 2018 Irish example** — A stationary high blocks the westerlies and diverts depressions. In late February 2018 a block over Scandinavia drew polar continental air from Russia (the Beast from the East); Storm Emma brought heavy snow on 1–2 March and the first nationwide Red warning. _(A5, G7)_
- **Sea breeze: how it forms, step by step** — By day land heats faster than the sea; air over the land warms, expands and rises, lowering pressure; cool air flows onshore from the higher pressure over the sea; the risen air returns seaward aloft and sinks. Strongest on hot, calm summer afternoons. _(B1)_
- **Land breeze: how it forms and why it is weaker than a sea breeze** — After sunset the land loses heat faster than the sea; air over the land cools, becomes denser and forms higher pressure, so it flows offshore to the lower pressure over the warmer sea. It is weaker because the night-time temperature difference is smaller. _(B2)_
- **Land and sea breezes: five effects on coasts** — Sea breezes lower afternoon temperatures and raise humidity; a sea-breeze front can trigger showers inland; sea breezes carry sea fog onshore and trap urban pollution; land breezes clear coastal fog; fishermen sailed out at dawn on the land breeze and back on the sea breeze. _(B3)_
- **Katabatic (mountain) winds: four effects** — Cold, dense air pooling on valley floors creates frost hollows and temperature inversions; orchards and vineyards are planted on slopes above the frost line; radiation fog forms in valleys; off the Antarctic and Greenland ice sheets they reach gale force. _(V2)_
- **Föhn winds: why the air arrives warm and dry, effects and two names** — Rising air cools at the slower saturated rate after condensation; descending the lee side it warms at the faster dry rate. Effects: rapid snowmelt, avalanche and fire risk. Föhn in the Alps; Chinook (the snow eater) on the lee side of the Rocky Mountains. _(V3)_
- **Thunderstorm: the three stages of a cell** — Cumulus stage: strong updrafts, latent heat keeps air rising, no rain. Mature: falling drops and ice create downdrafts; heavy rain, hail, lightning, thunder, an anvil top. Dissipating: downdrafts cut off the warm, moist air and the cloud breaks up. A single cell lasts about an hour. _(T2, T3, T4)_
- **Lightning and thunder: how they form and how to judge distance** — Collisions between ice crystals, hailstones and supercooled droplets separate charges; a discharge heats the air to around 30,000 °C, and its explosive expansion is thunder. Light travels faster than sound: about 3 seconds from flash to thunder per km. _(T5)_
- **Tropical cyclones: conditions needed and structure** — Sea at about 26–27 °C or more over deep water, at least about 5° from the equator (Coriolis), low wind shear and moist, unstable air. Latent heat lowers pressure; air spirals into the eyewall; the calm eye has sinking air. They weaken over land or cold water. _(C1, C2)_
- **Typhoon Haiyan and Hurricane Katrina: key impacts** — Haiyan (Yolanda), November 2013: one of the strongest at landfall; its storm surge devastated Tacloban on Leyte and over 6,000 died in the Philippines. Katrina, August 2005: levee failures flooded about 80% of New Orleans and about 1,800 died. _(C4)_

### 5. Climatic environments and climate change (37)

- **Equatorial climate: location and temperature, with reasons** — The Amazon Basin, Congo Basin and South-East Asia (Indonesia, Malaysia). Monthly means about 26–28 °C, annual range only about 2–3 °C, because the sun is always high and overhead at the equinoxes; cloud stops maxima rising much above the low 30s °C. _(Q1, Q2)_
- **Equatorial climate: rainfall amount, timing and causes** — Over 2,000 mm a year (Manaus, Brazil, about 2,300 mm), in every month, peaking after the equinoxes as the overhead sun and ITCZ pass. It is convectional: intense heating of very moist air, with the trade winds converging at the ITCZ in permanent low pressure. _(Q3, Q5)_
- **Equatorial climate: the daily weather cycle** — A clear, calm, humid morning; cumulus building by late morning; cumulonimbus and torrential rain with thunder in the afternoon; clearing in the evening. Highs about 30–32 °C, night lows about 22–24 °C, so the daily range exceeds the annual range. _(Q4, Q2)_
- **Indian monsoon climate: the three seasons** — The hot season (March–May), often over 40 °C on the northern plains before the rains; the wet south-west monsoon (June–September), slightly cooler under cloud and rain; the cool dry season (October–February), when dry north-east winds blow off the cooled land. _(M3, M6)_
- **Summer monsoon over India: how it forms, in four steps** — Land heating creates low pressure over north-west India; the ITCZ moves north; south-east trades cross the equator and Coriolis turns them into moist south-west winds; the subtropical jet shifts north and the monsoon bursts, reaching Kerala around 1 June. _(M4)_
- **Indian monsoon: rainfall pattern with named places** — Relief rain on the Western Ghats (Konkan and Malabar coasts) and the Khasi Hills, where Mawsynram and Cherrapunji are among the wettest places on earth; the Deccan is in rain shadow; rain decreases north-west to the Thar Desert; Kolkata is wetter than Delhi. _(M5)_
- **Indian monsoon: hazards and human response** — Late, weak or broken monsoons bring drought, heavy ones floods; El Niño years often weaken the monsoon; the Odisha super cyclone of October 1999 killed about 10,000. Kharif crops such as rice are sown with the rains; rabi crops such as wheat need irrigation. _(M7)_
- **Cool temperate oceanic climate: location and temperature figures** — West coasts at about 40° to 60° in the westerlies: north-west Europe including Ireland, British Columbia and the US Pacific north-west, southern Chile, New Zealand, Tasmania. Ireland: January about 4–7 °C, July about 14–16 °C, annual range about 10 °C; frost and snow infrequent on low ground. _(W1, W2)_
- **Cool temperate oceanic climate: five reasons for its character** — Prevailing south-westerlies bring maritime Atlantic air; the North Atlantic Drift warms winter air and seas; the sea heats and cools slowly; frequent depressions steered by the jet stream make weather changeable; occasional anticyclones give dry spells. _(W4, W5)_
- **Mediterranean climate: where it is found** — On the west sides of continents: the Mediterranean Basin (e.g. southern Italy), central and southern California, central Chile, the Cape Town area of South Africa, and south-western and southern Australia around Perth and Adelaide. _(D1)_
- **Mediterranean climate: temperature and rainfall figures** — Lowland July means about 25 °C or more; coastal January about 9–12 °C, with frost rare. Most rain falls from October to March, under 600 mm a year on parts of the Puglia lowlands and Sicily; summers have months of drought, and downpours cause flash floods. _(D2, D3)_
- **Mediterranean climate: why summers are dry and winters wet** — In summer the subtropical high moves poleward over the region, bringing sinking, warming air and clear skies. In winter the belts shift towards the equator, so westerly depressions bring rain; the warm Mediterranean Sea also spawns winter depressions. _(D4)_
- **Mediterranean climate: hazards and human response, with a named wildfire** — Summer water shortage; wildfires in hot, dry, windy summers, such as the Mati fire near Athens in July 2018, which killed about 100 people. Responses: drought-tolerant olives, vines, cork oak and maquis; irrigated citrus; water storage; tourism. _(D6)_
- **Continental climate: location and temperature figures** — Interiors of northern-hemisphere continents far from the sea: central and eastern Europe, Russia, the Canadian Prairies, the Great Plains. Moscow about −7 °C in January and 19 °C in July; Winnipeg about −16 °C and 20 °C. The range grows inland. _(N1, N2)_
- **Continental climate: precipitation and its four causes** — Low to moderate (Moscow about 600–700 mm), with a summer maximum from convection and winter snow that lies for months. Causes: continentality; westerlies have lost moisture crossing the land; the cold, dry winter Siberian High; summer low pressure. _(N3, N4)_
- **Continental climate: hazards and human response** — Blizzards and cold waves of arctic air; summer heatwaves, drought and dust storms, as in the Dust Bowl of the 1930s on the Great Plains; spring snowmelt floods. Temperate grassland on chernozems supports wheat and maize belts; boreal forest lies further north. _(N5, N6)_
- **Hot desert climate: four characteristics and their reasons** — Little, unreliable rain (subtropical high, offshore trades, cold currents, rain shadow); the highest temperatures on earth (56.7 °C, Death Valley, 10 July 1913) under clear skies and a high sun; a daily range of 20–30 °C as clear, dry air loses heat at night; fog where cold currents chill the coast. _(H1, H2)_
- **Climate change over space: five controls with an example of each** — Latitude (hot equatorial Amazon, cold poles); distance from the sea (Ireland's range about 10 °C, Verkhoyansk's over 60 °C); currents (cold Benguela: foggy Namib coast; North Atlantic Drift: mild Ireland); altitude (snow on Kilimanjaro near the equator); rain shadow (Western Ghats vs the Deccan). _(S1)_
- **Natural causes of climate change: five to name** — Milankovitch orbital cycles; changes in solar output; volcanic eruptions, whose sulphate aerosols reflect sunlight and cool the earth for a year or two; plate tectonics changing ocean currents; feedbacks such as ice–albedo and greenhouse gas release. _(K2)_
- **Volcanoes and climate: Tambora and Pinatubo** — Tambora, Indonesia, April 1815: 1816 became the year without a summer, with harvest failures across Europe and north-eastern North America. Mount Pinatubo, Philippines, June 1991: global temperature fell about 0.5 °C for about a year (1992). _(K2, I6)_
- **How past climates are known: five sources of evidence** — Ice cores (air bubbles give past CO2, oxygen isotopes past temperature; EPICA Dome C covers about 800,000 years); ocean-floor sediments; tree rings; pollen in Irish bogs and lake muds; historical records such as harvest dates, frost fairs and ships' logs. _(K4)_
- **Ice Ages: the Quaternary glaciation and Last Glacial Maximum figures** — Began about 2.6 million years ago; glacial cycles of about 100,000 years over the last 800,000; we live in an interglacial. Last Glacial Maximum, about 20,000 years ago: ice over much of North America and northern Europe; sea level about 120 m lower. _(I1, I2)_
- **Ice Ages in Ireland: the two cold stages and their evidence** — The Munsterian ice covered almost all of Ireland; the later Midlandian ice had gone by about 10,000 years ago. The evidence is drumlins, eskers, corries and U-shaped valleys; isostatic rebound and raised beaches are after-effects. _(I3)_
- **Milankovitch cycles: the three cycles and their periods** — Eccentricity (shape of the orbit), about 100,000 years; obliquity (tilt, between about 22° and 24.5°), about 41,000 years; precession (wobble of the axis), roughly 19,000–26,000 years. Calculated by the Serbian scientist Milutin Milanković. _(I4)_
- **Ice Ages: how a small orbital change becomes a glaciation** — Orbital cycles change where and when sunlight falls; when northern summers are cool, winter snow survives and ice sheets grow. More ice raises albedo, and colder oceans absorb more CO2 (about 180 ppm in glacials, 280 ppm in interglacials), cooling further. _(I4)_
- **Medieval Warm Period: dates and evidence** — Roughly AD 950–1250, mainly in the North Atlantic and Europe. Vines were grown further north in England than in the colder centuries that followed; Norse settlers led by Erik the Red settled Greenland from about AD 985, abandoning it in the 15th century as the climate cooled. _(I6)_
- **Little Ice Age: dates, three pieces of evidence and a possible cause** — Roughly the 14th to mid-19th century, coldest in the 17th. Evidence: Alpine glaciers advanced; frost fairs on the frozen Thames, the last in 1814; Ireland's Great Frost of 1739–40, then famine. Possible cause: the Maunder Minimum of few sunspots (about 1645–1715). _(I6)_
- **Global warming: evidence from CO2 and global temperature** — CO2 rose from about 280 ppm before the Industrial Revolution to over 420 ppm by the mid-2020s (Mauna Loa, since 1958); global temperature is up about 1.1–1.2 °C since the late 1800s; 2024 was the first year more than 1.5 °C above pre-industrial. _(G1)_
- **Global warming: evidence from ice, sea level and Ireland** — Arctic summer sea ice has shrunk about 13% per decade since satellite records began in 1979; glaciers are retreating; global sea level rose about 20 cm between 1901 and 2018; Ireland's average temperature is up about 0.9 °C since 1900. _(G1)_
- **Global warming: the mechanism and four feedbacks** — More CO2, methane and nitrous oxide absorb more outgoing long-wave radiation, so the heat budget is out of balance. Feedbacks: ice–albedo (why the Arctic warms fastest); water vapour; permafrost thaw releasing methane; less ocean CO2 uptake as water warms. _(G2, G3)_
- **Global warming: effects on world climate patterns** — A more intense water cycle, with heavier downpours and worse droughts; more intense heatwaves (Europe 2003, about 70,000 extra deaths, estimated); climate belts and storm tracks shifting poleward; stronger tropical cyclones; sea-level rise. _(G4)_
- **Global warming: effects on Ireland and the scientific verdict** — Warmer temperatures; wetter winters and more flooding; summer drought risk, as in 2018; stronger storms and coastal erosion. The IPCC (2021) called human influence on warming unequivocal; the Paris Agreement (2015) aims for well below 2 °C, pursuing 1.5 °C. _(G5, G6)_
- **Pacific in a normal year (Walker circulation): how it works** — Strong south-east trades push warm surface water west to a warm pool near Indonesia, where moist air rises (low pressure, heavy rain); the air returns east aloft and sinks over the cool east, keeping Peru dry; cold upwelling off Peru feeds the anchoveta. _(C-N)_
- **El Niño: how it develops** — Every 2–7 years the trade winds weaken or reverse; warm water flows back east towards South America; the thermocline deepens and upwelling stops; the central and eastern Pacific warm well above normal and the rain zone shifts east. _(C-EN)_
- **El Niño: five effects around the world** — Floods in normally dry coastal Peru and Ecuador; collapse of the anchoveta catch and fishmeal industry, with seabird deaths; drought and forest fires in Indonesia and the Philippines; often a weaker Indian monsoon; fewer Atlantic hurricanes. _(C-EF, M7)_
- **El Niño: five named events and what each did** — 1972–73, with overfishing, collapsed the Peruvian anchoveta fishery; 1997–98 brought floods to Peru and Ecuador and forest fires and haze in Indonesia; it, 1982–83 and 2015–16 were the three strongest; 2023–24 helped make 2023 and 2024 the warmest years. _(C-ES)_
- **La Niña: conditions, effects and a named event** — Stronger trades, a cooler eastern Pacific and stronger upwelling; heavier rain in Indonesia and Queensland, drought in Peru, more Atlantic hurricanes. The strong 2010–11 La Niña brought record floods to Queensland, including the Brisbane flood of January 2011. _(C-LN)_

### 6. Climate and economic development (26)

- **Irish agriculture: why grass is Ireland's most important crop** — Ireland's rain and mild temperatures give a long growing season: grass grows when soil temperature is above about 6 °C, which is most of the year, longest in the south and south-west. So most farmland is grassland for dairy, beef and sheep. _(A1)_
- **Irish farming: how rainfall shapes three farming regions** — Drier, sunnier south and east: tillage of barley, wheat, oats and potatoes (Co. Wexford, Carlow, Kildare). Golden Vale: moderate rain and brown earths suit intensive dairying. Very wet west and north-west: beef and sheep on rough grazing, small farms. _(A2)_
- **Irish farming: four problems caused by too much rain** — Cattle poach waterlogged soils, shortening the grazing season, so animals are housed and fed silage for longer, raising costs; silage, hay and cereal harvests are disrupted; nutrients leach and slurry runs off; damp favours potato blight and liver fluke. _(A3)_
- **Fodder crisis of spring 2013: causes and response** — The exceptionally wet summer of 2012 gave poor silage and hay harvests, and a cold, late spring in 2013 followed, so farmers ran out of feed. Fodder was imported, including from Britain and France, and the Government funded a fodder transport subsidy. _(A3)_
- **Summer 2018 drought: effects on Irish farming** — A long anticyclonic spell from late May to mid-July gave absolute drought at many stations. Grass almost stopped growing, so farmers fed winter fodder in summer and milk yields fell; Teagasc and the Government organised fodder imports for the following winter. _(A4)_
- **Egypt and the Nile: how the Aswan High Dam changed farming** — Egypt is almost rainless, so farming relies on Nile irrigation. The dam (1970) and Lake Nasser ended the flood's uncertainty, giving perennial irrigation, more than one crop a year and hydroelectricity; but silt is trapped, land salinised and the delta erodes. _(G2)_
- **California's Central Valley: irrigation and the 2012–2016 drought** — Dry summers mean fruit, nuts and vegetables need irrigation from state and federal water projects and groundwater. In the drought, land lay fallow and over-pumping caused land subsidence; Governor Jerry Brown ordered a 25% urban water cut in April 2015. _(G3, D3)_
- **Ireland's water paradox: why Dublin is short of water** — Most rain falls in the west but most people live in the east, where rain is lowest (about 750 mm around Dublin); Dublin county had about 1.45 million of Ireland's 5.1 million people in 2022. Leaking pipes waste treated water; treatment capacity is tight. _(W2)_
- **Dublin's water supply: present sources and the planned project** — Mainly the River Liffey via the Poulaphouca reservoir (Blessington lakes) and the Vartry reservoir at Roundwood, Co. Wicklow. The planned, unbuilt Water Supply Project would pipe water about 170 km from the Shannon at Parteen Basin, south of Lough Derg. _(W3)_
- **Drought and household water in Ireland: the 2018 hosepipe ban and rural wells** — Irish Water imposed a hosepipe ban (Water Conservation Order) in the Greater Dublin Area in early July 2018, later extended nationwide. Rural homes on private wells and group schemes face wells running dry in drought and contamination after heavy rain. _(W4, W5)_
- **Cape Town Day Zero: causes, measures and outcome** — Three dry winters (about 2015–2017) left reservoirs very low, and in early 2018 the city warned taps would be shut off. From 1 February 2018 use was capped at 50 litres per person per day; use roughly halved and Day Zero was avoided when the 2018 rains came. _(W6)_
- **Drought: economic effects to list** — Crop failure and livestock losses; higher food prices; lost farm income and rural jobs; famine and migration in poor countries; less hydroelectricity and cooling water for power stations; disrupted river transport; wildfires; government relief spending. _(D2)_
- **Millennium Drought, Australia: impacts and responses** — South-eastern Australia, roughly the late 1990s to 2009: farming in the Murray–Darling Basin was hit and towns faced water restrictions. Insurance, aid, water trading and technology absorbed losses; cities including Melbourne built desalination plants. _(D3)_
- **Horn of Africa drought, 2011: causes and first effects** — Two failed rainy seasons, linked to La Niña, brought drought across the Horn of Africa; crops failed, livestock died and food prices soared. The UN declared famine in parts of southern Somalia in July 2011. _(D4)_
- **Somalia famine, 2011: why it was so deadly and its toll** — Conflict (al-Shabaab) blocked food aid. A later UN-backed study estimated about 260,000 deaths in Somalia, about half of them children under five; large numbers fled to the Dadaab camps in Kenya and to camps in Ethiopia. _(D4)_
- **Ethiopian famine of 1983–85: causes and the world's response** — It followed drought and was worsened by civil war. It led to Band Aid (1984) and the Live Aid concerts (July 1985), organised by Bob Geldof. _(D4)_
- **Responses to drought: seven to name** — Early warning systems using rainfall, satellite and market data; water storage in dams, tanks and rainwater harvesting; drought-tolerant crop varieties; drip irrigation; food aid, cash transfers and insurance; restrictions such as hosepipe bans; desalination. _(D5)_
- **Sahel: economic effects of drought and desertification** — Droughts in the 1970s and 1980s brought failed harvests and livestock deaths; pastoralists lost livelihoods; food imports grew; people migrated to cities and abroad; herders and farmers clashed; Lake Chad has shrunk about 90% (widely quoted) since the 1960s. _(F2)_
- **Mediterranean sun tourism: why it grew and Spain's key figures** — Hot, dry, sunny summers; mass package tourism grew from the 1960s with cheap jet flights. Spain had about 85 million international tourists in 2023, a record, second only to France, and is the most popular foreign holiday destination for Irish people. _(T2)_
- **Sun tourism in Spain: problems linked to climate and overtourism** — Tourists come in the driest season, so water demand from hotels, pools and golf courses peaks when supply is lowest; jobs are seasonal; coasts are overdeveloped. In 2024 there were large protests against mass tourism in the Canary Islands and Barcelona. _(T2)_
- **Canary Islands: why they have a year-round tourist season** — They lie off north-west Africa at about 28° N; mild, dry winters and the cooling Canary Current attract northern Europeans in winter as well as summer. _(T3)_
- **Alpine ski tourism: how climate change threatens it and the responses** — It depends on reliable snow, but the snow line is rising and seasons are shortening, so lower resorts struggle or close. Resorts invest in snow-making, which needs water, energy and cold nights, and diversify into summer walking and cycling. _(T4)_
- **Hurricanes Irma and Maria (2017): the damage each caused** — Irma, a Category 5 hurricane in September 2017, damaged or destroyed almost every building on Barbuda, whose population was evacuated to Antigua. Maria, September 2017, devastated Puerto Rico, with about 3,000 deaths (official study). _(T5)_
- **Ireland's climate and tourism: limits and what Ireland markets instead** — Cool, cloudy, wet, unreliable summers limit beach tourism and send Irish people abroad. Ireland markets green scenery, culture, golf, walking and surfing, e.g. the Wild Atlantic Way, about 2,500 km from Inishowen to Kinsale, launched by Fáilte Ireland in 2014. _(T6)_
- **Climate change and Mediterranean tourism: heat and wildfire** — Hotter summers bring heatwaves and wildfires that deter or endanger tourists: in July 2023 wildfires on Rhodes forced thousands of tourists to be evacuated. Visits may shift to spring and autumn or to northern destinations, possibly including Ireland. _(T7)_
- **Climate and development: the judgement for an 'assess' question** — Climate influences development but does not decide it alone: rich countries with harsh climates, such as Israel, the Gulf states and Australia, overcome limits with capital and technology such as desalination, while poor rain-fed economies are most exposed. _(O3, X1)_

## Option culture identity

### 1. Racial groupings (26)

- **Race today: why it is seen as social, and why skin colour varies** — Humans share about 99.9% of their DNA, vary more within so-called races than between them, and traits change gradually across space. Melanin rises towards the tropics, where ultraviolet radiation is strongest; pale skin at high latitudes helps make vitamin D. _(R2, R5)_
- **Traditional (now rejected) racial groupings: where each lived before 1500** — Outdated textbook scheme: Caucasoid in Europe, North Africa, the Middle East and South Asia; Mongoloid in East and Central Asia, the Arctic and the Americas; Negroid in sub-Saharan Africa; Australoid in Australia and Melanesia; kept apart by oceans, deserts and mountains. _(R3, R4)_
- **Colonisation of the Americas: effect on native peoples and racial mixing** — After Columbus reached the Americas in 1492, smallpox and other disease, war and forced labour made the native population collapse within a century. Settlement produced mestizo (European and Native American) and, in the colonial term, mulatto (European and African) populations. _(C1)_
- **Atlantic slave trade: numbers, destinations and abolition dates** — About 12.5 million Africans were shipped in the 16th–19th centuries, mostly to Brazil (about 4–5 million, the most of any country) and the Caribbean; far fewer went to the American South. The USA abolished slavery in 1865 (13th Amendment); Brazil on 13 May 1888, last in the Americas. _(C2)_
- **Australia: colonisation and the White Australia Policy, with dates** — First Fleet at Sydney Cove, 26 January 1788; the Immigration Restriction Act 1901 began the White Australia Policy, dismantled 1966–1973; Stolen Generations, roughly 1910 to the 1970s; 1967 referendum, about 91% Yes to count Aboriginal people in the census. _(C3)_
- **Australia today: apology, Voice referendum and census figures** — Prime Minister Kevin Rudd's national apology, 13 February 2008; the Voice to Parliament referendum, 14 October 2023, rejected with about 60% No. Census 2021: about 3.2% Aboriginal and/or Torres Strait Islander; over a quarter born overseas. _(C3)_
- **New Zealand and Native Americans: settler-colony facts with dates** — Treaty of Waitangi between the British Crown and Māori chiefs, 6 February 1840; Māori are about one in six of the population. USA: Indian Removal Act 1830; forced removal of the Cherokee in 1838, the Trail of Tears; many Native Americans live on reservations. _(C3)_
- **Indentured Indian labour: destinations, dates and legacy** — After slavery was abolished, Britain shipped Indian labourers to sugar colonies: Trinidad and Guyana, Fiji (1879–1916), Mauritius and Natal (from 1860). People of Indian descent are now a large share of the population in Trinidad, Guyana, Fiji and Mauritius. _(C4)_
- **Genocide and racial classification: Nazi Germany and Rwanda** — Nuremberg Laws (1935) stripped Jews of citizenship; about 6 million Jews were murdered; Roma were also targeted. Rwanda: Belgian identity cards (1933) fixed Hutu, Tutsi or Twa labels; from 7 April 1994 Hutu extremists killed about 800,000, mostly Tutsi, in about 100 days. _(C5, K4, K5)_
- **Post-colonial migration to Europe: who moved where after 1945** — Caribbean migrants to Britain (the Empire Windrush reached Tilbury on 22 June 1948), then people from India, Pakistan and Bangladesh; Algerians and other North and West Africans to France; Indonesians and Surinamese to the Netherlands. _(C6)_
- **The Great Migration in the USA: numbers, dates and effects** — About 6 million African Americans moved from the rural South to cities such as Chicago, Detroit and New York, about 1910–1970, pushed by segregation and poverty and pulled by industrial jobs. It changed the racial make-up of both regions and created segregated inner-city neighbourhoods. _(M1)_
- **Guest workers: the West German and Belgian recruitment agreements, and the result** — West Germany's recruitment agreement with Turkey, 30 October 1961; Belgium's agreements with Morocco and Turkey, 1964. Guest workers were expected to go home, but many settled with their families, so Germany now has a large population of Turkish origin. _(M2)_
- **Refugees: the UNHCR total, Syria and Europe in 2015, the Rohingya** — Over 120 million forcibly displaced (2024). Syria, from 2011: over 6 million refugees, about 3.5 million in Turkey. In 2015 over 1 million refugees and migrants reached Europe; Germany took about 1 million asylum seekers, 2015–16. Over 700,000 Rohingya fled to Bangladesh from 2017. _(M3)_
- **Ireland's ethnic make-up: Census 2022 figures** — About 5.15 million people; about 20% born abroad; about 12% non-Irish citizens, the largest groups Polish and UK; about three-quarters White Irish; Polish the top home language after English and Irish; about 33,000 Travellers, recognised by the State as an ethnic minority on 1 March 2017. _(M4)_
- **Asylum seekers and Ukrainians in Ireland: key facts with dates** — Direct Provision from 2000, criticised for long stays in shared centres; limited right to work from 2018, after a 2017 Supreme Court ruling; over 13,000 protection claims in each of 2022 and 2023; over 100,000 Ukrainians under EU temporary protection. _(M4)_
- **Effects of migration on racial patterns in receiving and source areas** — Receiving area: more diversity, new places of worship and shops; ethnic neighbourhoods; labour supply; also discrimination and tension, e.g. the Dublin riot of 23 November 2023, fed by anti-immigrant agitation. Source area: loss of young adults; remittances. _(M5)_
- **USA Census 2020: racial and ethnic composition** — White (alone) about 62%; Hispanic or Latino (of any race) about 19%, the largest minority; Black about 12%; Asian about 6%; about 10% identified with two or more races, more than double the 2010 figure. _(S2)_
- **Brazil: racial mixing, Census 2022 figures and inequality** — Census 2022: pardo (mixed race) about 45%, the largest group for the first time since 1991; White about 44%; Black about 10%; Indigenous under 1%. Origins: Portuguese, African, Indigenous. Racial democracy is a claim: White Brazilians remain wealthier on average. _(S3)_
- **UK as a multi-racial society: Census 2021 and the Windrush scandal** — England and Wales, 2021: White about 82%, Asian or Asian British about 9%, Black about 4%, Mixed about 3%. Windrush scandal, 2018: long-settled Caribbean-born residents were wrongly detained, denied services or deported because they lacked documents. _(S4)_
- **Multiculturalism versus assimilation: examples and arguments** — Multiculturalism: Canada (official policy, 1971) and Australia; for: enrichment, labour, tolerance; against: segregation, parallel societies, far-right backlash. Assimilation: France expects one secular French identity and keeps no ethnic census statistics. _(S5)_
- **Racial conflict: five causes** — Competition for jobs and housing; segregation, which limits contact between groups; unequal power inherited from colonialism and slavery; racist ideologies; fear of newcomers. _(K1)_
- **Apartheid South Africa: the laws and forced removals** — The National Party won in 1948; the Population Registration Act 1950 classed people as White, Black, Coloured or Indian/Asian; the Group Areas Act 1950 zoned areas by race; Bantustans; District Six declared White in 1966, about 60,000 removed. _(K2)_
