- **Apartheid South Africa: resistance and the end, with dates** — Sharpeville, 21 March 1960: police killed 69; Soweto, 16 June 1976: students protested against being taught through Afrikaans; Mandela freed 11 February 1990 after 27 years; first all-race election 27 April 1994; Truth and Reconciliation Commission from 1996. _(K2)_
- **South Africa today: Census 2022 composition and inequality** — Black African about 81%, Coloured about 8%, White about 7%, Indian/Asian about 3%. Wealth and land ownership remain highly unequal by race. _(K2)_
- **USA: segregation and civil rights, key events with dates** — Jim Crow segregation in the South after the Civil War; Brown v Board of Education, 1954; Montgomery bus boycott, 1955 (Rosa Parks); Civil Rights Act 1964; Voting Rights Act 1965; Martin Luther King killed 4 April 1968; George Floyd killed 25 May 2020. _(K3)_
- **European urban unrest: four riots with dates** — Notting Hill, London, 1958: white mobs attacked Caribbean residents; Brixton, April 1981: mainly Black youths and police; French banlieues, 2005, after two teenagers died fleeing police in Clichy-sous-Bois; Nanterre, June 2023, after police shot Nahel Merzouk. _(K6)_

### 2. Language as a cultural indicator (26)

- **Language as a cultural indicator: three reasons it matters** — It carries a group's history, literature, songs, place names and ways of thinking; it marks who belongs to a group; it is easy to map from census questions on language, so it is used to define cultural regions. _(I1)_
- **Language and nationalism: one revival movement and three cases of suppression** — The Gaelic League (1893, Douglas Hyde and Eoin MacNeill) led a language revival that fed the Irish independence movement. Suppression: Basque and Catalan banned under Franco (1939–1975); the Welsh Not in some 19th-century schools; Kurdish banned in Turkey until the early 1990s. _(I2)_
- **Language and colonial power: colonial languages that are still official** — English in India and Nigeria; French in Senegal and Côte d'Ivoire; Spanish in most of Latin America; Portuguese in Brazil. The language of power brings jobs and status, so minority speakers shift to it. The Francophonie (1970) links French-speaking states. _(I3)_
- **Irish place names: five elements and four examples** — Baile (townland), cill (church), dún (fort), inis (island), cluain (meadow): Clonmel (Cluain Meala), Kilkenny (Cill Chainnigh), Dundalk (Dún Dealgan), Ennis (Inis). They show the Irish-speaking past of areas that now speak English. _(I4)_
- **World language families: the main families with example languages** — Indo-European, the largest, spoken by nearly half the world; Sino-Tibetan (Mandarin, Tibetan, Burmese); Afro-Asiatic (Arabic, Hebrew, Amharic, Hausa); Niger-Congo (Swahili, Yoruba, Zulu); Austronesian (Malay, Filipino, Māori); Dravidian (Tamil, Telugu). _(L2)_
- **The most spoken languages and how languages spread** — Mandarin has the most native speakers, Spanish the second-most; English the most speakers overall (about 1.5 billion). Languages spread through conquest and empire (Latin; English, Spanish, French, Portuguese), trade, religion (Arabic with Islam) and media. _(L3, L4)_
- **European languages: Indo-European branches and the languages outside them** — Germanic (English, German, Dutch, Swedish); Romance (French, Spanish, Italian, Romanian); Slavic (Russian, Polish, Czech); Celtic (Irish, Welsh, Breton); Hellenic; Baltic. Not Indo-European: Finnish, Estonian, Hungarian; Turkish; Maltese; Basque (an isolate). _(E1)_
- **Mass media and English: effects on other European languages** — About half of all websites are in English; film, music and streaming are dominated by Hollywood and US and UK music; loanwords such as le weekend enter other languages; English dominates business and science, pressing small national and minority languages. _(D1, D2)_
- **France, Quebec and Iceland: protecting a language against English** — France: Académie française (1635); Toubon Law (1994) requires French in advertising, labels and signage; radio must play at least 40% French songs. Quebec: Bill 101 (1977) made French the sole official language. Iceland coins new words, e.g. tölva (computer). _(D3)_
- **Minority-language broadcasters: names and launch dates** — Raidió na Gaeltachta, 1972; TG4, 31 October 1996, based in Baile na hAbhann, Conamara; S4C, 1 November 1982, after Gwynfor Evans threatened a hunger strike in 1980; BBC Alba (Scottish Gaelic), 2008; EITB Basque television, 1982. _(D4)_
- **Why minority languages decline: causes** — Conquest and official suppression; a dominant language needed for jobs, education and emigration; famine and emigration; in-migration of majority-language speakers; urbanisation; mass media and globalisation; parents no longer passing the language on. _(P2)_
- **Strategies for the survival of minority languages: the main headings** — Official status in law; education through the language and compulsory study; media and online content; jobs in the heartland; planning and housing controls; language commissioners; community, family, culture and festivals; EU recognition. _(P3)_
- **Irish: status in the Constitution and in the EU, with dates** — Article 8 of the 1937 Constitution makes Irish the national language and the first official language, with English second. Irish became an official EU language in 2007, with full working-language status from 1 January 2022; the EU has 24 official languages. _(P4, E2)_
- **Irish speakers: Census 1851 and Census 2022 figures** — 1851, the first count: about 1.5 million Irish speakers (about 23%). 2022: about 1.87 million (about 40%) said they could speak Irish, but only about 72,000 spoke it daily outside the education system. _(P4)_
- **The Gaeltacht: counties, main areas and Census 2022 figures** — Seven counties: Donegal, Mayo, Galway, Kerry, Cork, Waterford, Meath. Areas include Gaoth Dobhair, Conamara and the Aran Islands (largest), Corca Dhuibhne, Múscraí, An Rinn, Ráth Chairn. About 106,000 people; about 20,000 speak Irish daily outside education. _(P4)_
- **Irish: State bodies for the language, with dates** — Department of the Gaeltacht and Gaeltacht Areas Order, 1956; Údarás na Gaeltachta, 1980, grant-aids firms (e.g. Gaoth Dobhair industrial estate) and community projects; Foras na Gaeilge, 1999, all-island; An Coimisinéir Teanga, 2004, enforces language rights. _(P4)_
- **Irish: laws and strategies with their targets** — Official Languages Act 2003 (2021 target: 20% of new public-service recruits competent in Irish by 2030); 20-Year Strategy 2010–2030: 250,000 daily speakers; Gaeltacht Act 2012: 26 language planning areas with plans; 2022 Act recognised Irish in the North. _(P4)_
- **Irish: education and culture measures** — Irish compulsory through primary and post-primary school, with exemptions; Gaelscoileanna (umbrella body 1973); Gaeltacht summer colleges with Irish-speaking families; Seachtain na Gaeilge; Oireachtas na Gaeilge (first held 1897); TG4 programmes. _(P4)_
- **Irish in the Gaeltacht: how successful has policy been?** — The 2007 Comprehensive Linguistic Study: Irish is viable as a community language only where at least 67% speak it daily. A 2015 update warned it could cease to be the community language in most Gaeltacht districts within about ten years. High ability, low use. _(P4)_
- **Welsh: suppression and decline, with dates** — Laws in Wales Acts 1535 and 1542 barred Welsh speakers from public office unless they used English; the 1847 Blue Books blamed Welsh for backwardness; the Welsh Not; 19th-century coal and iron drew English and Irish workers into south Wales, spreading English. _(P5)_
- **Welsh: activism, Tryweryn and the S4C campaign** — Cymdeithas yr Iaith Gymraeg (1962), inspired by Saunders Lewis's Tynged yr Iaith, painted over English-only signs; the 1965 flooding of Capel Celyn (Tryweryn) for Liverpool's water became a symbol of powerlessness; S4C came after Gwynfor Evans's 1980 threat. _(P5)_
- **Welsh: laws and strategy with dates** — Welsh Language Act 1967 (limited equal validity); 1993 Act (equal treatment in the public sector; Welsh Language Board); 2011 Measure (official status; Commissioner from 2012); Senedd laws in both languages; Cymraeg 2050 (2017): 1 million speakers by 2050. _(P5)_
- **Welsh: education, Census 2021 figures and threats** — Compulsory in schools to age 16, with Welsh-medium schools; the Urdd (1922) and the Eisteddfod. Census 2021: about 538,000 (17.8%), down from 19.0% in 2011; highest in Gwynedd (about 64%). Second homes: up to a 300% council-tax premium from April 2023. _(P5)_
- **Basque: survival measures and how successful they are** — Statute of Gernika, 1979: co-official with Spanish in the Basque Autonomous Community; school models A, B and D, with all-Basque model D now chosen by most; ikastolak, begun under Franco; standard Batua from 1968; EITB television. Use rising among the young. _(P6)_
- **Breton: why it has declined despite local effort** — Article 2 of the French Constitution (added 1992): the language of the Republic is French; France signed the 1992 European Charter on minority languages in 1999 but has not ratified it; Diwan schools (from 1977) rely on volunteers; parts of the Molac Law were struck down in 2021. _(P7, P9)_
- **Māori and Hebrew: two language revivals outside Europe** — Te reo Māori became an official language of New Zealand in 1987 (Māori Language Act); kōhanga reo language nests began in 1982. Hebrew was revived as an everyday language from the late 19th century (Eliezer Ben-Yehuda) and is now Israel's main language. _(P8)_

### 3. Religion as a cultural indicator (25)

- **Religion as a cultural indicator: how it shows in identity, life and landscape** — Beliefs, values and moral codes; group identity, often stronger than nationality; landscape (churches, mosques, temples, synagogues, holy wells); diet (halal, kosher); dress (headscarf, turban, kippah); festivals (Eid, Diwali, Yom Kippur); education and law. _(I1)_
- **Religion linked to language and nationality: examples, and the trend in Ireland and France** — Language: Arabic and the Qur'an; Latin and the Catholic Church in the past; Hebrew and Judaism. Nationality: Catholicism with Irish and Polish identity; Orthodoxy with Serbian identity. Secularisation is weakening religious practice in Ireland and France. _(I2, I3)_
- **World religions by number of followers: Pew figures for 2020** — Christians about 2.3 billion (about 29%); Muslims about 2 billion (about 26%); Hindus about 1.2 billion (about 15%); Buddhists about 320 million (about 4%); Jews about 15 million. Not a religion: about 1.9 billion (about 24%) unaffiliated. Islam grew fastest, 2010–2020. _(D1)_
- **Hearths of the world religions: founders, places and dates** — Judaism: Jerusalem, oldest Abrahamic faith. Christianity: Jesus, Roman Palestine. Islam: Muhammad, Mecca, early 7th century (Hijra 622). Hinduism: India, no founder. Buddhism: Siddhartha Gautama, north-east India. Sikhism: Guru Nanak, Punjab, 15th century. _(D2)_
- **How religions spread: five processes with examples** — Empire: Christianity via Rome (official, 4th century); Islam via Arab conquests from the 7th century. Missionaries: St Columba (Iona), St Columbanus. Trade: Islam to Indonesia, West Africa. Colonialism: Christianity to the Americas. Migration: Islam, Hinduism, Sikhism to Europe. _(D2)_
- **Christianity's three branches: where each dominates and how they split** — Catholic: southern Europe, Ireland, Poland, Latin America, the Philippines. Protestant: northern Europe, the UK, the USA. Orthodox: eastern Europe, Russia, Greece, Serbia. Divided by the Great Schism (1054) and the Reformation (from 1517; Calvinism, Geneva). _(D3)_
- **Islam's distribution: key regions, branches and holy cities** — Middle East, North Africa, Central Asia, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Indonesia (largest Muslim population), parts of West and East Africa. Sunnis about 85–90%; Shias the majority in Iran and Iraq. Holiest cities Mecca and Medina; the Hajj to Mecca is a duty. _(D4)_
- **Hinduism, Buddhism, Judaism and Sikhism: where each is found** — Hinduism: India (about four in five Indians) and Nepal, pilgrimage city Varanasi. Buddhism: Thailand, Myanmar, Sri Lanka, Tibet, Japan, Vietnam. Judaism: mainly Israel and the USA. Sikhism: Punjab (Golden Temple, Amritsar), diaspora in the UK and Canada. _(D5)_
- **Religion in Ireland and Northern Ireland: latest census figures** — Republic, Census 2022: about 69% Catholic (about 78% in 2016); about 14% no religion; Orthodox and Muslim numbers growing. Northern Ireland, Census 2021: Catholic about 46%, Protestant and other Christian about 43%, so Catholics lead but are no majority. _(D7)_
- **Church and state: four types of relationship with examples** — Theocracy: Iran; Vatican City (Lateran Treaty, 1929). Established church: Church of England (monarch is Supreme Governor; 26 bishops in the Lords). Separation: France's laïcité; US First Amendment (1791). Dominant church: 20th-century Ireland, Poland. _(C1)_
- **Irish Constitution 1937: its religious content** — Preamble: In the Name of the Most Holy Trinity. Article 44: special position of the Catholic Church (not a state church; removed 1972); other named churches and Jewish congregations recognised; freedom of conscience; no endowment. Article 41 banned divorce. _(C2)_
- **Catholic Church influence in 20th-century Ireland: examples** — About nine in ten primary schools under Catholic patronage, and many hospitals; influence on censorship and contraception; the Mother and Child Scheme (1950–51) dropped after opposition from bishops and doctors, so Noel Browne resigned; papal visit, 1979. _(C3)_
- **Irish referendums that reduced religious influence: dates and results** — December 1972: special position removed (about 84% Yes); November 1995: divorce (about 50.3% to 49.7%); 22 May 2015: marriage equality (about 62% Yes); 25 May 2018: Eighth Amendment (1983) repealed (about 66%); October 2018: blasphemy removed (about 65%). _(C4, C2)_
- **Decline of Church authority in Ireland: reports and school patronage** — Ryan Report (May 2009) on abuse in institutions run by religious orders; Murphy Report (November 2009) on the Dublin archdiocese; Educate Together, a multi-denominational patron, founded 1984 (first school 1978); the State aims to divest some Catholic schools. _(C4)_
- **France's laïcité: three laws with dates** — The 1905 law separated the churches and the state; a 2004 law banned conspicuous religious symbols, including the Islamic headscarf, in state schools; a 2010 law banned face coverings such as the burqa and niqab in public places. _(C5)_
- **Iran, Saudi Arabia and India: religion and the state** — Iran: Islamic Republic since 1979, with a senior cleric as Supreme Leader. Saudi Arabia: Sharia-based law; no public practice of other religions. India: secular republic by its constitution, but Hindu nationalism has grown since the 1990s. _(C6)_
- **Why religion causes or feeds conflict: four reasons** — Competing claims to holy sites and territory; religion overlapping with national identity and political power; discrimination against religious minorities; extremist interpretations of religion. _(K1)_
- **Northern Ireland: origins of the conflict, 1609 to 1921** — Plantation of Ulster, 1609: English and Scottish Protestants settled on confiscated land; Battle of the Boyne, 1690, marked by Orange Order parades on 12 July (order founded 1795); Northern Ireland set up in 1921: six counties, unionist majority. _(K2)_
- **Northern Ireland: discrimination and the Troubles, key events** — Discrimination against Catholics in housing, jobs and voting (gerrymandering in Derry); civil rights from 1968; troops 1969; internment 1971; Bloody Sunday, 30 January 1972: British paratroopers shot dead 13 civil-rights marchers, a 14th died later; 1981 hunger strikes: ten died. Over 3,500 killed. _(K2)_
- **Northern Ireland: the peace settlement and lasting segregation** — Good Friday Agreement, 10 April 1998: power-sharing, North–South bodies, consent principle. Omagh bomb, 15 August 1998: Real IRA killed 29. Peace walls (first 1969) still stand; integrated schools (first, Lagan College, 1981) teach under one in ten pupils. _(K2)_
- **Partition of India and the Kashmir dispute** — August 1947: Hindu-majority India and Muslim-majority Pakistan, including East Pakistan (now Bangladesh); around 15 million displaced, hundreds of thousands killed. Kashmir: Muslim-majority, claimed by both, split by a Line of Control; wars fought over it. _(K3)_
- **India: Ayodhya and Operation Blue Star, with dates** — Babri Mosque, Ayodhya: demolished by Hindu nationalists, 6 December 1992; riots killed about 2,000; Ram temple consecrated there, January 2024. Operation Blue Star, June 1984: army stormed the Golden Temple; Sikh bodyguards killed Indira Gandhi that October; anti-Sikh riots in Delhi followed. _(K3)_
- **Israel and Palestine: Jerusalem's holy sites and the events of 1948 and 1967** — Western Wall (Jewish), Al-Aqsa Mosque and Dome of the Rock (Muslim), Church of the Holy Sepulchre (Christian). Israel declared 14 May 1948; about 700,000 Palestinians fled or were expelled (Nakba); 1967: West Bank, East Jerusalem, Gaza occupied. _(K4)_
- **Israel and Palestine: Oslo, settlements and the war since 2023** — Oslo Accords, 1993: mutual recognition, limited self-rule. West Bank settlements are illegal in the view of the UN and most states. 7 October 2023: Hamas-led attack killed about 1,200 in Israel; Israel's war killed tens of thousands in Gaza; ceasefire 2025. _(K4)_
- **Other religious conflicts: Lebanon, Nigeria and Iraq–Syria** — Lebanon: power shared by community (Maronite President, Sunni Prime Minister, Shia Speaker); civil war 1975–1990. Nigeria: mainly Muslim north, Christian south; Boko Haram (from 2009) took 276 Chibok schoolgirls, 2014. Iraq and Syria: Sunni–Shia violence after 2003; Islamic State caliphate, 2014. _(K5)_

### 4. Everyday expressions of culture and identity (24)

- **Everyday expressions of culture: five reasons they matter** — They mark belonging; they pass culture between generations; they assert identity under pressure, as Irish games and music did in the Gaelic Revival; they attract tourists and income; they can mark division (Northern Ireland parades, football rivalries). _(X2)_
- **Globalisation and everyday culture: pressures and responses** — Fast food, Hollywood, global sports and brands make cultures alike: McDonald's opened on Grafton Street in 1977; Halloween customs returned from the USA in commercial form. Responses: revival movements, State support, UNESCO's intangible heritage list. _(X3)_
- **Name three Irish entries on UNESCO's intangible heritage list, with years** — Uilleann piping (2017), hurling (2018) and Irish harping (2019), recorded as living traditions under UNESCO's 2003 convention on the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity. _(X3)_
- **Migration and everyday culture: effects with two examples** — Migrants bring food, festivals, religious celebrations and sports that blend with local ones in multicultural festivals and fusion food: Polish shops and food are now common in Irish towns; the döner kebab spread in Germany through Turkish immigrants. _(X4)_
- **The GAA: founding, aims and structure** — Founded 1 November 1884 at Hayes' Hotel, Thurles, by Michael Cusack and others (first president Maurice Davin) to preserve native games. Amateur; parish clubs and county teams build local identity; all-island, with diaspora clubs; Camogie Association, 1904. _(S1)_
- **Croke Park: capacity, Bloody Sunday 1920 and the 2007 opening** — Holds about 82,300, one of Europe's largest stadiums. 21 November 1920: British forces fired on a Gaelic football match, killing 14. From 2007 it hosted rugby and soccer while Lansdowne Road was rebuilt; England played Ireland at rugby there in February 2007. _(S1)_
- **GAA rule changes and sporting identity beyond the GAA** — The ban on foreign games (soccer, rugby, cricket, hockey) was lifted in 1971; the rule barring British security forces in 2001. The Ireland rugby team represents the whole island; in Northern Ireland, backing Irish or British teams can express identity. _(S1)_
- **Irish traditional music: instruments, organisations and the Fleadh** — Fiddle, uilleann pipes, tin whistle, flute, concertina, accordion, bodhrán and harp (the national symbol). Comhaltas Ceoltóirí Éireann, 1951; first Fleadh Cheoil, Mullingar, 1951, now in a different town each August. Sean-nós singing is strongest in Conamara. _(S2)_
- **Riverdance and Eurovision: Ireland on the European stage** — Riverdance began as the interval act of the Eurovision Song Contest in Dublin on 30 April 1994 and became a worldwide touring show, spreading Irish dance. Ireland has won Eurovision seven times, including three in a row, 1992–94. _(S2, E7)_
- **Irish national festivals: St Patrick's Day, St Brigid's Day and Samhain** — St Patrick's Day, 17 March: parades worldwide (e.g. New York), landmarks lit green, Dublin festival since 1996. St Brigid's Day (around 1 February): public holiday from 2023, the first named after a woman. Samhain (31 October–1 November): origin of Halloween. _(S3)_
- **Irish fairs and older traditions: places and timing** — Puck Fair, Killorglin, Co. Kerry, 10–12 August, when a wild goat is crowned king; Lisdoonvarna Matchmaking Festival, Co. Clare, each September; Ballinasloe Horse Fair, each October, one of Europe's oldest. Older traditions: wakes, holy wells, pattern days. _(S3)_
- **Irish diaspora, literary and arts festivals with dates** — Rose of Tralee, since 1959, open to women of Irish birth or descent worldwide; Bloomsday, 16 June, for Joyce's Ulysses (set on 16 June 1904); Galway International Arts Festival, since 1978; Wexford Festival Opera, since 1951. _(S3)_
- **Irish costume, craft and food as expressions of identity** — Aran sweater from the Aran Islands; Claddagh ring (hands, heart, crown) from Galway's Claddagh; dancing costumes; county GAA jerseys. Food: soda bread, Irish stew, colcannon, boxty, Dublin coddle, full Irish breakfast; Guinness at St James's Gate since 1759. _(S4, S5)_
- **Irish art and literature: Newgrange, early Christian art and Nobel laureates** — Book of Kells (around 800, Trinity College Dublin); high crosses such as Muiredach's Cross, Monasterboice; Newgrange (around 3200 BC), lit at the winter solstice. Nobel Prize for Literature: Yeats 1923, Shaw 1925, Beckett 1969, Heaney 1995. _(S6)_
- **Irish Travellers: features of their everyday culture** — Traditionally nomadic, with strong extended family ties; horse fairs such as Ballinasloe; their own language, Cant, Gammon or Shelta; about 33,000 people (Census 2022), recognised as an ethnic minority on 1 March 2017. _(S7)_
- **Northern Ireland: parades, murals and territorial markers** — Orange Order parades on 12 July mark the Battle of the Boyne (1690), with loyalist bonfires on 11 July; murals in Belfast and Derry show loyalist and republican identities; kerbstones painted red, white and blue or green, white and orange mark territory. _(S8)_
- **Spain: festivals with places and dates** — San Fermín, Pamplona, 6–14 July, with the running of the bulls; La Tomatina, Buñol, last Wednesday of August, since 1945; Semana Santa processions, especially in Seville; flamenco (Andalusia) and Catalan castells, both on UNESCO's list since 2010. _(E1)_
- **Spain: football, bullfighting and regional identity** — FC Barcelona's motto Més que un club reflects its role as a symbol of Catalan identity, especially under Franco; Athletic Bilbao fields only players born, raised or trained in the Basque Country; Catalonia banned bullfighting in 2010 (in force 2012), overturned in 2016. _(E1)_
- **Scotland: dress, music and festivals** — Kilt and tartan; bagpipes; Highland Games (caber toss, hammer throw, piping, Highland dancing); Burns Night, 25 January, celebrating Robert Burns with haggis, whisky and poetry; Hogmanay, the Scottish New Year. _(E2)_
- **Celtic and Rangers: football as religious and national identity** — Celtic was founded in 1887 in Glasgow's East End by Brother Walfrid to raise money for poor Irish Catholic immigrants; Rangers (1872) was traditionally backed by Protestants and unionists. The Old Firm rivalry carries religious and Irish or British identity. _(E2)_
- **Germany: everyday expressions of culture with dates** — Oktoberfest, Munich, Bavaria, held since 1810, when it began with a royal wedding; visitors wear lederhosen and dirndl; Christmas markets in German cities; the döner kebab, popularised by Turkish immigrants, shows migration changing everyday food. _(E3)_
- **France: everyday expressions of culture with dates** — Bastille Day, 14 July, marks the storming of the Bastille (1789) with a military parade in Paris; the Tour de France, first held 1903, each July; the gastronomic meal of the French, on UNESCO's list since 2010; the Cannes Film Festival each May. _(E4)_
- **Italy: festivals and food as expressions of identity** — Palio di Siena: a horse race around the Piazza del Campo between the city's contrade, on 2 July and 16 August; Venice Carnival, with masks, before Lent; Neapolitan pizza-making on UNESCO's list since 2017; regional food and football. _(E5)_
- **Norway, the Netherlands and Brittany: national and regional festivals** — Norway: Constitution Day, 17 May (1814 constitution), with children's parades and the bunad. Netherlands: King's Day, 27 April, when people wear orange. Brittany: Festival Interceltique de Lorient each August since 1971; fest-noz on UNESCO's list since 2012. _(E6)_

### 5. Nationality and the nation state (25)

- **States: the Montevideo criteria, UN membership and examples of state types** — Montevideo Convention (1933): a permanent population, a defined territory, a government and the capacity to deal with other states. The UN has 193 members. Nation states: Iceland, Portugal, Japan; multinational: the UK, Belgium, Switzerland, Spain. _(N1)_
- **Boundary types by form and by origin** — Form: physical (mountains, rivers, lakes, seas); geometric (straight lines, often latitude); cultural (language or religion). Origin: antecedent (before settlement); subsequent (following cultural divides); superimposed (colonial Africa); relict (Berlin Wall). _(B1)_
- **Physical boundaries: examples and a group one splits** — The Pyrenees (much of the France–Spain border since the Treaty of the Pyrenees, 1659); the Rhine (part of France–Germany); the Rio Grande (USA–Mexico); the Alps and Himalayas. They can split a cultural group: Basques live on both sides of the Pyrenees. _(B2)_
- **Geometric and colonial boundaries: two examples with dates** — The 49th parallel forms most of the western USA–Canada border (agreed 1818, extended 1846). The Berlin Conference (1884–85) set rules for dividing Africa; straight colonial borders ignored ethnic territories, splitting some groups and joining rivals. _(B3)_
- **How boundaries affect identity: effects with examples** — They build shared identity (citizenship, schools, sport); split groups (partition, Kurds, Basques); harden into barriers (Berlin Wall, 1961–1989; Cyprus Green Line, 1974); or soften (Schengen, 1985; Ireland stays out because of the Common Travel Area, 1923). _(B4)_
- **Partition of Ireland: Acts, treaty and the border** — Government of Ireland Act 1920; Northern Ireland, 1921: Antrim, Armagh, Down, Fermanagh, Londonderry/Derry, Tyrone; Treaty, December 1921; Cavan, Donegal, Monaghan to the Free State; the 1925 Boundary Commission left the border unchanged: about 499 km on old county lines. _(B5)_
- **Partition and identity: constitution, citizenship and Brexit** — Two states, two majorities, two minorities; Articles 2 and 3 changed after the May 1998 vote to unity by consent; people in Northern Ireland may be Irish, British or both; it voted about 56% Remain (2016); the Windsor Framework (2023) keeps the border open. _(B5)_
- **Cultural groups within states: why they exist and six ways states respond** — Causes: conquest and union, borders ignoring culture, migration. Responses: federalism and autonomy (Belgium, Switzerland, Spain); devolution (UK, 1999); power-sharing (Northern Ireland, Lebanon); multiculturalism (Canada); assimilation (France); suppression. _(W1, W2)_
- **Spain: autonomy for Basques and Catalans, with dates** — The 1978 constitution created 17 autonomous communities; the Basque Autonomous Community (Statute of Gernika, 1979) and Navarre collect their own taxes; Catalonia's statute dates from 1979, revised 2006. Both languages were suppressed under Franco. _(W3)_
- **The UK: devolution, the 2014 Scottish vote and the 2016 EU vote by nation** — Devolution, 1999: Scottish Parliament, National Assembly for Wales, Northern Ireland Assembly. Scotland, 18 September 2014: about 55% No to independence. EU vote, 2016: UK about 52% Leave; Scotland about 62% and Northern Ireland about 56% Remain. _(W4)_
- **Quebec: language law, sovereignty referendums and recognition** — Bill 101 (1977) made French the sole official language of Quebec; sovereignty referendums in 1980 (about 60% No) and 1995 (about 50.6% No); in 2006 Canada's House of Commons recognised that the Québécois form a nation within a united Canada. _(W5)_
- **The Sámi: an indigenous people within four states** — Indigenous people, traditionally reindeer herders, of the far north of Norway, Sweden, Finland and Russia's Kola Peninsula (Sápmi), with elected Sámi parliaments in Norway (1989), Sweden (1993) and Finland (1996). _(W8)_
- **Kurds: numbers, homeland and the treaties that denied them a state** — 30 million or more, the largest ethnic group without a state, in Kurdistan across Turkey (most), Iran, Iraq and Syria; Kurdish is Indo-European; Newroz, 21 March. Sèvres (1920) allowed a possible Kurdish state; Lausanne (1923) drew Turkey without one. _(G1)_
- **Kurds in Turkey: suppression and the PKK conflict** — Turkey long denied a Kurdish identity and banned Kurdish in public life and broadcasting until the early 1990s. The PKK, founded 1978, began an armed campaign in 1984; the conflict cost about 40,000 lives; the PKK announced its dissolution in May 2025. _(G1)_
- **Kurds in Iraq and Syria: key events with dates** — Halabja, 16 March 1988: Saddam Hussein's forces killed about 5,000 with chemical weapons; no-fly zone after 1991; autonomy in Iraq's 2005 constitution (Erbil); 2017: about 93% voted for independence, rejected by Iraq. Syria: Kurdish-led forces held Kobani against Islamic State, 2014–15. _(G1)_
- **Palestinians: refugees, self-rule and recognition of a state** — About 700,000 fled or were expelled in 1948; refugees live in Jordan, Lebanon, Syria, the West Bank and Gaza. Oslo (1993) created the Palestinian Authority. Recognised by Ireland, Spain, Norway (28 May 2024) and by the UK, Canada, Australia and France (2025). _(G2)_
- **Roma: numbers, origins and discrimination** — About 10–12 million in Europe, its largest ethnic minority, with no territory; Romani is related to languages of northern India; in south-eastern Europe by the 14th century; targeted in the Holocaust; Czech special schooling of Roma ruled discriminatory, 2007. _(G3)_
- **Tibetans: Chinese control and threats to their culture** — China's army entered Tibet in 1950; after a failed 1959 uprising the Dalai Lama fled to India (exile government in Dharamsala); Tibet Autonomous Region, 1965. Tibetans see Han migration, controls on monasteries and Mandarin-medium schooling as threats to their culture. _(G4)_
- **Uyghurs: who they are and what has happened since 2017** — A Turkic-speaking, mainly Muslim people of Xinjiang, north-west China. Since 2017 an estimated one million or more held in detention camps; a UN human rights office report (August 2022) found serious violations that may amount to crimes against humanity. _(G5)_
- **Stateless groups: how they respond and the range of outcomes** — They keep identity through language, festivals and diaspora groups, and use peaceful campaigns, autonomy demands or armed struggle. Outcomes range from autonomy (Iraqi Kurdistan; Basques and Catalans, far beyond the Kurds) to repression (Tibet, Xinjiang). _(G6, G7)_
- **Conflict between states and cultural groups: five general causes** — Boundaries that cut across cultural groups; a dominant group controlling the state; suppression of a group's language and religion; economic inequality between regions or groups; competing claims to the same territory. _(K1)_
- **Northern Ireland: a political structure redesigned for two groups** — A state built around a unionist majority; discrimination led to civil rights protest (1968) and the Troubles (over 3,500 killed). Good Friday Agreement, 10 April 1998 (about 71% Yes North, 94% South): power-sharing, North/South Council, consent principle. _(K2)_
- **ETA and the Basque conflict: key dates and figures** — ETA (Basque Homeland and Freedom), founded in 1959, killed over 800 people in its campaign for an independent Basque state; it declared a definitive end to violence in 2011 and dissolved in 2018. Spain's response included autonomy under the 1978 constitution. _(K3)_
- **Catalonia's independence crisis: events from 2010 to 2024** — 2010: court struck down parts of the 2006 statute, including Catalonia as a nation; 1 October 2017: referendum ruled illegal by Spain's courts, about 90% Yes on about 43% turnout; direct rule (Article 155); Puigdemont fled to Belgium; leaders jailed 2019, pardoned 2021; amnesty 2024. _(K4)_
- **Sri Lanka and Chechnya: states against cultural groups** — Sri Lanka: civil war 1983–2009 between a government led by the Sinhalese majority and the Tamil Tigers (LTTE), seeking a Tamil state in the north and east; LTTE defeated May 2009. Chechnya (Russia): wars 1994–96 and 1999–2009; Grozny largely destroyed. _(K6)_

### 6. A European regional case study of identity (27)

- **Switzerland: physical geography and how it shaped identity** — About 60% lies in the Alps, the Jura in the north-west, and most people live on the Swiss Plateau; the Rhine and Rhône rise in the Swiss Alps; Lake Geneva and Lake Constance form borders. Mountains isolated valleys, preserving dialects and Romansh. _(A1)_
- **Switzerland: formation of the Confederation, 1291 to 1848** — Federal Charter, 1 August 1291 (Uri, Schwyz, Unterwalden); independence recognised in 1648 (Westphalia); neutrality recognised in 1815 (Vienna), when Geneva, Valais and Neuchâtel joined; Sonderbund War, 1847; federal constitution, 1848. _(A2)_
- **Switzerland's political system: cantons, direct democracy and neutrality** — 26 cantons run schools, police, taxes and churches; referendums several times a year (an initiative needs 100,000 signatures); a seven-member Federal Council, language regions represented by custom; not in the EU; UN only from 2002; Schengen 2008. _(A2)_
- **Switzerland's languages: shares and the canton pattern** — German about 62%, French about 23%, Italian about 8%, Romansh about 0.5%. 17 German-speaking cantons; 4 French (Geneva, Vaud, Neuchâtel, Jura); 1 Italian (Ticino); 3 bilingual (Bern, Fribourg, Valais); 1 trilingual (Graubünden). _(A3)_
- **Switzerland: how language diversity is managed** — Swiss German is spoken but Standard German written; Romansh a national language from 1938 (Rumantsch Grischun, 1982); the Röstigraben runs roughly along the River Sarine; SRG SSR broadcasts in all four languages; pupils learn a second national language. _(A3)_
- **Switzerland: religion, and how it cuts across language** — Reformation: Zwingli (Zurich, from 1519), Calvin (Geneva). Protestant: Zurich, Bern, Basel, Geneva, Vaud; Catholic: central cantons, Fribourg, Valais, Ticino, Jura, so both language regions mix the two. Minaret ban 2009 (about 57.5%); face-covering ban 2021. _(A4)_
- **Switzerland: music, festivals and symbols of identity** — Yodelling and the alphorn; Schwingen; national day, 1 August; Basel Fasnacht (UNESCO 2017); Zurich's Sechseläuten burns the Böögg; Fête des Vignerons, Vevey (2019); Montreux Jazz (since 1967); fondue, rösti, chocolate; Red Cross founded in Geneva, 1863. _(A5)_
- **Switzerland: migration and immigration referendums** — Foreign nationals about 27% (2023), largely Italians, Germans, Portuguese, French and people from Kosovo; post-war Italian seasonal workers; EU free movement from 2002; Schwarzenbach Initiative rejected (1970); against mass immigration passed 9 February 2014. _(A6)_
- **Switzerland: new boundaries and changes in who belongs** — Canton of Jura created 1 January 1979, after French-speaking Catholic districts voted to leave Protestant Bern; Moutier voted in 2021 and moved on 1 January 2026; EEA membership rejected, 6 December 1992 (about 50.3% No); women's federal vote only in 1971. _(A7)_
- **Balkans: historic frontiers and empires** — The Great Schism line (1054) and the Ottoman–Habsburg frontier crossed the region. Battle of Kosovo, 1389; Ottomans ruled Serbia, Bosnia, Macedonia, Kosovo; Austria-Hungary ruled Slovenia and Croatia, annexed Bosnia 1908; Sarajevo assassination, 28 June 1914. _(B2)_
- **Yugoslavia from 1918 to 1989: key events** — Kingdom of Serbs, Croats and Slovenes, 1 December 1918, renamed Yugoslavia 1929; in the Second World War the Ustaše killed Serbs, Jews and Roma at Jasenovac and Chetniks also killed civilians; Tito (1945–1980): six republics, two provinces; Milošević rose from 1987; Kosovo's autonomy removed 1989. _(B2)_
- **Balkans: ethnicity, language and religion** — South Slavs largely share one language but differ in script (Serbs Cyrillic and Latin, Croats Latin) and faith: Catholic Slovenes, Croats; Orthodox Serbs, Montenegrins, Macedonians; Muslim Bosniaks. Non-Slav Albanians are mostly Muslim. Bosnia 1991: Muslims about 44%, Serbs 31%, Croats 17%. _(B3, B4)_
- **Wars of the 1990s: Slovenia, Croatia and Kosovo** — Slovenia and Croatia declared independence on 25 June 1991; ten-day war in Slovenia; Croatia 1991–95: Vukovar destroyed 1991; Operation Storm (August 1995): about 200,000 Serbs fled. Kosovo 1998–99: NATO bombed Serbia for 78 days; about 800,000 Albanians fled or were expelled. _(B5)_
- **The Bosnian war, 1992–95: key facts** — About 100,000 killed; half the population forced out by ethnic cleansing; Sarajevo besieged April 1992 to February 1996; Mostar's Old Bridge destroyed 9 November 1993, rebuilt 2004; Srebrenica, July 1995: about 8,000 Bosniak men and boys killed (genocide). _(B5)_
- **Balkans: festivals, music and sport as identity** — EXIT festival, Novi Sad (2000, begun against Milošević); Guča Trumpet Festival (1961); Sarajevo Film Festival (1995, during the siege); sevdalinka; kolo (UNESCO 2017); Mostar bridge diving; Maksimir riot, 13 May 1990; Eurovision wins 1989 and 2007. _(B6)_
- **Balkans: the role of migration** — Guest-worker agreement with West Germany, 1968, plus migration to Austria and Switzerland; 1990s wars displaced millions, leaving areas ethnically uniform; 2015 refugees crossed North Macedonia and Serbia on the Western Balkans route; young people emigrate. _(B7)_
- **Balkans: new states and boundaries since 1991** — Slovenia, Croatia, Macedonia 1991 (North Macedonia 2019); Bosnia 1992; Montenegro 2006; Kosovo 17 February 2008, not recognised by Serbia or five EU states. Dayton (1995): Federation and Republika Srpska, plus Brčko. EU: Slovenia 2004, Croatia 2013. _(B8)_
- **Celtic regions: the six regions and their language branches** — Goidelic: Ireland, Scotland, the Isle of Man (Irish, Scottish Gaelic, Manx). Brythonic: Wales, Cornwall, Brittany. Britons migrated to Armorica (Brittany) in the 5th–6th centuries as Anglo-Saxons settled eastern Britain; remoteness helped the Celtic languages survive. _(C1)_
- **Wales: history, boundary and devolution with dates** — Offa's Dyke (8th century); Edward I's conquest, 1282–83, and castles such as Caernarfon and Conwy; Laws in Wales Acts 1535 and 1542; devolution referendum, 18 September 1997 (about 50.3% Yes); Assembly 1999; Senedd 2020, with 96 members from 2026. _(C2)_
- **Wales: religion and the Nonconformist chapels** — The 1851 religious census showed most worshippers in Wales attended Nonconformist chapels rather than the Anglican church. Chapels were centres of Welsh-language education, singing and community life. The Church in Wales was disestablished in 1920. _(C4)_
- **Wales: music, festivals, sport and national symbols** — National Eisteddfod, in Welsh, alternating north and south; the Urdd Eisteddfod; male voice choirs; Welsh Rugby Union 1881, Principality Stadium (1999); St David's Day, 1 March; anthem Hen Wlad Fy Nhadau (1856); red dragon, daffodil, leek; cawl, Welsh cakes. _(C5)_
- **Wales: migration in and out, and its effect on identity** — 19th-century coal and iron drew English and Irish workers into south Wales, spreading English; the Mimosa took settlers to Patagonia in 1865; second-home buyers now enter Welsh-speaking areas: arson campaign from 1979; 300% council-tax premium from April 2023. _(C6)_
- **Brittany: boundaries, religion and culture** — United with France in 1532 and centralised after 1789; Loire-Atlantique (Nantes) separated in 1941; Catholic pardons; bagadoù, biniou and bombarde, fest-noz, Alan Stivell; Lorient festival since 1971; Bigouden coiffe; crêpes and cider; Gwenn-ha-Du flag (1923). _(C7, C8)_
- **Belgium: history, language border and federal structure** — Independence 1830; French long dominant in government; language border fixed by laws of 1962 and 1963 across flat, open land; first state reform 1970; federal state 1993, with three regions (Flanders, Wallonia, Brussels-Capital) and three language communities. _(D1)_
- **Belgium: population and language divisions** — About 11.8 million (2024): Flanders 6.8 million, Wallonia 3.7 million, Brussels 1.2 million; Dutch speakers about 6 in 10; German speakers under 80,000; Leuven university split in 1968; no nationwide parties; 541 days without a government, 2010–11. _(D2)_
- **Belgium: religion, art and festivals** — Catholic in both Flanders and Wallonia, so religion did not deepen the divide; Islam grew through Moroccan and Turkish migration. Ghent Altarpiece (van Eyck, 1432); Bruegel, Rubens; Tintin (1929); Carnival of Binche; Tomorrowland; 11 July Golden Spurs holiday. _(D3, D4)_
- **Belgium: migration and economic reversal** — Italian miners from 1946 (Marcinelle disaster, 8 August 1956: 262 died, 136 Italian); Moroccan and Turkish agreements, 1964; Brussels hosts the EU and NATO; Islamic State bombings of 22 March 2016 killed 32. Wallonia's coal and steel declined; Flanders grew via Antwerp. _(D5, D6)_

## Option geoecology

### 1. Soil composition and characteristics (15)

- **Soil composition: the four components of a typical good loam** — By volume, about 45% mineral matter, 5% organic matter, 25% water and 25% air; typical of a well-structured loam, not fixed for every soil. Water and air share the pore spaces, so when one rises the other falls. _(C1)_
- **Parent material: name residual and transported examples** — Residual: thin soils on the limestone of the Burren; soils on the granite of the Wicklow Mountains, which are acidic and sandy. Transported: boulder clay (till) of the Irish Midlands; alluvium of the Shannon Callows; loess (limon) on the Beauce in the Paris Basin. _(F2)_
- **Why Irish soils are young** — Most formed on glacial deposits of the Midlandian glaciation, which ended about 10,000 years ago, so they have developed for about 10,000 years or less and often differ from the solid rock beneath them. _(F3)_
- **Mineral matter: how the parent rock affects the soil** — Limestone gives base-rich, less acidic soils; granite gives sandy, acidic soils with quartz grains and clay from weathered feldspar; sandstone gives sandy, acidic, free-draining soils. As it weathers, mineral matter releases calcium, magnesium and potassium. _(C2)_
- **Humus: five ways it improves a soil** — It darkens the soil; binds particles into crumbs; holds water; holds nutrients and releases them slowly; and is the main store of nitrogen in the soil. _(C3)_
- **Soil organisms: what earthworms and nitrogen-fixing bacteria contribute** — Earthworms mix and aerate the soil, drag litter down and improve structure with their casts, as Charles Darwin's last book (1881) showed. Nitrogen-fixing bacteria in the root nodules of legumes, such as clover, add nitrogen. _(C3)_
- **Water movement: how the balance of rain and evaporation shapes a soil** — Where precipitation exceeds evapotranspiration, water moves down and leaches the soil, as in Ireland; where evaporation exceeds precipitation, capillary rise leaves salts and lime near the surface (deserts, grasslands); too much water causes waterlogging. _(C6)_
- **Soil air: what it supplies and what happens when it is lacking** — Oxygen for roots and aerobic decomposers; nitrogen for nitrogen-fixing bacteria; extra carbon dioxide, forming weak carbonic acid that aids weathering. In anaerobic soils decomposition is slow, so peat builds up, and reduced iron turns the soil grey. _(C7)_
- **Soil texture: particle sizes and the feel test** — Sand 2–0.05 mm (gritty); silt 0.05–0.002 mm (smooth, silky); clay under 0.002 mm (sticky, rolls into a ribbon when moist); over 2 mm is gravel. A loam is roughly 40% sand, 40% silt and 20% clay. _(K1, K2)_
- **Sandy and clay soils: advantages and disadvantages** — Sandy: free-draining, aerated, warm quickly and easy to plough, but hold little water or nutrients and leach easily. Clay: hold water and nutrients, but drain slowly (tiny pores), become waterlogged and cold, crack when dry and are hard to work. _(K2, K6)_
- **Soil structure: what builds peds, what destroys them and why it matters** — Humus, clay, earthworms, roots and freeze-thaw build peds; compaction by heavy machinery and poaching by livestock on wet ground destroy them. Structure controls the pores, so it decides drainage, aeration and root penetration. _(K4)_
- **Soil colour: what each colour tells you, with soil types** — Black or dark brown: humus (chernozem, peat). Brown: humus and iron (brown earth). Red or yellow: iron oxides, well aerated (latosol, terra rossa). Ash-grey: leached (podzol). Grey with rusty mottles: waterlogged (gley). White: lime or salt (desert soils). _(K5)_
- **Organic content: which soils have high and low humus, and why** — High in chernozems and peat; moderate in brown earths; low in desert soils (little vegetation) and, surprisingly, in tropical latosols, where litter decays so fast that little humus builds up. _(K7)_
- **Soil pH: causes of acidity and alkalinity, and Teagasc's targets** — Acidic where heavy rain leaches out bases or acid litter (conifer needles, heather) builds up; alkaline on limestone and chalk and in dry climates. Teagasc advises about pH 6.3 for grassland and 6.5 for tillage; spreading lime raises pH. _(K8)_
- **How soil characteristics influence soil development: four links** — Texture and structure control drainage, deciding whether leaching, gleying or capillary rise dominates; pH decides which organisms live there, so mull or mor humus; organic content drives structure and fertility; aeration decides aerobic or anaerobic decay. _(K10)_

### 2. Soil processes and the global pattern of soils (25)

- **Climate as a soil-forming factor: the two ways it works** — Temperature controls the speed of weathering and decomposition; the balance of precipitation and evapotranspiration decides whether water moves down (leaching) or up (capillary rise). This is why soil belts follow climate belts. _(E2)_
- **Soil erosion: forms by water and wind, and effects on the soil** — Water: rain-splash, sheet wash, rills and gullies; wind: deflation of dry, loose, bare soil. It thins the profile, strips the humus-rich A horizon and its nutrients, exposes infertile subsoil and worsens structure; the soil is redeposited as alluvium or loess. _(P2)_
- **Leaching: conditions and effects on the soil** — Where precipitation exceeds evapotranspiration, as in Ireland, water carries bases (calcium, magnesium, potassium, sodium) down, so the upper soil becomes more acidic and less fertile. Mild leaching gives brown earths; intense leaching gives podzols. _(P3)_
- **Humification: conditions that make it fast or slow** — Fast in warm, moist, well-aerated, base-rich conditions, producing mull; slow in cold, acid or waterlogged conditions, where partly decomposed matter builds up as mor or peat. It releases nutrients for plants as part of the nutrient cycle. _(P4)_
- **Podzolisation: the three conditions it needs** — A cool, wet climate where precipitation is well above evapotranspiration; acid parent material such as granite or sandstone; coniferous forest or heath vegetation giving acid mor humus. _(P5)_
- **Podzolisation: the steps in order** — Acid litter releases organic acids into percolating water; they bind iron and aluminium (chelation) and carry them down with bases and humus, leaving a bleached, sandy A/E horizon; iron, aluminium and humus build up in the B horizon, often forming an iron pan. _(P5)_
- **Laterisation: the steps in order** — Heat and heavy rain cause deep chemical weathering; silica and bases are leached out; insoluble iron and aluminium oxides are left, giving a deep red or yellow colour; litter decays so fast that little humus forms; if exposed and dried, a laterite crust forms. _(P6)_
- **Bauxite: how it forms and where it is mined** — Extreme laterisation of aluminium-rich rock forms bauxite, the ore of aluminium. It is mined from laterised rock in tropical countries such as Guinea, Jamaica and Australia (north Queensland). _(P6)_
- **Calcification: the steps in order** — Rain penetrates only part of the way, so leaching is weak; calcium carbonate is carried a short way down and deposited in the B horizon as nodules; in dry periods capillary action draws water and calcium back up; deep grass roots return calcium in litter. _(P7)_
- **Gleying: where it happens and the steps** — In heavy clay, impermeable till, hollows or above an iron pan, water fills the pores and air is excluded; bacteria reduce red ferric iron to grey ferrous iron; where air enters along roots or cracks, iron re-oxidises as orange mottles. _(P8)_
- **Zonal, intrazonal and azonal soils: examples of each** — Zonal, set by climate and vegetation: brown earth, podzol, chernozem, latosol. Intrazonal, set by one local factor: gley and peat (drainage), rendzina (limestone). Azonal, immature: alluvium, scree, sand dunes, fresh volcanic ash, recent glacial deposits. _(G1)_
- **World soil belts: name the regions for four zonal soils** — Podzols: boreal forest of northern Canada, Scandinavia, Finland, Siberia. Brown earths: Ireland, Britain, France, Germany, the eastern USA. Chernozems: steppes of Ukraine and southern Russia, the Prairies, the Pampas. Latosols: Amazon, Congo, South-East Asia. _(G2)_
- **Ireland's soil pattern: where each soil type is found** — Brown earths and grey-brown podzolics on the lowlands; podzols and peaty podzols on the uplands; gleys on poorly drained clay till, especially the drumlin belt; thin rendzinas on the limestone of the Burren; peat in bogs. _(G4)_
- **Irish peatland: extent and the two types of bog** — Peatland covers about a fifth of Ireland's land area. Blanket bog lies in the west (e.g. Co. Mayo, Connemara) and on mountains; raised bog lies in the Midlands (e.g. Co. Offaly). Peat is an intrazonal soil. _(G4)_
- **Brown earth: location and formation** — Ireland's main lowland soil, e.g. the Golden Vale (Co. Tipperary, Co. Limerick) and the south-east. Formed on glacial till under a mild, wet climate and deciduous woodland of oak, ash, elm and hazel, by mild leaching and earthworm mixing of mull humus. _(I1)_
- **Brown earth: profile, characteristics and uses** — O: thin mull. A: dark brown, loamy crumb, roughly 20–30 cm. B: lighter brown, some iron and clay washed in, gradual boundary and no bleached E horizon. Slightly acidic (roughly pH 5.5–6.5) and fertile: grassland and tillage; limed if pH falls. _(I1)_
- **Grey-brown podzolic: how it differs from a brown earth and where it is found** — A slightly more leached relative of the brown earth, with clay washed into the B horizon; typical of the limestone-rich till of the east Midlands, e.g. Co. Meath and Co. Kildare. _(I1, G4)_
- **Podzol in Ireland: location, acidity, uses and improvement** — Uplands such as the granite Wicklow Mountains, under heather moorland or Sitka spruce; often peaty podzols. Strongly acid, about pH 4 to 4.5. Used for sheep grazing and forestry; improved by deep ploughing to break the iron pan, heavy liming and fertiliser. _(I2)_
- **Podzol: the profile horizon by horizon** — O: thick mor or peat; A/E: ash-grey, bleached sand; a thin, black iron pan; B: reddish-brown, enriched with iron and humus; C: parent material. Horizons are sharply defined. _(I2)_
- **Gley in Ireland: location, characteristics, use and improvement** — Drumlin belt of Co. Cavan, Monaghan and Leitrim (impermeable boulder clay, wet hollows), the north Midlands and Co. Clare. Grey with orange mottles, wet, sticky and cold; grass for cattle, with a short grazing season; improved by field and arterial drainage. _(I3)_
- **Latosol: location, climate, profile and characteristics** — Amazon Basin, Congo Basin and South-East Asia; about 27 °C all year, over 2,000 mm of rain. Many metres deep: thin O, thin leached A, very deep red B of sesquioxides. Red or yellow, clay-rich, acidic, with very little humus. _(S1)_
- **Latosol: why it is infertile and what happens when the forest is cleared** — Nearly all nutrients are held in the living vegetation and recycled fast from the thin litter layer. Shifting cultivation used it for only a few years; once cleared, it loses fertility within a few years, erodes under heavy rain and can bake into laterite. _(S1)_
- **Chernozem: location, climate and vegetation** — Steppes of Ukraine and southern Russia, the Prairies (Great Plains of the USA and Canada) and the Pampas of Argentina. Continental: hot summers, very cold winters, about 250–500 mm of rain. Dense grass roots die back each year, adding much organic matter. _(S2)_
- **Chernozem: the profile and the role of burrowing animals** — A: very thick, black, humus-rich, about 1 m deep, crumb structure. B: calcium-rich with white lime nodules. C: loess. Susliks (steppe) and prairie dogs mix the soil; their filled burrows show as krotovinas. _(S2)_
- **Chernozem: characteristics and uses** — Neutral to slightly alkaline and very fertile: among the world's main wheat and maize lands, with sunflowers in Ukraine, the breadbasket of Europe. Ploughing the prairie for wheat, then 1930s drought, caused the Dust Bowl. _(S2)_

### 3. Human interference with soils (25)

- **Why soil is a non-renewable resource: the FAO figure** — Soil forms very slowly but can be lost quickly: the FAO has stated that it can take up to 1,000 years to form 1 cm of soil (quoted for the International Year of Soils, 2015). _(I1)_
- **Overcropping: effects on soil characteristics** — Nutrients are removed faster than they are replaced; humus falls; crumb structure breaks down; the soil becomes dusty or compacted, holds less water and erodes easily, so yields fall. Monoculture takes the same nutrients each year and encourages pests. _(OC2)_
- **Overgrazing: effects on soil characteristics** — Vegetation is eaten down and roots die, exposing soil to rain-splash and wind; trampling compacts it (poaching), cutting infiltration and raising runoff; sheet, rill and gully erosion remove topsoil and humus; bare peat erodes fast. _(OG2)_
- **West of Ireland hill commonage: why overgrazing happened and its effects** — From the 1980s EU headage payments paid per animal, so sheep numbers rose sharply in Connemara and the mountains of Co. Mayo, Donegal and Kerry. Heather and grass were stripped, blanket peat eroded, and silt harmed salmon and trout spawning grounds. _(B4, OG3)_
- **Hill commonage overgrazing: three responses** — Payments changed from per-head to per-area; Commonage Framework Plans (from the early 2000s) set lower stocking rates; REPS (the Rural Environment Protection Scheme, 1994) paid farmers to farm in environmentally friendly ways. _(B4)_
- **Desertification: physical and human causes** — Physical: drought, unreliable rainfall, high temperatures and evaporation. Human: population growth, overgrazing, overcropping, fuelwood cutting, poorly managed irrigation. Usually together: human pressure leaves soil bare, then drought and storms remove it. _(D2)_
- **Desertification: effects on soil characteristics** — Loss of topsoil and humus; lower fertility; surface crusting, so rain runs off instead of soaking in; reduced water retention; wind-blown sand and dust; salt crusts where irrigation is poorly managed; in extreme cases bare, sterile ground. _(D3)_
- **Sahel: location and climate** — The semi-arid belt along the southern edge of the Sahara, from Senegal and Mauritania through Mali, Burkina Faso, Niger and Chad to Sudan. Roughly 200–600 mm of rain in one short summer wet season when the ITCZ moves north; highly unreliable from year to year. _(B2, D5)_
- **Sahel: human causes of desertification** — Population growth meant more livestock around wells and boreholes, shorter fallow and more fuelwood cutting; cash crops (groundnuts in Senegal, cotton) pushed food crops and herders onto marginal land; nomads settled. In the droughts of the 1970s and 1980s, wind and rain eroded the bare soil. _(B2, OC4)_
- **Lake Chad: what has happened and why** — On the Sahel's border of Chad, Niger, Nigeria and Cameroon, it has shrunk dramatically since the 1960s (by about 90%, a widely quoted UN figure) through drought and water use for irrigation. _(B2)_
- **International responses to desertification: UNCCD and the Great Green Wall** — UNCCD adopted 1994 (17 June is World Day to Combat Desertification). Great Green Wall: launched by the African Union in 2007, about 8,000 km from Senegal to Djibouti, aiming to restore 100 million hectares by 2030; progress is slow and well short of target. _(B2, CM12)_
- **Zaï pits and stone lines: where they were revived and how they work** — In the Yatenga region of northern Burkina Faso, farmers such as Yacouba Sawadogo revived zaï: pits in crusted soil, filled with manure, that catch rain and concentrate nutrients. Stone lines along the contours slow runoff and trap soil. _(CM9)_
- **Farmer-managed natural regeneration in Niger: method and results** — Farmers protect and prune shoots regrowing from old stumps and roots; about 5 million hectares regreened since the 1980s, especially in Maradi and Zinder. Trees shade the soil, cut wind and add litter; Faidherbia albida adds nitrogen. _(CM9, CM12)_
- **Other negative human impacts on soil: five to name** — Deforestation exposes soil and stops litter; machinery and livestock on wet ground compact it; excess fertiliser and slurry wash nitrates and phosphates into water (eutrophication); bad irrigation salinises and waterlogs it (Punjab, Haryana); roads seal it. _(N1, N2, N3, N4, N6)_
- **Terracing: how it conserves soil and a named example** — Steep slopes are cut into level steps that hold soil and water instead of letting them run downhill; the rice terraces of Banaue, Luzon, Philippines, are over 2,000 years old. _(CM2)_
- **Soil conservation on farmland: methods that restore fertility and protect soil cover** — Crop rotation, fallow, cover crops and green manure such as clover restore nutrients and humus; manure and compost build humus; zero tillage and mulching leave residue on the surface; controlled stocking, rotational grazing and afforestation let cover recover. _(CM5, CM6, CM7, CM8)_
- **Improving Irish soils: four methods with examples** — Liming raises the pH of acid soils; field and arterial drainage improve gleys (Arterial Drainage Act, 1945); deep ploughing breaks a podzol's iron pan; on the Aran Islands soil was made by layering sand and seaweed on bare limestone inside stone walls. _(CM10)_
- **Irish agri-environment schemes and EU rules: names and dates** — REPS from 1994; GLAS (Green, Low-Carbon, Agri-Environment Scheme) from 2015; ACRES (Agri-Climate Rural Environment Scheme) from 2023. The EU Nitrates Directive (1991) limits organic nitrogen per hectare and bans slurry spreading in a closed winter period. _(CM11)_
- **The Burren: setting, soils and plants** — North-west Co. Clare, on Carboniferous limestone with extensive pavement of clints and grikes; thin, dark, lime-rich rendzinas mainly in grikes and hollows; Arctic-alpine and Mediterranean plants such as spring gentian and mountain avens grow side by side. _(B1)_
- **The Burren: how early farming is thought to have exposed the pavement** — Poulnabrone portal tomb, over 5,000 years old, shows early farming. It is thought the Burren once had thicker soil and woodland; clearance and grazing, then erosion of soil into the grikes and underground, helped expose the bare pavement. _(B1)_
- **The Burren: why undergrazing is now the main threat** — Winterage keeps grass and scrub down, as the rock stores heat and the ground is dry. From the late 20th century fewer farmers grazed the uplands in winter (farm changes, supplementary feeding, labour shortage), so hazel and blackthorn scrub spread. _(B1)_
- **BurrenLIFE and the Burren Programme: how results-based payments work** — BurrenLIFE (2005–2010), an EU project led by Dr Brendan Dunford with local farmers and Teagasc, tested scrub removal and grazing plans. The Burren Programme (from 2016) scores each field for habitat quality, e.g. 1 to 10, and pays more for higher scores. _(B1, CM12)_
- **Iceland: how settlement started soil erosion** — Settled by Norse people from about AD 874, when birch woodland covered about a quarter or more of the island; today about 1–2%. Settlers cut birch for fuel, charcoal and grazing, and year-round sheep grazing prevented regrowth. _(B3)_
- **Iceland's soils: why they erode so easily** — They are andosols from volcanic ash: light, loose and low in clay, so once vegetation is broken wind and rain strip them, leaving erosion escarpments (rofabörð). Eruptions smother vegetation with ash, and the cold climate means slow recovery. _(B3)_
- **Iceland's soil conservation: the service and its methods** — Founded 1907 (Landgræðslan), one of the oldest services. Fencing out sheep; lyme grass on drifting sand; aerial sowing of grass with fertiliser; replanting birch; Farmers Heal the Land from 1990; Nootka lupin fixes nitrogen but is invasive. _(B3, CM12)_

### 4. One major biome in detail (27)

- **Hot desert biome: location and named deserts** — Under 250 mm of rain a year, about 15–30° N and S, mainly on west sides and in continental interiors. The Sahara (about 9 million km²) is the largest; others are the Arabian, Thar, Namib, Atacama, Sonoran, Mojave, Great Victoria and Simpson Deserts. _(A1)_
- **Hot desert: five causes of aridity** — Subtropical high pressure, where sinking air warms so clouds cannot form; offshore trade winds from the dry interior; cold ocean currents that make the air stable, giving fog not rain; rain shadow behind mountains; continentality, far from the sea. _(A2)_
- **Hot deserts: match each to its cold current or rain shadow** — Canary Current: western Sahara. Benguela Current: Namib. Humboldt Current: Atacama. California Current: Baja California and the Sonoran coast. The Andes shelter the Atacama; the Sierra Nevada shelters the Mojave, including Death Valley. _(A2)_
- **Hot desert temperature: characteristics and the world record** — Summer days often exceed 40 °C; clear, dry air gives a diurnal range of 20–30 °C or more, with winter nights near freezing. Record: 56.7 °C at Furnace Creek, Death Valley, 10 July 1913 (WMO); the 58 °C El Azizia reading was rejected in 2012. _(A3)_
- **Hot desert rainfall: characteristics and two extreme deserts** — Rain is scarce and unreliable, often in short, intense convectional downpours causing flash floods; evaporation far exceeds rainfall. The Atacama is the driest non-polar desert; the Namib gets much of its moisture as fog from the cold Benguela Current. _(A4)_
- **Aridisols: characteristics and when they become fertile** — Thin and immature (slow, mainly physical weathering); coarse, with little humus, so pale grey, yellow or red; little leaching, so capillary rise leaves salt crusts and calcrete; alkaline. Fertile when irrigated, as in the Nile valley and the Imperial Valley. _(A6)_
- **Saguaro cactus: where it grows, three adaptations and its size and age** — Only in the Sonoran Desert (Arizona and Sonora, Mexico). Its pleated stem expands to store water after rain; spines replace leaves; shallow roots spread widely just below the surface; it can grow over 12 m tall and live over 150 years. _(A8)_
- **Desert plants: four ways leaves and stems cut water loss** — Spines in place of leaves, which also deter animals; a thick, waxy cuticle; few, sunken stomata that in many succulents open only at night (CAM photosynthesis); small or light-coloured leaves, or leaves shed in drought. _(A8)_
- **Desert plants: root and life-cycle strategies, with named plants** — Mesquite tap roots reach tens of metres to groundwater; acacias have deep roots, small leaves and thorns; ephemerals bloom within weeks of rain, as in the Atacama's flowering desert in wet El Niño years and Death Valley's superbloom of 2016. _(A8)_
- **Welwitschia and creosote bush: what makes each special** — Welwitschia mirabilis (Namib): only two leaves, which grow throughout its life; absorbs fog moisture; some are thought to be over 1,000 years old. Creosote bush (Mojave, Sonoran): evenly spaced plants whose roots and chemicals limit competitors. _(A7, A8)_
- **Dromedary camel: five adaptations to the desert** — Its hump stores fat, not water; it can go days without drinking, then drink over 100 litres in minutes; its body temperature rises by day to save sweat; long eyelashes and closable nostrils keep out sand; broad, padded feet spread its weight. _(A9)_
- **Fennec fox and kangaroo rat: how each survives the desert** — Fennec fox (Sahara): the smallest fox; very large ears radiate heat; nocturnal; burrows; furry soles for hot sand. Kangaroo rat (North America): gets water from seeds and its metabolism, has very concentrated urine, and seals its burrow by day. _(A9)_
- **Desert animals: three ways to get or save water** — The Namib fog-basking beetle stands head-down on dunes at dawn so fog runs to its mouth; the thorny devil lizard's skin grooves channel water to its mouth; the spadefoot toad estivates underground and its tadpoles develop before pools dry up. _(A9)_
- **Hot desert: biotic inter-relationships with named species** — Lesser long-nosed bats pollinate saguaro and agave flowers at night; Gila woodpeckers dig nest holes in saguaros that elf owls later use; food chains are short, and seed-eating rodents are prey for snakes, owls and foxes. _(A10)_
- **Tabernas Desert: Europe's example of a desert** — In Almería province, Andalusia, south-east Spain: semi-arid badlands in the rain shadow of surrounding mountains, often called Europe's only desert; a film set for spaghetti westerns in the 1960s and 1970s. _(A11)_
- **Tropical rainforest biome: location and main regions** — Mainly within about 10° of the equator. The Amazon Basin is largest (about 5.5 million km², roughly 60% in Brazil); then the Congo Basin; then South-East Asia (Indonesia, Malaysia, Papua New Guinea); smaller areas include the Daintree, Queensland. _(B1)_
- **Rainforest biodiversity: key figures** — Rainforests cover only about 6% of the earth's land but are widely estimated to hold over half of its land plant and animal species. Hundreds of tree species can grow in one hectare of Amazon forest, against a handful in an Irish wood. _(B1, B7)_
- **Rainforest temperature: figures and why it barely changes** — Monthly means about 26–28 °C, an annual range of only about 2–3 °C, because the sun is always high (overhead at the equinoxes in March and September) and day length barely changes. The daily range exceeds the annual range; there is no summer or winter season. _(B2)_
- **Rainforest rainfall: amount and the daily convection cycle** — Over 2,000 mm a year (Manaus about 2,300 mm). Morning heating makes moist air rise; it cools and cumulonimbus build; heavy afternoon thunderstorms follow almost daily, driven by the ITCZ. Humidity is often over 80% and much rain is recycled from the forest. _(B3)_
- **Rainforest nutrient cycle: how lush forest grows on infertile soil** — Latosols are acidic with little humus, but litter decomposes within weeks, and shallow roots and mycorrhizal fungi take up nutrients almost at once. Most nutrients are held in the living vegetation, so the cycle is rapid and closed. _(B4)_
- **Rainforest layers: heights and features of each** — Emergents about 45–60 m or more (kapok, Brazil nut, dipterocarps); dense canopy about 25–40 m, holding most animal life; understorey up to about 15 m; shrub layer; dark forest floor getting about 2% of the sunlight that reaches the canopy. _(B5)_
- **Rainforest trees: five adaptations to the climate and soil** — Buttress roots support tall, shallow-rooted trees such as kapok and Brazil nut; shallow roots catch nutrients from litter; drip tips and waxy leaves shed heavy rain; large understorey leaves catch dim light; thin, smooth bark, as no insulation is needed. _(B6)_
- **Rainforest climbers, epiphytes and plants of poor soils: named examples** — Lianas climb trees to the light; epiphytes such as orchids and bromeliads grow on branches; strangler figs enclose their host; pitcher plants (Nepenthes) digest insects; Rafflesia arnoldii, a parasite, has the largest flower, about 1 m across. _(B6)_
- **Rainforest canopy animals: adaptations of five named species** — Spider and howler monkeys: prehensile tails as a fifth limb. Three-toed sloth: slow metabolism suits a leaf diet; algae in its fur camouflage it. Toucan: large, light bill reaches fruit. Orangutan (Borneo, Sumatra only): largest tree-living mammal, long arms. _(B8)_
- **Rainforest animals: camouflage, warning colours and predators** — Poison dart frogs' bright colours warn of toxic skin; the jaguar, the largest cat in the Americas, has a spotted, camouflaged coat and swims strongly; the okapi of the Congo has striped legs for camouflage; the harpy eagle hunts monkeys and sloths. _(B8)_
- **Rainforest mutualism: three named examples** — Azteca ants live in Cecropia trees, which give shelter and food, and attack herbivores and climbers; leafcutter ants grow the fungus they eat; the Brazil nut needs orchid bees to pollinate it and agoutis to bury its nuts, so it yields poorly in plantations. _(B9)_
- **Rainforest peoples: three named groups and how they used the forest** — The Yanomami (Brazil–Venezuela border), Kayapó (Brazilian Amazon) and Mbuti (Ituri forest, Democratic Republic of the Congo). Hunting, gathering and shifting cultivation at low density let the forest and soil recover. _(B10)_

### 5. Human alteration of biomes (28)

- **Neolithic farmers: how they began altering Ireland's forest** — By about 6,000 years ago forest covered almost all of Ireland. Farming arrived then (about 4000 BC), clearing forest with stone axes and fire for crops and grazing. The elm decline in pollen dates from this time (farmers and elm disease both played a part). _(E1, E2)_
- **Céide Fields: what they show and how they were found** — North Co. Mayo: stone-walled Neolithic fields about 5,500 years old, among the oldest known field systems, later buried as clearance, a wetter climate and leaching turned forest soil to blanket bog. Patrick Caulfield found the walls cutting turf in the 1930s. _(E3)_
- **Ireland's great forest clearance: when, why and the result** — In the 16th and 17th centuries (Tudor conquest and plantations), woods were felled for timber exports, shipbuilding, barrel staves and charcoal for ironworks, and to remove cover for Irish resistance. About 1% of Ireland was forested by 1900; now about 11%. _(E4)_
- **Forest clearance in Ireland: three results for the biome** — Native deciduous forest was replaced by farmland, hedgerows, bog and grassland; woodland species declined, and the wolf was hunted to extinction (the last in the late 1700s); soils under heath were leached and podzolised, and blanket bog spread on the uplands. _(E5)_
- **Irish forests today: conifer plantations and native remnants** — State afforestation mostly uses Sitka spruce, native to western North America, giving species-poor plantations that acidify soils and water. Native remnants include the oakwoods of Killarney National Park and the Reenadinna yew wood on the Muckross peninsula. _(E6)_
- **Mediterranean forest clearance: causes and results** — Since ancient times, felling for shipbuilding, fuel and farming, plus goat grazing, degraded evergreen oak and pine forest to maquis and garrigue, with soil erosion. The cedars of Lebanon survive only in small groves such as the Cedars of God (UNESCO). _(E7)_
- **Amazon deforestation: INPE's figures and their trend** — Measured by satellite (PRODES): a peak of about 27,800 km² in 2004; a fall to about 4,600 km² in 2012; a rise to about 13,000 km² in 2021; a fall again from 2023 under renewed enforcement. Close to a fifth of the original forest has been cleared. _(F1)_
- **Amazon clearance: cattle, soya and logging** — Cattle ranching is the largest single cause, accounting for most cleared land; soya spread north from Mato Grosso, much of it exported as animal feed to Europe and China; selective logging of hardwoods opens roads and canopy gaps that lead to more clearance. _(F2)_
- **Amazon roads: two named roads and their effects** — The Trans-Amazonian Highway (BR-230), begun in the early 1970s by the military government, brought settlers from the drought-prone north-east. BR-364 into Rondônia, paved in the 1980s with World Bank support, left a fishbone pattern of clearings. _(F2)_
- **Amazon mining and dams: three named examples** — Carajás, Pará: a huge iron-ore mine (Vale, 1980s) whose pig-iron smelters burned forest charcoal; illegal gold mining (garimpo) on Yanomami land pollutes rivers with mercury; the Belo Monte dam on the Xingu (2019) flooded forest and displaced thousands. _(F2)_
- **Rainforest felling: impacts on plants, animals and soils** — Loss of habitat and many endemic species; fragmentation, with drier, hotter edge effects; the nutrient cycle breaks, so cleared latosols lose fertility within a few years and pasture is abandoned; soil erosion and laterite crust; rivers silt up. _(F3)_
- **Amazon felling: effects on climate and the tipping point** — Less evapotranspiration means less rain downwind, longer dry seasons and more fires; burning and decay release stored carbon as carbon dioxide. Carlos Nobre and Thomas Lovejoy warn that losing about 20–25% could tip parts of the forest to degraded savanna. _(F3)_
- **Amazon felling: impacts on indigenous and forest peoples** — Loss of land, disease, and violence from ranchers and miners. Chico Mendes, a rubber tapper and union leader, was murdered at Xapuri, Acre, on 22 December 1988; in 2023 Brazil declared a health emergency in Yanomami territory after illegal gold mining. _(F4)_
- **Amazon protection: five responses and the lesson they teach** — The Forest Code (keep 80% of land as forest; revised 2012); the Soy Moratorium (2006); the Amazon Fund (2008; Norway the largest donor); INPE satellite alerts guiding IBAMA; extractive reserves. Changing clearance rates show success depends on enforcement. _(F5)_
- **South-East Asia: palm oil and its impacts on the rainforest** — Indonesia and Malaysia produce about 85% of the world's palm oil. Rainforest and peat swamp in Borneo and Sumatra are cleared; burning drained peat caused haze in 1997 and 2015 over Singapore and Malaysia; the Bornean orangutan is Critically Endangered (2016). _(F6)_
- **Europe's link to rainforest felling** — European demand for beef, soya as animal feed, timber and palm oil drives felling. The EU Deforestation Regulation (adopted 2023) will make firms prove such products were not grown on land deforested after 2020; its application has been delayed. _(F7)_
- **Intensive dairying and tillage in Ireland: impacts on the biome** — After milk quotas ended (1 April 2015) herds grew; hedgerows were removed, wetlands and callows drained, meadows reseeded with ryegrass; slurry and fertiliser raise river nitrates; silage cutting destroys corncrake nests; bees and farmland birds decline. _(A2)_
- **Common Agricultural Policy: how it intensified farming** — Begun in 1962, its guaranteed prices encouraged hedgerow removal, larger fields and wetland drainage, notably in East Anglia; food surpluses (butter mountains, wine lakes) followed in the 1980s; reforms from 1992 began paying for environmental farming. _(A3)_
- **Aral Sea: why it shrank** — On the Kazakhstan–Uzbekistan border; the world's fourth-largest lake in 1960. From the 1960s Soviet planners diverted its feeder rivers, the Amu Darya and Syr Darya, to irrigate cotton and rice, so it shrank and split: irrigation, not climate change. _(A5)_
- **Aral Sea: effects and a partial recovery** — Salinity rose and fishing collapsed, leaving Moynaq far from shore with ships stranded; dust storms carry salt and pesticides onto farms and people; irrigated soils were salinised. The Kok-Aral Dam (2005) raised the North Aral Sea; fishing partly revived. _(A5)_
- **Acid rain in Scandinavia: cause and effects on lakes** — Sulphur dioxide and nitrogen oxides from British and Central European industry were carried across borders; from the 1960s to the 1980s thousands of lakes in southern Norway and Sweden were acidified, aluminium was released and fish died out in many. _(D1)_
- **Black Triangle: where it is and what happened to its forests** — Where Germany, Poland and the Czech Republic meet, lignite-burning power stations caused acid rain; large areas of spruce forest in the Ore Mountains (Erzgebirge) died in the 1970s and 1980s (Waldsterben), with needle loss, dieback and soil leaching. _(D1)_
- **Acid rain in Europe: responses and results** — The Convention on Long-Range Transboundary Air Pollution (1979); EU power-station emission limits; flue-gas desulphurisation; liming of lakes in Sweden and Norway. Sulphur dioxide emissions fell by well over half; many forests and lakes partly recovered. _(D1)_
- **Bord na Móna: its industrial peat harvesting and how it ended** — A state company set up in 1946, it harvested milled peat on a large scale for power stations and briquettes. The Shannonbridge and Lanesborough peat stations closed in December 2020, and peat harvesting ended in 2021 under its Brown to Green plan. _(D2)_
- **Irish bogs after industrial peat cutting: two named sites** — Cutaway bogs are being rehabilitated and rewetted: Lough Boora Discovery Park, Co. Offaly, is cutaway bog turned into wetland, woodland and amenity land. Clara Bog, Co. Offaly, is one of the largest relatively intact raised bogs, now a nature reserve. _(D2, V1)_
- **Athabasca oil sands: impacts on the boreal biome** — Around Fort McMurray, northern Alberta, Canada, open-pit mining strips away boreal forest and muskeg (peatland) to reach bitumen-soaked sand, leaving large tailings ponds and high emissions per barrel; little mined land has been certified as reclaimed. _(D3)_
- **Niger Delta: how oil has damaged the biome** — Oil has been produced since the late 1950s (Shell and others); pipeline spills and theft polluted creeks, mangroves and farmland. A 2011 UNEP study of Ogoniland said a clean-up could take 25 to 30 years. Campaigner Ken Saro-Wiwa was executed in 1995. _(D3)_
