- **Chernobyl: how the 1986 accident altered the biome** — The nuclear plant in Ukraine (then the USSR) exploded on 26 April 1986; radiation killed a pine forest, the Red Forest. A 30 km exclusion zone was evacuated; without people, wolves, elk, wild boar and reintroduced Przewalski's horses have become common. _(D4)_

## Option global interdependence

### 1. Views of development and underdevelopment (25)

- **How views of development have changed: the eras in order** — Colonial civilising mission; 1950s–60s modernisation (Rostow); 1960s–70s dependency theory; 1970s basic needs; 1980s–90s Washington Consensus (Williamson, 1989); sustainable development from 1987; human development from 1990; then goal- and rights-based views. _(V2)_
- **Underdevelopment: its two opposite meanings** — In the modernisation view it means 'not yet developed', a country behind on the same path. In the dependency view it means 'made poor by others', the result of being drained by the rich core. _(V4)_
- **Environmental determinism: what it claimed, who argued it, how it was used** — It claimed that climate decides how far a society can develop: temperate climates were said to make 'advanced' peoples, tropical ones poor. Ellen Churchill Semple (1911) and Ellsworth Huntington, Civilization and Climate (1915). It justified colonialism and racial hierarchies; now discredited. _(D1, D2)_
- **Environmental determinism: four criticisms and the possibilist reply** — It ignores colonialism, slavery, trade rules and choice; tropical Singapore is rich, and landlocked Botswana reached upper-middle income through diamonds and stable government; temperate places can be poor; it is racist. Possibilism (Paul Vidal de la Blache): the environment offers choices. _(D3, D4)_
- **Rostow's five stages of growth in order** — Traditional society (subsistence farming); preconditions for take-off (transport, education, trade); take-off (investment over 10% of national income, a few leading industries); drive to maturity; high mass consumption. Rostow, 1960. _(M2)_
- **Rostow's model applied to Ireland: a textbook reading** — Traditional: net emigration about 400,000 in 1951 to 1961. Preconditions: IDA (1949), Whitaker's programme (1958). Take-off: 1960s–70s industry, free secondary education (1967), EEC (1973). Maturity: MNC growth. Mass consumption: Celtic Tiger. 2008: reversal. _(M3)_
- **Rostow's model applied to South Korea** — The Korean War (1950–53) left it among the poorest countries; Park Chung-hee planned export-led industry from the 1960s; chaebol such as Samsung, Hyundai and LG and heavy education spending drove growth; OECD 1996; UNCTAD reclassified it as developed in 2021. _(M4)_
- **Modernisation theory: five criticisms** — Eurocentric, treating the Western path as the only one; linear, yet countries slip back (Zimbabwe's hyperinflation peaked in 2008; Syria's war from 2011); ignores colonialism and unequal trade; wealth often does not trickle down; mass consumption is unsustainable. _(M5)_
- **Dependency theory: who developed it and their key arguments** — Raúl Prebisch, head of ECLA (set up 1948), argued with Hans Singer (1950) that primary exporters' terms of trade decline. Andre Gunder Frank (1966): Latin America was underdeveloped by metropolis–satellite links to the core. _(P1, P2)_
- **Dependency theory: seven ways the core drains the periphery** — Colonial extraction and slavery; dependence on one or two low-priced, unstable primary exports; tariff escalation and rich-country subsidies; multinationals sending profits home; debt repayments; brain drain; local elites who gain from the link. _(P3)_
- **Dependency theory: four examples of dependent economies** — Brazil's colonial export cycles (brazilwood, sugar, gold, coffee, rubber); Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana grow over half the world's cocoa, mostly sold as beans; Zambia relies on copper; Sudan's Gezira Scheme (from 1925) grew cotton for Lancashire. _(P4)_
- **Wallerstein's world-systems theory: three zones and movement** — Wallerstein, The Modern World-System (1974): the core controls high-value production and finance; the periphery supplies cheap materials and labour; the semi-periphery lies between. Countries move up: South Korea; China (reforms 1978, WTO 2001). _(P5)_
- **Dependency theory: four criticisms** — It cannot explain how the Asian Tigers and China grew by trading with the core; some countries that cut links through self-reliance stayed poor; it blames outside forces and can excuse corruption and poor government; withdrawal from world trade has rarely worked. _(P6)_
- **Basic needs and appropriate technology: names and dates** — Basic needs (1970s): food, water, shelter, health and education before GNP, made central by the ILO World Employment Conference (1976). E. F. Schumacher (Small Is Beautiful, 1973) founded what is now Practical Action in 1966, for appropriate technology. _(A1, A2)_
- **Human development and other measures of well-being** — Amartya Sen, Development as Freedom (1999): development widens choices and capabilities; first Human Development Report, 1990. Bhutan's Gross National Happiness (1970s). Hans Rosling's Factfulness (2018): four income levels, not developed/developing. _(A3, A6)_
- **Negative media images of Africa: five effects** — Famine, war and helpless victims dominate. Effects: stereotypes such as 'Africa is a country'; donors see themselves as saviours; the people shown may see themselves as helpless; investors and tourists lose confidence; audiences tire, causing donor fatigue. _(I1, I3)_
- **Ethiopian famine 1984–85: how coverage shaped views of Africa** — Michael Buerk's BBC report (October 1984) led to Band Aid's single (1984) and Live Aid (July 1985), organised by Bob Geldof, raising huge sums. But it fixed an image of a helpless Africa and hid the causes: civil war in the north and government policy. _(I2)_
- **Three responses that challenge negative images of Africa** — The Dóchas Code of Conduct on Images and Messages, signed by Irish NGOs: respect dignity, avoid stereotypes, give context. Binyavanga Wainaina's satire How to Write About Africa (2005). Chimamanda Ngozi Adichie's talk The Danger of a Single Story (2009). _(I4)_
- **Three counter-images from sub-Saharan Africa** — Rwanda rebuilt after the 1994 genocide (about 800,000 people, mostly Tutsi, killed in about 100 days): since 2013 women have held over 60% of lower-house seats, the world's highest share. Kenya's M-Pesa (2007, Safaricom) lets people bank by text. Botswana: stable, diamond-funded growth. _(I5)_
- **Third World: who coined it, its Cold War meaning, why it is outdated** — Alfred Sauvy (1952), after the Third Estate: non-aligned former colonies outside the USA-led First and Soviet Second World (Bandung 1955; Non-Aligned Movement 1961). Outdated: the Soviet Union ended in 1991; it became a ranking; it lumps Singapore with Niger. _(F1, F2)_
- **North–South divide: how the South has risen** — The Asian Tigers (South Korea, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore) are rich; China is second-largest, India top five. BRIC, coined in 2001 (summit 2009), added South Africa (2011), then Egypt, Ethiopia, Iran and the United Arab Emirates (2024) and Indonesia (2025). _(F4)_
- **North–South divide: three more reasons it is outdated** — The G20 (1999) includes China, India, Brazil, South Africa, Indonesia, Mexico and Saudi Arabia; most people now live in middle-income countries (Rosling); in 2016 the World Bank stopped dividing countries into developing and developed in its main data tables. _(F4)_
- **North–South divide: why the idea is still partly valid** — The income, health and power gap between most of Africa and Europe remains huge. Global wealth is very unequal: the richest 10% of people own about 76%, the poorest half about 2% (World Inequality Report 2022). Four of the five permanent Security Council seats are Northern: USA, UK, France, Russia. _(F5)_
- **Eurocentric maps: three examples and one challenge** — Mercator's 1569 projection, made for navigation, puts Europe top centre; Africa is about 14 times Greenland's area yet looks similar. Greenwich became the prime meridian (1884); 'Middle East' and 'Far East' are named from Europe. Challenge: Peters' equal-area projection (1973). _(E2)_
- **Eurocentric thinking in history and development: examples** — Columbus's 1492 'discovery' of inhabited lands; European powers set the rules for claiming Africa at the Berlin Conference (1884–85); Said's Orientalism (1978) on the East shown as backward; 'developed' meant becoming like Western Europe. China's Belt and Road (2013) marks a shift. _(E3, E4, E5)_

### 2. An interdependent global economy (23)

- **Apple's iPhone chain: where each stage takes place** — Designed in Cupertino, California; chips from TSMC (Taiwan); screens from Samsung and LG (South Korea); cobalt mostly from the DR Congo; assembly mainly by Taiwanese Foxconn at Zhengzhou and Shenzhen, China, and by Foxconn and Tata in India. _(T2)_
- **Apple: who gains most, and how consumer regions are affected** — A 2011 study (Kraemer, Linden, Dedrick) estimated Apple kept about 58% of the 2010 iPhone's price as profit, Chinese assembly labour under 2%. Profits and design and marketing jobs stay in the USA; consumers get new technology; e-waste goes to poorer countries. _(T3, T4, T1)_
- **Apple: three benefits for producer regions** — Assembly and supplier firms employ millions of people; China gains skills, industrial growth and export earnings; supplier firms such as TSMC and Foxconn grow on the strength of Apple's orders. _(T3)_
- **Apple: four problems for producer regions** — Foxconn worker suicides in Shenzhen (2010) exposed long hours and pressure; Zhengzhou workers protested over pay and lockdown (November 2022), delaying iPhones worldwide; orders can move to India; child labour in the DR Congo's cobalt mines (Amnesty, 2016). _(T3)_
- **Apple in Cork: when it came and what it does there** — Apple, founded in 1976 by Steve Jobs and Steve Wozniak, opened at Hollyhill, Cork, in 1980, its first manufacturing plant in Europe. About 6,000 now work there in its European headquarters and support centre; Cork does not assemble iPhones. _(T5, T2)_
- **Apple: two decisions elsewhere that affected Ireland and Apple** — In 2016 the European Commission ruled Ireland gave Apple €13 billion in illegal State aid (not a fine); overturned in 2020, it was reinstated in September 2024, so Ireland got about €14 billion. US tariffs (2018, 2025) pushed Apple to assemble more in India. _(T5)_
- **Cadbury and Mondelēz: ownership history and Irish plants** — Founded by John Cadbury in Birmingham in 1824; bought by Kraft Foods in 2010; part of Mondelēz International, Chicago, since 2012. Irish plants: Coolock, Dublin (chocolate) and Rathmore, Co. Kerry (crumb from local milk for factories in Britain). _(T6)_
- **Cocoa producer regions: where cocoa grows and who adds the value** — Côte d'Ivoire grows about two-fifths of world cocoa and Ghana is second: together over half, mostly on small family farms, exported as beans. The value is added mostly outside Africa: the Netherlands (Amsterdam) is the world's largest cocoa-processing centre. _(T7)_
- **Cocoa: child labour and deforestation in producer regions** — A 2020 NORC study for the US Department of Labor found about 1.56 million children in cocoa child labour in Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana; Harkin–Engel Protocol (2001) deadlines were missed. Côte d'Ivoire has lost most of its forest since 1960, much to cocoa. _(T7)_
- **Cocoa's record price in 2024: why farmers gained little** — Poor West African harvests, from bad weather and the cocoa swollen shoot virus, pushed prices to a record above $10,000 a tonne. Farmers in Côte d'Ivoire and Ghana gained far less, because their governments fix farm-gate prices in advance. _(T7)_
- **Cadbury's responses to cocoa problems, and the criticism** — Cadbury Dairy Milk carried the Fairtrade Mark from 2009. Mondelēz began its own Cocoa Life programme in 2012 (about $400 million over ten years); in 2017 Dairy Milk in Britain and Ireland swapped Fairtrade for the Cocoa Life logo, criticised by campaigners. _(T7)_
- **Chocolate: consumer regions and how they pressure producers** — Europe and North America eat most chocolate; brands, marketing and retail take most of the price. The EU Deforestation Regulation (2023, delayed) will make firms prove cocoa, soya, palm oil and similar goods were not grown on land deforested after 2020. _(T8)_
- **Managed forests in Sweden and Finland: how they stay sustainable** — Forest covers about two-thirds of Sweden and three-quarters of Finland. Sweden's 1903 Forestry Act made owners replant; the 1993 Act balanced timber and nature. Growth exceeds felling, so standing volume has doubled since the 1920s. Buyers demand FSC or PEFC. _(D4)_
- **Scandinavian forestry: exports and criticisms** — Sweden and Finland are among the leading exporters of pulp, paper and sawn timber (UPM, Stora Enso); Swedish IKEA is a major wood buyer. Critics: clear-felling and replanting with Norway spruce and Scots pine creates monocultures, removes old-growth forest and destroys lichen Sámi reindeer graze. _(D4)_
- **Pakistan floods 2022: global warming's unequal impact** — Extreme monsoon rains and glacier melt flooded about a third of the country; over 1,700 people died and about 33 million were affected. Pakistan produces under 1% of global greenhouse gas emissions, so it suffers from emissions made elsewhere. _(G2)_
- **The Rohingya: how political decisions made them stateless refugees** — Myanmar's 1982 Citizenship Law left most stateless; a 2017 army campaign drove about 740,000 into Bangladesh, where about 1 million live around Cox's Bazar. The Gambia sued Myanmar at the International Court of Justice (2019); the 2021 coup ended return hopes. _(R4)_
- **Ukraine 2022: the EU decision on temporary protection** — After Russia invaded (24 February 2022) the EU activated its Temporary Protection Directive for the first time, on 4 March 2022. Over 4 million held it in the EU and over 100,000 came to Ireland (2024). Asylum seekers from Africa and the Middle East get no such status: an equality question. _(R5)_
- **Global migration: scale and four main corridors** — About 281 million international migrants in 2020, 3.6% of the world's people, most within their own region. Corridors: Mexico–USA; North and West Africa–Europe; South Asia–the Gulf; East to West Europe after 2004, when Ireland opened its labour market at once. _(M1, M2)_
- **Gulf migration: who goes, the kafala system and Qatar's reforms** — Migrants, mainly from India, Nepal, Bangladesh, Pakistan and the Philippines, make up most of the people of Qatar and the United Arab Emirates; kafala ties them to an employer. Qatar's 2020 reforms (minimum wage, job mobility) followed World Cup criticism, but enforcement is criticised. _(M2)_
- **Atlantic and Mediterranean migration routes: key facts** — Migrants from Senegal, Mauritania and Mali sail to the Canary Islands, with record arrivals in 2024; Senegalese fishermen blame foreign trawlers and fishmeal plants for falling catches (a reported cause). Over 360 drowned off Lampedusa in October 2013. _(M2)_
- **Migration: effects on source and receiving regions** — Source: about $650 billion in remittances to low- and middle-income countries (2023), India the largest recipient and Mexico second; brain drain of doctors and nurses. Receiving: workers for health care, building and farming; younger populations; housing pressure; anti-immigration parties. _(M3, M4)_
- **Xinjiang: forced labour and the US import ban** — UN Human Rights Office (2022): serious violations against Uyghurs, including mass detention. There is also evidence of forced labour in cotton; Xinjiang grows about a fifth of world cotton. The US Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act (in force June 2022) bans Xinjiang goods unless proven clean. _(H2)_
- **Dunnes Stores strike: one worker's decision and its effect** — In July 1984 Mary Manning, at Dunnes Stores, Henry Street, Dublin, refused to handle South African grapes in protest at apartheid; she and a few colleagues struck for almost three years. In 1987 Ireland banned South African fruit and vegetable imports. _(H3)_

### 3. Empowering people: linking economic growth with human development (30)

- **Zambia's debt story: adjustment, relief and default** — Copper is most of its exports; its state mines (ZCCM) were privatised in the late 1990s under reform conditions. HIPC completion point 2005; rural health fees abolished 2006. It defaulted in November 2020 and restructured under the G20 Common Framework. _(D3, D4, D5)_
- **HIPC debt relief: results in Uganda and Tanzania** — Uganda introduced Universal Primary Education in 1997 and in 1998 became the first country to receive HIPC relief, spending the savings on schools, health and water. Tanzania abolished primary school fees in 2001. _(D4)_
- **Debt crises of the 2020s: Sri Lanka, Ghana and the UNCTAD figure** — Sri Lanka defaulted in 2022; amid shortages and protests President Gotabaya Rajapaksa fled (July); IMF help (about $2.9 billion) followed. Ghana defaulted in December 2022. UNCTAD: 3.3 billion people live where interest exceeds health or education spending. _(D5)_
- **Hambantota port and the debt-trap debate** — Built in Sri Lanka with Chinese loans, it was leased to a Chinese state company for 99 years in 2017. Critics call it a Belt and Road (2013) debt trap; others argue Sri Lanka's debts were mostly owed to Western lenders and bondholders. _(D5)_
- **Aid cuts by big donors: two recent examples** — The UK cut aid from 0.7% to 0.5% of GNI in 2021 and in 2025 announced a cut to 0.3% by 2027. In 2025 the USA, until then the largest single donor by volume, cancelled most USAID programmes and folded the agency into the State Department. _(A2)_
- **How aid benefits recipients: five ways** — Emergency relief saves lives; schools, clinics, wells and vaccination; training; support for women's groups and small businesses; infrastructure. _(A3)_
- **Who benefits from aid: three ways donors gain** — Tied aid returns money to donor firms: UK aid for Malaysia's Pergau Dam, linked to an arms deal, was ruled unlawful by the High Court in 1994 as not for sound development. US food aid must largely be bought from US farmers and shipped on US ships. Aid also wins allies. _(A4)_
- **How aid can harm recipients: the Haiti rice example and others** — In the mid-1990s Haiti cut its rice tariff under lender pressure; cheap subsidised US rice ruined many Haitian farmers, and in 2010 Bill Clinton apologised for the policy. Aid can also be captured by elites, breed dependency, add to debt and displace people. _(A5)_
- **The aid debate: three named authors and their arguments** — Jeffrey Sachs, The End of Poverty (2005): more aid can end extreme poverty. Dambisa Moyo, Dead Aid (2009): aid breeds corruption and dependency in Africa. William Easterly, The White Man's Burden (2006): top-down planners fail; bottom-up searchers succeed. _(A6)_
- **EU aid programmes: named parts and one criticism** — Team Europe gives over two-fifths of world aid; Lomé (1975), Cotonou (2000) and Samoa (2023) deals with African, Caribbean and Pacific states; ECHO (1992); Global Gateway (2021), a rival to China's Belt and Road. EU farm and fishing policies have at times harmed the same states. _(A8)_
- **Irish aid programme: who runs it, since when, and its priorities** — Irish Aid (Department of Foreign Affairs) began bilateral aid in 1974; its aid is untied. Partners are mainly in sub-Saharan Africa, e.g. Ethiopia, Malawi, Uganda. A Better World (2019) priorities: furthest behind first, gender equality, climate, governance. Ireland has not reached 0.7%. _(A7)_
- **Irish NGOs: six, with founding dates and founders or causes** — Concern Worldwide (1968, Biafra famine); Trócaire (1973, Irish Catholic bishops, Lenten box); GOAL (1977, John O'Shea); Gorta (1965), merged with Self Help Africa (1984, Ethiopian famine) in 2014; Bóthar (1991, Limerick), whose donated animals are passed on. _(N3)_
- **Four international NGOs: origins and awards** — Oxfam: Oxford, 1942, as the Oxford Committee for Famine Relief. Médecins Sans Frontières: France, 1971; Nobel Peace Prize 1999. Amnesty International: 1961, Peter Benenson; Nobel Peace Prize 1977. International Committee of the Red Cross: Geneva, 1863. _(N4)_
- **NGOs: three advantages and three limits** — Advantages: close to communities and bottom-up; fast in emergencies; not tied to donor trade interests. Limits: donors' priorities shape their work; they can duplicate or replace state services; scandal, as when misconduct by some Oxfam staff in Haiti emerged in 2018. _(N5, N6)_
- **Nineteenth-century Ireland: four features of the land system** — A small, mainly Protestant landlord class, many absentee, created after the plantations; tenants-at-will with no security, facing rack-rents and eviction; holdings subdivided into tiny plots under conacre and rundale; dependence on the potato. _(L2)_
- **The Great Famine and the land: toll, causes and aftermath** — 1845–49: about 1 million died and about 1 million emigrated. Causes included blight, potato dependence, the land system and government policy. The Encumbered Estates Act (1849) let bankrupt estates be sold, often to new landlords who cleared tenants. _(L2)_
- **The Land League and the Land War: names, dates and demands** — The Irish National Land League (1879), led by Michael Davitt of Co. Mayo and Charles Stewart Parnell, demanded fair rent, fixity of tenure and free sale. In 1880 Captain Charles Boycott, a land agent at Lough Mask, Co. Mayo, was ostracised: hence boycott. _(L3)_
- **Irish land reform: the Land Acts in order and the outcome** — 1870 and 1881 Land Acts (the 1881 Act gave the Three Fs and the Land Commission); Ashbourne (1885) and Wyndham (1903) Acts let tenants buy with loans repaid by annuities; Congested Districts Board (1891) for the west. Most farmers came to own their land. _(L3)_
- **Land ownership in Africa: three contrasting examples** — South Africa's Natives Land Act (1913) limited Black ownership to reserves of about 7% of the land (later 13%); reform since 1994 has been slow. Kenya's White Highlands were kept for European settlers. Zimbabwe's seizures from 2000 cut farm output. _(L5)_
- **Land reform that helped development: three examples** — South Korea (from 1950) and Taiwan's land to the tiller programme (1953) gave tenants ownership and raised rural incomes before industrialisation; Kerala abolished landlord tenancy from 1970 and has about 94% literacy against about 62% in Bihar (Census 2011). _(L6)_
- **Irish co-operatives: four milestones** — First co-operative creamery at Dromcollogher, Co. Limerick (1889); Horace Plunkett's IAOS (1894); dairy co-ops grew into Kerry Group (from Listowel, 1972), Glanbia (Kilkenny) and Ornua (Kerrygold, 1962); first credit unions, Dublin 1958, with Nora Herlihy. _(L9)_
- **Co-operatives in Europe and India: five named examples** — Rochdale Pioneers, England (1844); Denmark's first co-operative dairy, Hjedding (1882); Mondragon, Basque Country (1956), about 70,000 workers; Amul, Anand (1946), spread across India by Operation Flood (1970); SEWA, Ahmedabad (1972), a women workers' union with a co-operative bank. _(L9)_
- **Participation: the ladder and five reasons it matters** — The ladder runs from being informed, to consulted, to sharing decisions, to controlling them. Participation uses local knowledge; projects people own are maintained; it builds skills and confidence; it reduces corruption; it gives women and minorities a voice. _(P2, P3)_
- **Participation in Ireland: local enterprise and its limits** — County Enterprise Boards (1993) were replaced in 2014 by 31 Local Enterprise Offices in the local authorities, giving small firms grants, training and mentoring; LEADER groups (from 1991); Údarás na Gaeltachta (1980). Critique: Ireland is highly centralised. _(P4)_
- **Participation in the developing world: three examples** — Porto Alegre, Brazil, began participatory budgeting in 1989; India's 73rd Amendment (1992) created village panchayats with a third of seats for women; Medha Patkar's Narmada Bachao Andolan opposed the Sardar Sarovar dam, and the World Bank withdrew in 1993. _(P5)_
- **Modern slavery and local exploitation: figures and examples** — Modern slavery (2021): about 50 million (28 million forced labour, 22 million forced marriage). Bonded labour, banned in India in 1976, persists in brick kilns. Ireland's Atypical Working Scheme (2016), set up after exploitation reports, is criticised for tying non-EEA fishing crew to one employer. _(X3, X4)_
- **Women's work and the barriers they face** — Women do about three-quarters of unpaid care work (ILO, 2018) and are about 43% of farm labour in developing countries but under one in five landholders (FAO). Barriers: less schooling, credit and pay. Afghanistan banned girls from secondary school in 2022. _(G2, G3, L7)_
- **Why empowering women links growth to development** — Educated, earning women have fewer, healthier, better-educated children; income spent by women goes more on food, health and schooling; more women working and running businesses raises GDP. Kerala shows women's education going with human development. _(G4)_
- **Microfinance: the Grameen Bank and its limits** — Muhammad Yunus began Grameen in Bangladesh in 1976 (a bank from 1983), lending small sums without collateral, mostly to poor women in groups; Nobel Peace Prize 2006. Limit: in Andhra Pradesh, India (2010), borrowers were trapped in multiple loans. _(G5)_
- **Gender roles in Ireland: three changes, and the world's most equal country** — The public service marriage bar ended in 1973; a 2012 Act cut State funding to parties with under 30% women candidates (from 2016), then 40% (from 2023); a record 44 women won seats in the 174-seat Dáil (November 2024). Iceland tops the gender gap index. _(G6)_

### 4. Sustainable development as a model for the future (25)

- **Sustainable development: how the idea developed, in order** — The Limits to Growth (1972, Club of Rome) warned growth would exhaust resources within about a century; Brundtland's Our Common Future (1987); the Rio Earth Summit (1992) and Agenda 21; the MDGs (2000–2015); the 17 SDGs and the Paris Agreement (2015). _(S3)_
- **Sustainable development: the three pillars and why it is a priority** — Environmental, economic and social pillars must be balanced. It is a priority because resources are finite and overused; climate change and biodiversity loss threaten the future; the poorest suffer first; growth that destroys resources cannot last. _(S2, S4)_
- **Sustainable development: four problems with the model** — It is vague and open to greenwashing; its pillars conflict, as when jobs clash with protecting the environment; poorer countries argue that rich ones grew without limits and now ask them to slow down; progress on the SDGs is off track. _(S5)_
- **Irish peatlands: Bord na Móna's shift from brown to green** — Bord na Móna (1946) ended peat harvesting in January 2021, after the Lanesborough and Shannonbridge peat-burning power stations closed in December 2020. Cutaway bogs are rewetted to store carbon, as at Lough Boora, Co. Offaly; Bellacorick, Co. Mayo, has wind farms. _(R3)_
- **Irish renewable energy: current share and targets** — Wind gives about a third of Irish electricity, renewables about two-fifths (early 2020s). 2030 targets: 80% renewable electricity, at least 5 GW offshore wind, a 51% emissions cut; net zero by 2050. East–West Interconnector (2012); Celtic Interconnector due. _(R4)_
- **Sustainable farming in Ireland: schemes and the dairy tension** — BurrenLIFE (2005–2010) led to the Burren Programme, paying farmers by habitat scores; REPS (1994), GLAS (2015), ACRES (2023); Origin Green (2012). But dairy grew strongly after quotas ended in 2015; only about half of Irish waters are in good or high status. _(R5)_
- **Irish waste and forestry: two successes and one gap** — The plastic bag levy (March 2002, 15 cent) cut bag use by over 90%; the Deposit Return Scheme began on 1 February 2024. Forest cover is only about 11%, mostly Sitka spruce (Coillte, 1988); more native broadleaf woodland, like the Killarney oakwoods, is needed. _(R6, R7)_
- **Costa Rica: how it reversed deforestation** — Clearing for cattle pasture in the 1970s–80s cut forest to about a fifth of the land. The Payment for Environmental Services programme (1997, FONAFIFO), funded largely by a fuel tax, pays landowners to protect and replant; forest now covers over half. _(R8)_
- **Costa Rica: energy, spending choices, recognition and limits** — Almost all electricity renewable since 2015 (mainly hydro, plus geothermal and wind); ecotourism a top earner; army abolished in 1948, its budget going to education and health; first Earthshot Prize (2021). Limits: transport runs on imported oil; tourism pressure. _(R8)_
- **The problem fair trade addresses: five features of unfair trade** — Low, unstable commodity prices; small farmers' tiny share of the final price; middlemen taking much of the value; poor conditions and child labour; tariff escalation, as when coffee beans face low tariffs but roasted coffee faces higher ones. _(F1)_
- **How fair trade works: six features** — A minimum price covering sustainable production (or market price plus premium if higher); a premium spent on projects members choose; long-term contracts and pre-harvest credit; democratic co-ops; no forced or child labour; restricted pesticides, no GM crops. _(F3)_
- **Fair trade: origins, scale and products** — Max Havelaar, the first label, began in the Netherlands in 1988 for Mexican coffee; Garstang, England, was the first Fairtrade town (2000). Close to 2 million farmers and workers in about 70 countries are certified, growing coffee, cocoa, tea and bananas. _(F2, F4)_
- **Kuapa Kokoo and Divine Chocolate, Ghana: key facts** — A Fairtrade cocoa co-operative founded in 1993 (good cocoa farmer in Twi). It is a part-owner of Divine Chocolate (Britain, 1998), so farmers share brand profits as well as the Fairtrade price and premium. The premium paid for wells, schools and mobile clinics; women are urged to lead. _(F5)_
- **Oromia Coffee Farmers Co-operative Union, Ethiopia: key facts** — Founded in 1999 and led by Tadesse Meskela, it sells members' coffee directly to buyers, cutting out middlemen; its premium paid for schools, clinics and water. Its story is told in the film Black Gold (2006). Ethiopia is arabica coffee's original home. _(F6)_
- **Fair trade's potential impact on development: eight points** — Higher, steadier income to invest; premium-funded schools, clinics and water; democratic co-ops empower members; women gain voice and income; organic farming and less clearing; less child labour; processing and marketing skills; consumer pressure on big firms. _(F7)_
- **Fair trade in Ireland: named examples** — Fairtrade Ireland licenses the Fairtrade Mark; Clonakilty, Co. Cork, became Ireland's first Fairtrade town in 2003; towns, schools and universities can apply for Fairtrade status; Fairtrade Fortnight runs in late February and March; supermarkets stock ranges. _(F8)_
- **Fair trade: limits and the SOAS criticism** — Limits: tiny share of world trade; higher prices deter some buyers; certification costs exclude the poorest; trade rules unchanged; value added in rich countries. SOAS (2014): some hired labourers on certified farms in Ethiopia and Uganda earned less than on similar uncertified ones (disputed). _(F9)_
- **Amazon indigenous peoples: land loss and two justice cases** — Indigenous peoples have lost Amazon land to ranching, soya, mining and dams (Brazil has about 1.7 million indigenous people, Census 2022). Brazil declared a Yanomami health emergency in 2023 over illegal gold mining; the Belo Monte dam (2019), opposed by the Kayapó, displaced thousands. _(J3)_
- **Irish Travellers: recognition, inequality figures and responses** — About 33,000 (2022), formally recognised as an ethnic minority in 2017. AITHS (2010): men live about 15 years less, women 11.5; male suicide about six times the general rate. About 80% in the labour force unemployed (2016). Responses: Equal Status Acts (2000); Traveller and Roma Inclusion Strategy. _(J4)_
- **The Roma: size, problems and legal responses** — Europe's largest ethnic minority, about 10–12 million, many in Romania, Bulgaria, Hungary, Slovakia and Spain; poverty and segregated schools. D.H. v Czech Republic (2007) ruled their placement in special schools unlawful. EU frameworks: 2011, 2020–2030. _(J5)_
- **The Sámi and the Fosen wind farm: green but unjust?** — On 11 October 2021 Norway's Supreme Court ruled that Fosen turbines on reindeer grazing land violated Sámi herders' right to their culture. They kept running, so in 2023 activists, with Greta Thunberg, blocked buildings in Oslo; agreements followed in 2023–24. _(J6, J1)_
- **Nyerere's Tanzania: what self-reliance achieved** — Tanganyika (independent 1961) joined Zanzibar as Tanzania in 1964. The Arusha Declaration (1967) set out Ujamaa: villages, collective farming, education for self-reliance. Gains: Kiswahili as national language, unity, peace, more literacy and schooling. _(H2)_
- **Nyerere's Tanzania: where self-reliance failed** — Villagisation in 1973–76 moved millions, often by force, into planned villages; food and export output fell and the economy went into crisis. Nyerere stepped down in 1985 and in 1986 Tanzania agreed an IMF programme: social gains, economic failure. _(H2, H8)_
- **Community self-help: three African examples and a global movement** — The Green Belt Movement (Kenya, 1977, Wangari Maathai, Nobel Peace Prize 2004): women planting tens of millions of trees; zaï pits (Yacouba Sawadogo, Burkina Faso); farmer-managed regreening of about 5 million hectares in Niger; La Via Campesina (1993). _(H5)_
- **Irish self-help: four examples** — Muintir na Tíre, founded in 1937 by Fr John Hayes in Co. Tipperary; co-operative creameries (from 1889) and credit unions (from 1958); LEADER (from 1991); NGOs that help people help themselves, such as Self Help Africa (1984) and Bóthar (1991). _(H7, H6)_

## Plate tectonics

### 1. The internal structure of the earth (10)

- **Earth's layers from the surface inward, with their depths** — Crust, about 5–70 km thick; mantle, to about 2,900 km; liquid outer core, from about 2,900 to 5,150 km; solid inner core, from about 5,150 km to the centre at about 6,370 km. Density and temperature increase towards the centre. _(L1, L6, L10, L11)_
- **Continental vs oceanic crust: thickness, density and rock** — Continental: about 30–70 km thick, thickest under high mountains; density about 2.7 g/cm³; granite-type, rich in silica and aluminium (sial). Oceanic: about 5–10 km thick; density about 3.0 g/cm³; basalt, rich in silica and magnesium (sima). _(L3, L4)_
- **Why oceanic crust is much younger than continental crust** — Oceanic crust is continually created and destroyed, so none is older than about 200 million years, while some continental rock is billions of years old. _(L4)_
- **The Moho: who identified it, when, and what happens there** — Andrija Mohorovičić, a Croatian seismologist, identified it in 1909 from earthquake records. At this boundary between the crust and the mantle, earthquake waves speed up suddenly. _(L5)_
- **Mantle: depth, rock type and the layers in its upper part** — The thickest layer, to about 2,900 km, of dense rock rich in iron and magnesium. Its upper part holds the semi-molten asthenosphere; its solid top joins the crust as the lithosphere, about 100 km thick on average. _(L6, L7, L8)_
- **Outer core vs inner core: state, metals and key facts** — Both are made of iron and nickel. The outer core is liquid, and movement of this liquid metal generates the earth's magnetic field. The inner core is solid, the hottest part of the earth at about 5,500 °C, kept solid by enormous pressure. _(L10, L11, L12)_
- **Two sources of the earth's internal heat** — The decay of radioactive elements, and heat left over from the earth's formation. This heat powers convection currents and endogenic forces. _(H1)_
- **Convection currents in the mantle: the three stages** — First, heated rock near the core becomes less dense and rises; then, near the top of the mantle, it spreads out sideways and cools; finally, the cooled, denser rock sinks again, completing the circle. _(H2)_
- **Endogenic forces: five examples, and how they differ from exogenic** — Plate movement, folding, faulting, volcanic activity and earthquakes. They come from inside the earth and are powered by its internal heat; exogenic forces, such as weathering and erosion, act at the surface. _(H3)_
- **Seismic waves: how they reveal the earth's structure** — Waves change speed and direction as they pass through layers of different density, which is how the discontinuities were found. P-waves pass through solids and liquids, S-waves only through solids, so S-waves failing to cross the outer core show it is liquid. _(K1, K2)_

### 2. The plate tectonics model (19)

- **Plate tectonics theory: three scientists and their ideas** — Alfred Wegener, a German meteorologist: continental drift, 1912. Arthur Holmes, a British geologist: in the late 1920s, that mantle convection could move continents. Harry Hess, an American geologist: sea-floor spreading, early 1960s. _(D1, F2)_
- **Continental drift: Wegener's book and how Pangaea broke up** — Wegener published The Origin of Continents and Oceans in 1915. Pangaea began to break up about 200 million years ago into Laurasia in the north and Gondwanaland in the south, separated by the Tethys Sea; these broke up into today's continents. _(D1, D2)_
- **Continental drift evidence: jigsaw fit and fossils** — South America and Africa fit together, best at the edge of the continental shelves. Mesosaurus, a freshwater reptile, is found in Brazil and southern Africa; Glossopteris, a fern-like plant, in South America, Africa, India, Antarctica and Australia. _(D3, D4)_
- **Continental drift evidence: matching mountains and past climates** — The Appalachians of eastern North America match the Caledonian mountains of Ireland, Scotland and Norway in rock type, age and structure. Glacial deposits about 300 million years old occur in India, Africa, South America and Australia; coal in Antarctica. _(D5, D6)_
- **Why continental drift was rejected at first, and what rescued it** — Wegener could not explain what force moved the continents. Sea-floor spreading later supplied the missing mechanism and turned continental drift into the theory of plate tectonics. _(D7, F6)_
- **Mapping the ocean floor: what echo-sounding revealed** — Detailed sonar mapping after the Second World War revealed long mid-ocean ridges with a central rift valley, and deep ocean trenches. The Mid-Atlantic Ridge is about 16,000 km long. _(F1)_
- **Sea-floor spreading: who proposed it, and what happens at ridges and trenches** — Harry Hess, an American geologist, early 1960s: magma rises at mid-ocean ridges and cools to form new basalt crust, which pushes older crust away on both sides; crust is destroyed again at ocean trenches. _(F2)_
- **Sea-floor spreading evidence: age of rocks and sediment** — Oceanic rocks are youngest at the ridge and get progressively older with distance from it, symmetrically on both sides. Ocean-floor sediment is thin or absent at the ridge and thicker further away. _(F3, F4)_
- **Magnetic striping: how it proves sea-floor spreading** — The magnetic field reverses from time to time; iron minerals in cooling basalt line up with it, so the ocean floor holds stripes of normal and reversed magnetism mirrored either side of the ridge. Fred Vine and Drummond Matthews explained this in 1963. _(F5)_
- **Name the seven major lithospheric plates and four smaller ones** — Major: Pacific (the largest), North American, South American, Eurasian, African, Indo-Australian (or Indian and Australian) and Antarctic. Smaller: any four of Nazca, Cocos, Caribbean, Juan de Fuca, Philippine, Arabian and Scotia. _(P2)_
- **How plates move: three driving forces** — Convection currents in the mantle drag the plates; ridge push, as new, hot crust slides away from the ridge under gravity; slab pull, as the cold, dense subducting edge of a plate pulls the rest of the plate after it. _(P3)_
- **How fast plates move: the Atlantic figure** — Plates move a few centimetres a year; the Atlantic is widening by about 2.5 cm a year, roughly the rate a fingernail grows, so the map of continents and oceans changes over geological time. _(P4)_
- **Ireland's plate position today and its consequences** — Ireland is on the Eurasian plate, near its western edge and far from any boundary; the nearest is the Mid-Atlantic Ridge to the west, so Ireland moves slowly away from North America. It has no active volcanoes and only rare, minor earthquakes. _(I1, I2)_
- **Iapetus Ocean: Ireland's two halves and where they joined** — About 500 million years ago north-west Ireland lay on the North American (Laurentian) side of the Iapetus Ocean and the south-east on the European side. As it closed they joined along the Iapetus suture, roughly Shannon Estuary to Clogherhead, Co. Louth. _(I3, I4)_
- **Caledonian folding: date and named Irish mountains** — About 400 million years ago, as the Iapetus Ocean closed. It built north-east to south-west mountains: those of Donegal, Mayo and Connemara, and the Wicklow Mountains; the Leinster granite batholith was intruded in this period. _(I4)_
- **Armorican folding: date, cause and named Irish mountains** — About 300 million years ago, when Africa (part of Gondwanaland) collided with Europe as Pangaea came together. It built east–west ridges across the south: Macgillycuddy's Reeks, the Galtee Mountains, the Knockmealdowns and the Caha Mountains. _(I6)_
- **Ireland's drift north: what two rock types record** — Old Red Sandstone formed in hot, dry conditions after the Caledonian folding; Carboniferous limestone formed about 350 million years ago, when Ireland lay near the equator under a warm, shallow tropical sea. _(I5)_
- **Antrim basalts: link to the opening of the Atlantic** — As North America and Greenland separated from Europe after Pangaea split, magma rose through cracks in north-east Ireland and formed the Antrim basalts about 60 million years ago. _(I7)_
- **Alpine folding: what it built, and why it is not Irish** — The most recent period of fold mountain building, producing the Alps (the African plate colliding with the Eurasian plate) and the Himalayas. It did not build Ireland's mountains; the two Irish periods are Caledonian and Armorican. _(I8)_

### 3. Plate boundaries as zones of crustal construction and destruction (17)

- **Constructive boundary: how new crust forms, in three steps** — First, convection currents carry the plates apart; then pressure drops as they separate, so the mantle partly melts; finally, magma rises into the gap and cools to form new basaltic oceanic crust. _(C1)_
- **Mid-Atlantic Ridge: the plates either side and the islands on it** — It separates the North American and Eurasian plates in the North Atlantic and the South American and African plates in the South Atlantic. Iceland and the Azores lie on it; in Iceland the rift is visible on land at Þingvellir (Thingvellir). _(C2)_
- **Constructive boundaries: five features to name** — A mid-ocean ridge with a rift valley along its crest; transform faults offsetting the ridge; volcanic islands where it rises above sea level, such as Surtsey off Iceland, formed from 1963; fissure eruptions of runny basalt lava; shallow earthquakes. _(C4, C2)_
- **Continental rifting: the process and two African examples** — The crust stretches and a block drops between parallel faults to form a rift valley with volcanoes nearby, as in the East African Rift Valley (Mount Kilimanjaro, Tanzania). If spreading continues the sea floods in, as in the Red Sea. _(C3)_
- **Oceanic–continental boundary: what happens and what forms** — The denser oceanic plate is subducted, forming an ocean trench; scraped-off sediments are crumpled into fold mountains; the sinking plate melts and magma rises to form volcanoes on the continent; earthquakes occur along the sinking plate. _(X1, X2)_
- **Oceanic–continental boundary: two named examples** — The Nazca plate subducts beneath the South American plate: the Peru-Chile Trench offshore and the Andes, with many volcanoes, on land. The Juan de Fuca plate subducts beneath the North American plate: the Cascade Range volcanoes, including Mount St Helens. _(X2)_
- **Oceanic–oceanic boundary: what forms, with two examples** — The older, denser plate subducts, forming a trench and an island arc, with frequent earthquakes. The Pacific plate subducts beneath the Philippine plate at the Mariana Trench, about 11 km deep, the deepest place on earth; the Aleutian Islands are another arc. _(X3)_
- **Continental–continental collision: what happens to the crust** — Both plates are too light to subduct, so the crust and old ocean sediments between them are crushed, folded and pushed up into high fold mountains, such as the Alps. Crust is modified, not destroyed; earthquakes are common but volcanoes are few or absent. _(X4)_
- **Himalayas: the plates, the timing and the fossil evidence** — The Indo-Australian (Indian) plate has been colliding with the Eurasian plate since about 50 million years ago, forming the Himalayas and the Tibetan Plateau. Mount Everest, 8,849 m, has marine fossils in limestone near its summit, from the Tethys Sea floor. _(X4)_
- **Destructive boundaries: two tsunami disasters** — Tōhoku, Japan, 11 March 2011: magnitude 9.0; the tsunami killed about 18,000 (dead and missing) and caused the Fukushima Daiichi nuclear disaster. Indian Ocean, 26 December 2004: magnitude 9.1 off Sumatra; the tsunami killed about 230,000 in 14 countries. _(X5)_
- **Destructive boundaries: a named earthquake and a named eruption** — Nepal, 25 April 2015: a magnitude 7.8 earthquake on the Himalayan collision boundary killed about 9,000 people. Mount St Helens, on the Juan de Fuca subduction zone, erupted on 18 May 1980, killing 57 people. _(X5)_
- **Transform boundary: how earthquakes happen and why no volcanoes** — Two plates slide past horizontally along a transform fault, in opposite directions or one way at different speeds; friction locks them until stress is released suddenly as an earthquake. No magma forms, and crust is neither created nor destroyed. _(T1, T2, T3)_
- **San Andreas Fault: plate movement and two earthquakes** — The Pacific plate moves north-west relative to the North American plate. The San Francisco earthquake of 18 April 1906, magnitude about 7.8, was followed by great fires; the Loma Prieta earthquake of 1989, magnitude 6.9, struck near San Francisco. _(T1)_
- **Turkey–Syria earthquake, 2023: boundary type and impact** — On 6 February 2023 a magnitude 7.8 earthquake on the East Anatolian Fault, a transform fault in Turkey, killed over 50,000 people in Turkey and Syria. _(T1)_
- **What happens to crust at each type of plate boundary** — Constructive (divergent): plates move apart and crust is created. Destructive (convergent): plates move together and crust is destroyed, or modified where two continents collide. Transform (passive, conservative): plates slide past and crust is conserved. _(G2)_
- **Pacific Ring of Fire: boundary types and share of activity** — A belt of destructive and some transform boundaries around the edge of the Pacific plate and its neighbours, with about 75% of the world's active volcanoes and about 90% of its earthquakes. _(G3)_
- **How plate tectonics explains where earthquakes, volcanoes and fold mountains occur** — All three are concentrated in narrow belts along plate boundaries (ridges, subduction zones, collision zones and transform faults), with very few in plate interiors. Fold mountains form only at convergent boundaries, as long chains along the plate edge. _(G4)_

## Population migration

### 1. Changing population characteristics (25)

- **Census 2016 to 2022: natural increase or net migration?** — Population change = natural change + net migration. Between 2016 and 2022 the population grew by about 360,000 (about 7.6%): natural increase about 170,000 and net migration about 190,000, so net migration was slightly larger. _(K2)_
- **How governments use population statistics: name six uses** — Planning schools, hospitals and housing; pensions (the State pension age has been 66 since 2014); transport and infrastructure; constituency boundaries, as population sets the number of TDs; funding for regions and workforce planning. _(K4)_
- **Low population density: four reasons** — High, steep or cold land is hard to farm, build on and reach; deserts and dense forest offer little water, farmland or access; poor soils and remoteness; few jobs, so young people leave and density drops. _(D4)_
- **Ireland's population distribution: Census 2022 figures** — State about 5.15 million, about 73 people per km²; Greater Dublin Area (Dublin, Meath, Kildare, Wicklow) about 2.1 million, about 40% of the total; Co. Dublin about 1,600 per km²; Mayo about 25 per km²; Leitrim and Roscommon also sparse. _(D7)_
- **Ireland's distribution over time, and why density is rising** — Before the Famine the population was rural and weighted to the west; now it is eastern and urban, with fast growth in Meath, Kildare and Wicklow. Density rises because natural increase and net immigration since the 1990s add people to a fixed area. _(D7, D8)_
- **World population growth: the pattern and the contrast** — Slow until about 1800 (about 1 billion), then a J-curve driven by falling death rates, reaching 8 billion on 15 November 2022; now slowing. Global fertility about 2.3 (early 2020s), down from about 5 in the 1950s. Growth is fastest in sub-Saharan Africa. _(G1, G2)_
- **Demographic Transition Model: the five stages with examples** — Stage 1 high stationary: pre-industrial societies (no country today). Stage 2 early expanding, when the death rate falls most: Niger. Stage 3 late expanding: India. Stage 4 low stationary: Ireland, USA. Stage 5 declining: Germany, Italy, Japan. _(G3)_
- **DTM stage 2: why the death rate falls** — Clean water and sanitation; vaccination; a better food supply; medical care. The death rate falls fastest in stage 2 while the birth rate stays high, so growth is rapid. _(G4)_
- **DTM: why the birth rate falls after stage 2** — Female education and paid work; contraception; falling infant mortality, so fewer insurance births; urbanisation, as children cost more and earn less; pensions replace children as old-age security; later marriage; weaker religious influence. _(G5)_
- **DTM stage 5 countries: challenges and responses** — Challenges: a shrinking workforce, labour shortages, rising pension and health costs, closing schools, rural depopulation. Responses: immigration, pro-natalist incentives to raise birth rates, later retirement. _(G6)_
- **Population pyramids: uses and how to read them** — Uses: planning services, forecasting needs, comparing countries, showing past events. Reading: bulges show baby booms or immigration of young adults; notches show emigration, low birth years or war; more women than men at the oldest ages. _(S1, S3)_
- **Pyramid shapes: developing vs developed countries** — Wide base, narrow top: high fertility and short life expectancy (Niger). Straight sides: stable, low birth and death rates. Narrow base, wide top: ageing (Japan). Falling fertility narrows the base and longer lives widen the top. _(S2, F3)_
- **Why urban dependency ratios are lower than rural ones** — Young working adults migrate to cities for jobs and college, leaving older people, and sometimes children, behind in rural areas, so rural areas have more dependants for every person of working age. _(S5)_
- **Ageing population: effects and planning responses in Ireland** — Effects: higher pension costs; more demand for health care, nursing homes and carers; a smaller tax base; pressure to raise the pension age. Responses: home-care supports, pension reform, geriatric services, immigrant workers, age-friendly towns. _(S6)_
- **Birth rates: factors that influence them** — Income and economic development; the status and education of women; religion and culture; access to contraception; government policy; infant mortality, as high rates encourage larger families; urban or rural living. _(F1)_
- **Death rates: factors in rich and poor countries** — Medical technology (vaccines, antibiotics), sanitation, diet, income and health services. Rich countries: lifestyle diseases such as heart disease and cancer. Poor countries: conflict, famine and disease such as malaria and HIV/AIDS. _(F2)_
- **Life expectancy: why it is long in Ireland and how to raise it in Niger** — Ireland (about 82–83 years): good diet and housing, health services, vaccination, clean water, fewer smokers, safer work. Niger: clean water and sanitation, vaccination, mosquito nets and malaria treatment, more clinics, better nutrition, female education. _(F4, F5, I4)_
- **Ireland's census population, 1841 to 2022: key figures** — 1841: about 6.5 million; 1851: 5.1 million; 1926: 3.0 million; 1961: 2.8 million, the lowest ever; 1981: 3.4 million; a small fall 1986 to 1991; 2002: 3.9 million; 2016: 4.76 million; 2022: about 5.15 million, the first time over 5 million since 1851. _(I1)_
- **Irish births and fertility since the 1970s: key figures** — Fertility about 4 in the early 1970s; births peaked at about 74,000 in 1980; fertility fell below replacement around 1990 and was about 1.5 in 2023, with about 55,000 births and 35,000 deaths (natural increase about 20,000). _(I2)_
- **Why Irish fertility fell: six reasons (three with dates)** — Free secondary education (1967, Donogh O'Malley) and more women at third level; marriage bar removed (1973); contraception legalised (Health (Family Planning) Act 1979); more women in paid work and later marriage; secularisation; housing and childcare costs. _(I3)_
- **Ireland since the 1950s: migration and urbanisation in brief** — Heavy net emigration in the 1950s (about 400,000 between 1951 and 1961) and again in the 1980s; net immigration in the 1970s, from the mid-1990s to 2008 and from the mid-2010s. Census 1971 was the first with a town majority; about 63% urban by 2016. Households are smaller. _(I5, I7, I8)_
- **Ireland's changing age structure: Census 2022** — About 776,000 aged 65 and over (about 15%), up about 22% since 2016; average age 38.8, up from 37.4 in 2016; about one-fifth under 15; about one in five people (just over 1 million) born outside Ireland. _(I6)_
- **Europe's ageing population: the EU, Germany, Italy and Bulgaria** — EU (2023): fertility about 1.4, median age about 44.5, about 21% aged 65+, so immigration supplies most growth. Germany: deaths above births every year since 1972. Italy: fertility about 1.2. Bulgaria: about 9 million (1989) to 6.5 million (2021). _(E1, E2, E3, E4)_
- **Niger: a young, fast-growing population (DTM stage 2)** — Landlocked West Africa, in the Sahel and Sahara; about 27 million (2023), growing over 3% a year; fertility about 6 and median age about 15, among the most extreme in the world; a very wide-based pyramid; most people live in the rainier south. _(E5)_
- **Japan: an old, shrinking population (DTM stage 5)** — About 29% aged 65 or over (2023), the world's highest share; population peaked at about 128 million around 2008–2010 and was about 124 million in 2024; fertility about 1.2; few immigrants; a top-heavy pyramid. _(E6)_

### 2. Overpopulation (19)

- **Density is not overpopulation: two contrasting examples** — The Netherlands has over 500 people per km² but is not overpopulated, thanks to wealth, technology and trade. The Sahel is thinly populated but overpopulated, because its fragile resources, drought and poverty cannot support its people. _(O3)_
- **Population theories: Malthus, Boserup and the Club of Rome** — Malthus (1798): population grows geometrically and food arithmetically, so famine, disease and war check growth. Boserup (1965): population pressure drives farming innovation, so food supply can rise. Club of Rome, The Limits to Growth (1972). _(O4)_
- **Sahel desertification: how it happens** — Overgrazing, overcultivation as fallow periods are abandoned, and cutting trees and shrubs for fuelwood strip the land; drought dries it out; wind and rain then erode the bare soil. Lake Chad has shrunk by about 90% since the 1960s. _(R1, X2)_
- **Sahel: four responses to desertification** — The Great Green Wall, an African Union initiative launched in 2007, to restore a belt of land across the Sahel; stone lines (diguettes) along contours and zaï planting pits in Burkina Faso to trap water and soil; farmer-managed regeneration of trees in Niger. _(R1)_
- **Niger: how society and culture keep growth high** — Early marriage of girls is common, female literacy is low and large families carry prestige. Fertility is about 6 children per woman, growth over 3% a year and median age about 15; Niger is among the lowest-ranked in the UN Human Development Index. _(R2, R3)_
- **Pre-Famine Ireland: why it was overpopulated** — About 8.2 million people on the whole island in 1841; the rural poor depended heavily on one resource, the potato; holdings were subdivided among sons into ever-smaller plots; landlords were often absent; conacre rented small plots for one season. _(R1)_
- **The Great Famine, 1845–49: what happened** — Potato blight (Phytophthora infestans) struck in 1845; about 1 million people died and about 1 million emigrated, and the island's population fell to about 6.6 million by 1851. It is often cited in support of Malthus. _(R1, R4, O4)_
- **Effects of overpopulation: six to name** — Food shortages and famine (Sahel: late 1960s–1970s, early-to-mid 1980s); soil erosion, desertification and water shortages; unemployment and poverty; pressure on schools, hospitals and housing; migration to cities and abroad; conflict over land and water. _(X1, X2, X3, X4, X5)_
- **India: Kerala and Bihar compared** — Kerala: fertility about 1.8 (2019–21), literacy about 94% (2011), the highest of any state, with high female literacy and good health care. Bihar: fertility about 3.0 and literacy about 62%, the lowest. India as a whole: 943 females per 1,000 males (2011). _(R2)_
- **Income levels: why poor countries have high birth rates** — In low-income countries children are economic assets, infant mortality is high and there are no pensions. As incomes rise, children cost more and women work and study, so fertility falls: Niger about 6, Ireland about 1.5, the EU about 1.4 (2023). _(R3)_
- **Bangladesh: how fertility fell while the country was still poor** — Fertility fell from about 6–7 in the early 1970s to about 2 in the 2020s through a national family planning programme with community health workers visiting homes, NGOs, girls' education, and millions of women employed in the garment industry. _(R3)_
- **Technology and population growth: four impacts** — Medical technology (vaccines, antibiotics, clean water; penicillin 1928; smallpox eradicated 1980) cut death rates faster than birth rates; the Green Revolution raised carrying capacity; contraception cut birth rates; subsistence farming limits the Sahel. _(R4)_
- **India's Green Revolution: what it achieved and its side effects** — From the mid-1960s, high-yield seeds (wheat bred by Norman Borlaug, introduced under M. S. Swaminathan), irrigation and fertilisers raised yields; food-grain output rose from about 51 million tonnes (1950–51) to about 330 million (2022–23). Side effects: lower water tables, soil damage, debt. _(R4, R1)_
- **India's family planning: two key events** — India launched the world's first national family planning programme in 1952. Forced sterilisations during the Emergency (1975–77) caused a backlash. Fertility has fallen from about 6 in the 1950s to about 2.0 (2019–21). _(R5)_
- **China's one-child policy: exceptions and later changes** — Introduced in 1979–80, with exceptions for many rural families whose first child was a girl and for ethnic minorities. Replaced by a two-child policy from 2016 and a three-child policy in 2021; China's population fell in 2022, the first fall since 1961. _(R5)_
- **China's one-child policy: four effects** — Fertility fell well below replacement; son preference and sex-selective abortion pushed the sex ratio at birth above 115 boys per 100 girls in the 2000s, leaving millions more men; the 4-2-1 family (four grandparents, two parents, one child); rapid ageing. _(R5)_
- **How rapid population growth slows development** — Savings go on feeding and educating more people instead of investment; per-capita income gains are cancelled out; jobs cannot keep up; the environment is degraded; high fertility traps families and countries in poverty. _(D1)_
- **Demographic dividend: examples, including Ireland** — East Asian economies such as South Korea and China; Ireland from the 1990s, when the large generation born around 1980 (births peaked at about 74,000) entered the workforce in the late 1990s and 2000s, helping the Celtic Tiger. _(D2)_
- **Very slow or negative growth: effects on development** — Ageing, labour shortages, a shrinking tax base and high pension and health costs, as in Japan, Italy (fertility about 1.2, 2023) and South Korea (fertility about 0.7, 2023, the lowest in the world). _(D3)_

### 3. Changing migration patterns in Ireland (17)

- **Push and pull factors associated with migration in Ireland** — Push: unemployment, low wages, recession and the decline of farm labouring. Pull: jobs in the Celtic Tiger economy; high wages in the UK, the USA and Australia; the availability of work permits. _(M2)_
- **Barriers (intervening obstacles) to migration: name six** — Distance and the cost of travel; immigration laws, visas and quotas; language; family ties and age; lack of information; cultural difference. _(M3)_
- **Ravenstein's laws of migration and Lee's model** — Ravenstein: most migrants move short distances, in steps; each stream has a counter-stream; young adults are most likely to migrate; cities attract migrants. Lee: push and pull factors drive migration, and intervening obstacles reduce it. _(M4)_
- **From the Famine to 1961: emigration and population decline** — The Great Famine (1845–49): about 1 million died and about 1 million emigrated. The State fell from about 6.5 million (1841) to about 2.8 million (1961), the lowest, with only a slight rise from 1946 to 1951. Over 30 million Americans report Irish ancestry. _(T1, T9)_
- **1950s emigration: how many and where they went** — Net emigration of about 400,000 between 1951 and 1961, the highest since the 1880s; most went to Britain (London, Birmingham, Manchester, Coventry, Liverpool); more women than men left some rural areas. A Commission on Emigration reported in 1954. _(T2, M5)_
- **1950s emigration: causes** — Stagnation and protectionism; unemployment; the decline of small farms and farm labouring as farming mechanised; Britain's post-war demand for workers in building, factories, nursing and transport; low fares and the Common Travel Area; chain migration. _(T2)_
- **1960s and 1970s: why emigration fell and immigration began** — Whitaker's Economic Development and the First Programme for Economic Expansion (1958); the IDA attracting foreign investment from 1969; EEC entry on 1 January 1973. The 1970s were the first decade of net immigration since the Famine, with emigrants returning. _(T3)_
- **1980s emigration: causes, numbers and destinations** — Recession, high unemployment and high taxes drove net emigration of over 200,000 between 1981 and 1991, including many graduates (brain drain). They went to Britain and the USA, where many were undocumented until the Donnelly and Morrison visas. _(T4)_
- **Celtic Tiger immigration, 1996 to 2008: who came** — EU migrants after enlargement on 1 May 2004, when only Ireland, the UK and Sweden opened their labour markets at once; returning Irish; non-EU workers on permits; asylum seekers. Census 2006: about 420,000 non-Irish nationals (about 10%), Polish about 63,000. _(T5)_
- **Post-2008 emigration: causes, peak and destinations** — The property crash, banking crisis (2008), unemployment and the EU–IMF bailout (November 2010). Emigration peaked at about 89,000 in the year to April 2013, about half Irish, many young graduates; mainly to Australia, the UK, Canada and the USA. _(T6)_
- **Immigration since the mid-2010s: causes** — Economic recovery; job growth in multinationals and services; returning Irish; non-EU skilled workers; international protection applicants; over 100,000 Ukrainians under EU temporary protection by 2024, after Russia's invasion on 24 February 2022. _(T7)_
- **Ireland's immigrant population: Census 2022** — About 630,000 non-Irish nationals (about 12%) plus about 170,000 dual citizens; just over 1 million born abroad. Largest groups: Polish about 94,000, UK 83,000, Indian 45,000, Romanian 43,000, Lithuanian 31,000, Brazilian 27,000. _(T8)_
- **Ireland as a donor region: negative impacts** — Loss of young workers and skills, and of the State's investment in their education; an ageing rural population and imbalanced age structure; rural decline, with closed schools, fewer marriages and depopulated parishes; lower birth rates; family separation. _(P1)_
- **Ireland as a donor region: positive impacts** — Emigrant remittances; less pressure on jobs and welfare; emigrants returned with skills, capital and experience in the 1970s and 1990s; diaspora links support trade, tourism and investment. _(P2)_
- **Ireland as a receiver region: positive impacts** — Fills labour shortages in construction, health care, hospitality and technology; young workers pay taxes and offset ageing; new skills and enterprise; cultural diversity in food, sport, languages and religions; keeps rural schools and parishes open. _(P3)_
- **Ireland as a receiver region: negative impacts** — Pressure on housing, schools and health services; exploitation of some migrant workers; language barriers in schools; integration challenges and racism; remittances leave Ireland. _(P4)_
- **Why immigration fell from 2007 to 2010 and rose after 2010** — Fell: the recession, the collapse of construction and rising unemployment; many EU accession migrants returned home. Rose: economic recovery and job creation, multinational investment, EU free movement, returning Irish, an English-speaking EU country. _(P5, P6)_

### 4. Migration policy in Ireland and the EU (21)

- **Why people seek asylum: three reasons** — War and conflict force people to flee; persecution for religion, ethnicity, political opinion or sexuality; human rights abuses by the state against its own people. _(W3)_
- **Why EU states attract asylum seekers and migrants: four pull factors** — Safety, the rule of law and respect for human rights; high living standards, jobs and welfare; existing family and diaspora communities; nearness to conflict zones in the Middle East and Africa. _(W4)_
- **1951 Refugee Convention: the five grounds of persecution** — Race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion. Signed in Geneva in 1951; its 1967 Protocol removed the geographic and time limits. UNHCR is the UN refugee agency. _(W2)_
- **EU free movement in Ireland: the 2004 and later openings** — EU, EEA and Swiss citizens may live and work in Ireland without permits. On 1 May 2004 only Ireland, the UK and Sweden gave the new member states immediate access to their labour markets; Bulgaria and Romania followed after a delay. _(I2)_
- **Irish employment permits: two types and how the system works** — Issued by the Department of Enterprise: the Critical Skills Employment Permit (e.g. ICT, engineering, medicine) and the General Employment Permit. A list of ineligible occupations and a labour-market test protect Irish and EU workers from displacement. _(I3)_
- **Ireland's asylum system: the laws and recent numbers** — Refugee Act 1996; International Protection Act 2015, with a single application procedure; the International Protection Office decides first-instance claims. Over 13,000 applications in 2022, a record at the time, and comparable numbers in 2023. _(I4)_
- **Direct provision: what it is and its criticisms** — Introduced in 2000: accommodation and meals in centres plus a small weekly allowance, now run by IPAS. Criticised, including by the McMahon Report (2015), for long stays, lack of privacy, unsuitability for children, rural isolation and poor mental health. _(I5)_
- **Direct provision: reforms since 2017** — A 2017 Supreme Court ruling led to a right to work after a waiting period from 2018. The February 2021 White Paper planned to end direct provision by the end of 2024, but the plan was abandoned as numbers rose; some applicants went unaccommodated. _(I5)_
- **Citizenship referendum, June 2004: what changed** — The 27th Amendment, passed by about 79%, ended the automatic right to citizenship for children born in Ireland to non-national parents. Non-nationals may naturalise after a period of residence and attend a citizenship ceremony. _(I6)_
- **Ireland's refugee programmes: resettlement and Ukrainians** — Irish Refugee Protection Programme (September 2015): up to 4,000 people, including refugees from Syria, through EU relocation and UNHCR resettlement. Over 100,000 Ukrainians came by 2024 under temporary protection, with an immediate right to live and work. _(I7, I8)_
- **Irish migration policy: evaluation** — Open to EU citizens and skilled workers but restrictive for low-skilled non-EU workers; the asylum system is slow and relies on direct provision; labour needs clash with housing pressure; anti-immigration protests show public opposition. _(I9)_
- **EU free movement and Schengen: key dates** — Free movement of workers: Treaty of Rome (1957); EU citizenship: Maastricht (1992). Schengen: signed 1985, in force 1995, 29 countries from 2025. Ireland (because of the Common Travel Area, kept after Brexit) and Cyprus are EU members outside it. _(E1, E2, I1)_
- **Dublin system: dates and why it broke down** — Dublin Convention 1990, then Dublin II (2003) and Dublin III (2013). Making the state of first entry responsible put most pressure on frontier states such as Italy, Greece, Spain and Malta, and the rule broke down in 2015. _(E3)_
- **Frontex and the EU Blue Card: border control and legal migration** — Frontex, the EU border and coast guard agency, set up in 2004 with headquarters in Warsaw, runs patrols and returns. The EU Blue Card (2009) admits highly skilled non-EU workers to ease labour shortages in ageing populations. _(E4, E5)_
- **The 2015–16 migration crisis: causes and routes** — War in Syria from 2011, plus conflict in Afghanistan, Iraq and Eritrea. About 1.3 million EU asylum applications in 2015; over 1 million crossed the Mediterranean, most from Turkey to the Greek islands (Lesbos), others from Libya to Italy (Lampedusa). _(E6)_
- **Lampedusa shipwreck, October 2013: what happened and the response** — Over 360 people died off Lampedusa, south of Sicily, a main landing point on the Central Mediterranean route. Italy responded with the Mare Nostrum search-and-rescue operation (2013 to 2014). _(E6)_
- **EU responses in 2015: Germany, relocation and Hungary** — Merkel's Wir schaffen das (August 2015): Germany took the most asylum seekers. The EU agreed to relocate 160,000 from Italy and Greece; Hungary, Poland and the Czech Republic refused (ruled illegal in 2020). Hungary built a fence on its border with Serbia. _(E7)_
- **EU–Turkey statement, March 2016: terms and result** — Irregular migrants reaching the Greek islands were to be returned to Turkey; the EU was to provide €6 billion for refugees in Turkey; arrivals in Greece fell sharply. It is an example of externalisation, as are deals with Libya and other North African states. _(E7)_
- **EU responses since 2022: temporary protection and the 2024 Pact** — The Temporary Protection Directive (2001) was first activated on 4 March 2022; over 4 million held it in 2024. Pact on Migration and Asylum (May 2024, applies from mid-2026): faster border procedures and mandatory solidarity. Ireland opted in. _(E7, I8)_
- **Challenges for EU states from large numbers of asylum seekers** — Cost of reception and housing; slow processing and backlogs; integration (language, jobs, schooling); an unequal burden on frontier states; political division and anti-immigration parties; security screening; deaths at sea and smuggling gangs. _(E8)_
- **EU migration policy: three tensions to evaluate** — Humanitarian duty against border control; solidarity between member states against national sovereignty, as the relocation dispute showed; Europe's need for workers against public opposition to immigration. _(E9)_

### 5. Ethnic, racial and religious issues arising from migration (19)

- **How ethnic and religious issues arise from migration: seven ways** — Competition for jobs, housing and services; visible difference (skin colour, hijab); religious practice (dress, halal food, faith schools); segregated poor areas; language barriers; far-right politics and misinformation; bias against migrants' children. _(M1, M2, M3, M4, M5, M6, M7)_
- **Migration: positive cultural outcomes** — Cultural enrichment through food, music, sport and festivals; new places of worship; intercultural schools; mixed marriages. _(M8)_
- **Ireland's religious change: Census 2022 figures** — About 69% Roman Catholic (about 78% in 2016); about 14% no religion (about 10% in 2016); about 100,000 Orthodox Christians, 82,000 Muslims and 33,000 Hindus. The Islamic Cultural Centre of Ireland is in Clonskeagh, Dublin. _(C1)_
- **Ireland's growing diversity and the schools issue** — Census 2022: just over 1 million people (about one in five) born abroad and about 630,000 non-Irish nationals. About 9 in 10 primary schools are under Catholic patronage, so religion once decided many admissions (the baptism barrier). _(C1)_
- **Ireland: anti-immigration protests and the Dublin riot** — East Wall, Dublin: protests from November 2022 against housing protection applicants in a former office building. Dublin riot, 23 November 2023: after a knife attack on children on Parnell Square, buses, a Luas tram and shops were burned and looted. _(C1)_
- **Irish equality and anti-hatred laws** — Prohibition of Incitement to Hatred Act 1989; Employment Equality Act 1998 and Equal Status Act 2000 (nine grounds, including race, religion, Traveller community); Irish Human Rights and Equality Commission (2014); Criminal Justice (Hate Offences) Act 2024. _(C2)_
- **Irish integration and school policies** — Migrant Integration Strategy 2017–2020; National Action Plan Against Racism (March 2023); the Education (Admission to Schools) Act 2018 removed the baptism barrier in most Catholic primary schools; Educate Together, a multi-denominational body founded in 1984. _(C2)_
- **Irish Travellers: ethnic status and recognition** — An indigenous Irish ethnic minority, not a migrant group; formally recognised by the State as an ethnic minority on 1 March 2017; about 33,000 Travellers were counted in Census 2022. _(C1, C2)_
- **Irish policy on ethnic issues: evaluation** — The legal framework is strong, but protests from November 2022, the Dublin riot of November 2023 and arson attacks on buildings proposed for asylum accommodation (2023–24) show hostility persists; direct provision isolates applicants in rural centres and works against integration. _(C2)_
- **France: where its post-war immigrants came from** — Post-war immigrant labour came from Algeria, Morocco, Tunisia and Portugal; large-scale Algerian migration followed the war of independence (1954–62) and independence in 1962. France has the largest Muslim population in western Europe. _(C3)_
- **France: three laws on religion in public life** — 1905: laïcité, the strict separation of church and state; 2004: conspicuous religious symbols, including the Islamic headscarf, banned in state schools; 2010: face coverings such as the burqa and niqab banned in public places, from 2011. _(C3)_
- **France: the banlieues and the riots of 2005 and 2023** — Banlieues are segregated high-rise estates with high unemployment. In October–November 2005 riots began in Clichy-sous-Bois after two teenagers died fleeing police and spread nationwide; June 2023 riots followed a police shooting of a 17-year-old in Nanterre. _(C3)_
- **France: terrorism, Islamophobia and the far right** — The Charlie Hebdo attack (January 2015) and the Paris attacks of 13 November 2015 (130 killed) were followed by rising Islamophobia. The National Front, founded 1972 by Jean-Marie Le Pen, became the National Rally in 2018 under Marine Le Pen. _(C3)_
- **Germany: Turkish guest workers** — A 1961 agreement with Turkey brought guest workers until recruitment stopped in 1973. Many stayed, brought families and long lacked citizenship; segregated districts formed, such as Kreuzberg, Berlin. About 3 million people of Turkish origin live in Germany. _(C4)_
- **Germany: citizenship reforms and the AfD** — 2000 law: citizenship for children born in Germany to long-resident foreign parents; 2024 reform: dual citizenship generally allowed, shorter residence requirement. The AfD (founded 2013) grew after the 2015 refugee arrivals on an anti-immigration platform. _(C4)_
- **Britain: the Windrush generation and its legacy** — The Empire Windrush reached Tilbury in June 1948 with Caribbean passengers, who met racism in housing and work; Notting Hill riots, 1958; Race Relations Act 1965, the first such UK law; 2018 Windrush scandal: settled residents wrongly detained or deported. _(C5)_
- **Plantation of Ulster: the migration and its religious legacy** — From 1609, after the Flight of the Earls (1607), Protestant settlers from Scotland and England took land confiscated from Catholic Irish. Division led to the Troubles (late 1960s to 1998, about 3,500 killed) and the Good Friday Agreement (10 April 1998). _(C6)_
- **Northern Ireland today: religious balance and division** — Census 2021: about 46% Catholic and about 43% Protestant and other Christian. Most children attend schools of their own community, and peace walls still divide some Belfast neighbourhoods. _(C6)_
- **The Roma: key facts** — About 10–12 million in Europe, its largest ethnic minority; came from northern India about 1,000 years ago; face discrimination in housing, education and jobs; France expelled Roma to Romania and Bulgaria in 2010, drawing EU criticism. _(C7)_

### 6. Rural-to-urban migration in developed and developing regions (18)

- **Rural-to-urban migration: push factors from rural areas** — Lack of jobs and low farm incomes; mechanisation of farming; small, subdivided holdings; poor services (schools, hospitals, broadband, transport); drought, famine and land degradation in the developing world; isolation and lack of social life. _(P3)_
- **Rural-to-urban migration: pull factors to cities** — Jobs in industry and services; higher wages; third-level education; better health care and services; entertainment and the bright lights of the city; family already there, creating chain migration. _(P4)_
- **Ireland's urbanisation: when it happened and why** — Census 1971 was the first with more people in towns than rural areas; about 63% were urban by 2016. Farming mechanised and small farms became unviable, while industrial and service jobs concentrated in Dublin and the other cities. _(I1)_
