- **Growth in Irish cities: Census 2022 figures** — Dublin city and suburbs about 1.26 million; Co. Dublin about 1.46 million; Greater Dublin Area about 2.1 million (about 40% of the State). Cork about 220,000; Limerick about 100,000; Galway city about 85,000; Waterford about 60,000. _(I2)_
- **Dublin as a receiver region: positive impacts** — A large labour pool attracts multinationals and service firms; economies of scale support transport, universities and hospitals; a rich cultural life. _(I3)_
- **Dublin as a receiver region: negative impacts** — Housing shortages, high rents and prices, and homelessness; sprawl into Meath, Kildare and Wicklow and commuter towns such as Navan, Drogheda, Naas and Newbridge; long commutes and congestion; pressure on water supply and services. _(I3, I2)_
- **The West and North-West as a donor region: negative impacts** — An ageing population and higher dependency ratios; fewer young adults and births; closed schools, post offices, Garda stations, shops and pubs; derelict houses; GAA teams amalgamating; farms with no successor; Gaeltacht decline. _(I4)_
- **Rural decline in Ireland: Leitrim, Mayo and Census 2022** — Leitrim fell from about 155,000 people (1841) to about 35,000 (2022), the least populous county; Mayo has about 25 people per km², against about 1,600 in Co. Dublin. Every county grew from 2016 to 2022, so decline is now local, in remote areas. _(I4)_
- **Irish responses to rural decline: name six** — National Spatial Strategy (2002): gateways and hubs; Western Development Commission (from 1999); CLÁR (2001) for depopulated rural areas; decentralisation of public service jobs (2003); Údarás na Gaeltachta (1980); rural broadband and remote working hubs. _(I5)_
- **National Planning Framework (Project Ireland 2040): its aims** — Published February 2018: plans for about 1 million more people by 2040, with about half of the growth in the five cities (Dublin, Cork, Limerick, Galway, Waterford) and their suburbs, and the four cities other than Dublin growing faster than Dublin. _(I5)_
- **Italy and France: European examples of rural-to-urban migration** — Italy: post-war migration from the Mezzogiorno to Turin and Milan; €1 house schemes in depopulating villages such as Sambuca di Sicilia. France: Gravier's Paris et le désert français (1947); Île-de-France holds about 18% of France's people. _(E1, E2)_
- **India: why people migrate from rural to urban areas** — Migrants leave poorer rural states, Bihar (the densest, about 1,100 per km²) and Uttar Pradesh, for Mumbai, Delhi and Kolkata, pushed by small holdings, debt, landlessness and drought, and pulled by jobs in construction, factories and the informal sector. _(D1)_
- **India: impacts on the receiver cities** — About 65 million people lived in slums (2011): Dharavi, Mumbai, about 2 km² near the centre, with small industries (recycling, leather, pottery, garments); bustees in Kolkata. Water, sanitation and transport are strained, but cities gain cheap labour. _(D1)_
- **India: impacts on the donor villages** — Male out-migration leaves women, children and the old to farm; remittances fund houses, education and farm inputs; working-age labour is lost. In the March 2020 COVID-19 lockdown, millions of migrant workers walked or travelled home. _(D1)_
- **Brazil: migration from the North-East, from where to where** — Nordestinos left the sertão, the drought-prone semi-arid interior of the North-East, for São Paulo and Rio de Janeiro, and some helped build Brasília (capital from 1960). Brazil is now about 87% urban (2020s). _(D2)_
- **Brazil: impacts on the cities and on the North-East** — São Paulo city reached about 11.5 million (Census 2022), over 20 million in its metropolitan region; favelas grew on hillsides and at the urban edge, such as Rocinha in Rio; informal work, and gang crime in some favelas; the North-East loses young people. _(D2)_
- **China: rural migrant workers and the hukou system** — Nearly 300 million rural migrant workers (2023), many in coastal cities such as Shenzhen, which grew to over 17 million after becoming the first Special Economic Zone (1980). Under hukou (1958) they lack full urban services; children and elderly are left behind. _(D3)_
- **Rural-to-urban migration: developed and developing regions contrasted** — Developed: slower migration, absorbed by planned housing and services; the donor problem is ageing rural decline. Developing: fast, huge migration; cities lack housing, jobs and services, so slums and informal work grow; villages rely on remittances. _(D4)_

## Region concept

### 1. Physical regions (19)

- **Regions at different scales: give one example at each scale** — Local: Co. Clare (or a city's hinterland); national: the West of Ireland; continental: North West Europe; global: the Islamic world. _(R3)_
- **Climatic regions in matching questions: three types and their examples** — Cool temperate oceanic: Ireland and North West Europe. Warm temperate oceanic: the Mediterranean type, with hot, dry summers and mild, wet winters. Hot desert: e.g. the Sahara. _(C2)_
- **Cool temperate oceanic climate: Ireland's temperature figures** — Met Éireann long-term averages: mean January (coldest month) about 4–7°C; mean July (warmest month) about 14–16°C; so the annual range is only about 10°C. Snow is rare on low ground; rain falls in every month. _(C3)_
- **Annual rainfall across Ireland: the west–east figures** — About 1,000–1,250 mm a year across much of the west and over 2,000 mm on many western mountains, but only about 750 mm in the driest parts of the east, around Dublin, which lies in a rain shadow. _(C5)_
- **Wet days and sunshine in Ireland: the west–east contrast** — About 150 wet days (days with measurable rain) a year on the east and south-east coasts, rising to about 225 a year in parts of the west. The south-east is the sunniest region. _(C5)_
- **Why Ireland has a cool temperate oceanic climate: six controls** — Latitude (about 51.5°N to 55.5°N, moderate sun angle); the North Atlantic Drift (mild winters, ice-free coasts); maritime influence (small range); prevailing south-westerlies off the Atlantic; frequent Atlantic depressions; relief rain on western mountains. _(C4)_
- **North West Europe: which countries share Ireland's climate, and why** — Ireland, Britain, north-west France (Brittany), Belgium, the Netherlands, Denmark and coastal Norway. All face the Atlantic, share the North Atlantic Drift and lie in the path of westerly depressions. _(C6)_
- **Cool temperate oceanic region: how the climate changes moving east across Europe** — Away from the Atlantic, winters get colder, summers warmer, the annual range larger and rainfall lower, with a summer maximum (continental influence). The change is gradual, so the region's boundary is a transition zone, not a line. _(C7, R5)_
- **Cool temperate oceanic region: natural vegetation, soils and farming** — Deciduous forest, mainly oak and ash; brown earth soils; grass grows for most of the year, which favours pastoral farming in Ireland, mainly cattle and dairying. _(C8)_
- **The Burren: location, rock, name and evidence of glaciation** — North-west Co. Clare, on Carboniferous limestone; the name comes from the Irish Boireann, meaning rocky place. Granite erratics from Connemara rest on the limestone, showing that ice scraped off the soil and exposed the pavement. _(G2)_
- **The Burren: five karst features, naming the caves and the hill** — Limestone pavement of clints and grikes; turloughs; Aillwee Cave and Doolin Cave, which holds the Great Stalactite, about 7.3 m long; Mullaghmore, a hill of folded, terraced limestone; very few surface rivers, as drainage is underground. _(G2)_
- **The Burren: plant life, farming, heritage and protection** — Arctic-alpine and Mediterranean plants side by side (spring gentian, mountain avens); winterage cattle grazing; Poulnabrone, a Neolithic portal tomb on the pavement; Burren National Park near Mullaghmore; the Burren and Cliffs of Moher UNESCO Global Geopark. _(G2)_
- **Munster ridge and valley: how the landscape formed** — Armorican folding about 300 million years ago (east–west) made anticlines and synclines; differential erosion left the harder Old Red Sandstone as ridges and wore the Carboniferous limestone into valleys, which hold the towns, routes and best farmland. _(G3)_
- **Munster ridge and valley: named ridges, valleys and rias** — Sandstone ridges (anticlines): Macgillycuddy's Reeks, the Galtee, Knockmealdown and Caha Mountains. Limestone valleys (synclines): the Blackwater and Lee valleys. Rias: Bantry Bay, Dingle Bay and the Kenmare River. _(G3)_
- **River Blackwater: how its course shows trellised drainage** — It flows east along its limestone valley between parallel sandstone ridges, through Mallow, Fermoy and Lismore, with tributaries flowing off the ridges at right angles; then it turns sharply south at Cappoquin, Co. Waterford, to reach the sea at Youghal. _(G3)_
- **Northern European Plain: extent and rivers** — A vast, low, flat to gently rolling lowland from northern France through Belgium, the Netherlands, northern Germany, Denmark and Poland into Russia. The Rhine and Elbe flow north to the North Sea; the Oder and Vistula flow north to the Baltic. _(G4)_
- **Northern European Plain: surface deposits and farming** — Glacial deposits (moraines, outwash sands and lakes) shape the north; a belt of fertile loess, a wind-blown silt, along the southern edge grows wheat and sugar beet. _(G4)_
- **Northern European Plain: polders and human consequences** — Polders are reclaimed from the sea in the Netherlands: the Afsluitdijk, completed 1932, closed off the Zuiderzee. Flat, fertile land gives easy transport, a dense population and major cities such as Berlin, Warsaw, Hamburg and Amsterdam. _(G4)_
- **Three geomorphological regions: the criterion that defines each** — The Burren: rock type (Carboniferous limestone). Munster ridge and valley: geological structure (folded sandstone and limestone). Northern European Plain: relief and surface deposits (glacial, loess). _(G5)_

### 2. Administrative regions at different scales (14)

- **Administrative regions in Ireland: the hierarchy of scales, smallest to largest** — Townland and electoral division; local authority area (county or city council); Dáil constituency; regional assembly area; the State; the European Union, a supranational scale of 27 member states since the United Kingdom left in 2020. _(A3)_
- **Irish counties and provinces: the numbers and their status today** — There are 26 counties in the Republic and 32 on the island. The four provinces (Leinster, Munster, Connacht, Ulster) are historic and have no administrative function today. _(I1)_
- **County Dublin: two changes to its local government** — In 1994 County Dublin was divided into Fingal, South Dublin and Dún Laoghaire–Rathdown county councils, alongside Dublin City Council. Dublin Corporation became Dublin City Council in 2002 under the Local Government Act 2001. _(I2, I3)_
- **Local Government Reform Act 2014: the 31 local authorities and other changes** — 26 county councils, 3 city councils (Dublin, Cork, Galway) and 2 city and county councils (Limerick, Waterford); 949 councillors. Town councils were replaced by municipal districts, and North and South Tipperary merged into Tipperary County Council. _(I3, I2)_
- **Irish local councils: functions and funding** — Housing; roads and traffic; planning permission and development plans; libraries; the fire service; environment and waste; parks and amenities. Funded partly by the Local Property Tax and commercial rates; elections every five years (2019, 2024). _(I4)_
- **Cork City boundary extension, 2019: what it took in** — The boundary was extended in 2019 to take in the growing suburbs of Ballincollig, Blarney and Glanmire, and Cork Airport: an administrative boundary changing as population grows. _(I5)_
- **Irish regional assemblies and the 2000–2006 EU funding regions** — Three regional assemblies since 2015 (Northern and Western; Southern; Eastern and Midland) prepare regional plans and manage EU funds. From 2000 to 2006 the BMW region held Objective 1 status, the highest EU aid; the rest was the Southern and Eastern region. _(I6)_
- **Dáil constituencies: the rules on seats and representation** — Each constituency elects 3 to 5 TDs by PR-STV. The Constitution requires one TD for every 20,000 to 30,000 people, so boundaries are redrawn as population shifts between censuses. _(E1, E2)_
- **Electoral Commission's 2023 review: the outcome** — The Electoral Commission, established in 2023 to replace constituency commissions, created 43 constituencies electing 174 TDs, first used in the 2024 general election. _(E3)_
- **Why constituencies cross county boundaries and change over time** — A county's population may not fit a 3-to-5-seat constituency, so some combine counties, e.g. Sligo–Leitrim. Growing areas such as Dublin and its commuter belt gain seats; declining areas lose them. _(E4)_
- **French départements: when and why they were created** — Created in 1790 during the French Revolution to replace the old provinces, weaken regional loyalties and centralise power in Paris. Each was drawn so every place could reach its chef-lieu within about a day's ride on horseback. _(F1)_
- **French départements: the numbers to know, with named examples** — 101 départements: 96 metropolitan and 5 overseas. Each has a number, used for example as the first two digits of postcodes (Paris is 75). Corsica is split into two départements, numbered 2A and 2B. Many are named after rivers or mountains, e.g. Bouches-du-Rhône, Haute-Garonne, Pyrénées-Atlantiques. _(F2, F3)_
- **French département: who governs it and what it is responsible for** — A prefect (préfet) appointed by the national government, and an elected departmental council responsible for social welfare, roads and lower secondary schools (collèges). _(F4)_
- **France's three tiers of government and the 2016 regional reform** — About 35,000 communes (each with a mayor), départements and regions. In 2016 metropolitan regions were cut from 22 to 13 by merging, to compete in Europe; regions handle economic development, regional transport and lycées. Ireland is far more centralised. _(F5, F6)_

### 3. Cultural regions (20)

- **The Gaeltacht: its seven counties and why Irish survived there** — Scattered pockets in Donegal, Mayo, Galway, Kerry, Cork, Waterford and Meath, mainly along the western seaboard. Irish survived in remote, mountainous areas with poor farmland and communications, which had least contact with English-speaking trade and towns. _(L1, L2)_
- **Name the main Gaeltacht areas and their counties** — Gaoth Dobhair (Gweedore), Co. Donegal; Conamara and the Aran Islands, Co. Galway, the largest; Corca Dhuibhne (the Dingle Peninsula), Co. Kerry; Múscraí and Oileán Chléire, Co. Cork; An Rinn (Ring), Co. Waterford; Ráth Chairn and Baile Ghib, Co. Meath. _(L1)_
- **Meath Gaeltacht: how the State created a cultural region** — In the 1930s the State resettled Irish-speaking families from Connemara in Co. Meath, forming the two Meath Gaeltachtaí, Ráth Chairn and Baile Ghib: a cultural region created deliberately. _(L3)_
- **State support for the Irish language and the Gaeltacht: six measures** — Irish is the first official language (Article 8, 1937 Constitution); Údarás na Gaeltachta (1980) promotes jobs and Irish; Raidió na Gaeltachta (1972); TG4 (1996, as Teilifís na Gaeilge); Official Languages Act 2003; Irish-medium schools and summer colleges. _(L4)_
- **Threats to the Gaeltacht, and the Census 2022 speaker gap** — Out-migration of young people; in-migration of English speakers and holiday homes; English-language media and tourism. Census 2022: about 1.9 million said they could speak Irish, but only about 72,000 spoke it daily outside the education system. _(L5)_
- **Belgium: its language regions and where each lies** — Flanders (north): Dutch (Flemish), with more than half of Belgians; Wallonia (south): French; a German-speaking community in the east, around Eupen; Brussels-Capital: officially bilingual, inside Flanders but mostly French-speaking. _(B1)_
- **Belgium's language border and federal state: origin and dates** — The border marks the old boundary between Germanic- and Latin-speaking peoples and was fixed by law in 1962–63. Belgium became a federal state in 1993, with three regions and three language communities, each with its own parliament. _(B2, B3)_
- **Belgium: two examples of tension between the language groups** — In 1968 Leuven university split, and its French-speaking section moved to a new town, Louvain-la-Neuve, in Wallonia. After the 2010 election Belgium went 541 days without a government because Flemish and French-speaking parties could not agree. _(B4, B3)_
- **Belgium: how the language divide matches an economic divide** — Wallonia's coal and steel industries in the Sambre–Meuse valley (Charleroi, Liège) declined, while Flanders, with the port of Antwerp and newer industries, became the wealthier region; many Flemish people resent money transferred to Wallonia. _(B5)_
- **Basque Country and Catalonia: the key facts for each** — Basque Country: Basque (Euskara) is unrelated to any other European language, and the region crosses the border of Spain and France. Catalonia, north-east Spain: Catalan language, capital Barcelona, an unofficial independence referendum on 1 October 2017. _(O1, O2)_
- **Religion in the Republic of Ireland: Census 2022 figures** — About 69% Roman Catholic, down from about 78% in Census 2016; about 14% said they had no religion, a rising share. Immigration has added other religions, such as Orthodox Christians and Muslims. _(P1)_
- **Plantation of Ulster: how it created a religious region** — Begun in 1609, it brought Protestant settlers from Britain and created a Protestant majority in much of the north-east of Ulster. Religion became tied to political identity: mostly Catholic nationalists and mostly Protestant unionists. _(P2)_
- **Northern Ireland: religion at the local scale, and the Census 2021 change** — In Belfast, religion defines segregated residential areas, separated in places by peace walls (peace lines). Census 2021 showed, for the first time, more Catholics than Protestants in Northern Ireland. _(P3)_
- **Islam: founder, holiest cities and the start of its calendar** — Founded by the Prophet Muhammad in Arabia in the 7th century. Mecca and Medina, in present-day Saudi Arabia, are its holiest cities. The Islamic calendar begins with the Hijra, Muhammad's move from Mecca to Medina, in 622. _(M1)_
- **The Islamic world: its extent and size** — From Morocco in North Africa through the Middle East and Central Asia to Pakistan, Bangladesh and Indonesia, the country with the largest Muslim population. Muslims are almost a quarter of the world's population, so Islam is second only to Christianity. _(M2)_
- **How Islam spread, and its legacy in southern and south-eastern Europe** — Arab conquest across North Africa and into Spain from 711 (Moorish rule until Granada fell in 1492); trade routes to South and South-East Asia; the Ottoman Turks into the Balkans, leaving Muslim communities in Bosnia and Herzegovina, Albania and Kosovo. _(M3, M6)_
- **What makes the Islamic world a region: traits to name** — A shared faith built on the Five Pillars, including prayer facing Mecca and the Hajj; Arabic as the language of the Qur'an; mosques and minarets in the landscape; Islamic law (Sharia) in some states; shared customs of dress, food and calendar. _(M4)_
- **Divisions and migration in the Islamic world: Sunni, Shia and western Europe** — Sunni Muslims are the large majority; Shia are the majority in Iran and Iraq, and their rivalry adds to Middle East conflict. Migration has brought North Africans to France, Turks to Germany, and Pakistanis and Bangladeshis to Britain. _(M5, M6)_
- **Europe's three Christian regions: name the countries in each** — Roman Catholic south and centre: Italy, Spain, Portugal, Poland, Ireland, southern Germany. Protestant north: Scandinavia, northern Germany, Britain. Orthodox east and south-east: Greece, Russia, Romania, Serbia. _(Y1)_
- **Europe's religious divisions: origins and one conflict** — The Catholic and Orthodox churches split (Rome and Constantinople); Martin Luther began the Protestant Reformation in Germany in 1517. In the 1990s the break-up of Yugoslavia set Orthodox Serbs, Catholic Croats and Muslim Bosniaks at war in Bosnia. _(Y2, Y3)_

### 4. Socio-economic regions (17)

- **Region types in matching questions: core, peripheral or industrial decline?** — Core: the Paris Basin, the Greater Dublin Area. Peripheral: the West of Ireland, the BMW region, the Mezzogiorno, northern Norway. Industrial decline: the Sambre–Meuse valley, the Ruhr, the South Wales coalfield. _(C5, P4, D5)_
- **Core regions: seven characteristics** — High GDP per head, low unemployment; a large tertiary sector (finance, headquarters, high-tech); most foreign investment; net in-migration of young, educated workers; dense infrastructure (motorways, airports, ports, rail); universities; usually the capital. _(C2)_
- **Core regions: why they develop, and the problems of success** — Causes: accessibility; a large market and labour force; an early start; government and capital functions; agglomeration, as firms attract more firms. Problems of success: congestion, high house prices, urban sprawl and pressure on services. _(C3, C4)_
- **Greater Dublin Area as a core region: key facts** — Dublin, Kildare, Meath and Wicklow. County Dublin: about 1.45 million in Census 2022 (State about 5.1 million). Dublin has the seat of government, Dublin Port, Dublin Airport and European headquarters of technology firms such as Google in the docklands. _(C5)_
- **Paris Basin and the Blue Banana: core region facts** — Île-de-France, the region around Paris at the heart of the Paris Basin, has about 12 million people. The Blue Banana is the curved belt of Europe's main core, from south-east England and London through the Benelux countries and the Rhine valley to northern Italy (Milan). _(C5)_
- **Peripheral regions: characteristics** — Remoteness and poor access; mountains, high rainfall, poor soils, short growing season; small farms, fishing, forestry; low incomes, underemployment; out-migration of young adults, leaving an ageing population; fewer services; scattered settlements. _(P2)_
- **Peripheral regions: how governments and the EU respond** — EU regional and cohesion funds; state agencies channelling investment; better roads and broadband; decentralisation of government offices; tourism built on scenery and culture. _(P3)_
- **Name four peripheral regions, with one fact about each** — West of Ireland: the Northern and Western regional assembly area, where Co. Leitrim has the smallest population of any county. The Mezzogiorno, southern Italy. Northern Norway: Arctic, remote, reliant on fishing. North-west Scotland: the Highlands and Islands. _(P4)_
- **Peripheral vs less-developed regions: the overlap and the difference** — Most less-developed regions in Europe are also peripheral, such as the Mezzogiorno and much of eastern Europe (Romania, Bulgaria). A peripheral region can still lie in a rich country, such as the West of Ireland or northern Norway. _(L2)_
- **Ireland and EU Objective 1 status: what changed in 2000** — Ireland was a single Objective 1 region, receiving the highest level of EU aid, until 1999. From 2000 to 2006 only the BMW (Border, Midland and Western) region kept Objective 1 status. _(L2)_
- **Regions of industrial decline: five causes** — Coal seams exhausted or too costly to work; cheaper imports and competition from newly industrialised countries; coal replaced by oil and gas; out-of-date plant; automation needing fewer workers. _(D2)_
- **Regions of industrial decline: effects** — High unemployment, especially among older male workers; derelict factories, mines and spoil heaps; polluted land; out-migration; falling services; a poor image that deters new investment. _(D3)_
- **Regions of industrial decline: responses** — Retraining workers; clearing and cleaning derelict land; attracting new light industry and services; universities and technology parks; reusing industrial heritage for tourism and culture; EU funds. _(D4)_
- **The Ruhr: key facts as a region of industrial decline** — North Rhine-Westphalia, Germany: Europe's largest coal and steel region (Essen, Dortmund, Duisburg). Germany's last hard-coal mine, Prosper-Haniel in Bottrop, closed in 2018; Essen's Zollverein coal mine complex became a UNESCO World Heritage Site in 2001. _(D5)_
- **Sambre–Meuse valley and South Wales coalfield: industrial decline facts** — Sambre–Meuse, Wallonia, Belgium: coal mining and steel along the Sambre and Meuse, e.g. Charleroi and Liège, in contrast to prosperous Flanders. South Wales coalfield, including the Rhondda valleys: coal mining collapsed in the late 20th century. _(D5)_
- **Industrial decline in Ireland: two examples** — Belfast: the Harland and Wolff shipyard, which built the Titanic (launched 1911), declined greatly in the late 20th century. Dublin docklands: port jobs fell with containerisation; the IFSC, set up in 1987 on derelict dockland, regenerated the area. _(D6)_
- **Core vs peripheral regions: how economic activity differs** — Primary: large commercial farms on good land vs small, fragmented farms on poor land. Secondary: manufacturing and multinational investment vs few small, often grant-aided factories. Tertiary: finance, headquarters, government vs tourism, public services. _(K1)_

### 5. Nodal, city and urban regions (14)

- **City and hinterland: four flows that link them** — Jobs: commuters travel in daily. Services: shops, hospitals, third-level colleges, entertainment, offices. Goods: the hinterland supplies food, water, raw materials and labour; the city supplies manufactured goods. Information: local newspapers, radio and TV. _(F1, F2, F3, F4)_
- **Measuring a city's hinterland: methods to name** — Census commuting data; the catchment area of a hospital, college or secondary school; the circulation area of a local newspaper or reach of a local radio station; bus and rail routes and timetables; business delivery areas; home addresses of shoppers. _(M1)_
- **Hinterland boundaries: why they are not sharp lines, and how they change** — Neighbouring hinterlands overlap and different services give different edges; bigger cities have bigger hinterlands. Motorways, car ownership, faster rail and remote working extend commuter belts; new services in smaller towns can shrink a city's hinterland. _(M2, M3)_
- **Dublin as a nodal region: its transport focus and problems** — Dublin is the focus of the national motorway network, with radial routes meeting the M50 ring road, and of the main rail lines; it also has Dublin Port and Dublin Airport, Ireland's busiest airport. Problems: urban sprawl, long commutes and congestion, high house prices. _(I1, I2)_
- **Dublin's hinterland: name its commuter towns, and its national reach** — Navan (Co. Meath), Naas and Newbridge (Co. Kildare), Bray (Co. Wicklow) and Drogheda (Co. Louth). For government, national media, specialist hospitals and the main airport, Dublin's hinterland is the whole country. _(I1)_
- **Cork as a city region: key facts** — Ireland's second city, on the River Lee at the head of Cork Harbour; pharmaceutical plants at Ringaskiddy; commuter towns Carrigaline, Midleton and Cobh; its hinterland covers much of Munster. _(I3)_
- **Galway as a city region: key facts** — The main city of the West, serving a large rural hinterland that includes Connemara; a centre of the medical devices industry and of higher education, with the University of Galway. _(I4)_
- **National Planning Framework: its aim for Irish city regions** — Part of Project Ireland 2040, published in 2018, it aims to direct more growth to Cork, Limerick, Galway and Waterford to counterbalance Dublin. _(I6, I5)_
- **Paris as a nodal region: key facts** — France's primate city, at the centre of the Paris Basin; Île-de-France has about 12 million people. Radial motorways and TGV lines meet there (the first, Paris to Lyon, opened 1981), so its hinterland reaches across France; Charles de Gaulle Airport. _(E1)_
- **London as an urban region: key facts** — Capital of the United Kingdom; Greater London has about 9 million people (Census 2021); the City of London is a global financial centre; its commuter belt covers much of south-east England. _(E2)_
- **Brussels: why its influence extends across the EU** — Capital of Belgium and seat of the European Commission and the Council of the EU; the European Parliament meets both there and in Strasbourg. NATO headquarters is in Brussels too. _(E3)_
- **Rotterdam: an example of a port hinterland crossing borders** — Europe's largest port, at the mouth of the Rhine (Europoort). Its hinterland is the whole Rhine basin, linked by river barges, rail and pipelines, so it crosses national borders. _(E4)_
- **The Randstad: a polycentric city region** — A ring of cities in the Netherlands (Amsterdam, Rotterdam, The Hague, Utrecht) forming one large urban region around a rural Green Heart (Groene Hart), unlike the single-centre Paris and Dublin regions. _(E6)_
- **Milan and other cities named as urban regions in matching questions** — Milan: main city of Lombardy, northern Italy's core, and Italy's financial centre (Borsa Italiana). Also matched as urban regions: Munich (capital of Bavaria), Berlin, Rome, London, Brussels and Limerick. _(E5)_

## Region continental

### 1. India (36)

- **Sketch map of India: recommended features and how to place them** — Relief: outline the Himalayas as a band along the northern border, or the Deccan south of the Narmada. River: the Ganges from Uttarakhand south-east past Varanasi to its delta. Cities as dots: Mumbai (west coast), Kolkata (on the Hooghly). Farming region: outline Punjab. _(O3)_
- **South-west monsoon: how it forms, in sequence** — Heating of the land in early summer creates low pressure over north-west India and Tibet; the ITCZ moves north; south-east trades cross the equator and turn (Coriolis force) into moist south-west winds; the jet stream shifts north of the Himalayas and the monsoon bursts. _(C3, C4)_
- **India's three seasons and the timing of the monsoon** — Hot season, March–May; wet south-west monsoon, June–September, reaching Kerala about 1 June and all India by mid-July and bringing about three-quarters of the year's rain; cool dry season, October–February, when north-east winds blow off the land. _(C2, C6, C9)_
- **Monsoon branches: where the heaviest rain falls and why** — The Arabian Sea branch rises over the Western Ghats, soaking the Konkan and Malabar coasts; the Deccan lies in rain shadow. The Bay of Bengal branch hits the north-east hills (Mawsynram, Cherrapunji), then turns west along the Ganges plain, drier westwards. _(C5, C7, C8)_
- **Monsoon hazards, and why the monsoon matters to India** — A late or weak monsoon brings drought, a heavy one floods; cyclones strike the east coast (Odisha super cyclone, October 1999, about 10,000 deaths); climate change makes it more erratic. It sets the farming calendar, refills reservoirs and groundwater, and affects GDP. _(C12, C13)_
- **India's main soil types: where they are found and what they grow** — Alluvial: Indo-Gangetic Plain, most extensive and fertile. Black (regur): Deccan Traps, cotton. Red: south and east Deccan, millets, groundnuts, pulses. Laterite: Western Ghats and Kerala, tea, coffee, cashew with fertiliser. Desert: Thar, productive only where irrigated. _(S1, S2, S3, S4, S5)_
- **The Himalayas: formation, highest peak and effects on India** — Young fold mountains formed as the Indian plate collides with the Eurasian plate (from about 50 million years ago); Kangchenjunga (8,586 m) is India's highest peak. Effects: block cold Central Asian air; trigger monsoon rain; feed snow-fed rivers; earthquakes; sparse settlement. _(R2, R3)_
- **The Deccan Plateau and the Ghats: key features** — An ancient, stable shield of crystalline rock, once part of Gondwanaland, tilting west to east so most rivers flow east; minerals on the Chota Nagpur Plateau; Deccan Traps basalt in the north-west; the steep Western Ghats (UNESCO 2012) and lower, broken Eastern Ghats. _(R5, R6, R7)_
- **The Ganges: source, course, cities and delta** — Rises as the Bhagirathi at Gaumukh (Gangotri Glacier, Uttarakhand) and becomes the Ganga at Devprayag; flows about 2,500 km past Haridwar, Kanpur, Prayagraj (where the Yamuna joins), Varanasi and Patna to the Ganges–Brahmaputra delta, the world's largest. _(D2)_
- **Indian agriculture: importance and human problems** — About 55% of workers farmed (Census 2011), yet farming gives under a fifth of GDP: low productivity. Holdings average about 1.1 hectares, 86% under 2 hectares (2015–16), subdivided at inheritance; landless labourers; little credit, so debt; middlemen; post-harvest losses. _(A1, A9)_
- **Types of agriculture in India and where they are found** — Subsistence farming on small plots; wet-rice (paddy) in the wet east, south and deltas; irrigated commercial wheat in Punjab and Haryana; tea plantations in Assam, Darjeeling and the Nilgiri Hills; coffee in Karnataka; cotton on black soils; jute in West Bengal; dairying. _(A2)_
- **Physical advantages and problems for farming in India** — Advantages: heat allows two or three crops a year; monsoon rain; fertile alluvium and black soil; flat plains; perennial Himalayan rivers for irrigation. Problems: unreliable monsoon; poor laterite and desert soils; steep slopes; erosion; salinisation; falling water tables. _(A4, A5)_
- **Green Revolution: methods, results and costs** — From the mid-1960s: high-yielding wheat (Borlaug; brought in by M. S. Swaminathan) and rice, fertilisers and irrigation. Grain output rose from about 51 million tonnes (1950–51) to about 330 million (2022–23). Gains went mainly to Punjab and Haryana; costs: groundwater loss, debt. _(A7)_
- **White Revolution: how India became the largest milk producer** — Operation Flood (1970), run by the National Dairy Development Board under Verghese Kurien, spread co-operative dairying on the Amul model (Anand, Gujarat). Religion helps: the cow is sacred to Hindus, so cattle are kept for milk and draught, not beef. _(A8, G7)_
- **Indian farming: government support and recent conflict** — Minimum support prices, with grain bought by the Food Corporation of India; PM-KISAN (2019) pays ₹6,000 a year to landholding farm families. Conflict: the 2020–21 farmers' protests against three farm laws, repealed in November 2021; stubble burning worsening Delhi's smog. _(A10, A11)_
- **India's mining and energy: named examples** — The Chota Nagpur Plateau (Jharkhand, Odisha, Chhattisgarh) holds most coal and iron ore. India is the second-largest coal producer; coal gives about 70% of electricity. Mumbai High oilfield (1974). Bhadla Solar Park; target of 500 GW non-fossil capacity by 2030. _(P3, P4, P5, P6)_
- **Raw materials and the location of Indian industry: three examples** — Tata Iron and Steel (1907) at Jamshedpur used Chota Nagpur coal and iron ore; cotton from black soils fed mills in Mumbai and Ahmedabad, the 'Manchester of India'; jute from the Ganges delta fed mills along the Hooghly near Kolkata. _(M1, M5)_
- **Government policy and Indian manufacturing, 1951 to today** — 1951–1991: five-year plans, state steel plants (Bhilai, Rourkela, Durgapur), protection, the Licence Raj. 1991: Narasimha Rao and Manmohan Singh scrapped most licences, cut tariffs and opened to foreign investment. Later: SEZ Act 2005, Make in India 2014, incentives from 2020. _(M2, M3, M4)_
- **Labour, markets and infrastructure as factors for Indian industry** — Labour: a huge, young, low-cost workforce and engineers from the IITs (first at Kharagpur, 1951). Markets: over 1.4 billion people and a growing middle class. Infrastructure: ports, railways, the Golden Quadrilateral, but power cuts and congestion raise costs. _(M6, M7, M8)_
- **India's main manufacturing regions** — Mumbai–Pune; Ahmedabad–Vadodara (Gujarat); the Hooghly belt round Kolkata; Chota Nagpur and the Damodar valley (Jamshedpur); the Delhi National Capital Region (Gurugram, Noida); Bengaluru, Chennai, Coimbatore. Bihar, Uttar Pradesh and Odisha have less industry. _(M10)_
- **Key Indian industries with named examples** — Steel: second-largest producer. Cars: Maruti Suzuki (1983) at Gurugram and Manesar; Hyundai at Chennai. Medicines: Hyderabad; the Serum Institute, Pune, the largest vaccine maker. Electronics: Foxconn iPhones at Sriperumbudur. Aerospace: HAL and ISRO in Bengaluru. _(M11)_
- **Challenges facing manufacturing in India** — Poor infrastructure and power; land conflicts (at Singur in 2008 protests forced Tata to move its Nano plant to Sanand, Gujarat); bureaucracy; competition from China; accidents (Bhopal gas leak, 1984); old industries declining, as Mumbai's mills closed after the 1982 strike. _(M12)_
- **India's IT industry: centres and reasons for growth** — Centres: Bengaluru ('Silicon Valley of India'), Hyderabad, Pune, Chennai, Gurugram, Noida. Reasons: many English-speaking graduates; the IITs; low wages; a time difference allowing round-the-clock work for the USA; 1991 liberalisation and software technology parks. _(T2, T3)_
- **Tourism in India: name eight attractions** — The Taj Mahal, Agra, built by Shah Jahan for Mumtaz Mahal (UNESCO 1983); the Golden Triangle of Delhi, Agra and Jaipur; Goa's beaches; Kerala's backwaters at Alappuzha; Varanasi's ghats; Jim Corbett National Park (1936) and Kaziranga; the Golden Temple, Amritsar. _(T4)_
- **Tourism in India: why tourists come, and government support** — Mughal and Hindu monuments and festivals; varied landscapes; a warm, dry winter (October–March) matching Europe's; low costs; yoga, Ayurveda and medical tourism. Support: 'Incredible India' (2002); e-visas (2014); low-cost airlines. About 11 million foreign arrivals in 2019. _(T5, T6)_
- **Tourism in India: positive and negative impacts** — Positive: jobs in hotels, guiding, crafts and transport; foreign currency; infrastructure; protection of heritage and wildlife. Negative: crowding and pollution (air pollution yellows the Taj Mahal's marble); water shortages and waste in Goa; cultural erosion; seasonality. _(T7, T8)_
- **Transport in India: two named types with facts** — Rail: first train Bombay–Thane, 16 April 1853; Indian Railways runs about 68,000 km and employs over 1 million; Vande Bharat trains (2019); Mumbai–Ahmedabad high-speed line being built. Road: the Golden Quadrilateral (from 2001) links Delhi, Mumbai, Chennai and Kolkata. _(T9, T10)_
- **India's population: size, growth and demographic transition** — Census 1951 about 361 million; Census 2011 about 1.21 billion; India passed China in 2023 (UN). Death rates fell first (medicine, vaccination, Green Revolution food) while births stayed high; fertility was 2.0 in 2019–21 (NFHS-5), below replacement: late stage 3. _(Y1, Y2)_
- **India's population policy and sex ratio** — First national family-planning programme in the world (1952); forced sterilisation in the Emergency (1975–77) caused a backlash. Census 2011: 943 females per 1,000 males (918 aged 0–6), due to son preference; sex determination banned 1994; 'Beti Bachao Beti Padhao' (2015). _(Y4, Y5)_
- **Population distribution in India: dense and sparse areas, with reasons** — Dense: the Ganges plain (fertile alluvium, water, flat land), deltas, the Kerala and West Bengal coasts, big cities. Sparse: the Thar (aridity), the Himalayas (cold, steep, thin soils), north-east hills. Census 2011: Bihar about 1,100 per km², Arunachal Pradesh about 17. _(Y8, Y9)_
- **Languages in India: diversity, families and official status** — 22 languages in the Eighth Schedule; 121 spoken by 10,000 or more (Census 2011). Indo-Aryan in the north (Hindi, Bengali), Dravidian in the south (Tamil, Telugu). Hindi, mother tongue of about 44%, is the Union's official language; states were redrawn by language in 1956. _(L1, L2, L3, L4)_
- **Religion in India: composition and distribution** — Census 2011: Hindu about 80%; Muslim about 14% (about 172 million); Christian 2.3%; Sikh 1.7%; Buddhist 0.7%; Jain 0.4%. Sikhs are concentrated in Punjab; Christians are the majority in Nagaland, Mizoram and Meghalaya; Muslims in Jammu and Kashmir. _(G1, G2)_
- **The caste system in India today** — A hereditary social system linked to Hinduism, not a religion. The Constitution (1950) abolished untouchability; Scheduled Castes (about 17%) and Scheduled Tribes (about 9%) have reserved places in education, government jobs and parliament. Caste still shapes rural life. _(G6)_
- **Mumbai: factors in its growth** — Seven islands joined by reclamation round a deep, sheltered harbour; a port built up by the East India Company; 19th-century cotton mills; the financial capital (Reserve Bank, Bombay Stock Exchange 1875); Bollywood; rural migrants. Greater Mumbai: about 18.4 million (2011). _(U4, U2)_
- **Mumbai: urban problems and responses** — Dharavi, one of Asia's largest slums; overcrowding, high housing costs, packed suburban trains; the 26 July 2005 flood (about 944 mm in a day at Santacruz); poor water and sanitation. Responses: Navi Mumbai twin city (from the 1970s), slum redevelopment, metro lines. _(U4)_
- **Rural India: problems and development policies** — About 833 million rural people (69%, 2011) face poverty, debt, tiny holdings and poor services. Policies: Panchayati Raj (1992, a third of seats for women); MGNREGA (2005, 100 days' paid work); rural roads (2000); Swachh Bharat (2014); village electrification (2018). _(V1, V2, V3)_

## Region european

### A Core European Region: the Paris Basin (26)

- **Sketch map of the Paris Basin: what to show and where** — River: the Seine, with Paris, Rouen and Le Havre (at its mouth) on it. Cities: Paris plus Rouen, Le Havre or Reims. Relief: the Beauce lowland, with rim uplands drawn as its edge. Farming: the Beauce (cereals) or Champagne (vines). Industry: the Lower Seine. _(R3)_
- **Paris Basin: location, boundaries and why it is a core region** — A low sedimentary basin in northern France, open to the English Channel, ringed by the Armorican Massif, Massif Central, Vosges and Ardennes. Core: the capital; France's largest concentration of people, wealth, industry and services; fertile farmland; the transport hub. _(R1, R2)_
- **Paris Basin climate: figures and the west–east change** — Paris: January about 5°C, July about 20°C, about 600–650 mm of rain spread through the year. Oceanic in the west, more continental eastwards, with colder winters and warmer summers inland. Champagne lies near the northern limit of commercial vine growing. _(P1)_
- **Paris Basin relief and soils** — A saucer of limestone, chalk, clay and sand layers dipping towards Paris, mostly below 300 m, with outward-facing escarpments and clay vales in the east. Deep, fertile limon covers the Beauce, Brie and Picardy plateaux; Champagne has thin chalk soils; clay suits grass. _(P2, P3)_
- **The Seine: course, tributaries and flooding** — About 775 km from the Langres plateau through Troyes, Paris and Rouen to Le Havre; the Aube, Yonne, Marne and Oise converge on Paris; gentle gradients make it navigable. It flooded in January 1910 and June 2016; upstream reservoirs such as Lac du Der-Chantecoq regulate it. _(P4)_
- **Paris Basin agriculture: physical advantages** — Low, gently rolling relief suits large mechanised fields; limon soils are fertile and easily worked; warm summers ripen cereals and grapes over a long growing season; dry weather at harvest. France is the EU's largest farm and wheat producer. _(A1)_
- **The Beauce: cereal farming in the 'granary of France'** — South-west of Paris around Chartres. Wheat, barley and maize, with sugar beet and oilseed rape; many farms exceed 100 hectares; large, highly mechanised farms on flat limon land give high yields. _(A2)_
- **Champagne: how and where its vines are grown** — Vines grow on chalk escarpment slopes around Reims and Épernay (on the Marne). Only sparkling wine from this AOC area may be called Champagne; houses such as Moët & Chandon age it in chalk cellars, adding value. UNESCO World Heritage Site since 2015. _(A3)_
- **Dairying and market gardening in the Paris Basin** — Wetter clay grassland supports dairying in Normandy (Camembert) and Brie (Brie cheese). Market gardening of vegetables and flowers on the edge of Paris gets fresh produce quickly to a huge urban market. _(A4)_
- **Human and economic factors in Paris Basin farming** — Remembrement consolidated scattered plots after the Second World War; mechanisation; co-operatives and agribusiness; France gets the largest share of CAP spending; the Paris market, served by Rungis (1969, replacing Les Halles); rural depopulation as farms mechanised. _(A5, T4)_
- **Energy in the Paris Basin: nuclear power and oil refining** — Nuclear power gives about two-thirds of France's electricity, from stations by cooling water: Nogent-sur-Seine (Seine), Dampierre and Saint-Laurent (Loire), Paluel and Penly (Normandy coast). Imported crude is refined at Gonfreville-l'Orcher near Le Havre. _(A8)_
- **Why manufacturing concentrated in and around Paris** — France's largest market and labour force; head offices and capital; universities and research; the hub of road, rail, river and air transport; government. Food processing uses local sugar beet, cereals and Champagne grapes, saving the cost of moving bulky crops. _(M1, M4, M7)_
- **Paris Basin car industry and industry leaving the inner city** — Renault at Flins and PSA (now Stellantis) at Poissy, both west of Paris. Renault's Boulogne-Billancourt plant on Île Seguin closed in 1992 as industry moved from the inner city to suburban and provincial sites. _(M2)_
- **Lower Seine industrial axis and Paris-Saclay** — Le Havre, France's largest container port, and Rouen, its main grain-exporting port, have oil refining and petrochemicals; with Paris they form HAROPA (2021). Paris-Saclay, south-west of Paris, is a research and high-technology cluster. _(M3, M5)_
- **Decentralisation policy and its effect on Paris industry** — From the 1950s–60s new industry in Paris was restricted and steered to the provinces: DATAR (1963); eight métropoles d'équilibre (1964), such as Lyon, Marseille and Lille; the 1965 plan's new towns. Paris lost manufacturing but kept head offices and research. _(M6)_
- **Name the five new towns built around Paris** — Cergy-Pontoise, Évry, Marne-la-Vallée, Melun-Sénart and Saint-Quentin-en-Yvelines, created under the 1965 regional plan (schéma directeur) for Paris. _(M6, H6)_
- **Paris as a primate city and service centre: key facts** — Government, finance, head offices and universities; OECD and UNESCO headquarters; La Défense, one of Europe's largest business districts. Île-de-France has about 12 million people (about 18% of France) and produces about 30% of French GDP. _(T1)_
- **Paris Basin tourism: named attractions and problems** — Eiffel Tower (1889); the Louvre, the most visited art museum; Notre-Dame (fire 2019, reopened 2024); Versailles; Disneyland Paris, Marne-la-Vallée (1992); the 2024 Olympic Games; Champagne cellar tours. Problems: congestion, seasonal pressure, wear on sites. _(T2)_
- **Paris Basin transport: road, rail, air and river** — Radial network centred on Paris; Périphérique (1973); TGV Paris–Lyon (1981); Channel Tunnel and Eurostar (1994); RER; Grand Paris Express (about 200 km, 68 stations); Charles de Gaulle (1974), France's busiest airport, and Orly; river port at Gennevilliers. _(T3)_
- **Population distribution in the Paris Basin, with reasons** — Île-de-France about 1,000 per km²; City of Paris about 2.1 million on 105 km², about 20,000 per km²; far lower in rural Champagne and the outer basin. Reasons: jobs, services and transport concentrate in Paris; mechanised farms need few workers. _(H1)_
- **Population dynamics and migration in the Paris Basin** — Gravier's 'Paris and the French desert' (1947) argued Paris drained France; rural-to-urban migration gave the region a young age structure; retirees move out; post-war immigrants came from Algeria, Morocco, Tunisia and Portugal. _(H2)_
- **Culture of the Paris Basin: language, religion, politics and sport** — Standard French grew from the Paris dialect; traditionally Catholic, with low attendance, laïcité (1905) and a large North African Muslim minority; a centralised state; Paris Saint-Germain, Roland-Garros, the Tour de France finish, Stade de France (1998). _(H3, H4, H5)_
- **Factors in the growth of Paris as an urban area** — Site: the Île de la Cité, a Seine crossing. Situation: centre of the basin, where the Seine's tributaries converge (the Marne joins just upstream, the Oise just downstream). Capital of a centralised state; hub of radial transport; rich limon farmland; France's largest market and labour force. _(H8)_
- **Urban development of Paris: main planning stages** — Haussmann rebuilt the centre (1853–1870) with boulevards, apartment blocks, parks and sewers; grands ensembles such as Sarcelles (1950s–70s); the Périphérique; new towns (1965 plan); La Défense; Métropole du Grand Paris (2016, about 130 communes); Grand Paris Express. _(H6)_
- **Urban problems of Paris** — Congestion; high housing costs; segregation and deprivation in the banlieues, where Seine-Saint-Denis is the poorest département in mainland France; riots that began in Clichy-sous-Bois in October 2005; urban sprawl. _(H7)_
- **Rural development in the Paris Basin** — Fewer farmers on larger holdings; depopulation of the outer basin, part of the 'diagonale du vide'; commuter villages growing near Paris; second homes and rural tourism bringing visitors and money. _(H9)_

### 2. A Peripheral European Region: the Mezzogiorno (26)

- **The Mezzogiorno: its eight regions and its share of Italy** — Abruzzo, Molise, Campania, Puglia, Basilicata, Calabria, Sicily and Sardinia (not Rome or Lazio). About 40% of Italy's area and about a third of its people: about 20 million of 59 million. 'Mezzogiorno' means midday. _(R1)_
- **Why the Mezzogiorno is a peripheral region** — Far from the EU core and northern Italian markets; mountainous land and dry summers; history (joined united Italy in 1861, with land held in large estates); organised crime; emigration. It makes about 22% of Italy's GDP; unemployment is two to three times the North's. _(R2)_
- **Sketch map of the Mezzogiorno: features to name** — Rivers: Volturno (Campania), Ofanto (Puglia), Basento (Basilicata). Cities: Naples, Bari, Taranto, Palermo, Catania. Relief: the Apennines, the Sila, Etna, Vesuvius; the Tavoliere lowland. Areas: the Tavoliere (durum wheat); the Bari–Brindisi–Taranto industrial triangle. _(R3)_
- **Mezzogiorno climate: causes and figures** — Hot, dry summers as subtropical high pressure moves north (July 25°C or more); mild, wet winters from Atlantic depressions (January 9–12°C), most rain October–March. Under 600 mm on parts of Puglia and Sicily, more on the Apennines. Sirocco: hot, dusty wind from the Sahara. _(P1)_
- **Impact of climate on primary activity in the Mezzogiorno** — Summer drought causes water shortage, so farmers grow drought-tolerant olives, vines and durum wheat and irrigate citrus; winter downpours cause flash floods and soil erosion; summer wildfires destroy woodland. The Apulian Aqueduct carries water to dry Puglia. _(P1, A5, P5)_
- **Relief of the Mezzogiorno and its effects on people** — The Apennines form the spine (Corno Grande, 2,912 m), with the Calabrian uplands and the Sila; lowlands are small and coastal: the Tavoliere di Puglia, the Campanian plain, the plain of Catania. Steep slopes limit farming and transport and push villages onto hilltops. _(P2)_
- **Earthquakes and volcanoes in the Mezzogiorno: named examples** — Where the African and Eurasian plates meet. Earthquakes: Messina (28 December 1908, tens of thousands killed), Irpinia (1980), L'Aquila (2009, magnitude 6.3). Volcanoes: Vesuvius (AD 79; last erupted 1944), Etna (about 3,300 m), Stromboli; Campi Flegrei near Naples. _(P3)_
- **Soils of the Mezzogiorno: types and problems** — Thin, stony upland soils; terra rossa on limestone (Puglia); fertile volcanic soils on Vesuvius and Etna; alluvial lowland soils. Problems: erosion from deforestation, overgrazing and downpours (calanchi in Basilicata); Sarno mudflows (1998, about 160 killed). _(P4)_
- **Traditional farming in the Mezzogiorno and the 1950 land reform** — Latifundia of absentee landlords, worked by braccianti, grew wheat extensively and grazed sheep; hill farms were small and fragmented; productivity was low. Land reform laws in 1950 took parts of the large estates and gave plots to peasants. _(A1, A2)_
- **Cassa per il Mezzogiorno: what it did for the land** — The Fund for the South (1950–1984) paid for land reclamation, drainage, irrigation dams, aqueducts and roads, and new farmhouses and villages on lowlands made safe once malaria was eradicated; it reafforested the Sila. From the late 1950s it also funded industry. _(A2, A5)_
- **Modern agriculture in the Mezzogiorno: crops and where they grow** — Durum wheat for pasta on the Tavoliere around Foggia; olive oil in Puglia, Italy's leading region; blood oranges on the plain of Catania; lemons on the Amalfi coast and around Sorrento; San Marzano tomatoes near Vesuvius; wine in Puglia and Sicily. _(A3)_
- **Problems facing agriculture in the Mezzogiorno** — Drought and water shortage; soil erosion; Xylella fastidiosa, found in the Salento in 2013, has killed millions of olive trees; ageing farmers and rural depopulation; caporalato, the exploitation of migrant farm workers; dependence on CAP support. _(A4)_
- **Why industry is weak in the Mezzogiorno** — Distance from markets; poor roads, railways and ports; a small local market with low incomes; little capital or enterprise; organised crime demanding extortion (pizzo); few raw materials. _(M1)_
- **Growth poles in the Mezzogiorno: named examples and criticism** — State plants backed by the Cassa: the Taranto steelworks (Italsider, 1960s), one of Europe's largest; petrochemicals at Brindisi and Priolo–Augusta (Sicily); a refinery at Gela; the Bari–Brindisi–Taranto triangle. 'Cathedrals in the desert': few jobs or spin-offs. _(M2)_
- **Car and aerospace plants in the Mezzogiorno** — Alfasud at Pomigliano d'Arco near Naples (1972), now building the Fiat Panda; Fiat's Melfi plant, Basilicata (early 1990s, now Stellantis); Fiat's Termini Imerese plant, Sicily, closed in 2011. Leonardo builds Boeing 787 fuselage sections at Grottaglie, near Taranto. _(M3, M5)_
- **Problems for Mezzogiorno industry: pollution, crime, black economy** — In 2012 a court seized parts of the Taranto steelworks over pollution linked to illness; Cosa Nostra, the 'Ndrangheta, the Camorra and Sacra Corona Unita demand pizzo, deterring investment; lavoro nero (undeclared work) is widespread. _(M4)_
- **Recent policy to develop the Mezzogiorno** — Most of the South is an EU 'less developed region' for 2021–27; the single Special Economic Zone for the South (ZES Unica) began in January 2024 with tax credits and simpler permits; Italy's post-Covid recovery plan reserves at least 40% of allocated funds for the South. _(M5)_
- **Tourism in the Mezzogiorno: attractions and problems** — Climate, coast, volcanoes and food; UNESCO sites: Pompeii and Herculaneum, the Amalfi Coast, Agrigento's Valley of the Temples (all 1997), Alberobello's trulli (1996), Matera's sassi (1993). Problems: seasonality, abusivismo on coasts, crime, slow transport. _(T1)_
- **Transport in the Mezzogiorno and its importance** — Autostrada del Sole (A1, Milan–Naples, 1964); decades of slow upgrading on the A2 to Reggio Calabria; high-speed rail only to Naples and Salerno; Gioia Tauro, Italy's largest transhipment port; ferries to Sicily, Greece and Albania. Poor links keep the region remote. _(T2)_
- **Population distribution in the Mezzogiorno, with reasons** — Dense coastal lowlands and cities: Campania has about 400 people per km². Sparse interior uplands: Basilicata has about 55 per km² and only about 540,000 people. Fertile volcanic lowlands, water and city jobs attract people; thin soils and steep, dry uplands do not. _(H1)_
- **Emigration from the Mezzogiorno: when and where** — From the late 19th century to the USA and Argentina; after the Second World War to Turin and Milan and to Germany, Switzerland and Belgium; today young graduates still leave for northern Italy and abroad. _(H2)_
- **Fertility, ageing and immigration in the Mezzogiorno** — Italy's fertility is about 1.2 children per woman (2023), Sardinia's among the lowest; inland villages age and empty, some selling €1 houses (Sambuca di Sicilia); Lampedusa, south of Sicily, is a main landing point for migrants crossing the Mediterranean. _(H2)_
- **Culture of the Mezzogiorno: language, religion, music and sport** — Neapolitan and Sicilian dialects; Arbëreshë, Griko, Sardinian and Catalan (Alghero) minorities; strong Catholic practice, as at the Feast of San Gennaro, Naples; the tarantella; 'O sole mio'; SSC Napoli (Serie A 2023, 2025); Neapolitan pizza (UNESCO 2017). _(H3, H4, H5)_
- **Factors in the growth of Naples** — A site on the Bay of Naples beside Vesuvius; a port for sea trade; capital of the Kingdom of the Two Sicilies until 1861; a fertile volcanic hinterland; rural in-migration. About 900,000 people in the city and about 3 million in the metropolitan city. _(H8)_
- **Urban problems of Naples** — Overcrowding in the Spanish Quarter; the Vele di Scampia estates, linked to Camorra drug dealing (several demolished); the 2007–08 waste crisis; illegal dumping and burning in the Terra dei Fuochi; unplanned growth and illegal building. _(H6)_
- **Rural settlement and development in the Mezzogiorno** — Hilltop villages were built for defence and to escape malarial lowlands; land-reform farmhouses dot the plains; Matera's sassi were evacuated in the 1950s, became a UNESCO site (1993), and Matera was European Capital of Culture (2019); interior villages are emptying. _(H7)_

### 3. The complexity of regions (i) (20)

- **Northern Ireland and the Republic: two political regions, key facts** — Northern Ireland (1921): six counties, Antrim, Armagh, Down, Fermanagh, Londonderry (Derry) and Tyrone, part of the UK. The Irish Free State (1922) became a republic in 1949. Population: Republic about 5.15 million (2022); Northern Ireland about 1.9 million (2021). _(I1)_
- **Cultural groups in Northern Ireland: identity, religion and schooling** — Nationalists, mostly Catholic, identify as Irish; unionists, mostly Protestant, as British; some as Northern Irish. Census 2021: Catholic about 46%, Protestant and other Christian about 43%, the first Catholic plurality. Schools are largely segregated. _(I2)_
- **Good Friday Agreement: what it set up and how it was approved** — Signed 10 April 1998; passed by referendum on 22 May 1998 (about 71% Yes in Northern Ireland, 94% in the Republic). It set up a power-sharing Assembly and the North/South Ministerial Council, the consent principle, and the right to Irish or British citizenship or both. _(I3)_
- **The Troubles and the Northern Ireland Assembly since 1998** — The Troubles (late 1960s to 1998) killed over 3,500 people. Under the Agreement the Republic changed Articles 2 and 3 of its Constitution. The Assembly has often been suspended; restored in February 2024, it has Michelle O'Neill as first nationalist First Minister. _(I3)_
- **Economic links between the Republic and Northern Ireland** — Conflict deterred investment and tourism until 1998. Cross-border bodies: InterTradeIreland, Waterways Ireland, Tourism Ireland. Single Electricity Market (2007); Enterprise rail and the M1/A1. International Fund for Ireland (1986); EU PEACE (from 1995; now PEACE PLUS). _(I4)_
- **Cultural interaction across the Irish border** — The GAA, the Irish rugby team, the Catholic Church and the Church of Ireland are all-island bodies; each church's Archbishop of Armagh is Primate of All Ireland. The Identity and Language Act 2022 recognised Irish and Ulster-Scots; parades and flags still cause tension. _(I5, I2)_
- **Brexit and the Irish border: key facts** — Referendum, 23 June 2016: UK about 52% Leave; Northern Ireland about 56% Remain. The UK left on 31 January 2020, so the 499 km border became a UK–EU land border. The Protocol, revised by the Windsor Framework (2023), avoids border checks; Irish passports keep EU rights. _(I6)_
- **Belgium: language groups and regions** — Independent 1830; about 11.8 million people (2024). Flanders (Dutch, about 6.8 million) in the north; Wallonia (French, about 3.7 million) in the south; bilingual Brussels (about 1.2 million); German speakers under 80,000. French long dominated government. _(B1)_
- **Belgium: economic reversal and political consequences** — Wallonia's coal and steel (Liège, Charleroi, Sambre–Meuse) declined as Flanders grew through Antwerp, Europe's second-largest port. Leuven university split (1968); no nationwide parties; 541 days without a government (2010–11); N-VA's Bart De Wever became Prime Minister. _(B1)_
- **Spain and Scotland: cultural groups seeking independence** — Basque (Euskara) is unrelated to other European languages; ETA ended violence in 2011 and dissolved in 2018. Catalonia's 1 October 2017 referendum was ruled illegal and Spain imposed direct rule (Article 155). Scotland voted about 55% No to independence in 2014. _(B3, B4)_
- **EU political union: the Constitution, Lisbon and Parliament** — French and Dutch voters rejected the draft EU Constitution in 2005. The Lisbon Treaty (2009) created a permanent European Council President and a High Representative for Foreign Affairs and extended qualified majority voting. Parliament: 720 MEPs, 14 from Ireland. _(F1)_
- **The euro: key dates, members and the European Central Bank** — Launched 1 January 1999 for banks; notes and coins from 1 January 2002. 21 members in 2026: Croatia joined in 2023 and Bulgaria on 1 January 2026, replacing the lev. The European Central Bank in Frankfurt sets one interest rate for the euro area. _(F2)_
- **Why some EU states do not use the euro** — Denmark has a legal opt-out; Sweden voted No in a 2003 referendum and stays out by not joining the exchange-rate mechanism; Poland, Czechia, Hungary and Romania have not adopted it, by choice or because they have not met the convergence criteria. _(F2)_
- **Euro convergence criteria: the five conditions, with figures** — Low inflation; a government deficit no more than 3% of GDP; government debt no more than 60% of GDP; a stable exchange rate, held for two years in ERM II; low long-term interest rates. _(F2)_
- **Benefits of the euro for people living in the eurozone** — No currency exchange costs; easy price comparison between countries; no exchange-rate risk in trade and travel; generally low interest rates and inflation. _(F2)_
- **Economic union: the debt crisis, banking union and joint borrowing** — Ireland's EU/IMF bailout of about €85 billion (November 2010, exited December 2013); Greece had three bailouts (2010, 2012, 2015), ending in 2018. Banking union: ECB supervision of big banks from November 2014. NextGenerationEU (2020): about €750 billion of joint EU borrowing for recovery. _(F2)_
- **Sovereignty: Ireland and EU treaty referendums** — Crotty v An Taoiseach (1987): major EU treaty changes need a referendum. Ireland rejected Nice (2001), then passed it (2002); rejected Lisbon (2008), then passed it (2009). Ireland is militarily neutral, outside NATO, and outside Schengen because of the Common Travel Area. _(F3)_
- **Sovereignty: tax, Brexit and rule-of-law disputes** — Tax, defence and foreign policy stay largely national, decided by unanimity. Ireland's 12.5% rate now has a 15% minimum for large firms (2024); in September 2024 the EU Court of Justice ruled Ireland must recover €13 billion from Apple. Brexit reasserted UK sovereignty. _(F3)_
- **Social and economic challenges facing the EU** — Social: an ageing population; migration and asylum pressure; youth unemployment; populism and euroscepticism; regional inequality. Economic: public debt; competitiveness; energy security after Russia invaded Ukraine (24 February 2022); the cost of enlargement; Brexit. _(F4)_
- **Future EU enlargement: candidates and arguments against** — Candidates: Ukraine and Moldova (June 2022), Bosnia and Herzegovina (December 2022), Georgia (December 2023), Albania, Montenegro, North Macedonia, Serbia, Turkey (since 1999, talks frozen). Against: accession and funding costs, slower decisions, doubts about readiness. _(F5)_

### 4. The complexity of regions (ii) (25)

- **Irish speakers 1851 to 1891, and why the language declined** — Census 1851: about 1.5 million Irish speakers (23%); 1891: about 680,000 (14%). Causes: the Famine (1845–49) and emigration hit poor western Irish-speaking areas hardest; national schools (1831) taught through English; English was the language of law, trade and emigration. _(G1, G2)_
- **Gaeltacht boundaries: how they were drawn and redrawn, 1926 to 2012** — 1926: Coimisiún na Gaeltachta drew a large Gaeltacht across many western and southern counties. 1956: the Gaeltacht Areas Order shrank it to where Irish was still the community language. 2012: the Gaeltacht Act based it on language use, with 26 Language Planning Areas. _(G4, G5, G8)_
- **Supports for the Irish language and the Gaeltacht since 1893** — Gaelic League (1893, Douglas Hyde, Eoin MacNeill); Department of the Gaeltacht (1956); Údarás na Gaeltachta (1980), e.g. Gaoth Dobhair estate; Raidió na Gaeltachta (1972); TG4 (1996); Official Languages Act 2003; full EU working language (2022); 20-Year Strategy 2010–2030. _(G3, G5, G7)_
- **The Gaeltacht today: counties and Census 2022 figures** — Seven counties: Donegal, Mayo, Galway, Kerry, Cork, Waterford and Meath (colonies at Ráth Chairn, 1935, and Baile Ghib, 1937). Galway has the largest Gaeltacht population, then Donegal. Census 2022: about 106,000 people, about 20,000 speaking Irish daily outside education. _(G6)_
- **Continuing decline of Irish in the Gaeltacht: evidence and pressures** — The 2007 study found Irish survives as a community language only where 67% or more speak it daily; its 2015 update warned most districts could lose it within about ten years. Pressures: in-migrants who speak English, Galway's spread west, tourism, youth emigration. _(G9)_
- **Strategies to address the decline of Irish in the Gaeltacht** — Housing with language conditions; jobs through Údarás na Gaeltachta; Irish-medium schools and preschools; a language plan for each Language Planning Area; family supports and Irish-language media such as TG4 and Raidió na Gaeltachta. _(G10)_
- **Belgium: how a language boundary was made official** — The border between Dutch- and French-speaking areas was fixed by laws of 1962 and 1963. The first state reform came in 1970, and Belgium became a federal state in 1993, with three regions: Flanders, Wallonia and bilingual Brussels-Capital. _(G11)_
- **Dublin's urban growth and the Greater Dublin Area** — Myles Wright's plan (1967) proposed new towns at Tallaght, Clondalkin, Lucan and Blanchardstown. Ballymun's seven 15-storey towers (1966–69) were demolished 2004–2015. Census 2022: County Dublin about 1.46 million; Greater Dublin Area about 2.1 million (about 40%). _(U1)_
- **Dublin council structures: how and why they changed** — Rathmines and Pembroke were added to the city in 1930. On 1 January 1994 Dublin County Council was split into Fingal, South Dublin and Dún Laoghaire–Rathdown, because the county was too big to govern as one; Dublin Corporation became Dublin City Council in 2002. _(U2, U3)_
- **Regional assemblies and other Irish boundary changes** — In 2015 the Dublin Regional Authority (1994) was replaced by the Eastern and Midland Regional Assembly, one of three (with Northern and Western, and Southern). Limerick and Waterford city and county councils merged (2014); Cork city took in Ballincollig, Glanmire and Blarney (31 May 2019). _(U4, U6)_
- **Dublin city region: problems and planning responses** — Problems: sprawl, long commutes, congestion, housing costs, dormitory towns. Responses: National Planning Framework (2018), half of growth in five cities; M50 (from 1990), Luas (2004), DART; Docklands and Ballymun regeneration; a directly elected mayor recommended in 2023. _(U5)_
- **Origins of the EU: reasons and founding steps** — Reasons: peace after two world wars, rebuilding, shared control of coal and steel, growth, the Cold War. Schuman Declaration (9 May 1950); ECSC (1951); EEC and Euratom (Treaty of Rome, 1957). The Six: Belgium, France, West Germany, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands. _(E1)_
- **Deepening of the EU: key treaty and market dates** — Single European Act 1986; single market from 1 January 1993; Maastricht Treaty signed 7 February 1992, creating the EU (in force 1 November 1993); euro 1999, with notes and coins in 2002; Lisbon Treaty in force 1 December 2009. _(E2)_
- **EU enlargement, 1973 to 1995: who joined when** — 1973: Ireland, the UK and Denmark. 1981: Greece. 1986: Spain and Portugal. 1990: East Germany, through German reunification. 1995: Austria, Finland and Sweden. _(E3)_
- **EU enlargement since 2004: who joined, and who left** — 2004: Cyprus, Czech Republic, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Malta, Poland, Slovakia and Slovenia. 2007: Bulgaria and Romania. 2013: Croatia, the most recent member. The UK left on 31 January 2020, leaving 27 members. _(E3)_
- **Eastern European expansion: background and effects** — After the Berlin Wall fell (9 November 1989), ex-communist states sought entry under the Copenhagen criteria (1993); most 2004 entrants and both 2007 entrants were ex-communist. New members gained growth and EU funds but lost emigrants; older members faced competition. _(E4)_
- **Impact of EU enlargement on Ireland: advantages and disadvantages** — Advantages: a larger market; workers for the Celtic Tiger (Ireland opened its labour market at once in 2004); diversity, with Polish the main other home language. Disadvantages: competition for FDI (Dell to Łódź, 2009); less EU regional funding; pressure on services. _(E5)_
- **Benefits of further EU expansion** — For the EU: a larger single market with more consumers, and greater stability on its borders. For candidate countries: access to the single market and EU funding, and reforms to meet the Copenhagen criteria. _(E6)_
- **Partition of Ireland: how the border was drawn** — The Government of Ireland Act 1920 partitioned the island; Northern Ireland (1921) took six of Ulster's nine counties, leaving Cavan, Donegal and Monaghan to the Free State. The Anglo-Irish Treaty followed (1921); the Boundary Commission (1925) left the border unchanged. _(D1)_
- **Partition of Ireland: impact on cultural groups** — It left a nationalist minority in the north and a small Protestant minority in the south. Discrimination against northern nationalists fuelled the civil rights movement (1968) and the Troubles (over 3,500 killed); the border divided communities, parishes and farms. _(D1)_
- **Break-up of Yugoslavia: boundary changes and impact on cultural groups** — Borders followed old republic lines, so the independence of Slovenia and Croatia (1991) and Bosnia and Herzegovina (1992) stranded Serb, Croat and Bosniak minorities. War and ethnic cleansing followed: Srebrenica (July 1995, about 8,000 killed); Dayton (1995) split Bosnia in two. _(D2)_
- **Post-war Poland and Germany: boundary changes and their impact** — In 1945 Poland's western border moved to the Oder–Neisse line and the USSR took its eastern lands; millions of Germans were expelled westwards and Poles resettled from the east. Germany was divided (Berlin Wall 1961–1989) and reunified on 3 October 1990. _(D3)_
- **Break-up of the USSR and Czechoslovakia: impact on cultural groups** — The USSR split into 15 states in 1991, leaving ethnic Russians as minorities, about a fifth to a quarter of the people in Estonia and Latvia. Czechoslovakia split peacefully into the Czech Republic and Slovakia on 1 January 1993: the Velvet Divorce. _(D4)_
- **Cyprus: a divided island inside the EU** — Divided since the Turkish invasion of July 1974, with the UN Green Line through Nicosia; only Turkey recognises the north. In the 2004 Annan Plan vote about 65% of Turkish Cypriots said Yes, 76% of Greek Cypriots No. Cyprus joined the EU in 2004; EU law is suspended in the north. _(D5)_
- **Partition of India, 1947: impact on cultural groups** — In August 1947 India and Pakistan (including East Pakistan, now Bangladesh) were created along religious lines; around 15 million people were displaced and hundreds of thousands were killed in communal violence. _(D6)_

## Region ireland

### 1. A Core Irish Region (25)

- **Greater Dublin Area sketch map: what to draw** — The four counties (Dublin and Wicklow on the coast, Meath to the north with a short coastline, Kildare to the west); the Liffey from the Wicklow Mountains to Dublin Bay; the Boyne through Trim and Navan; Dublin, Navan, Naas and Bray; the Wicklow Mountains and the Meath–Kildare lowland. _(R3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area climate: why the east is the driest part of Ireland** — Prevailing south-westerlies drop their rain on the Wicklow Mountains and uplands to the west, so the east is in a rain shadow: about 750–1,000 mm a year (Dublin Airport about 750 mm) vs 1,000–1,250 mm in the west. The south-east is Ireland's sunniest part. _(P1)_
- **Greater Dublin Area relief: lowland and uplands, with named peaks** — Most of Meath, Kildare and north Co. Dublin is low, gently undulating lowland of Carboniferous limestone under glacial deposits. The Wicklow and Dublin Mountains have a Leinster granite core; Lugnaquilla (925 m) is Leinster's highest peak; Kippure about 757 m. _(P2)_
- **Greater Dublin Area soils: types, locations and land use** — Brown earths on limestone boulder clay in Meath, Kildare and north Co. Dublin: top farmland. Free-draining sand and gravel on the Curragh: horse training. Raised bog: the Bog of Allen. Acidic podzols and blanket bog (Sally Gap) on Wicklow granite: forestry. _(P3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area drainage and Dublin's water supply** — The Liffey rises in the Wicklow Mountains and enters Dublin Bay; its Poulaphouca dam formed the Blessington Lakes (hydro-electricity, drinking water). The Vartry Reservoir at Roundwood also supplies Dublin; Uisce Éireann has proposed a Shannon pipeline. _(P4)_
- **Greater Dublin Area agriculture: factors that favour farming** — A dry, relatively sunny climate for tillage and horticulture; flat lowland for large, mechanised fields; deep, fertile brown earths; the huge Dublin market on the doorstep; Dublin Port and Airport for exports; EU and Irish policy and modern technology. _(A1)_
- **Greater Dublin Area agriculture: farm types and where they are** — Meath: beef fattening and tillage on large farms. Kildare: bloodstock, with the Curragh racecourse and the Irish National Stud at Tully. Fingal: horticulture (field vegetables, glasshouse crops) around Rush and Lusk. Wicklow: sheep on upland pasture. _(A1)_
- **Greater Dublin Area agriculture: problems farmers face** — Urban sprawl takes farmland; high land prices push farmers to sell to developers; part-time farming near the city; costly and scarce seasonal labour for horticulture; competition from imported vegetables; changes to the CAP. _(A2)_
- **Tara Mines and other mining and quarrying in the Greater Dublin Area** — Tara Mines, Navan: underground zinc and lead mine, producing since 1977, Europe's largest zinc mine, owned by Boliden. Sand and gravel from glacial deposits; granite at Ballyknockan, Co. Wicklow. Bord na Móna has ended peat harvesting in the Bog of Allen. _(A5)_
- **Greater Dublin Area energy: named sites and facts** — Poolbeg, Ringsend: gas-fired power and the Dublin Waste-to-Energy incinerator (since 2017). Turlough Hill: ESB pumped storage (1974). Arklow Bank: Ireland's first offshore wind farm. Data centres used about a fifth of Ireland's metered electricity in 2023. _(A5)_
- **Greater Dublin Area manufacturing: how its location pattern changed** — Older brewing, food and port industries near Dublin Port and the inner city declined; newer high-tech and pharmaceutical plants are in suburban business parks and towns near the M50 and motorways, such as Grange Castle, Leixlip and Blanchardstown. _(S1)_
- **Intel, Pfizer and Guinness: Greater Dublin Area manufacturing examples** — Intel, Leixlip, Co. Kildare: opened 1989, Ireland's largest private-sector investment (billions of euro), near the M4. Pfizer: biopharmaceuticals at Grange Castle Business Park, Clondalkin. Guinness: St James's Gate Brewery, founded 1759 on a 9,000-year lease. _(S2)_
- **Greater Dublin Area manufacturing: markets as a factor** — About 2.1 million people, the largest concentration of consumers in the State; Dublin Port and Dublin Airport give access to the EU single market; ferries to Holyhead reach Britain; high-value products are flown out quickly. _(S3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area manufacturing: labour as a factor** — A large, young, educated workforce; graduates from Trinity College Dublin, UCD, DCU, TU Dublin and Maynooth University; immigrant workers add skills and languages; commuters from Meath, Kildare and Wicklow widen the pool. _(S3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area manufacturing: government policy as a factor** — IDA Ireland promotes the region to multinationals with sites and grants; corporation tax is 12.5%, rising to 15% from 2024 for very large groups (turnover over €750 million) under the OECD agreement; EEC membership since 1973 opened European markets. _(S3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area manufacturing: problems and challenges** — High costs of land, housing and wages; traffic congestion; a housing shortage for workers; competition from lower-cost countries; dependence on multinationals that can relocate; global corporation tax changes; strain on electricity and water supply. _(S4)_
- **Greater Dublin Area tourism: named attractions with dates** — Guinness Storehouse (2000), Ireland's most visited fee-paying attraction; Book of Kells, Trinity College; Convention Centre Dublin (2010); Brú na Bóinne (UNESCO, 1993; Newgrange about 5,000 years old); Glendalough, founded by St Kevin in the 6th century. _(T1)_
- **Greater Dublin Area transport: port, airport and roads** — Dublin Port, Ireland's busiest, with ferries to Holyhead; Dublin Airport, Ireland's busiest, over 30 million passengers in 2023, north runway 2022; radial M1, M3, M4, M7 and M11 joined by the M50; the Dublin Port Tunnel (2006). _(T3)_
- **Greater Dublin Area transport: problems and public transport responses** — Congestion on the M50 and radial roads, long commutes from the commuter counties and car dependence from sprawl. Responses: DART (1984), Luas (2004), BusConnects, the planned MetroLink to the airport and Swords, cycle lanes, the NTA's 2022–2042 strategy. _(T4, T3)_
- **IFSC and Silicon Docks: Dublin's financial and technology services** — The International Financial Services Centre was set up in 1987 at Custom House Docks; the Dublin Docklands Development Authority (1997) regenerated the docklands; Google's European headquarters is on Barrow Street, Grand Canal Dock, in 'Silicon Docks'. _(T5)_
