- **Greater Dublin Area population: Census 2022 figures** — About 2.1 million, about 40% of the State's 5.1 million. Co. Dublin about 1.45 million; Kildare about 250,000; Meath about 220,000; Wicklow about 156,000. Dublin city and suburbs about 1.26 million. _(H1)_
- **Greater Dublin Area: account for the distribution of population** — Highest densities in Dublin city and suburbs (jobs), about 1,600 people per km² in Co. Dublin against a State average of about 73; then commuter towns along motorways and rail lines; lowest on the Wicklow uplands (steep relief, poor soils). _(H1)_
- **Dublin's development: site and early growth** — Founded as a Viking settlement in 841 at the lowest bridging point of the Liffey, sheltered by Dublin Bay; Norman rule expanded it; the Wide Streets Commissioners (1757) laid out Georgian streets. Port, route focus and capital functions drove growth. _(H3)_
- **Dublin's development: 20th-century expansion and renewal** — Wright Report (1967): new towns at Tallaght, Clondalkin and Lucan. Ballymun flats (1960s) demolished in regeneration from the 2000s. Temple Bar regenerated in the 1990s; IFSC and Docklands renewal; commuter towns: Navan, Naas, Maynooth, Bray, Greystones. _(H3)_
- **Language and religion in the Greater Dublin Area** — English-dominant, made multilingual by immigration; growing Gaelscoileanna; the Ráth Chairn Gaeltacht, Co. Meath (1935, Connemara families). Catholic share below the State's 69%; Christ Church and St Patrick's cathedrals; Islamic Cultural Centre, Clonskeagh. _(H6, H7)_

### 2. A Peripheral Irish Region: the West of Ireland (26)

- **West of Ireland (Galway, Mayo, Roscommon): why it is a peripheral region** — Remote from Dublin and European markets; high rainfall, poor soils, rugged relief; a small, scattered, ageing population after long out-migration; dependence on primary activities and the public sector; weaker infrastructure. Galway city is the exception. _(R1, R2)_
- **West of Ireland sketch map: what to draw** — The outline of Galway, Mayo and Roscommon; the River Corrib (or the Moy, or the Shannon on the eastern edge); Galway city and Castlebar; an upland such as the Twelve Bens, Croagh Patrick or Mweelrea; the limestone lowland of east Galway and Roscommon. _(R3)_
- **West of Ireland climate: why it is so wet and windy** — Prevailing south-westerlies bring Atlantic depressions and relief rain on western mountains: about 1,000–1,250 mm a year on lowlands, over 2,000 mm on some mountains. The North Atlantic Drift keeps winters mild; frequent gales (Storm Éowyn, January 2025). _(P1)_
- **West of Ireland relief: named uplands and their rocks** — Mweelrea (814 m), Connacht's highest peak; Nephin (806 m); Croagh Patrick (764 m, quartzite); the quartzite Twelve Bens and the Maumturks in Connemara, with marble and mica schist. East Galway and Roscommon are low limestone lowland. _(P2)_
- **West of Ireland soils: types and where they are found** — Blanket bog over much of west Mayo (Erris, Bellacorick) and Connemara; acidic, leached podzols on uplands; poorly drained gleys on drumlins; better brown earths on limestone in east Galway and Roscommon; Aran Islands soil made from sand and seaweed. _(P3)_
- **West of Ireland drainage: named lakes, rivers and turloughs** — The short Corrib flows from Lough Corrib, the Republic's largest lake, through Galway to the sea; Lough Mask drains underground to Lough Corrib near Cong; Rahasane turlough; the Moy reaches Killala Bay at Ballina; the Shannon and Lough Ree edge Roscommon. _(P4)_
- **West of Ireland agriculture: physical factors that limit it** — Heavy rain waterlogs the land, making tillage and hay saving difficult; leached, acidic soils, steep uplands and exposure shorten the effective grazing season, so most land suits only grazing. Better land lies on the limestone of east Galway and Roscommon. _(A1, P1)_
- **West of Ireland agriculture: farm structure, problems and responses** — Small, fragmented farms, a rundale legacy, partly consolidated by the Congested Districts Board (1891); older farmers, low incomes, part-time farming, reliance on CAP; overgrazed commonage after 1980s headage payments. Responses: REPS (1994), diversification. _(A2, A4)_
- **West of Ireland farm types** — Cattle rearing with suckler herds, selling store cattle at local marts to eastern farmers for fattening; sheep on hill commonage in Connemara and Mayo; little tillage or dairying; Connemara ponies; the Ballinasloe October Fair, an ancient horse fair. _(A3)_
- **Fishing and aquaculture in the West: named places** — Rossaveel: a State Fishery Harbour Centre and the Aran Islands ferry port. Rope-grown mussels in Killary Harbour; salmon farms in Connemara bays; seaweed processing at Kilkieran (Arramara Teoranta); salmon angling on the Moy at Ballina. _(A6)_
- **Corrib gas field: key facts** — Off north-west Mayo: discovered in 1996; gas first flowed in December 2015 to the Bellanaboy terminal. The 'Shell to Sea' campaign opposed it, and the 'Rossport Five' were jailed in 2005. _(A7)_
- **Energy in the West: from peat to wind and waves** — Bellacorick's peat-fired station closed in 2003; Bellacorick wind farm (1992) was Ireland's first commercial wind farm, and Oweninny wind farm is on nearby cutaway bog. The Atlantic Marine Energy Test Site off Belmullet tests wave energy. _(A7)_
- **Galway medical devices cluster: how it developed** — Digital Equipment Corporation closed its Galway plant in 1993, leaving skilled workers; Boston Scientific opened in 1994, then Medtronic (Parkmore); suppliers and start-ups clustered, linked to the University of Galway: now a leading European medtech cluster. _(S2)_
- **Manufacturing outside Galway city: name four Mayo multinationals** — Outside Galway city, manufacturing is limited to a few towns: Baxter Healthcare, Castlebar; Allergan (now part of AbbVie), Westport, making Botox; Hollister, Ballina; Coca-Cola concentrate plant, Ballina. _(S3, S1)_
- **West of Ireland manufacturing: government policy as a factor** — IDA Ireland; Údarás na Gaeltachta grants; EU Structural Funds for roads and broadband; the Western Development Commission (1998); Galway a gateway in the National Spatial Strategy (2002), to grow at least 50% by 2040 (National Planning Framework, 2018). _(S4)_
- **West of Ireland manufacturing: problems** — Peripheral location and high transport costs; small, scattered markets; a small labour pool outside Galway and out-migration of skilled young people; dependence on a few multinationals; closures such as DEC; poor rural infrastructure. _(S5, S4)_
- **West of Ireland tourism: landscape attractions and routes** — Connemara National Park (Letterfrack), Killary Harbour fjord, Croagh Patrick, Achill Island, Clew Bay, Dún Aonghasa on Inis Mór; the Wild Atlantic Way (2014, about 2,500 km); the Great Western Greenway (about 42 km, Westport to Achill, 2010). _(T1)_
- **West of Ireland tourism: culture and heritage attractions** — Céide Fields (Neolithic fields over 5,000 years old under bog); Kylemore Abbey; Westport House; Strokestown Park and the National Famine Museum; Rathcroghan; Knock Shrine; Gaeltacht culture; Galway's Arts Festival, Races and 2020 European Capital of Culture. _(T1)_
- **West of Ireland transport: improvements, with dates** — M6 Dublin–Galway (2009); M17/M18 Galway–Tuam (2017); Ireland West Airport Knock (1986, promoted by Monsignor James Horan); rail to Dublin from Galway, Westport and Ballina; Western Rail Corridor reopened Ennis–Athenry (2010), but not north of Athenry. _(T3)_
- **West of Ireland population: Census 2022 figures and density** — About 486,000, about 9% of the State. Co. Galway including the city about 278,000 (Galway City Council area about 85,000); Mayo about 138,000; Roscommon about 70,000. Mayo has about 25 people per km², against a State average of about 73. _(H1)_
- **West of Ireland: account for the distribution of population** — Highest in Galway city and its suburbs (jobs, services, transport); moderate on the east Galway, Roscommon and east Mayo lowlands (better land, roads); very low on the uplands and bogs of west Mayo and Connemara (poor soils, steep slopes, exposure). _(H1)_
- **West of Ireland population dynamics** — The Great Famine (late 1840s) hit the West hardest; long emigration left populations far below pre-Famine levels. Young adults still leave (brain drain), so it is ageing, but every county grew 2016–2022 with return migration, immigration and Galway's growth. _(H2)_
- **Galway city: factors in its development, and its problems** — Lowest crossing of the Corrib on Galway Bay; 13th-century Anglo-Norman walled town; trading port, 'City of the Tribes' (14 merchant families), Spanish Arch; university since 1845; DEC then medtech. Problems: congestion, housing costs, no ring road. _(H3)_
- **Gaeltacht areas in the West, and why daily Irish speaking is declining** — Galway (Connemara, Cois Fharraige, the Aran Islands: the largest Gaeltacht) and Mayo (Erris, Achill, Tourmakeady); Roscommon has none. Decline: emigration, English-language media, tourism, in-migration of English speakers, English in the home. _(H6)_
- **Strategies to address the decline in daily Irish speakers in the Gaeltacht** — Language-planning areas under the Gaeltacht Act 2012; the 20-Year Strategy 2010–2030; Údarás na Gaeltachta jobs for young people; Irish-medium schools and summer colleges; housing reserved for Irish speakers; TG4 and Raidió na Gaeltachta. _(H6)_
- **Religion in the West: named sites and facts** — Higher Catholic share than Dublin. Knock Shrine: apparition reported 1879; papal visits 1979 and 2018. Croagh Patrick: Reek Sunday pilgrimage, last Sunday in July. Ballintubber Abbey (1216): Mass celebrated continuously. Tuam: Catholic archdiocese seat. _(H7)_

## Rivers

### 1. Fluvial processes, patterns and associated landforms (17)

- **River Shannon: where it rises and where it reaches the sea** — Ireland's longest river rises at the Shannon Pot, Co. Cavan, and enters the Atlantic through the Shannon Estuary below Limerick, where fresh and salt water meet and deposition builds mudflats and salt marsh. _(R1, D4)_
- **Long profile: which landforms belong to each course** — Upper (youthful) course: V-shaped valleys, interlocking spurs, waterfalls, rapids, potholes; vertical erosion dominates. Middle (mature): meanders as the valley widens. Lower (old): wide floodplain, levees, meanders with oxbow lakes, a delta or estuary. _(R2, R4)_
- **River energy: what it depends on and what it is used for** — Energy depends on volume (discharge) and gradient. Energy left after overcoming friction is used for erosion and transport. Volume increases downstream as tributaries join. _(R3)_
- **River deposition: five situations in which it happens** — Velocity falls when the river enters a lake or the sea, on the inside of a bend, as floodwater recedes, or on a gentler gradient; deposition also happens when the load is too great for the river's energy. Heaviest material is dropped first, finest last. _(T3, T4)_
- **V-shaped valley and interlocking spurs: how they form, with an example** — A steep gradient gives the river energy to cut down by hydraulic action and abrasion; weathering and mass movement widen the valley top into a V; the river winds around harder rock, leaving interlocking spurs, e.g. the upper River Liffey, Wicklow Mountains. _(L1)_
- **Waterfall and gorge: how they form, naming the erosion processes** — Hard rock lies over softer rock; hydraulic action and abrasion erode the soft rock faster, undercutting the hard rock into an overhang; falling water and load scour a plunge pool; the overhang collapses and the falls retreat upstream, leaving a gorge. _(L2, X2)_
- **Name three waterfalls, one abroad and two Irish, with a fact about each** — Niagara Falls, USA–Canada border: a dolomite cap over weaker shale; its retreat has cut the Niagara Gorge. Powerscourt Waterfall, River Dargle, Co. Wicklow: Ireland's highest, about 121 m. Torc Waterfall, near Killarney, Co. Kerry. _(L2)_
- **Meander: how erosion and deposition shape it** — Water flows fastest on the outside of a bend, eroding a river cliff by hydraulic action and abrasion; on the slow inside it deposits a point bar (slip-off slope); helicoidal flow carries material across, and lateral erosion makes the bends more pronounced. _(L4)_
- **Name an Irish example of meanders on a floodplain** — The Shannon Callows, between Athlone and Portumna: meanders on a wide floodplain of seasonally flooded meadows. _(L4, D1)_
- **Oxbow lake: how it forms, with an example** — Meanders grow until only a narrow neck separates them; in a flood the river cuts through the neck and takes the shorter course; deposition seals off the old bend as an oxbow lake, which later silts up into a meander scar. Example: the lower Mississippi. _(L5)_
- **Floodplain and levees: how floods build them** — Migrating meanders widen the valley floor. In flood the river spreads out, slows and drops alluvium, building a flat floodplain; the coarsest material drops first beside the channel, raising the banks into levees, and the bed may rise above the floodplain. _(D1, D2)_
- **Delta: conditions needed and how it forms** — A river carrying a large load enters calm water with a small tidal range and weak currents, and slows sharply; in salt water, clay flocculates and sinks; deposits block the channel, so it splits into distributaries; topset, foreset and bottomset beds build up. _(D3)_
- **Delta types: describe each, with examples where checked** — Arcuate (fan-shaped): the Nile delta, Egypt. Bird's-foot: the Mississippi delta, USA. Estuarine: fills a drowned river mouth. Lake delta: between the Upper and Lower Lakes at Glendalough, Co. Wicklow, which divided a single lake. _(D3)_
- **Drainage patterns: Irish examples of trellised, deranged and centripetal** — Trellised: the ridge-and-valley landscape of Munster, where rivers follow the limestone valleys and tributaries flow off the sandstone ridges at right angles. Deranged: the drumlin belt of Co. Cavan. Centripetal: Lough Neagh. _(P2, P4, P5)_
- **OS map skill: how to recognise each drainage pattern** — Dendritic: branching like a tree, joining at acute angles; trellised: parallel streams with tributaries joining at right angles; radial: spreading out from a high point; centripetal: converging on a lake; deranged: no clear pattern, between many small lakes and marshes. _(P6, P1, P3)_
- **OS map skill: how to spot a V-shaped valley and a floodplain** — In a V-shaped valley the contours form a V that points upstream. On a floodplain the contours are widely spaced. _(L1, D1)_
- **Classify river landforms: erosion, deposition or both** — Erosion: V-shaped valley, interlocking spurs, waterfall, gorge, potholes, rapids. Both erosion and deposition: meander, oxbow lake. Deposition: floodplain, levee, delta, point bar, estuary mudflats. _(X1)_

### 2. Fluvial adjustment to base level (11)

- **Base level: ultimate vs temporary, with three temporary examples** — Sea level is the ultimate base level. A lake, a band of resistant rock, or the main river (for its tributary) acts as a temporary, local base level. _(B1)_
- **Rejuvenation: four causes** — A eustatic fall in sea level, as water is locked in ice sheets in a glacial period; isostatic uplift, as land pressed down by ice rises after it melts; tectonic uplift by earth movements; less commonly, more discharge from a wetter climate or river capture. _(C1, C2, C3, C4, C5)_
- **Rejuvenation: how it spreads along a river** — It begins at the mouth and works upstream by headward erosion. For a long time the river has two graded sections, a new, steeper lower one and the old, gentler upper one, reflecting two base levels: a landscape temporarily out of balance. _(C6, B2)_
- **Knickpoint: how it forms and changes over time** — Rejuvenation cuts down from the mouth, making a new graded section below the old one; where they meet, a break of slope forms, often with a waterfall or rapids; it retreats upstream over time and disappears once the whole river grades to the new base level. _(K1)_
- **Incised meanders: how they form, with two examples** — Renewed vertical erosion after base level falls cuts existing meanders down into the land, leaving them in steep-sided valleys with no floodplain. Examples: the River Wye, Wales–England border; the Goosenecks of the San Juan River, Utah, USA (entrenched). _(K2)_
- **River terraces: how they form, with an example** — First, base level falls and the river cuts down into its own floodplain; then it forms a new floodplain at the lower level; remnants of the old floodplain are left as flat steps above it. Example: the River Thames in and around London, England. _(K3)_
- **Gorge from uplift: name an example and how it formed** — The Grand Canyon, Arizona, USA, cut by deep incision of the Colorado River as the Colorado Plateau was uplifted. _(K4)_
- **Rejuvenation diagram: how to count the number of rejuvenations** — On a long profile, count the knickpoints: one per rejuvenation. On a valley cross-section, count the terrace levels above the present floodplain: one per rejuvenation. _(K5)_
- **Name five rejuvenation features to give when asked for features not shown** — Knickpoint; a waterfall or rapids at the knickpoint; incised meanders; river terraces (e.g. paired terraces); gorge. _(K6)_
- **Rise in base level: effects on a river and its mouth** — Sea level rising, or land sinking, reduces the river's energy; the lower course is drowned, deposition increases and the mouth becomes a ria or an estuary. Coastal landforms from changes in base level: raised beaches (a fall) and rias (a rise). _(S1, S2)_
- **Rivers of north-west Ireland: why they were rejuvenated** — The land rose isostatically after the Midlandian ice melted about 10,000 years ago. The north, under thicker ice, rose more, as shown by raised beaches at Malin Head, Co. Donegal. _(S3, C3)_

### 3. Cyclic landscape development and peneplains (11)

- **Davis's cycle of erosion: who, when and its key assumption** — William Morris Davis, an American geographer, published the geographical cycle in 1899. It assumes rapid uplift followed by a long period of stability while rivers and weathering wear the land down; landscape depends on structure, process and stage (time). _(D1)_
- **Davis's youth stage: what the landscape looks like** — An uplifted plateau with high relief; steep gradients and vertical erosion; deep V-shaped valleys with waterfalls and rapids; wide, flat interfluves not yet eroded; few tributaries. _(D2)_
- **Davis's maturity stage: what the landscape looks like** — Maximum relief; valleys widen by lateral erosion; meanders and floodplains develop; the drainage network is fully developed; interfluves are reduced to ridges. _(D3)_
- **Davis's old age stage: what the landscape looks like** — Low relief; very wide floodplains, large meanders, oxbow lakes and levees; rivers flow sluggishly close to base level; divides are lowered to gentle rises. The end product is a peneplain, with monadnocks of resistant rock rising above it. _(D4, D5, D6)_
- **Cycle of erosion: what interrupts it and what results** — Uplift, isostatic rebound or a fall in sea level rejuvenates the rivers and starts a new cycle before the old one is finished. A landscape with remnants of more than one cycle is polycyclic. _(D7, I2)_
- **Davis's model: four criticisms and one strength** — Walther Penck: uplift and denudation act together, not in turn; the cycle is too slow to observe; it ignores climate change, glaciation and the isostatic response to erosion; few true peneplains exist at sea level. Strength: a simple framework by stage. _(D9)_
- **Munster: how folding created its rock structure** — Folding about 300 million years ago (Variscan, also called Armorican) formed east–west ridges of resistant Old Red Sandstone (anticlines) and valleys of softer Carboniferous limestone (synclines). _(M1)_
- **Munster planation surface: how it formed and the evidence for it** — Over a very long period of denudation the region was worn down towards a near-level surface cutting across the folds. Evidence: flat or gently sloping upland surfaces and summit levels at similar heights that cut across folded rocks of different types. _(M2, M3, D8)_
- **Munster: how later uplift re-exposed the ridges and valleys** — Uplift raised the planation surface and rejuvenated the rivers, which cut down again; renewed erosion removed the softer limestone faster than the sandstone, so the ridge-and-valley relief was re-exposed by differential erosion. _(M4)_
- **Munster Blackwater: its course and the usual explanation for its turn** — It flows east through Mallow, Fermoy and Lismore, then turns sharply south at Cappoquin, Co. Waterford, to the sea at Youghal. The usual explanation is superimposed drainage: it first flowed south across an older surface and kept that course as it cut down. _(M5)_
- **Munster: why its landscape is temporarily out of balance** — Uplift has outpaced denudation, so an old, uplifted erosion surface survives at height while the rivers are still adjusting by cutting down. _(M6, I1)_

### 4. Human interaction: river processes (14)

- **Shannon Scheme: how the water reaches Ardnacrusha** — Parteen Weir diverts most of the Shannon's water into a headrace canal to the power station at Ardnacrusha, near Limerick; a tailrace returns it to the river. Lough Derg acts as the reservoir. _(H6)_
- **Shannon Scheme: effects on the river and its fish** — The Old River Shannon between Parteen and Limerick now carries much less water. Salmon and eel stocks declined, so the ESB built a fish pass and traps silver eels and transports them past the dam. _(H6, H5)_
- **Shannon Scheme: who built it and when** — Built 1925 to 1929 by the German firm Siemens for the Irish Free State; Ireland's first large hydro-electric station. _(H6)_
- **Name four other Irish hydro-electric dams and their rivers** — Poulaphouca on the Liffey (Blessington Lakes, Co. Wicklow); Inniscarra and Carrigadrohid on the Lee (Co. Cork); Cathaleen's Fall on the Erne at Ballyshannon, Co. Donegal. _(H7)_
- **Aswan High Dam: when completed, its reservoir, and four benefits** — Flood control; year-round irrigation, allowing more than one crop a year; hydro-electricity; protection from drought. Completed 1970; its reservoir, Lake Nasser, extends into Sudan. _(H8)_
- **Aswan High Dam: impacts on river processes** — Silt is trapped in Lake Nasser, so farmers downstream rely on artificial fertilisers; clear water scours the bed below the dam; the Nile delta coast is eroding, for example at the Rosetta promontory. _(H8, H2, H3)_
- **Aswan High Dam: impacts on people and land** — Waterlogging and salinisation of irrigated land; schistosomiasis (bilharzia) spread through irrigation canals; the delta sardine fishery declined; Nubian people were displaced and Abu Simbel's temples moved. _(H8)_
- **Three Gorges Dam: key facts** — On the Yangtze in Hubei, China; the world's largest power station by installed capacity; completed 2006, fully operational 2012; over one million people relocated; aims to cut downstream flood peaks like the 1998 Yangtze floods, which killed thousands. _(H9)_
- **Canalisation: why floods downstream get worse** — A straighter, shorter channel has a steeper gradient and less friction, so velocity rises and floodwater is passed downstream faster, giving higher flood peaks that arrive sooner. _(C2)_
- **Canalisation: one Irish and two international examples** — Irish arterial drainage by the OPW under the Arterial Drainage Act 1945 (Moy and Boyne schemes); Mississippi cut-offs by the US Army Corps of Engineers; the Upper Rhine straightened in the 19th century under Tulla. _(C3, C4)_
- **Embankments and levees: effect on river processes** — Flow is confined to the channel, so deposition raises the bed and the banks must be raised again; the floodplain gets no silt; if a levee fails the flood is sudden and severe. _(F2)_
- **Cork flood, November 2009: what happened** — During very heavy rain the ESB released large volumes from the full Inniscarra dam on the Lee; University College Cork and the city water treatment works flooded and many homes lost their water supply. _(F4)_
- **Mississippi levee system: three floods to know** — The 1927 flood led to the federal levee and floodway system; the 1993 flood breached many levees on the upper Mississippi and Missouri; in August 2005 Hurricane Katrina caused levee and floodwall failures in New Orleans, and about 80% of the city flooded. _(F5)_
- **Room for the River (Netherlands): what it does** — Begun after near-disastrous high water on the Rhine and Maas in 1993 and 1995: dykes moved back, floodplains lowered and side channels dug so the river can spread out safely. _(F6)_

## Rocks weathering

### 1. The rock cycle and rock formation (19)

- **Rock cycle: the processes that link the three rock types** — Magma crystallises into igneous rock; weathering and erosion make sediment; deposition, compaction and cementation make sedimentary rock; heat and pressure make metamorphic rock; melting makes magma again. Any rock can be weathered, metamorphosed or melted. _(R1, R2)_
- **Plate settings: where igneous rocks form and where rock is recycled** — Basalt forms at constructive (divergent) boundaries and hotspots; andesite and granite batholiths form above subduction zones at destructive boundaries. Rock is metamorphosed at collision zones and destroyed or recycled at subduction zones. _(I4, R3)_
- **Irish granite: three locations, and the ages of two** — The Leinster batholith, the granite core of the Wicklow Mountains, about 400 million years old and the largest granite mass in Ireland and Britain; the Derryveagh Mountains, Donegal; the Mourne Mountains, Co. Down, much younger at about 56 million years. _(I2, IR2)_
- **Irish basalt: where it is found and how it formed** — The Antrim plateau, Co. Antrim, was built by lava flows about 60 million years ago as the Atlantic began to open; the Giant's Causeway shows basalt columns. Basalt forms when runny lava cools quickly, so its crystals are small. _(I3, IR2, IR3)_
- **Rock uses: what granite and basalt are used for** — Granite: building stone, kerbs, worktops and headstones. Basalt: crushed for road chippings and aggregate. _(I5)_
- **Sedimentary rock: how it forms, from weathering to solid rock** — Older rock is weathered and eroded; the sediment, plant and animal remains, or minerals from water are laid down in layers in seas, lakes, river basins or deserts; compaction squeezes out water and air; silica, calcite or iron oxide cement the grains. _(S1, S2)_
- **Sedimentary rock: three groups, with examples of each** — Clastic, from broken fragments: sandstone from sand, shale from mud and clay. Organic, from living things: limestone from shells and skeletons of sea creatures, coal from swamp plants. Chemical: rock salt and other evaporites from evaporating seawater. _(S3)_
- **Carboniferous limestone: when and how it formed, and where it is found** — About 360 to 300 million years ago Ireland lay near the equator under a warm, shallow, clear sea, where shells and skeletons built up the deposit. It floors most of the central lowland and forms the Burren, Co. Clare. _(S5, IR3)_
- **Old Red Sandstone: how it formed, and three places it forms mountains** — In the Devonian period, in hot, semi-arid conditions, rivers laid down sand and pebbles; iron oxide gives the red colour. It forms the Macgillycuddy's Reeks, the Galtees and the Comeraghs, and the ridges of Munster. _(S5, IR1)_
- **Name Irish locations for shale, chalk, coal and rock salt** — Shale, with siltstone and sandstone: the Cliffs of Moher, Co. Clare. Chalk: beneath the basalt along the Antrim coast. Coal, from swamp plants: Arigna, Co. Roscommon, and Castlecomer, Co. Kilkenny. Rock salt: Kilroot mine, near Carrickfergus, Co. Antrim. _(S5, IR2)_
- **Metamorphism: what heat and pressure do to a rock** — Existing rock is changed without melting: its minerals recrystallise while it stays solid, so it usually becomes harder and denser, and it may develop banding or cleavage. The two causes are heat and pressure. _(M1)_
- **Name the metamorphic rock formed from limestone, sandstone, shale and granite** — Limestone becomes marble; sandstone becomes quartzite; shale (mudstone) becomes slate, and with more heat and pressure schist; granite becomes gneiss. _(M4)_
- **Name Irish locations for marble, quartzite and slate** — Marble: green Connemara marble, Co. Galway. Quartzite: Errigal, Co. Donegal; Croagh Patrick, Co. Mayo; the Great Sugar Loaf, Co. Wicklow; the Twelve Bens, Connemara. Slate: Valentia Island, Co. Kerry. _(M4, IR2)_
- **Schist and gneiss: where they are found in Ireland** — Mica schist occurs in Donegal and Connemara, and as a metamorphic aureole around the Leinster granite. Gneiss on Inishtrahull, off Co. Donegal, is about 1.7 billion years old, the oldest rock in Ireland. _(M4, M2, IR2)_
- **Map of Ireland: where each main rock type is found** — Limestone floors the central lowland; Old Red Sandstone forms the ridges of Munster; granite in Wicklow, Donegal and the Mournes; basalt in Co. Antrim; metamorphic rocks in Donegal, Connemara and Mayo. _(IR1)_
- **North America's active western margin: rocks and features formed** — Pacific plates subduct beneath it or slide past it. Volcanic: Cascade volcanoes, e.g. Mount St Helens (erupted 1980). Plutonic: the Sierra Nevada batholith. Regional metamorphism: western mountain belts. Transform boundary: the San Andreas Fault, California. _(NA1)_
- **North America's trailing eastern margin: what forms there** — No plate boundary at the coast, so no active volcanoes. Thick sediments build up on the Atlantic coastal plain, on the continental shelf and in the Mississippi delta. The Appalachians are old, worn-down fold mountains of sedimentary and metamorphic rock. _(NA2, S6)_
- **Photo skill: how to identify granite, basalt, sandstone and limestone** — Granite: speckled, with large interlocking crystals. Basalt: dark and fine-grained, and may form columns. Sandstone: gritty grains, often reddish or buff, and layered. Limestone: pale grey, and may show fossils. _(ID1)_
- **Photo skill: how to identify marble, quartzite and slate** — Marble: white or coloured, with sparkling crystals and no fossils. Quartzite: pale, very hard and glassy. Slate: dark grey, and splits into thin sheets. _(ID1)_

### 2. Human interaction with the rock cycle (25)

- **Human interaction with the rock cycle: three ways extraction changes it** — It removes rock far faster than natural weathering and erosion; it exposes fresh rock to weathering; it creates new deposits of waste on the surface. Removal is the human equivalent of erosion, dumping the equivalent of deposition. _(H1, H2)_
- **Mining: how ore bodies form, and Tara Mines as an Irish example** — Ore bodies form where igneous activity or hot fluids moving through rock concentrate minerals. Tara Mines, Navan, Co. Meath: underground zinc and lead mine in Carboniferous limestone; Europe's largest zinc mine; opened in 1977; owned by Swedish firm Boliden. _(M1, M4)_
- **Mining: the stages from exploration to waste disposal** — Surveys and test drilling find the ore; open-cast mining strips the overburden to reach shallow ore (e.g. Bingham Canyon copper mine, Utah); underground mining uses shafts and tunnels; ore is crushed and separated; waste goes to spoil heaps and tailings ponds. _(M2, M3, M5)_
- **Aberfan disaster, 1966: what happened** — On 21 October 1966 a colliery spoil tip built over springs on a hillside above Aberfan, south Wales, collapsed after heavy rain as a flow slide of saturated waste, burying Pantglas Junior School; 144 people were killed, 116 of them children. _(M5)_
- **Mining: four negative impacts and three positive ones** — Landscape scars and subsidence; spoil heaps and tailings taking up land; acid mine drainage and heavy-metal pollution, as copper mining left in the Avoca River, Co. Wicklow; dust, noise and heavy traffic. Positive: jobs, exports and local spending. _(M6)_
- **Mining: how its impacts are managed, with an Irish example** — Environmental licensing and monitoring of waste and water leaving the site; lining tailings ponds to stop seepage and capping them at closure; rehabilitation after closure, as at Lisheen, Co. Tipperary, a zinc and lead mine that closed in 2015. _(M7)_
- **Quarrying: five materials and what each is used for** — Limestone: cement, aggregate and agricultural lime. Sand and gravel from glacial and fluvio-glacial deposits: concrete. Granite and other dimension stone: building, kerbs, headstones. Slate: roofing. Marble: decoration. _(B1)_
- **Quarrying: the stages of working a quarry** — First, the overburden of soil and glacial till is removed; then benches are cut into the rock face; the rock is drilled and blasted; finally it is crushed and screened (graded by size). Dimension stone is cut rather than blasted. _(B2)_
- **Cement: how it is made and why it releases carbon dioxide** — Limestone is crushed, mixed with shale and heated in a kiln to make clinker, which is ground with gypsum. Heating turns calcium carbonate into lime and carbon dioxide, returning to the air carbon locked in rock for hundreds of millions of years. _(B3)_
- **Name an Irish quarry site for cement, sand and gravel, granite, marble and slate** — Cement: Irish Cement at Platin, near Drogheda, Co. Meath, and Castlemungret, Co. Limerick (Carboniferous limestone). Sand and gravel: Blessington, Co. Wicklow. Granite: Ballyknockan, Co. Wicklow. Marble: Connemara. Slate: Valentia Island, Co. Kerry. _(B4)_
- **Quarrying: five negative impacts and two positive ones** — Permanent scars; loss of farmland and habitat; dust, noise and blasting vibration; heavy lorries on rural roads; a lowered local water table. Positive: jobs, and the materials needed for housing and roads. _(B5)_
- **Quarrying: how its impacts are managed** — Planning permission with conditions on working hours and limits on dust and noise; earth banks and trees to screen the quarry; rehabilitation of worked-out quarries as lakes, wildlife habitats, landfill or amenity areas. _(B6)_
- **Oil and gas: how they form and become trapped** — Remains of tiny marine organisms are buried in sea-floor sediment; over millions of years heat and pressure make oil and gas, which rise through porous, permeable reservoir rock (e.g. sandstone) until trapped under cap rock in an anticline, fault or salt dome. _(O1)_
- **Oil and gas: the stages of exploitation** — Seismic surveys locate possible traps; exploratory wells test them; production platforms or subsea wells bring up the oil or gas; pipelines carry it to an onshore terminal. _(O2)_
- **Kinsale Head and Corrib gas fields: locations and key dates** — Kinsale Head, off Co. Cork: discovered 1971, produced from 1978 until depleted in 2020; Ireland's first commercial gas field. Corrib, off north-west Co. Mayo: discovered 1996, first gas December 2015, piped to Bellanaboy terminal, originally run by Shell. _(O3, O4)_
- **Corrib gas: why local people opposed it** — The Shell to Sea campaign objected to a high-pressure raw-gas pipeline near homes. In 2005 five local men, the Rossport Five, were jailed for refusing to obey a court order not to obstruct the pipeline work. _(O4)_
- **Deepwater Horizon: what happened and its effects** — The oil rig, leased by BP in the Gulf of Mexico, exploded on 20 April 2010, killing 11 workers. About 4.9 million barrels of oil spilled before the well was sealed, damaging fishing, tourism and wildlife along the US Gulf coast. _(O5)_
- **Fracking: two concerns, and Ireland's response** — Hydraulic fracturing of shale releases gas, but raises concerns over groundwater pollution and induced earthquakes. Ireland banned onshore fracking by law in 2017. _(O6)_
- **Oil and gas: positive and negative impacts** — Positive: energy supply and security, revenue, jobs. Negative: oil spills; damage to marine life and fishing; conflict with local communities; burning fossil fuels returns stored carbon to the atmosphere, adding to climate change. _(O7)_
- **Geothermal energy: where the heat comes from and where it is greatest** — Heat comes from the earth's interior, from radioactive decay and residual heat; temperature rises with depth by about 25 to 30 °C per kilometre. Heat is greatest near plate boundaries and hotspots, where magma lies close to the surface. _(G1)_
- **High-temperature geothermal plant: how it works, in stages** — Groundwater is heated by hot rock near magma; production wells bring steam and hot water to the surface; steam drives turbines to generate electricity and hot water heats buildings (district heating); cooled water is pumped back down a reinjection well. _(G2)_
- **Iceland: why it has geothermal energy and how it uses it** — On the Mid-Atlantic Ridge above a hotspot. About 9 out of 10 homes use geothermal heat; it gives a quarter to a third of the electricity. Hellisheiði is the largest power station; Reykjavík has district heating; the Blue Lagoon uses Svartsengi's waste water. _(G5)_
- **Larderello and Wairakei: why each is significant** — Larderello, Tuscany, Italy: the world's first geothermal electricity (1904) and first commercial geothermal power station (1913). Wairakei, New Zealand: a geothermal power station opened in 1958. _(G6)_
- **Geothermal energy in Ireland: its potential and main use** — Ireland is far from a plate boundary, so it has only low-temperature potential; warm springs at Mallow, Co. Cork, show heated groundwater exists. Ground-source heat pumps, using the steady warmth of shallow ground, are the main use. _(G7, G3)_
- **Geothermal energy: advantages and disadvantages** — Advantages: renewable, low carbon, reliable, cheap once built. Disadvantages: few suitable places; costly drilling; hydrogen sulphide smell; minerals clog pipes; induced earthquakes, e.g. Basel, Switzerland (2006), where an enhanced project was abandoned. _(G8, G4)_

### 3. Weathering processes (11)

- **Weathering: name five physical and five chemical processes** — Physical: freeze-thaw, exfoliation, granular disintegration, pressure release, salt crystallisation. Chemical: carbonation, hydrolysis, oxidation, hydration, solution. Biological weathering works both ways. _(W3)_
- **Freeze-thaw action: the conditions it needs and where it is active** — Temperatures that move above and below 0 °C often, water, and jointed rock. It is most effective on mountains in upland Ireland, such as the summit of Carrauntoohil, Co. Kerry, on high mountains and in periglacial areas. _(P1)_
- **Freeze-thaw action: the process step by step, and its result** — First, water enters cracks and joints; at night it freezes and expands by about 9%, widening the crack; by day the ice thaws and water seeps deeper; repeated cycles split off angular fragments, which build scree slopes at the foot of mountain cliffs. _(P1)_
- **Exfoliation (onion weathering): how it works, step by step** — In hot deserts such as the Sahara, with a large daily range, the outer rock heats and expands by day and cools and contracts at night more than the interior, as rock conducts heat poorly; stress builds and curved sheets peel off, leaving rounded boulders. _(P2)_
- **Pressure release: how it forms domes, with an example** — When overlying rock is eroded, buried rock such as granite expands upward and cracks into sheets parallel to the surface; the sheets peel off to leave domes, such as Half Dome, Yosemite, California, USA. _(P4)_
- **Biological weathering: two physical and two chemical ways** — Physical: plant roots grow into cracks and widen them; burrowing animals expose fresh rock. Chemical: decaying plants produce humic acids; lichens and mosses release acids that attack rock surfaces. _(P6, C6)_
- **Carbonation: the process step by step, and where it operates** — Rainwater absorbs carbon dioxide from the air and soil, forming weak carbonic acid, which reacts with calcium carbonate in limestone to form soluble calcium bicarbonate, carried away in solution along joints and bedding planes, e.g. on the Burren, Co. Clare. _(C1)_
- **Hydrolysis of granite: what it produces, with an example** — Water reacts with the feldspar in granite and turns it into clay (kaolin, or china clay); the quartz grains are left as sand, and the granite crumbles. China clay is quarried around St Austell, Cornwall, and on the edge of Dartmoor. _(C2)_
- **Weathering: five factors that influence it** — Climate (temperature and rainfall); rock type, e.g. limestone is vulnerable to carbonation, granite to hydrolysis, while quartzite resists both; rock structure, as joints, bedding planes and faults let water in; vegetation and soil; time, relief and aspect. _(F1, F2, F3, F4, F5)_
- **Climate: which type of weathering each climate favours** — Warm, wet: fast chemical weathering, as reaction rates roughly double for every 10 °C rise (Van 't Hoff's rule). Cold, around freezing: freeze-thaw. Hot deserts: exfoliation and salt crystallisation. Ireland's mild, wet climate favours chemical weathering. _(F1)_
- **Role of temperature in physical weathering: the three processes and why** — Freeze-thaw needs temperatures crossing the freezing point; exfoliation and granular disintegration need a large daily range, as rock and its minerals expand and contract unevenly. In each, temperature change, not just warmth, causes the stress. _(F6, P3)_

### 4. Landforms associated with particular rock types (20)

- **Limestone: two reasons it develops karst landforms** — It is calcium carbonate, which dissolves in weak carbonic acid by carbonation. It is permeable: water passes along its bedding planes (horizontal) and joints (vertical), not through the solid rock, so drainage goes underground. _(K2, K1)_
- **Permeable, porous and impermeable rock: the difference** — Permeable rock lets water pass through it, mainly along cracks such as joints and bedding planes. Porous rock holds water in tiny spaces between its grains. Impermeable rock lets no water pass through. _(K2)_
- **Limestone pavement: how it forms, in stages** — First, glacial ice scrapes away the soil, exposing the jointed, bedded limestone to rain; then carbonation widens the joints into grikes; the blocks left standing between them are clints. Example: the Burren, Co. Clare. _(S1, S2, S3)_
- **Limestone pavement: how karren and solution hollows form on clints** — Karren (runnels) are small grooves cut into clint surfaces by rainwater running across them. Solution hollows (kamenitza) are small enclosed hollows where rainwater collects on a clint and dissolves the rock. _(S4, S5)_
- **The Burren: key facts for a karst answer** — Ireland's best-known karst landscape, in Co. Clare, of Carboniferous limestone with extensive pavement of clints and grikes. Its name comes from the Irish Boireann, meaning rocky place. Mullaghmore is a Burren hill of folded, terraced limestone. _(KC2, S1)_
- **Swallow hole to river of resurgence: what happens to a stream** — A stream flowing off impermeable rock reaches limestone and sinks down a swallow hole widened by carbonation along a joint; it flows underground through caves and re-emerges as a river of resurgence, usually where limestone meets impermeable rock below. _(S6, S11)_
- **Turlough: how it fills and empties, with an Irish example** — In winter the water table rises and floods a hollow in the limestone, forming a seasonal lake; in summer it drains away through the floor. Turloughs are common in the Burren and east Co. Galway; Rahasane, near Craughwell, is one of the largest. _(S10, K3)_
- **Doline, uvala, polje and dry valley: what each is** — Doline: a hollow formed by solution of the surface or collapse of a cave roof. Uvala: several merged dolines. Polje: a large, flat-floored, seasonally flooded depression (Slovenia). Dry valley: cut by a river when the ground was frozen or water table higher. _(S7, S8, S9)_
- **Stalactites, stalagmites and pillars: how they form** — Water dripping from a cave roof carries dissolved calcium bicarbonate; as it loses carbon dioxide and evaporates it deposits calcite. Stalactites grow down from the roof, stalagmites up from the floor beneath, and a pillar forms where the two join. _(U2, U3, U4, U5)_
- **Caves: how they form, with three Irish examples** — Water dissolves passages along bedding planes and joints, and underground rivers enlarge them by erosion. Examples: Aillwee Cave, Co. Clare; Doolin Cave, Co. Clare, with the Great Stalactite, about 7.3 m long; Mitchelstown Cave, Co. Tipperary. _(U1, U3)_
- **Karst cycle: the three stages** — Youth: surface drainage begins to sink and swallow holes form. Maturity: most drainage goes underground, with caves, dolines and uvalas. Old age: cave roofs collapse, the limestone is mostly removed and isolated residual hills remain. _(KC1)_
- **Marble Arch upland: how its drainage works** — On Cuilcagh Mountain, Co. Fermanagh, streams flow off impermeable shale and sandstone and sink into swallow holes at the limestone, flow through the Marble Arch Caves and re-emerge as the Cladagh River; the Marble Arch is a natural limestone arch over it. _(KC2, V4)_
- **Mask–Corrib lowland: two signs of underground karst drainage** — Water from Lough Mask flows underground through the limestone to Lough Corrib near Cong, Co. Mayo. The Cong Canal, built in the mid-1800s to link the two lakes, never held water because it leaked into the permeable limestone: the dry canal. _(KC2)_
- **Name four international karst areas and a feature of each** — Slovenia: the Kras plateau, which gives karst its name, with Postojna Cave and the Škocjan Caves. S.W. China: tower karst at Guilin and the Li River. Kentucky: Mammoth Cave, the world's longest known cave system. Jamaica: cockpit karst in Cockpit Country. _(KC3, K1)_
- **Granite landscapes: four characteristics of granite that shape them** — Hard, crystalline and resistant to erosion; impermeable, so water runs off, giving many surface streams and boggy ground; jointed; weathers mainly by hydrolysis of its feldspar, which forms clay (china clay). _(G1, V3)_
- **Wicklow granite landscape: its main features** — The Leinster batholith, the largest granite mass in Ireland and Britain, forms rounded, dome-shaped uplands, worn down from rock that once covered it; impermeable rock and high rainfall give thin, acidic soils and blanket bog, e.g. around the Sally Gap. _(G2)_
- **Tors: the two-stage (deep weathering) theory of formation** — First, under warm, wet conditions, hydrolysis rotted the granite deep along its joints, most where they were close together; later, mass movement under periglacial conditions stripped the rotted rock, leaving widely jointed blocks as tors (D. L. Linton, 1955). _(G3)_
- **Dartmoor: four granite landscape features with examples** — A granite upland in Devon, England, with tors such as Haytor and Hound Tor, clitter (boulder fields) on the slopes below them, blanket bog, and china clay quarried at Lee Moor on its south-west edge. _(G4, G5)_
- **Rock type and the Irish landscape: saucer shape and quartzite peaks** — A low central lowland of Carboniferous limestone is ringed by uplands of harder rock (granite, quartzite, sandstone). By differential erosion, resistant rock forms uplands and peaks, e.g. the quartzite peaks of Errigal, Croagh Patrick and the Great Sugar Loaf. _(V1, V2)_
- **Two contrasting landscapes either side of Lough Corrib** — East of Lough Corrib lies the limestone lowland of east Co. Galway, with underground drainage, few rivers and dry surfaces; to the west lie the granite and metamorphic uplands of Connemara, with many surface streams and bog. _(V4, V3)_

### 5. Mass movement (mass wasting) processes and the factors governing them (19)

- **Mass movement: the forces on a slope and when material moves** — Gravity pulls material downslope; friction, cohesion and plant roots resist it. Movement happens when the downslope force exceeds the resistance. Weathering supplies the material, and no transporting agent is needed. _(I2, I1)_
- **Mass movements: which are fast and which are slow** — Slow: soil creep and solifluction. Fast: rockfall, landslide, slump, mudflow, lahar, bog burst and avalanche. Movements are also classed by water content (dry or wet) and type of movement (heave, slide, flow or fall). _(I3)_
- **Mass movement: where and when it is most active** — In space: most intense on steep, wet and unvegetated slopes. In time: more frequent after heavy rain, snowmelt, earthquakes and seasonal freeze-thaw. _(I4)_
- **Soil creep: five pieces of evidence on a slope** — Terracettes (small steps on grassy slopes); bent tree trunks; tilted fences, walls and poles; soil piled up behind walls; cracked roads. _(SL1)_
- **Rockfall: what causes it and what it leaves** — Blocks loosened by freeze-thaw, or undercut by the sea or a river, fall freely from a steep cliff and collect as scree (talus) at its foot. _(F1)_
- **Landslide vs slump: how the movement differs** — Landslide: rock or debris slides suddenly along a flat plane, such as a bedding plane or joint dipping with the slope. Slump: saturated material rotates backward on a curved slip plane, leaving a crescent-shaped scarp, a back-tilted block and a bulging toe. _(F2, F3)_
- **Bog burst: how it happens and its effects** — Prolonged or intense rain saturates sloping blanket bog; the peat gains weight and its base loses grip on the mineral soil or rock below, so it bursts and flows downhill as a black slurry, blocking rivers, killing fish and wrecking roads, bridges and land. _(F6)_
- **Pollatomish, Co. Mayo, 2003: what happened** — On 19 September 2003 an intense thunderstorm with very heavy rain triggered about 40 landslides and peat slides on Dooncarton Mountain in a few hours; houses, roads, a bridge and a graveyard were damaged and several families were evacuated. _(CS2)_
- **Knocknageeha, Co. Kerry: the classic Irish bog burst** — In December 1896, near Rathmore, Co. Kerry, a bog burst swept away a house, killing a family of eight. _(CS2)_
- **Antrim coast landslides: the geology and the process** — Between Larne and Cushendall, hard basalt lies on chalk over soft Lias clay. Rain soaks through the jointed basalt and chalk into the clay, which turns slippery and plastic; the rock above slumps seaward in rotational slides and clay flows out as mudflows. _(CS1, FA3)_
- **Antrim Coast Road: effects of the landslides and the responses** — Mudflows have repeatedly blocked the Antrim Coast Road (A2); Garron Point is a classic landslip site. Engineers have built retaining walls and drainage, and fitted rock-catch fences and netting to protect the road. _(CS1)_
- **Sarno, southern Italy, 1998: what happened** — On 5–6 May 1998, after prolonged heavy rain, loose volcanic ash and soil on steep limestone slopes in Campania became saturated and slid off as fast mudflows into Sarno and neighbouring towns; about 160 people were killed. _(CS3)_
- **Vajont Dam, Italy, 1963: what happened and why** — On 9 October 1963 a landslide from Monte Toc fell into the reservoir; the wave over the dam destroyed Longarone, killing about 2,000 people, though the dam survived. Causes: weak clay layers in the valley side, and filling the reservoir saturated the slope. _(CS3)_
- **Armero, Colombia, 1985: what happened** — On 13 November 1985 a small eruption of Nevado del Ruiz melted part of its summit ice cap; lahars rushed down river valleys and buried the town of Armero, killing about 23,000 people. Warnings had been given, but the town was not evacuated. _(CS4)_
- **Vargas, Venezuela, 1999: what happened** — In December 1999 days of torrential rain triggered mudslides and debris flows on the steep mountains above coastal towns in Vargas state, killing thousands of people. _(CS4)_
- **Blatten, Switzerland, 2025: what happened** — On 28 May 2025 the Birch Glacier collapsed, burying most of Blatten in ice, rock and mud. Rockfalls onto the glacier had been detected, so the village was evacuated days earlier. The debris dammed the River Lonza, forming a lake upstream and a flood risk. _(CS5)_
- **Marmolada glacier, Italy, 2022: what happened** — On 3 July 2022, during a heatwave, a large section of the Marmolada glacier in the Dolomites collapsed as an ice avalanche, killing 11 people: glacier retreat as a new cause of mass movement. _(CS5, F7)_
- **Mass movement factors: how gradient and water affect it** — The steeper the slope, the stronger the downslope pull of gravity: gentle slopes favour creep, steep slopes falls and slides. Some water adds cohesion, but saturation fills pores, adds weight, reduces friction and lubricates, so material flows. _(FA1, FA2)_
- **Mass movement factors: rock type, vegetation, undercutting and tectonics** — Wet clay turns slippery and plastic, and rock dipping towards the slope slides; roots bind soil and plants absorb water, so bare slopes move more easily; erosion at the base removes support and weight at the top adds force; earthquakes shake material loose. _(FA3, FA4, FA6, FA7)_

### 6. Erosion by wind (9)

- **Wind erosion: where it works best, and why it is weaker than water** — In hot and cold deserts, such as the Sahara, and on coasts, with loose, dry, fine material, sparse vegetation and strong winds. Air is far less dense than water or ice, so wind moves only sand and finer particles, mostly near the ground. _(C1, C2)_
- **Wind erosion: three processes and what each does** — Deflation: wind lifts and blows away loose, dry, fine particles, lowering the surface. Abrasion (sandblasting): wind-blown sand wears rock away, mostly within a metre or two of the ground. Attrition: grains collide and become smaller, rounder and frosted. _(E1, E2, E3)_
- **Wind transport: three methods** — Suspension: fine dust is carried high, sometimes thousands of kilometres in dust storms. Saltation: sand grains bounce along in short hops; most sand moves this way. Surface creep: larger grains are pushed and rolled along by the impact of saltating grains. _(T1, T2, T3)_
- **Mushroom (pedestal) rock: how it forms, in stages** — First, a pillar of rock stands on the desert floor; then sandblasting, which is concentrated near the ground, undercuts its base; finally the top, above the zone of abrasion, stays wide, leaving a mushroom shape. _(L6, E2)_
- **Zeugen: how it forms, in stages** — Where horizontal layers of hard cap rock lie over softer rock and joints run across the wind: first, abrasion cuts down along joints in the cap rock; then the soft rock beneath is undercut; finally a flat-topped ridge is left. _(L5)_
- **Deflation hollow: how it forms, with an example** — Deflation removes loose material until it reaches the water table or a resistant layer, leaving a depression. The Qattara Depression, Egypt, below sea level, is attributed mainly to deflation down to the water table. _(L1)_
- **Desert landscapes: erg, reg and hamada** — Erg: a sand sea. Reg: stony desert. Hamada: bare rock. The Sahara Desert, North Africa, has all three. _(D1)_
- **Barchan vs seif dunes: shape and the winds that form them** — Barchan: crescent-shaped, horns pointing downwind, gentle windward slope and steep slip face on the lee side; it migrates downwind. Seif (linear): a long ridge roughly parallel to the dominant wind, formed where winds blow from two directions. _(D2, D3)_
- **Dust Bowl: where and when it happened, and what the wind did** — On the Great Plains of the USA, including Oklahoma and Kansas, in the 1930s, bare, dry soil was blown away in huge dust storms, known as black blizzards. _(H1)_

### 7. Human interaction: mass movement (19)

- **Human activity: three ways it makes mass movement more frequent** — Removing the vegetation that binds soil and absorbs water; adding water or weight to slopes; removing the support at the base of slopes. Bare soil is also stripped by rain splash, sheet wash, gullying and wind. _(H1, H2)_
- **Overgrazing: how it leads to soil erosion and mass movement** — Too many animals for the carrying capacity eat vegetation to the roots and trample the ground, compacting soil and breaking the peat; without roots, soil and peat lose cohesion, rain runs off or saturates the peat, creep speeds up and peat slides occur. _(OG1, OG2)_
- **Western Irish commonage: how overgrazing happened and its effects** — From the 1980s, EU (CAP) headage payments paid per animal, so sheep numbers rose sharply on western hill commonage (Connemara; the Mayo, Donegal and Kerry mountains). Heather and grass were stripped, peat eroded and silt hurt salmon and trout spawning grounds. _(OG3, OG2)_
- **Overgrazing: how it has been managed, with Irish measures** — Payments changed from per-head to per-area; Commonage Framework Plans (from the early 2000s) set lower stocking rates; REPS, introduced in 1994, paid farmers to farm in environmentally friendly ways. Also destocking, rotational grazing and fencing. _(OG3, OG5)_
- **The Sahel: how overgrazing and overcropping led to desertification** — Population growth brought more cattle, goats and sheep around wells and boreholes, shorter fallow periods, more cash crops and more fuelwood cutting; in droughts, notably in the 1970s and 1980s, wind and the short wet season's heavy rain eroded the bare soil. _(OG4, OC4)_
- **Overcropping: how it leads to soil erosion on slopes** — Cropping land every year without fallow or fertiliser uses up nutrients, so soil structure breaks down and ground lies bare; loose, dry soil is washed downslope into rills and gullies or blown away; ploughing up and down slopes channels water and soil down. _(OC1, OC2)_
- **Dust Bowl, 1930s: how overcropping caused it and its effects** — On the Great Plains of the USA, including Oklahoma and Kansas, natural grassland was ploughed up for wheat; severe drought then left bare, dry soil that strong winds blew away in huge dust storms; many farming families were forced to leave. _(OC3)_
- **Dust Bowl: how the USA responded** — The US Soil Conservation Service was set up in 1935, and shelter belts of trees, contour ploughing and crop rotation were promoted. _(OC3, OC5)_
- **Overcropping: five management methods, with an example** — Crop rotation; fallow periods; contour ploughing along the slope, not up and down; strip cropping; terracing of steep slopes, e.g. the rice terraces of Banaue, Luzon, Philippines, over 2,000 years old. _(OC5)_
- **Deforestation: how it increases landslides and mudflows** — Roots bind soil to bedrock, the canopy breaks the force of rain, and trees take up water. Without trees, more rain reaches and saturates the soil and rotting roots no longer hold the slope, so landslides and mudflows become more frequent. _(DF1, DF2)_
- **Haiti, 2004: how deforestation worsened a disaster** — Haiti has lost almost all its natural forest, mainly for farmland and charcoal, unlike the neighbouring Dominican Republic. In September 2004 Tropical Storm Jeanne caused floods and mudslides off the bare hills around Gonaïves, killing about 3,000 people. _(DF3)_
- **Hurricane Mitch, 1998: what happened** — In October 1998 torrential rain over Honduras and Nicaragua caused floods and landslides, made worse by deforested slopes; about 11,000 people were killed. A mudflow from the Casita volcano, Nicaragua, buried villages. _(DF3)_
- **Ireland's forests: two figures and a modern risk to peat slopes** — About 1% of Ireland was forested around 1900; forest cover is now about 11%, mostly conifer plantations. Clear-felling plantations on upland peat, and building forestry and wind-farm roads, can destabilise peat slopes. _(DF4)_
- **Deforestation: four ways to manage its effect on slopes** — Afforestation and reforestation of steep slopes; buffer strips of trees beside rivers; selective felling instead of clear-felling; restricting felling on steep or peaty slopes. _(DF5)_
- **Derrybrien, Co. Galway, 2003: what happened** — On 16 October 2003, during construction of a wind farm on a forested blanket-bog hillside in the Slieve Aughty Mountains, about 450,000 cubic metres of peat slid down the slope into the Owendalulleegh River, killing large numbers of fish. _(OH1)_
- **Derrybrien: why the EU fined Ireland** — In 2019 the EU Court of Justice fined Ireland a lump sum of €5 million plus €15,000 a day, because no proper environmental impact assessment had been carried out for the wind farm. _(OH1)_
- **Meenbog, Co. Donegal, 2020: what happened** — In November 2020 a peat slide during construction of the Meenbog wind farm, near Ballybofey, flowed into the Mourne Beg River and crossed into Northern Ireland, polluting a river system important for salmon. _(OH1)_
- **Other human causes of mass movement: name five** — Road and rail cuttings that steepen slopes or remove their support; buildings adding weight at the top of slopes; leaking pipes, drainage and irrigation saturating slopes; reservoirs saturating valley sides (Vajont); unstable mining spoil heaps (Aberfan). _(OH2)_
- **Reducing the risk of mass movement: engineering and planning** — Engineering: retaining walls, gabions, rock bolts, netting and rock-catch fences, regrading slopes, drainage pipes. Planning: hazard mapping, avoiding unstable slopes, environmental impact assessments before building on peat, monitoring and warning systems. _(R1, R2)_

## Sea

### 1. Coastal processes, patterns and associated landforms (23)

- **Waves: three factors that control their size, and why they break** — Wind speed, how long the wind blows, and fetch. In shallow water, friction with the sea bed slows the base of the wave while the crest keeps moving, so the crest topples forward and the wave breaks. _(W1, W2)_
- **Constructive vs destructive waves: how each affects a beach** — Constructive: low, long wavelength, low frequency; they spill, swash is stronger than backwash, so they build the beach up (calm weather). Destructive: high, steep, short wavelength, high frequency; they plunge, backwash is stronger, so they erode it (storms). _(W3, W4)_
- **Wave refraction: what happens to wave energy at headlands and bays** — Waves slow first in the shallow water off headlands, so the wave fronts bend; energy converges on headlands, causing more erosion, and spreads out in bays, where it is weaker and deposition is favoured. Wave fronts end up roughly parallel to the shore. _(W5)_
- **Headlands and bays: how differential erosion forms them, with an example** — Where bands of hard and soft rock meet the coast at right angles (a discordant coast), the soft rock is eroded back into bays and the hard rock is left as headlands. Example: the chalk headland of Handfast Point, Dorset, between Studland Bay and Swanage Bay. _(P2, L7)_
- **West vs east coast of Ireland: why they differ** — West: the long Atlantic fetch and prevailing south-westerly winds give high-energy waves and rugged, cliffed, erosional coasts. East: the narrow Irish Sea gives a short fetch and weaker waves, and soft glacial deposits favour beaches, spits, lagoons and dunes. _(P1)_
- **Rate of coastal erosion: five factors that control it** — Rock type, as soft boulder clay erodes fastest, e.g. the Holderness coast, Yorkshire; rock structure (joints, faults, bedding); wave energy (fetch, storms, refraction); whether a beach protects the cliff foot; human activity, such as removing beach material. _(E6)_
- **Cliffs: how weathering and mass movement help wear them back** — Above the waves, freeze-thaw and salt crystallisation weather the cliff face, and rockfalls and slumping on soft clay cliffs bring material down; the waves then remove the fallen debris. _(E5)_
- **Cliff and wave-cut platform: how they form, in stages** — First, waves cut a notch between high and low tide by hydraulic action and abrasion; then the overhang collapses and waves remove the debris, so the cliff retreats, staying steep; the platform left at its foot widens until waves lose energy crossing it. _(L1, L2)_
- **Cliffs of Moher: their height and rock type** — The Cliffs of Moher, Co. Clare, rise to 214 m at their highest and are made of layers of shale and sandstone, not limestone. _(L1)_
- **Cave, arch, stack and stump: how the sequence forms** — Waves exploit a joint or fault at the base of a headland, enlarging it into a cave by hydraulic action and abrasion; caves meet through the headland as an arch; waves and weathering bring down the roof, leaving a stack; the stack is undercut to a low stump. _(L3, L4, L5)_
- **Name examples of sea arches and sea stacks** — Arches: the Bridges of Ross, Co. Clare (originally three, now one); Durdle Door, Dorset, England. Stacks: Dún Briste, off Downpatrick Head, Co. Mayo; Old Harry Rocks, chalk stacks off Handfast Point, Dorset. _(L4, L5)_
- **Blowhole and geo: how they form** — Hydraulic action, with compressed air, enlarges a cave upward along a vertical joint until it breaks through to the cliff top; storm spray is forced up through the hole; if the roof between the blowhole and the sea collapses, a narrow inlet (a geo) forms. _(L6)_
- **Longshore drift: how it works and the landforms it builds** — Waves approach at an angle set by the prevailing wind; swash carries material up the beach at that angle and backwash drags it straight down, moving it along in a zigzag. It builds spits, bars, lagoons and tombolos, e.g. Spurn Head from Holderness sediment. _(T2)_
- **Coastal deposition: three conditions that cause it** — Waves lose energy (constructive waves, sheltered bays, shallow water, or after refraction); the coast changes direction, e.g. at a river mouth or bay; and there is a large supply of sediment from rivers, cliff erosion or glacial deposits offshore. _(D1)_
- **Beach: how its material is sorted, two features on it, and two Irish examples** — Finer sand lies lower down the beach and coarser shingle higher up; a storm beach is a ridge of large shingle thrown above normal high tide; berms are ridges marking successive high-tide levels. Examples: Tramore, Co. Waterford; Curracloe, Co. Wexford. _(D2)_
- **Sand spit: how it forms, in stages** — Longshore drift carries material past a point where the coast changes direction and drops it in calmer, deeper water, building a ridge attached to land at one end; refraction or a wind shift curves the tip; silt fills the sheltered water behind as salt marsh. _(D3)_
- **Name four sand spits, three of them Irish** — Inch and Rossbeigh, Co. Kerry, growing out from opposite sides of Dingle Bay; Tramore, Co. Waterford, a spit with dunes and the Back Strand lagoon behind it; Spurn Head, at the mouth of the Humber estuary, England. _(D3)_
- **Sand bar and lagoon: how they form, with Irish examples** — A spit grows right across the mouth of a bay, joining two headlands as a sand bar; the water trapped behind it is a lagoon, which slowly fills with sediment and may become marsh. Examples: Lady's Island Lake and Tacumshin Lake, Co. Wexford. _(D4, D5)_
- **Tombolo: how it forms, with an Irish example** — Longshore drift, and waves refracting around an island and meeting in its shelter, build a ridge of sand or shingle that joins the island to the mainland. Example: Sutton, Co. Dublin, joining Howth Head, once an island. _(D6)_
- **Sand dunes: how they form, in stages** — First, wind blows dry sand from a wide beach at low tide inland, piling it against obstacles above high tide; then marram grass colonises it, its roots binding the sand; embryo dunes grow into fore dunes, and grey (fixed) dunes form inland as soil develops. _(D7)_
- **Name four Irish sand dune systems** — Brittas Bay, Co. Wicklow; Curracloe, Co. Wexford; Inch, Co. Kerry; North Bull Island, Dublin Bay. _(D7)_
- **Classify coastal features as erosional or depositional** — Erosional: cliff, wave-cut notch, wave-cut platform, cave, sea arch, sea stack, sea stump, blowhole, headland. Depositional: beach, storm beach, sand spit, sand bar, lagoon, tombolo, sand dunes, salt marsh. _(K3)_
- **Skill: how to work out the average annual rate of coastal erosion** — Subtract the earlier year from the later to get the number of years, then divide the distance retreated by it. Illustrative, invented figures: a cliff retreats 50 m between 1990 and 2015, so 50 ÷ 25 = 2 m per year. _(K2)_

### 2. Human interaction: coastal processes (22)

- **Human activity on coasts: four ways it upsets coastal processes** — It interrupts longshore drift; removes sediment; destroys the vegetation that holds dunes together; reflects wave energy. The effects often appear further along the coast, downdrift, rather than where the work was done. _(H1)_
- **Trampling of sand dunes: how it leads to blowouts** — Walkers and sunbathers kill the marram grass; with no roots binding it, wind removes the sand and cuts blowouts into the dunes; a blowout can breach the dune ridge and let the sea reach the land behind. _(R1)_
- **Recreation: other pressures on dunes, cliffs and birds** — Vehicles, parking, caravan parks and holiday homes on dunes kill plants and compact sand; golf links stabilise some dunes but replace habitats and bring demands for defences; footpath and cliff-edge erosion at busy viewpoints; litter and disturbance of birds. _(R2, R3, R4)_
- **Spanish Mediterranean coast: how resorts caused coastal squeeze** — Since mass tourism grew in the 1960s, high-rise resorts on the Costa del Sol and at Benidorm, Costa Blanca, were built on the beach and dune line, removing the sediment store and blocking landward movement, so beaches narrowed and defences were needed. _(R5)_
- **Ley de Costas, 1988: what Spain did and why** — Spain passed its Coastal Law (Ley de Costas) in 1988 in response to overdevelopment; it restricts building on the shore zone. _(M3)_
- **Hard vs soft engineering: how they differ in approach and effect** — Hard engineering builds structures to resist the sea; it is usually expensive, effective locally, and can increase erosion elsewhere. Soft engineering works with natural processes; it is cheaper and more sustainable, but slower and less certain. _(D1, V1)_
- **Sea walls: how they work, their effects on the coast and their cost** — Often curved to throw waves back, they protect the land behind but reflect wave energy, so backwash scours the beach in front, lowering it and undermining the wall; erosion often increases at its ends. They are expensive to build and maintain. _(D2)_
- **Rock armour: how it works, two advantages and three disadvantages** — Large boulders are piled at the foot of a cliff or in front of a sea wall, and the gaps absorb and break up wave energy. It is cheaper than a sea wall and quick to place, but it can be unsightly and hard to walk over, and big storms may move the boulders. _(D3)_
- **Groynes: effect on longshore drift updrift and downdrift** — Built across the beach at right angles to the shore, groynes trap sand moving by longshore drift on their updrift side, widening the beach so it absorbs wave energy; downdrift, the beach is starved of sediment and erosion increases. _(D4)_
- **Rock armour vs groynes: how to tell them apart in a photo** — Rock armour is a pile of loose boulders lying along the foot of a cliff or wall. Groynes are low barriers running out across the beach at right angles to the shore. Rosslare Strand, Co. Wexford, an eroding sandy resort coast, is protected by both. _(D3, D4, C3)_
- **Beach nourishment and dune regeneration: pros and cons** — Nourishment widens a beach with sand or shingle: it looks natural and keeps the beach for tourism, but must be repeated, and dredging can harm the sea bed. Dune regeneration (sand fencing, marram replanting, fencing off): cheap and natural, but slow. _(D7, D8)_
- **Managed retreat: how it works, with an example** — Defences are removed or not replaced and the sea floods low-value land, creating salt marsh that absorbs wave energy and gives habitat: cheap and sustainable, but landowners lose land. Medmerry, West Sussex (2013): a new embankment inland, the sea let in. _(D9, M8)_
- **Mappleton, Holderness: what was built and what happened** — In 1991 two rock groynes and rock armour at the cliff foot, costing about £2 million, protected the village and the main coast road. The beach built up between the groynes, but cliffs just south of the scheme, starved of sediment, eroded faster than before. _(C1)_
- **North Bull Wall: how a human structure created North Bull Island** — The North Bull Wall was built in the 1820s to stop sand silting up the entrance to Dublin Port. Sand built up against and beside it, and North Bull Island grew from a sandbank into an island with dunes, salt marsh and mudflats. _(C2, D10)_
- **Netherlands, 1953: the flood and the Delta Works** — The 1953 North Sea storm surge flooded the south-west of the Netherlands and killed about 1,800 people there. The Delta Works followed: dams, sluices and storm-surge barriers that shortened and sealed the coast of Zeeland. _(C4)_
- **Oosterschelde barrier: why it balances defence with conservation** — Completed in 1986, this storm-surge barrier has movable gates that stay open in normal weather, so the tides still flow in and the estuary's ecosystem and shellfish industry are kept. _(C4)_
- **ICZM: why a whole sediment cell is managed as one unit** — Work in one place affects places downdrift, so integrated coastal zone management plans a whole stretch of coast together, balancing defence, tourism, development and conservation. _(M2, M1)_
- **Legal protection of coasts: EU and Irish measures** — The EU Habitats and Birds Directives create Special Areas of Conservation and Special Protection Areas (birds), forming the Natura 2000 network, overseen in Ireland by the National Parks and Wildlife Service. Planning controls limit building near the shore. _(M3)_
- **Blue Flag beaches: who runs the award and its four criteria** — Run by the Foundation for Environmental Education and administered in Ireland by An Taisce. Criteria: water quality; environmental education and information; environmental management; safety and services. It encourages councils to manage beaches well. _(M4)_
- **Brittas Bay, Co. Wicklow: the pressures and how they are managed** — A dune system heavily used by day-trippers from Dublin in summer; trampling and parking damaged the dunes, part of a protected Special Area of Conservation. Managed with fencing, boardwalks and car parks set back from the dunes. _(R1, M5)_
- **Cliffs of Moher: how a busy cliff site is managed** — A visitor centre built into the hillside opened in 2007; paths and a wall or barrier set back from the cliff edge control trampling and keep people safe. _(M6)_
- **North Bull Island as a conservation site: designations and pressures** — A UNESCO Biosphere Reserve since 1981, extended in 2015 as the Dublin Bay Biosphere; its salt marsh and mudflats are a major wintering ground for Brent geese. Pressures: the Royal Dublin and St Anne's golf courses, cars on the beach and many visitors. _(M7, R3)_

## Urban

### 1. Pre-historic and historic settlement (19)

- **Locational factors that settlers used in every period** — Water supply; defence (hills, cliffs, river loops, islands); fertile, well-drained land; shelter in valleys, bays and estuaries; routeways, fords and bridging points; resources such as fish, timber and stone; religious or political importance. _(K2)_
- **Mount Sandel: location, age and how its people lived** — Near Coleraine, Co. Derry, on a bluff above the River Bann: the earliest known house site in Ireland, Mesolithic and over 9,000 years old. Its hunter-gatherers fished for salmon and eels and gathered hazelnuts. _(P1)_
- **Neolithic farmers: how settlement changed, with the Céide Fields** — Farmers cleared forest for fields and lived in permanent farmsteads; megalithic tombs stood on high, visible ground. Céide Fields, near Ballycastle, Co. Mayo: stone-walled fields under blanket bog, over 5,000 years old, evidence of organised farming. _(P2)_
- **Brú na Bóinne: location, tombs and two dates** — In a bend of the River Boyne, Co. Meath: the passage tombs of Newgrange, Knowth and Dowth. Newgrange is about 5,000 years old (about 3200 BC). A UNESCO World Heritage Site since 1993. _(P2)_
- **Bronze Age and Iron Age: settlement features and named sites** — Standing stones and stone circles; fulachta fia beside streams; hillforts and promontory forts, e.g. Dún Aonghasa, a stone fort on a sheer sea cliff on Inis Mór; crannógs; royal sites at Tara (High Kings) and Navan Fort (Ulster), not towns. _(P3)_
- **Ringforts: where they were built, how many survive, place-name clues** — Early Christian farmsteads, from the 5th century, dispersed on well-drained hillsides. Over 40,000 survive in Ireland. Place names with rath, lios or lis and dun mark ringforts; kil or cill marks a church. _(P4)_
- **Early Christian monasteries: where they were sited, three examples** — On routeways, rivers, eskers or remote islands: Glendalough (St Kevin, 6th century, glaciated valley); Clonmacnoise (St Ciarán, where the Esker Riada crosses the Shannon); Skellig Michael (island). Kells and Armagh grew into towns from monasteries. _(P4)_
- **Viking settlement: site factors and Norse place names** — From the 9th century, raids gave way to longphorts on sheltered estuaries and harbours at river mouths, for trade and access by sea; they became the first true towns. Norse names: Wexford, Waterford, Wicklow, Arklow, Leixlip (salmon leap). _(P5)_
- **Viking towns: three foundation dates and their sites** — Dublin, 841, on the Liffey near its mouth; Waterford, 914, on the Suir estuary; Limerick, from about 922, on an island in the Shannon. Wexford and Cork were also Viking towns. _(P5)_
- **Normans: arrival dates and two named castles** — Landed at Bannow Bay, Co. Wexford, 1169; Strongbow took Waterford, 1170. Motte and bailey first, then stone castles: Trim, on the Boyne, the largest Anglo-Norman castle in Ireland, begun by Hugh de Lacy in the 1170s; Carrickfergus, by John de Courcy from 1177. _(P6)_
- **Norman walled towns: site factors, features and two examples** — Sited at bridging points and on navigable rivers in fertile lowlands, for defence and control of land and trade. Walls and gates, market square, charter, burgage plots, parish church, abbeys. Athenry and Fethard: among the most complete town walls. _(P6)_
- **Norman Kilkenny: three facts with dates** — A Norman town on the River Nore, with a stone castle built by William Marshal around 1200; the Statutes of Kilkenny, 1366, tried to stop the Normans adopting Irish language and customs; the Confederation of Kilkenny was formed in 1642. _(P6)_
- **Kilkenny today: how its historic phases show in the city** — St Canice's Cathedral and its round tower mark the early Christian monastery that named the city, Cill Chainnigh (church of Canice); Kilkenny Castle and the Norman core on the Nore; the street plan linking castle and cathedral is the Medieval Mile. _(P12)_
- **Plantation towns: three plantations with a named town from each** — Laois and Offaly, 1556: Maryborough (Portlaoise), Philipstown (Daingean). Munster, from the 1580s: Bandon. Ulster, from 1609: Donegal town around a diamond; Derry, renamed Londonderry in 1613, whose walls (1613–1618) are Ireland's only complete city walls. _(P8)_
- **Name three 18th-century landlord towns and who laid them out** — Westport, Co. Mayo: Browne family of Westport House; the Octagon and a tree-lined Mall on the Carrowbeg River. Birr, Co. Offaly: Parsons family (Earls of Rosse), beside Birr Castle. Kenmare, Co. Kerry: first Marquess of Lansdowne, 1775. _(P9)_
