- **Georgian Dublin and Limerick: who shaped them and when** — Dublin's Wide Streets Commissioners, set up in 1757, widened and laid out the city's streets; Georgian Dublin also has squares such as Merrion Square and Fitzwilliam Square, and red-brick terraces. Limerick's Newtown Pery was laid out in the late 18th century on land of Edmund Sexton Pery. _(P9)_
- **19th-century settlement: four changes** — Canals and railways created junction towns and resorts (first railway, Dublin–Kingstown, 1834); the 1838 Poor Law Act built a workhouse in each union; the Famine (1845–49) and emigration emptied much of the rural west; Belfast grew on linen and shipbuilding. _(P10)_
- **20th-century planned settlement: Shannon and Dublin's new towns** — Shannon, Co. Clare: Ireland's first planned new town, built from the 1960s for workers at the Shannon Free Zone (set up 1959) beside Shannon Airport. The Wright Report, 1967, planned new towns at Tallaght, Clondalkin, Lucan and Blanchardstown. _(P11)_
- **Modern Irish town: which period each historic feature dates from** — Round tower and church on a monastic site: early Christian; walls, gates and castle: Norman; central square or diamond and market house: plantation and landlord towns; Georgian terraces: 18th century; mills and railway station: 19th century. _(P12)_

### 2. Rural settlement (17)

- **Census 2016: share of Ireland's population living in urban and rural areas** — About 63% of Ireland's population lived in urban areas and about 37% in rural areas. _(R1)_
- **Dispersed settlement: four reasons it dominates in rural Ireland** — Family-owned farms, each family living on its holding; Land Act consolidation moved houses out of clusters onto single farms; fertile, well-drained lowland with plentiful water allows houses almost anywhere; pastoral farmers live near their animals. _(R2)_
- **Land reform that spread farmhouses out: three bodies or Acts with dates** — Land Commission, 1881 (Land Law (Ireland) Act 1881); Congested Districts Board, 1891, for the poor west: consolidated holdings, built roads and piers, rehoused families on separate farms; Wyndham Land Act, 1903, helped tenants buy their holdings. _(R2, R4)_
- **Clustered settlement: reasons buildings group at one place** — Route focus at a crossroads; a bridging point or ford; a church, castle or estate gates; defence on a hill; a spring or well (wet point) or a dry point above flood land; a harbour for a fishing village; services such as a shop, pub, school and Garda station. _(R3)_
- **Name two Irish estate villages and the estates they grew beside** — Adare, Co. Limerick, on the Dunraven estate, with thatched cottages; Enniskerry, Co. Wicklow, at the gates of the Powerscourt estate. _(R3)_
- **Clachans: where they were common and why most broke up** — Common in the west before the Famine, with land worked in rundale. The Famine (1845–49) hit clachan districts hardest; as population fell, the Congested Districts Board and Land Commission consolidated holdings and moved houses onto separate farms. _(R4)_
- **Linear settlement: lines it follows and land it avoids** — It follows roads, coast roads, valley floors, rivers and raised ridges such as an esker or the edge of a drumlin, and avoids marshy or steep land. _(R5)_
- **Absence of settlement on a map: five reasons** — Uplands are steep, exposed and have poor soils; bogs are wet and poorly drained; flood plains flood; lakes take up the land; forestry plantations are large areas of planted trees with no houses. _(R6)_
- **Ribbon development: causes** — Cheaper sites than in towns; road frontage gives access to electricity, water mains and broadband; farmers sell roadside sites; demand for rural living near towns; car ownership; weak planning control in the past; tax incentives and the Celtic Tiger boom. _(B2)_
- **Ribbon development: problems** — Traffic hazards from many entrances onto fast roads; costly to service (school buses, post, waste, water, sewers); septic tanks pollute groundwater and private wells; loss of scenic landscape; car dependence; town centres decline; town and country blur. _(B3)_
- **Ribbon development: three advantages from the rural point of view** — It keeps rural populations, schools, GAA clubs and shops alive; it lets families live near relatives and farms; housing is cheaper than in towns. _(B4)_
- **Irish planning system: who decides, who hears appeals, which plan applies** — The local authority grants planning permission; appeals go to An Bord Pleanála (set up 1977, renamed An Coimisiún Pleanála in 2025); under the Planning and Development Act 2000 each authority makes a County Development Plan every six years. _(S1)_
- **Sustainable Rural Housing Guidelines 2005: two area types and the policy for each** — Areas under strong urban influence: rural houses limited to people with a local economic or social need. Structurally weak areas: rural housing is encouraged to keep the population. _(S2)_
- **County Development Plan: typical rural housing policies** — Local-needs criteria; fewer new entrances onto national and regional roads; housing directed into villages and serviced sites; design rules on siting, materials and hedgerows; minimum septic-tank site sizes; protected scenic views; zoning around towns. _(S3)_
- **Septic tanks: the law that regulates them and how many Ireland has** — Water Services (Amendment) Act 2012: domestic septic tanks must be registered and are inspected under an EPA plan. Ireland has about half a million domestic wastewater treatment systems, mostly septic tanks (EPA estimate). _(S4)_
- **Rural development programmes: four schemes with dates** — LEADER (EU), from 1991; CLÁR, for areas suffering depopulation, from 2001; Town and Village Renewal Scheme, from 2016; Our Rural Future, 2021–2025, which backs remote working hubs. All respond to depopulation and lost post offices, Garda stations and pubs. _(S5)_
- **Rural planning in Ireland: four evaluation points** — Keeping rural communities alive conflicts with preventing sprawl; local-needs conditions are called unfair, and a 2013 EU Court of Justice ruling against a Flemish rule raised doubts; ribbon development reduced, not stopped; villages need sewage treatment. _(S6)_

### 3. Urban settlement: site, hierarchy, hinterland and central place theory (19)

- **Name an Irish town on each site: bridging point, island, estuary, route focus** — Bridging point: Dublin, lowest on the Liffey. Island: Limerick, King's Island in the Shannon. Estuary or harbour: Waterford, Suir estuary; Galway, mouth of the Corrib. Route focus: Athlone, a Shannon crossing in the centre of Ireland. _(U1)_
- **Ireland's urban hierarchy, Census 2022: cities and largest towns** — Dublin about 1.26 million; Cork about 222,000; Limerick about 102,000; Galway about 86,000; Waterford about 60,000 (cities and suburbs). Largest towns: Drogheda about 44,000, Dundalk about 43,000, Swords about 41,000. State: about 5.1 million. _(H2)_
- **Dublin as a primate city: size compared with Cork and causes** — Dublin is about six times the size of Cork (Census 2022) and the GDA holds about 40% of the State's population. Causes: capital city, port, routeway focus, colonial administration, multinational firms and services. The rank-size rule does not fit Ireland. _(H3)_
- **Hinterland: how it is measured and what changes its size** — Measured by newspaper circulation, bus routes, school and hospital catchments, shop surveys, commuter flows and sports clubs. Size depends on functions, transport and competition; cars, motorways and online shopping widen it, e.g. Galway's across the West. _(N1, N2, N3)_
- **Central place theory: who published it, when, and the location rule** — Walter Christaller, a German geographer, in 1933, from a study of southern Germany. A service locates where its threshold (a number of people) falls within its range (a distance). _(C1, C2)_
- **Central place theory: five assumptions** — A flat, uniform (isotropic) plain; an evenly spread population with equal purchasing power; equal ease of transport in all directions; consumers buy at the nearest centre; no excess profits. _(C3)_
- **Central place theory: three predictions** — Hexagonal market areas (circles overlap or leave gaps); many small, closely spaced low-order centres and fewer, widely spaced high-order ones, each also supplying all lower-order goods. Nesting: k = 3 marketing, k = 4 transport, k = 7 administrative. _(C4)_
- **Central place theory: evidence from the Noordoostpolder and Ireland** — Noordoostpolder, Netherlands, drained 1942: Emmeloord planned as the central town with ten villages around it (including Marknesse, Ens, Kraggenburg, Bant), spaced so farmers could cycle to one. Market towns in parts of the Irish Midlands are regularly spaced. _(C5)_
- **Central place theory: limitations** — Relief, coasts and population are uneven; transport routes are linear; people make multi-purpose trips; cars, motorways, out-of-town centres and online shopping alter ranges and thresholds; government and history fix locations; it suits only market towns. _(C6)_
- **Central place theory applied to Ireland: how well it fits** — Dublin at the top, then the regional cities, county and market towns, then villages: broadly hierarchical, but distorted by Dublin's primacy and by the coastline. _(C7)_
- **How urban functions have changed over time: general trends** — Defence functions lost; markets and fairs declined as marts and supermarkets took over; industry left the inner city or closed; services grew (offices, tourism, education, health); towns near cities became commuter towns; village services closed. _(F1)_
- **Galway: early functions, from Norman port to decline** — Norman town founded by the de Burgo family in the 13th century; governed by 14 merchant families (the Tribes); royal charter 1484; wine trade with Spain and France; Spanish Arch, 1584; surrendered to Cromwellian forces in 1652, then a long decline. _(F2)_
- **Galway: education and industry functions with dates** — Queen's College Galway, founded 1845 (now the University of Galway, its name since 2022); Digital Equipment Corporation opened a plant in 1971 and closed its manufacturing in 1993; now a medical devices centre, with multinationals such as Medtronic and Boston Scientific. _(F2)_
- **Galway: tourism, culture and planning status** — European Capital of Culture 2020, with Rijeka, Croatia; the Galway Arts Festival and the Galway Races at Ballybrit draw visitors; a gateway in the NSS 2002; the NPF 2018 aims for at least 50% growth by 2040; regional capital of the West. _(F2)_
- **Limerick: early functions with dates** — Viking settlement from about 922; King John's Castle built in the early 13th century on King's Island; Treaty of Limerick, 1691; a Georgian commercial port with Newtown Pery; then an industrial city based on its port and flour milling. _(F3)_
- **Limerick: the Dell plant, opening and closure** — Dell opened in Limerick in 1990. In January 2009 it announced that it would move manufacturing to Łódź, Poland, with the loss of about 1,900 jobs. _(F3)_
- **Limerick: education, culture and local government changes** — University of Limerick founded in 1972 as the National Institute for Higher Education, a university since 1989; Ireland's first National City of Culture, 2014; City and County Councils merged, 2014; Ireland's first directly elected mayor, 2024. _(F3)_
- **Market town to commuter town: how functions change, with GDA examples** — The mart and shops serving farmers decline; housing, services and shops shift to serve commuters who work in a larger city. Greater Dublin Area examples: Navan, Ashbourne, Naas, Newbridge, Maynooth, Bray, Greystones. _(F5)_
- **Reasons for the growth of an urban area: checklist** — Site and situation (bridging point, island, estuary, harbour); route focus and port; administrative role; industry and multinationals; services and education; tourism and culture; government policy (gateways, growth targets); in-migration for work and study. _(G1)_

### 4. Urban land use and planning (20)

- **The CBD: characteristics to describe** — Highest land values; tall buildings; department and specialist stores; offices, banks and head offices; government and cultural buildings; few residents, so busy by day and quiet at night; pedestrian streets; vertical zoning: shops below, offices above. _(Z2)_
- **Urban land-use models: one strength and one limitation of each** — Burgess: shows land value and density falling outwards, but ignores transport routes. Hoyt: explains sectors along roads, rail and rivers, but misses suburban centres. Multiple nuclei: explains suburban centres, but gives no simple pattern. _(Z3)_
- **How Dublin fits the three urban land-use models** — Dublin fits the multiple nuclei and sector models better than concentric rings: the CBD is one nucleus, suburban shopping centres and business parks are others, and growth follows the main roads out of the city. _(Z3)_
- **Dublin's land-use zones: CBD and inner city** — CBD on both sides of the Liffey: Grafton Street (south) and Henry Street (north), both pedestrianised, and O'Connell Street. Georgian core of Merrion and Fitzwilliam Squares now largely offices. Docklands and north inner city in transition and renewal. _(Z4, Z2)_
- **Dublin's land-use zones: outer city, industry and open space** — Western new towns Tallaght, Clondalkin, Lucan, Blanchardstown (Wright Report 1967); business parks near the M50 and N7: Grange Castle, Citywest, Park West, Sandyford; Dublin Port and Airport; Phoenix Park (about 700 hectares, 1662). _(Z4)_
- **Paris: size and the pattern of its land-use zones** — City of Paris: about 2.1 million on about 105 km², 20 arrondissements, CBD and government in the inner ones; Haussmann's boulevards (1853–1870); La Défense offices to the west; Périphérique (1973) rings the inner city; banlieues with grands ensembles. _(Z5)_
- **Industry moving out of the inner city: reasons** — Lack of space to expand; cheaper land on the edge; road access for lorries; less congestion; planning policy; new industries need clean, modern sites. The vacated inner-city sites become brownfield land for redevelopment. _(M1)_
- **Industrial land-use change: Jacob's, Guinness and Renault** — Jacob's biscuit factory moved from Bishop Street to Tallaght in the 1970s; its old factory houses the National Archives. Guinness, an exception, has stayed at St James's Gate since 1759. Paris: Renault's Boulogne-Billancourt plant closed in 1992. _(M1, M3)_
- **Residential land use: three stages of change in Dublin** — Inner-city tenements were cleared in the 20th century and residents moved to new suburbs and new towns; suburbs sprawled into commuter counties; now a return to inner-city apartments in areas such as the Docklands. Issues: density, height, social mix. _(M2)_
- **Dublin's suburban shopping centres: names and opening dates** — The Square, Tallaght, 1990; Blanchardstown Centre, 1996; Liffey Valley, 1998; Dundrum Town Centre, 2005. All are beside major roads or the M50, for car access, free parking and cheaper land. _(M3)_
- **Commercial land use moving out: effects on the city centre and responses** — Effects: vacant units and lost trade as shoppers move to the edge. Responses: pedestrianisation, regeneration of central streets and limits on the size of retail parks. Offices also move to out-of-town office parks and, in Paris, to La Défense. _(M3)_
- **Planning issues raised by land-use change** — Zoning through development plans; brownfield versus greenfield development; building height and density; linking new zones to public transport; mixing land uses; conflict between business, residents and heritage. _(M4)_
- **Bid-rent theory: how land values and uses change across a city** — Land is dearest at the most accessible point, the PLVI, and falls with distance; commerce outbids industry, which outbids housing; secondary peaks at suburban centres, ring-road junctions and main roads; tall buildings at the centre, low density at the edge. _(V1)_
- **Social segregation in cities: causes and results** — Causes: land and house prices, social housing allocation, planning decisions, transport access, household choice and discrimination. Results: affluent areas set against areas of concentrated disadvantage; gated developments; gentrification of inner-city areas. _(V2)_
- **Social stratification in Dublin: three examples** — Affluent south-east suburbs (Ballsbridge, Donnybrook, Dalkey, Foxrock) versus lower-income parts of the north inner city and some outer estates; Ballymun's seven 15-storey towers (1966–69), concentrated social housing; gentrification in the Docklands, with Google at Grand Canal Dock. _(V3)_
- **Social stratification in Paris: contrasts and the 2005 riots** — Wealthy western districts against the poorer north-eastern banlieues of Seine-Saint-Denis, the poorest département in mainland France; immigrants concentrated in grands ensembles; riots began in Clichy-sous-Bois in October 2005. _(V4)_
- **Urban expansion: five causes** — Population growth and in-migration; demand for cheaper, larger houses; car ownership and motorways; high house prices in the city; jobs moving to the suburbs. _(E1)_
- **Pressures on rural land at the rural-urban fringe** — Farmland lost as rising prices push farmers to sell; hobby farms and horse paddocks; commuter villages gain estates and lose character; retail and business parks, data centres, landfill, quarries, golf courses; lost hedgerows and habitats; flooding. _(E2)_
- **Dublin's expansion: sprawl, commuter towns and data centres** — Sprawl into Meath, Kildare and Wicklow; GDA about 2.1 million in 2022, about 40% of the State; commuter towns Navan, Ashbourne, Naas, Newbridge, Maynooth, Bray, Greystones; data centres used about a fifth of Ireland's metered electricity in 2023. _(E3)_
- **Responses to urban expansion: green belts and new towns, with dates** — London's Metropolitan Green Belt, protected from the 1940s–50s; Britain's New Towns Act 1946: Stevenage first (1946), Milton Keynes (1967); Paris's five new towns from the 1965 plan; Dublin's western new towns; compact growth and brownfield targets in the NPF. _(E4)_

### 5. Urban problems in developed-world cities (25)

- **Traffic congestion in cities: causes** — More cars; low-density suburbs poorly served by public transport; radial roads into a historic centre with narrow streets and few river crossings; work and school trips at one peak; delivery lorries; car-only suburban jobs and shops; weak rail investment. _(T1)_
- **Traffic congestion in cities: effects** — Lost time and productivity; stress; air and noise pollution and carbon emissions; road accidents; slower buses, which pushes more people into cars; businesses and tourism harmed; less space for pedestrians and cyclists. _(T2)_
- **Why Dublin has high traffic congestion: four reasons** — Sprawl and car dependence bring commuters in by car; radial routes converge on the centre and there are few Liffey crossings; light rail arrived late (Luas, 2004); port traffic ran through the city centre until the Port Tunnel opened in 2006. _(T3)_
- **Dublin public transport measures with dates** — DART, 1984; Luas, 2004, with Luas Cross City linking the Red and Green lines in December 2017; Dublin Bikes, 2009; Leap card integrated ticketing, 2011; BusConnects redesigning the bus network; MetroLink planned. _(T4)_
- **Road, freight and demand measures to cut congestion in Dublin** — M50 orbital motorway, built in stages from 1990; Port Tunnel, 2006, then a 2007 ban on most five-axle heavy goods vehicles in the centre; bus lanes and quality bus corridors; park and ride; pedestrianisation; parking charges; remote working. _(T4)_
- **London: two charges used to cut traffic and emissions** — The Congestion Charge, from February 2003, a daily charge to drive into central London on weekdays; traffic in the zone fell in its early years. The Ultra Low Emission Zone, from 2019, extended London-wide in August 2023. _(T4)_
- **Urban decay: causes** — Old industries and port jobs close; better-off residents move to the suburbs; buildings, especially social housing, are left derelict or poorly maintained; reduced rates income; dereliction attracts crime and vandalism; road schemes cause planning blight. _(D1)_
- **Urban decay: effects** — Unemployment and poverty; derelict sites standing empty for years; population decline; drug and crime problems; poor services; stigma that deters investment and visitors; a cycle of decline in which each problem worsens the others. _(D2)_
- **Dublin's inner city in the 1970s and 1980s: how it decayed** — The docks lost most of their jobs with containerisation from the 1960s and 1970s; inner-city tenements were cleared and residents moved to the suburbs; dereliction of streets and buildings followed. _(D3)_
- **Limerick: decay in social-housing estates and the response** — Concentrated disadvantage in Moyross, Southill, St Mary's Park and Ballinacurra Weston. After an arson attack on two children in Moyross in 2006, John Fitzgerald's 2007 report led to the Limerick Regeneration programme. _(D4)_
- **Detroit: why the city decayed** — Known as Motor City, home of Ford, General Motors and Chrysler. Middle-class, largely white families moved to the suburbs from the 1950s, helped by freeways; car-industry jobs moved out or were automated; tens of thousands of houses were abandoned. _(D5)_
- **Detroit: population figures, bankruptcy and recovery** — Population peaked at about 1.85 million (1950 US census) and fell to about 640,000 (2020). Filed for bankruptcy in July 2013, the largest municipal bankruptcy in US history, and exited in December 2014. Downtown and Midtown have since seen new investment. _(D5)_
- **Urban sprawl: causes and effects** — Causes: population growth, high inner-city prices, demand for gardens, cars and motorways, greenfield zoning, Celtic Tiger tax incentives. Effects: long commutes, car dependence, lost farmland, thinly spread services, dormitory towns, city-centre decline. _(S2)_
- **Dublin's sprawl: where it spread and the ghost estates** — Into the commuter counties, to towns such as Navan, Ashbourne, Naas, Newbridge, Maynooth, Bray and Greystones. A Government survey in 2010 found almost 3,000 unfinished housing estates (ghost estates) after the 2008 property crash. _(S4)_
- **Urban sprawl: five solutions** — Green belts; compact growth and brownfield development under the National Planning Framework; higher housing densities near public transport; new towns with their own services; regeneration of vacant or derelict inner-city sites. _(S5)_
- **Absence of community: causes** — Estates built quickly without schools, shops, community centres or public transport; high-rise flats with no shared space; long commutes; transient renting populations; old inner-city communities broken up by tenement clearance; social segregation. _(C1)_
- **Absence of community: three examples** — Ballymun, Dublin: seven 15-storey towers built 1966–69, demolished 2004–2015. Commuter and ghost estates in Dublin's commuter counties. Paris banlieues: grands ensembles such as Sarcelles, high unemployment, and riots from Clichy-sous-Bois in October 2005. _(C3)_
- **Absence of community: effects and responses** — Effects: isolation, loneliness, anti-social behaviour, little sense of belonging. Responses: community facilities built with housing (phasing conditions); regeneration with resident participation; mixed tenure; sports clubs and family resource centres. _(C2, C4)_
- **Urban heritage: three benefits and five threats** — Value: a sense of identity; tourism income; economic reuse for shops, hotels and venues. Threats: demolition for offices and roads; neglect and dereliction; out-of-scale new buildings; traffic vibration and pollution; tourist pressure. _(H1, H2)_
- **Dublin heritage losses: Fitzwilliam Street and Wood Quay** — Lower Fitzwilliam Street: the ESB demolished a row of 16 Georgian houses in 1965 for its head office. Wood Quay, one of Europe's largest Viking-age sites: despite protests and court cases in the late 1970s, Dublin Corporation built its civic offices there. _(H3)_
- **Dublin heritage: Moore Street and two cases of positive reuse** — 14–17 Moore Street, where the 1916 leaders spent their last hours before surrender, was declared a national monument in 2007. Positive reuse: Temple Bar regenerated as a cultural quarter in the 1990s; Docklands warehouses reused. _(H3)_
- **Urban heritage protection: four methods with examples** — Record of Protected Structures and Architectural Conservation Areas (Planning and Development Act 2000); national monuments; An Taisce, founded 1948; UNESCO listing, e.g. Edinburgh's Old and New Towns (1995) and Rome's Historic Centre (1980). _(H4, H5)_
- **Dublin's smoky coal ban: dates and results** — Smoky coal was banned in Dublin from 1 September 1990 after severe winter smog in the 1980s; black smoke levels and winter death rates fell afterwards. The ban was extended nationwide in October 2022. _(E1)_
- **Water and waste in Dublin: three named responses** — Ringsend wastewater treatment plant treats most of Dublin's sewage, discharging into Dublin Bay, and is being upgraded; the plastic bag levy, March 2002, at 15 cent, cut bag use sharply; the Poolbeg waste-to-energy incinerator has operated since 2017. _(E2, E3)_
- **Urban green space and flooding: Dublin examples** — Phoenix Park (about 700 hectares, 1662), the Royal and Grand Canals and North Bull Island give green space, but small gaps are lost to infill. Paved surfaces speed runoff into rivers and drains; rivers also flood, as when Hurricane Charley, August 1986, caused severe flooding of the River Dodder. _(E5, E6)_

### 6. Urban planning and renewal (20)

- **Wright Report 1967: what it did and how effective it was** — Planned the western new towns of Tallaght, Clondalkin, Lucan and Blanchardstown, housing rapid growth. But homes came before services, jobs and transport (The Square, Tallaght, opened in 1990; the Luas in 2004), causing sprawl and absence of community. _(P1)_
- **National Spatial Strategy 2002: aims and why it fell short** — It named gateways, including Dublin, Cork, Limerick/Shannon, Galway, Waterford, Dundalk and Sligo, and hubs to balance growth. Weakened by too many centres, the 2003 decentralisation to towns not in the plan and the property boom; Dublin kept sprawling; replaced in 2018. _(P2)_
- **National Planning Framework 2018: four key targets** — About 1 million more people by 2040; half of growth in Dublin, Cork, Limerick, Galway and Waterford; Galway, Cork, Limerick and Waterford each to grow by at least 50%; 40% of new homes within the built-up footprint of existing settlements. _(P3)_
- **National Planning Framework: delivery tools and effectiveness so far** — Regional Spatial and Economic Strategies prepared by the three regional assemblies; the Urban Regeneration and Development Fund, set up in 2018. It is too early to judge fully, but housing supply has lagged behind the targets. _(P3)_
- **Housing strategies: what they are and how effective they have been** — Housing for All (2021): about 33,000 new homes a year to 2030; Rent Pressure Zones from December 2016, first in Dublin and Cork; Part V. Completions rose but stayed below need; over 15,000 people in emergency accommodation in 2025; high rents. _(P4)_
- **Transport strategies in Dublin: successes and failures** — Success: the Luas (2004) and Luas Cross City (2017); the Port Tunnel (2006) and 2007 lorry restriction; Dublin Bikes (2009). Limits: Metro North shelved in 2011 and MetroLink repeatedly delayed; congestion persists as land use is still dispersed. _(P5)_
- **Pollution strategies: what worked and what problems remain** — The smoky coal ban (Dublin 1990, nationwide 2022) largely removed smoke and smog; plastic bag levy (2002); Ringsend upgrade for Dublin Bay; Clean Air Strategy (2023). Still problems: traffic nitrogen dioxide and particulates from solid fuel. _(P6)_
- **Dublin Docklands: redevelopment actions with dates** — Urban Renewal Act 1986 tax incentives; Custom House Docks Development Authority, 1986; IFSC set up 1987; Dublin Docklands Development Authority, 1997–2016; North Lotts and Grand Canal Dock Strategic Development Zone, 2014. _(R1)_
- **Dublin Docklands: flagship projects and results** — Samuel Beckett Bridge and the Luas extension to The Point (2009); Convention Centre Dublin and Bord Gáis Energy Theatre (2010); new offices, homes and culture venues; tech firms in Silicon Docks, including Google's European headquarters at Grand Canal Dock. _(R1)_
- **Dublin Docklands: criticisms** — Offices and costly apartments came first; locals got few jobs; social housing was slow; gentrification raised prices; the DDDA was damaged by its role in buying the Irish Glass Bottle site, Ringsend, in 2006 for about €412 million, at the peak of the bubble. _(R1)_
- **Temple Bar: how it was renewed, and criticisms** — CIÉ bought property in the 1980s for a bus station; artists moved in on short leases; the plan was dropped and Temple Bar Properties (1991) made a cultural quarter (Irish Film Institute, Project Arts Centre). Criticism: pubs, night-time tourism, few residents. _(R2)_
- **Ballymun regeneration: actions with dates** — Ballymun Regeneration Ltd was set up by Dublin Corporation in 1997; the seven 15-storey towers (1966–69) were demolished 2004–2015; new low-rise homes, Main Street, the Axis arts and community centre, and IKEA's first Republic of Ireland store (2009). _(R3)_
- **Ballymun regeneration: how effective was it?** — Success: housing quality improved and residents were involved in the regeneration. Limits: the recession stalled the town centre; the promised Metro North rail link was shelved in 2011 and never built; unemployment stayed high. _(R3)_
- **Limerick Regeneration: actions and criticisms** — Agencies set up in 2007 after the Fitzgerald report demolished and rebuilt housing in Moyross, Southill, St Mary's Park and Ballinacurra Weston, with social and economic programmes. Criticism: slow; demolition outpaced rebuilding; estate populations fell. _(R4)_
- **Adamstown: why it is a model and what went wrong** — Near Lucan, South Dublin: a Strategic Development Zone from 2001 with its own station on the Kildare line, and schools and services phased in alongside housing. Building stalled after the 2008 crash and resumed in the late 2010s. _(R5)_
- **Smaller-city renewal: the Living City Initiative and Cork** — Living City Initiative, 2015: tax relief to refurbish older buildings in Dublin, Cork, Limerick, Galway, Waterford and Kilkenny. Cork: Area Strategic Plan 2001; boundary extended 2019; Luas Cork proposed (2020 strategy); Cork Docklands the next big scheme. _(R6)_
- **London Docklands: redevelopment actions with dates** — London Docklands Development Corporation, 1981–1998, an unelected body with planning powers; Enterprise Zone on the Isle of Dogs; Docklands Light Railway and London City Airport, 1987; One Canada Square, Canary Wharf, 1991; Jubilee Line extension, 1999. _(R7)_
- **London Docklands: results and criticisms** — Results: a second financial centre, jobs and housing. Criticisms: the corporation was top-down and bypassed local councils; jobs went to commuters from outside the area; local residents were priced out; transport came late. _(R7)_
- **Paris planning: three responses and their outcomes** — Five new towns from the 1965 plan took growth from the city; the grands ensembles in the banlieues became problem estates; the Grand Paris Express, about 200 km of new automatic metro lines with 68 stations, aims to link the banlieues. _(R8)_
- **Urban planning and renewal: three conclusions on effectiveness** — Planning works best when housing, jobs, services and transport arrive together and residents are involved; flagship property-led schemes bring jobs and buildings but not always local benefits; national plans fail when other policies pull against them. _(E1)_

### 7. Developing-world cities (21)

- **World urbanisation: UN figures for 1950, 2018 and 2050** — About 30% of the world's population was urban in 1950 and about 55% in 2018, projected about 68% by 2050; over half urban since about 2007. Close to 90% of the increase to 2050 is expected in Asia and Africa. Over 1 billion people live in slums (UN-Habitat). _(G1)_
- **Developing-world cities: reasons for rapid growth** — Natural increase: a young population, high birth rates, falling death rates. Push: rural poverty, landlessness, mechanisation, drought, conflict. Pull: jobs, higher wages, schools, hospitals, family in the city. Primate cities draw investment. _(G2)_
- **Developing-world cities: housing problems** — Slums on marginal land: river banks, steep slopes, beside railways, rubbish dumps and floodplains; insecure tenure, so residents can be evicted; severe overcrowding; homeless pavement dwellers. _(G3)_
- **Developing-world cities: health, employment and transport problems** — No piped water or sewers, so open drains spread cholera, typhoid and dysentery; informal work (vendors, rickshaw pullers, waste pickers) with low pay, no security, child labour and underemployment; overcrowded trains and buses; congestion; road deaths. _(G4, G5, G6)_
- **Developing-world cities: pollution, hazards and social problems** — Air pollution from traffic, industry and cooking fuels; untreated sewage in rivers; uncollected rubbish; floods on low, badly drained land, landslides on hillside slums, fires; crime, drugs, street children, visible inequality, weak local government. _(G7, G8, G9)_
- **Slum solutions: why clearance fails and what works better** — Clearance by demolition and eviction usually fails, moving people further out or into new slums. Site-and-service schemes and upgrading in place (paving, taps, toilets, drains, power, secure tenure) keep people housed and let them improve their own homes. _(G10, G11, G12)_
- **Developing-world cities: other solutions with named examples** — Satellite and new towns divert growth (Navi Mumbai; Kolkata's Salt Lake and New Town); metros, suburban rail and bus rapid transit (Curitiba); rural development, land reform and jobs in smaller cities slow migration; NGOs and community groups help. _(G13, G14, G15)_
- **Curitiba: how its bus rapid transit works and who copied it** — Begun in 1974 under architect-mayor Jaime Lerner with dedicated bus lanes; raised tube stations for fast boarding were added later; the system cut car use and was copied by Bogotá's TransMilenio (2000). Recyclable waste could be exchanged for food or bus tokens. _(C1)_
- **Kolkata: site and history with dates** — On the Hooghly near the Bay of Bengal, on low-lying delta land; founded in 1690 by the British East India Company (Job Charnock); capital of British India until 1911; a port with jute mills; Howrah Bridge opened 1943; renamed from Calcutta in 2001. _(K1)_
- **Kolkata: reasons for growth** — Colonial port and capital; jute and engineering jobs drew migrants from Bihar, Odisha and rural Bengal; the 1943 Bengal famine; refugees after Partition (1947) and the 1971 Bangladesh war; natural increase. About 14.1 million in Census 2011. _(K2)_
- **Kolkata: problems of rapid growth** — Bustees and pavement dwellers; overcrowding; poor water and sewerage; monsoon flooding of low-lying streets; air pollution and congestion; Partition cut off the jute lands, so jobs lagged and many rely on informal work, including hand-pulled rickshaws. _(K3)_
- **Kolkata: planning responses with dates** — Calcutta Metropolitan Development Authority, 1970: bustee improvement with taps, toilets, paved lanes and drains; India's first metro, 1984; Salt Lake City (Bidhannagar) from the 1960s, with the Sector V IT hub; New Town (Rajarhat) from the late 1990s. _(K4)_
- **Kolkata: wetlands, NGOs and how effective the responses were** — East Kolkata Wetlands, a Ramsar site since 2002, naturally treat much of the city's sewage; Mother Teresa's Missionaries of Charity (1950; Nobel Peace Prize 1979). Upgrading improved conditions, but slums remain and the new towns serve the middle classes. _(K4)_
- **Mumbai: site and reasons for growth** — Seven islands joined by reclamation, with a deep sheltered harbour; British East India Company port; cotton mills from the 19th century; financial capital (Reserve Bank of India, Bombay Stock Exchange 1875); Bollywood; migrants from Bihar and Uttar Pradesh. _(M1)_
- **Mumbai: problems of rapid growth** — Dharavi, one of Asia's largest slums, with home-based recycling, leather and pottery; high land costs on a narrow peninsula; overcrowded suburban trains; congestion; the flood of 26 July 2005, after about 944 mm of rain in one day at Santacruz; mill closures after the 1982 strike. _(M2)_
- **Mumbai: housing responses and their criticisms** — Navi Mumbai twin city, begun in the 1970s by CIDCO; Slum Rehabilitation Authority (1995): developers rehouse slum dwellers free, then sell other flats; Dharavi project awarded to Adani, 2022. Flats criticised for poor design and breaking up home industries. _(M3)_
- **Mumbai: transport responses with dates** — Bandra–Worli Sea Link, 2009; Mumbai Metro Line 1, 2014; Mumbai Trans Harbour Link (Atal Setu), January 2024, India's longest sea bridge, linking Mumbai to Navi Mumbai. _(M3)_
- **São Paulo: site and early growth with dates** — On a plateau inland from its port, Santos; founded by Jesuits on 25 January 1554; 19th-century coffee wealth carried on the Santos–Jundiaí railway (1867); Italian immigration; Japanese immigration from 1908 (Kasato Maru), centred on Liberdade. _(S1)_
- **São Paulo: industrial growth, migration and size today** — Brazil's industrial heartland from the 1950s, with car plants such as Volkswagen in the ABC region; nordestinos came from the drought-prone North-East. Census 2022: city about 11.5 million, metropolitan region about 21 million; South America's largest city. _(S1)_
- **São Paulo: problems of rapid growth** — Favelas: Paraisópolis beside wealthy Morumbi, and Heliópolis, the largest; extreme inequality; severe congestion and air pollution; the Tietê floods in summer rain; the 2014–2015 drought drained the Cantareira reservoirs, causing rationing; crime. _(S2)_
- **São Paulo: responses and how effective they were** — Rodízio from 1997 bans cars at rush hour on one weekday by plate digit; metro from 1974; Rodoanel ring road from 2002; Projeto Cingapura flats on favela sites (1990s); Minha Casa, Minha Vida (2009). These helped, but favelas and congestion persist. _(S3)_

### 8. The future of urbanism (16)

- **Megacities in 2018: how many, the largest, its successor, and where growth to 2050 lies** — There were 33 megacities of over 10 million in 2018. Tokyo was the largest urban agglomeration, about 37 million; the UN projected Delhi to overtake it around 2028. Close to 90% of urban growth to 2050 is expected in Asia and Africa. _(F1)_
- **Housing as an issue for cities of the future: causes and responses** — Demand rises with population growth, smaller households and migration; Dublin rents and prices are high; nationally, over 15,000 people were in emergency accommodation in 2025. Responses: Housing for All (about 33,000 homes a year to 2030), homes near transport, cost-rental. _(I1)_
- **Future urban transport: named responses in four cities** — Dublin: MetroLink planned, BusConnects; Paris: Grand Paris Express, about 200 km of new automatic metro lines and 68 stations; London: ULEZ from 2019, extended London-wide in August 2023; Copenhagen: cycling is the most common way to commute. _(I2)_
- **The 15-minute city and Vauban: two low-car neighbourhood designs** — Paris: everyday needs within 15 minutes on foot or by bike, an idea of Carlos Moreno promoted by Mayor Anne Hidalgo in 2020, not yet fully built. Vauban, Freiburg: a low-car eco-district from the late 1990s on a former barracks, built to high energy standards. _(I2)_
- **Climate change and cities: risks and Ireland's legal targets** — Cities produce most emissions and face sea-level rise, flooding and heat waves made worse by the urban heat island; Cork's low-lying centre suffers repeated tidal flooding. Climate Act 2021: emissions cut 51% by 2030 from 2018 levels, net zero by 2050. _(I3)_
- **Climate responses in cities: adaptation, mitigation and Masdar City** — Adaptation: flood defences, green roofs, green infrastructure. Mitigation: energy-efficient buildings, district heating, renewable energy. Masdar City, Abu Dhabi, was begun in 2006 as a planned low-carbon city; its zero-carbon target has been scaled back. _(I3)_
- **Sprawl versus compact cities: the issue and the responses** — Sprawl wastes land and energy. Responses: compact growth under the NPF (40% of new homes within existing built-up footprints; half of growth in the five cities); brownfield regeneration; higher buildings, though height and density are debated; green belts. _(I4)_
- **Ageing populations in developed cities: needs and problems** — More older residents need accessible housing, accessible transport and health care close to home; age-friendly design of streets, buildings and services keeps them independent; fewer workers paying taxes puts pressure on the funding of services. _(I5)_
- **Smart cities: what they do, two examples and three risks** — Sensors and data manage traffic, energy and waste. Songdo, South Korea, built from the 2000s on land reclaimed from the Yellow Sea near Incheon; Smart Dublin, a programme of the four Dublin councils. Risks: high cost, privacy and a digital divide. _(I6)_
- **City centres after COVID-19: how changing work affects them** — After the COVID-19 pandemic began in 2020, remote and hybrid working cut commuting, leaving offices and shops empty; city centres are converting offices to homes and leisure; remote-working hubs in towns are backed by Our Rural Future (2021–2025). _(I7)_
- **Shrinking cities: Detroit and the responses** — Deindustrialised cities lose people and jobs: Detroit fell from about 1.85 million (1950) to about 640,000 (2020) and filed for bankruptcy in 2013. Responses: vacant land reused for parks and urban farming; managed decline that plans for a smaller population. _(I8)_
- **Social inequality as an issue for cities of the future** — Rich and poor areas drift apart: gentrification displaces lower-income residents; gated communities separate the wealthy; immigrants concentrate in districts such as the Paris banlieues (Seine-Saint-Denis riots, 2005). Response: social mix, inclusive planning. _(I9)_
- **Governance of city regions: Dublin, Limerick and Paris** — Dublin is split between four councils (Dublin City, Fingal, South Dublin, Dún Laoghaire–Rathdown); a 2023 Citizens' Assembly recommended a directly elected mayor, not yet in place. Limerick elected its first in June 2024. Métropole du Grand Paris, 2016. _(I10)_
- **Resources for future cities: water, energy and waste** — Water: Uisce Éireann's proposed pipeline from the River Shannon to the Dublin region; São Paulo's 2014–2015 Cantareira drought. Energy: data centres used about a fifth of Ireland's metered electricity in 2023 (CSO). Waste services must keep pace with growth. _(I11)_
- **Developing-world cities of the future: challenges and one solution** — Megacities grow fastest in Asia and Africa, with slums, informal work and poor sanitation; low-lying Kolkata (Hooghly delta) and Mumbai (about 944 mm of rain in one day, 2005) face climate risk; Curitiba's bus rapid transit (1974) is a cheap solution. _(I12)_
- **Irish cities to 2040: key planned changes** — About 1 million more people by 2040 (NPF 2018); Galway, Cork, Limerick and Waterford each to grow by at least 50%; Housing for All targets; Dublin's MetroLink planned and Cork light rail proposed (2020 strategy); data centres raising electricity demand. _(R1)_

## Volcanoes earthquakes

### 1. The geography of volcanoes (19)

- **Volcano diagram: the parts to label** — Magma chamber; main vent (pipe or conduit); secondary (parasitic) vent on the flank; crater at the top of the main vent; cone of alternating layers of lava and ash; lava flow; ash cloud; volcanic bombs. _(V2)_
- **Name three gases released in a volcanic eruption** — Water vapour, carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide. They escape easily from runny basic lava, but viscous acidic lava traps them, which makes eruptions explosive. _(V3, V5)_
- **Active, dormant and extinct volcanoes: an example of each** — Active: Mount Etna, Sicily; Kilauea, Hawaii; Stromboli, Italy. Dormant: Mount Fuji, Japan, which last erupted in 1707. Extinct: Slemish, Co. Antrim, the hill linked with St Patrick. _(V6)_
- **Why volcanoes form at constructive and destructive boundaries** — Constructive: basic magma rises where plates separate, mostly along mid-ocean ridges under the sea. Destructive: subducted oceanic crust melts and acidic magma rises to form explosive volcanoes in fold mountains (the Andes) or island arcs (Japan, Indonesia). _(D1, D3, D4)_
- **Volcanoes: which boundaries have few, and why Ireland has none today** — Continental–continental collision boundaries and transform (conservative) boundaries have few or no volcanoes. Ireland lies in the middle of the Eurasian plate, far from any boundary, so it has no active volcanoes. _(D5, D7)_
- **Iceland: plate setting and two named volcanoes** — Iceland is where the Mid-Atlantic Ridge rises above sea level, with the North American and Eurasian plates moving apart; it also lies over a hot spot. Its volcanoes include Hekla and Eyjafjallajökull. _(D3)_
- **Ring of Fire volcanoes: share and four named examples** — A belt, mainly of destructive boundaries, around the edges of the Pacific with about three-quarters of the world's active volcanoes, including Mount St Helens, USA; Mount Fuji, Japan; Mount Pinatubo, the Philippines; and Krakatoa, Indonesia. _(D2)_
- **Hawaii hot spot: how the island chain forms** — Hawaii sits mid-Pacific plate over a stationary hot spot. The plate moves north-west, so the islands get older to the north-west; the active volcanoes, Kilauea and Mauna Loa, are on the Big Island in the south-east. Other hot spots: Yellowstone, Iceland. _(D6)_
- **Antrim basalts: cause, type of eruption and date** — As North America and Greenland separated from Europe with the opening of the Atlantic, the crust stretched and cracked; basic lava poured out of long fissures in north-east Ireland, flow upon flow, about 60 million years ago. _(A1)_
- **Mount St Helens, 1980: setting and sequence of events** — Cascade Range, Washington State, USA, where the Juan de Fuca plate subducts beneath the North American plate. For weeks magma bulged the north flank; on 18 May a magnitude 5.1 earthquake collapsed it in a huge landslide, releasing a sideways blast. _(N3)_
- **Mount St Helens, 1980: effects and why few died** — The sideways blast flattened forests; the summit lost about 400 m, leaving a horseshoe-shaped crater; ash fell across several US states; lahars down the Toutle River destroyed bridges and homes. 57 died; most people had been kept out of a restricted zone. _(N3)_
- **Negative effects of volcanoes: three named examples** — Vesuvius, AD 79, buried Pompeii and Herculaneum under ash and pyroclastic flows. Krakatoa, 1883: tsunamis killed about 36,000 people. Eyjafjallajökull, April 2010: an ash cloud closed much of European airspace for about a week; about 100,000 flights cancelled. _(N1)_
- **Mount Pinatubo, 1991: effect on global climate** — Sulphur dioxide from the eruption in the Philippines reached the upper atmosphere and cooled global average temperatures by about 0.5°C over the following year or so. _(N1)_
- **Positive effects of volcanoes: five, with examples** — Fertile soils (vineyards and orchards on Etna and Vesuvius); geothermal energy, which heats about nine in ten homes in Iceland; tourism (the Blue Lagoon, Geysir); new land (Surtsey, 1963); minerals and building materials such as sulphur, pumice and basalt. _(N2)_
- **Predicting eruptions: four monitoring methods** — Seismometers record swarms of small earthquakes as magma rises; tiltmeters, GPS and satellite radar measure swelling of the cone; gas monitoring tracks changes, especially in sulphur dioxide; thermal imaging detects heating of the ground or crater lakes. _(P2, P1)_
- **Eruption prediction: the use of past eruptions, and the limits** — Deposits and records of past eruptions show how often a volcano erupts and which areas are at risk, shown on hazard maps. Scientists can often forecast that an eruption is likely, but not its exact time, size or style. _(P3, P4)_
- **Reducing the effects of eruptions: four methods** — Monitoring by volcano observatories; hazard zoning to keep settlement out of high-risk zones; evacuation plans, warning systems and public education; diverting or slowing lava with barriers and channels, or cooling it with water. _(P5)_
- **Heimaey, 1973: how the harbour was saved** — During the Eldfell eruption on Heimaey, Westman Islands, Iceland, about 5,000 residents were evacuated overnight, mostly by the fishing fleet, and sea water was pumped onto the advancing lava to cool and halt it. _(P6, P5)_
- **Pinatubo, 1991: a successful eruption forecast** — Scientists from the US Geological Survey and the Philippine volcano institute monitored earthquakes, gas emissions and swelling and forecast the eruption; tens of thousands were evacuated, including from the US Clark Air Base, saving thousands of lives. _(P6)_

### 2. The geography of earthquakes (22)

- **How an earthquake happens: the four stages** — First, plates moving past, towards or away from each other lock by friction; then stress builds up in the rocks; when it exceeds the rock's strength, the rock fractures or slips suddenly along a fault; finally, the stored energy is released as seismic waves. _(Q2)_
- **Seismic waves: order of arrival and which cause most damage** — P (primary) waves are fastest and arrive first; S (secondary) waves are slower and arrive second; surface waves travel along the surface, arrive last and cause most of the damage. _(Q5)_
- **Seismic station: what it records and how it locates an epicentre** — Its seismograph records the arrival times of P and S waves; the gap between them shows the distance to the earthquake, and three or more stations locate the epicentre by triangulation. _(M2, M1)_
- **Richter, moment magnitude and Mercalli scales: what each measures** — Richter and moment magnitude measure magnitude (energy released), the same everywhere; each whole Richter number up is ten times the wave amplitude and about 30 times the energy. Modified Mercalli (I to XII) measures intensity (effects), which varies by place. _(M3, M4)_
- **Earthquakes at constructive and destructive boundaries** — Constructive: frequent, shallow, generally weaker earthquakes along mid-ocean ridges and in Iceland. Destructive: the most powerful, with foci from shallow to very deep along the sinking plate; undersea ones cause tsunamis (Japan, Chile, Sumatra). _(G3, G4)_
- **Earthquakes at collision and transform boundaries** — Collision: powerful, shallow earthquakes in fold-mountain belts such as the Himalayas. Transform: plates stick, then slip, giving shallow and often very damaging earthquakes, as on the San Andreas Fault and the East Anatolian Fault, Turkey. _(G5, G6)_
- **Nepal, 2015: plate setting and effects** — On 25 April 2015 a magnitude 7.8 earthquake from the collision of the Indian and Eurasian plates killed about 9,000 people, severely damaged Kathmandu and triggered avalanches on Mount Everest. _(G5)_
- **San Francisco, 1906: magnitude, deaths and secondary effect** — On 18 April 1906 an earthquake of magnitude about 7.8 on the San Andreas Fault killed about 3,000 people; fires from broken gas mains burned for days and destroyed much of the city. _(G6)_
- **Primary and secondary effects of earthquakes** — Primary (direct from shaking): building and bridge collapse; deaths and injuries; ground rupture; broken gas, water and power lines; damaged roads. Secondary: fires; tsunamis; landslides; liquefaction; disease from contaminated water; homelessness. _(F1, F2)_
- **Factors affecting earthquake damage: name eight** — Magnitude; depth of focus (a shallow focus causes more surface damage); distance from the epicentre; rock and soil type (soft sediment shakes more and can liquefy); population density; time of day; building quality and building codes; level of development and preparedness. _(F3, Q4)_
- **Haiti, 2010: why the damage was so severe** — On 12 January 2010 a magnitude 7.0 earthquake struck near Port-au-Prince; poorly built concrete buildings collapsed and the poor country had little capacity to respond. Death-toll estimates vary widely, all over 100,000. _(F3)_
- **Kobe, 1995: date, magnitude and plate setting** — The Great Hanshin earthquake, 17 January 1995: magnitude 6.9 on the moment magnitude scale; shallow focus, epicentre near the northern tip of Awaji Island. Kobe lies near where the Philippine Sea plate subducts beneath the Eurasian plate. _(F4)_
- **Kobe, 1995: four effects** — Elevated sections of the Hanshin Expressway toppled over; many older wooden houses with heavy tiled roofs collapsed; fires from broken gas mains burned large areas; liquefaction badly damaged the port, which was built on reclaimed land. _(F4)_
- **Kobe, 1995: death toll, homelessness and response** — About 6,400 people died and about 300,000 were left homeless. The government was criticised for a slow response; stricter building codes and retrofitting followed. _(F4)_
- **Indian Ocean, 2004: what caused the tsunami** — On 26 December 2004 a magnitude 9.1 earthquake struck off the west coast of northern Sumatra, Indonesia, where the Indo-Australian plate subducts beneath the Burma plate; the seabed was thrust upwards, displacing the ocean. _(F4)_
- **Indian Ocean, 2004: effects and response** — About 230,000 people died in 14 countries; Banda Aceh, Sumatra, was worst hit, and Sri Lanka, India and Thailand were badly hit. There was no tsunami warning system in the Indian Ocean; one was set up afterwards, operational from 2006. _(F4)_
- **Earthquake prediction: four methods** — Mapping active faults and plate boundaries; studying the history and frequency of past earthquakes; seismic gap theory; monitoring foreshocks, ground deformation, radon gas and well-water levels. The exact time, place and size cannot be predicted. _(R2, R1)_
- **Haicheng 1975 and Tangshan 1976: prediction success and failure** — Haicheng, China, 4 February 1975: after foreshocks the city was evacuated hours before a magnitude 7.3 earthquake, saving many lives. Tangshan, China, 28 July 1976: no warning was given; the official death toll was about 240,000. _(R3)_
- **L'Aquila, 2009: the earthquake and the scientists' trial** — On 6 April 2009 a magnitude 6.3 earthquake killed about 300 people in L'Aquila, Italy. In 2012 six scientists and an official were convicted of manslaughter for reassuring the public; the scientists were acquitted on appeal in 2014. _(R3)_
- **Earthquake early warning: how it works and what it allows** — Detecting the fast P waves gives seconds of warning before the destructive waves arrive, enough to stop trains, shut off gas and take cover; Japan has a national system. Tsunami warning systems use seabed pressure sensors and buoys. _(R4)_
- **Earthquake-proof buildings: four design features** — Deep foundations anchored to bedrock; base isolation on rubber and steel bearings; flexible steel or reinforced-concrete frames with cross-bracing and shear walls; tuned mass dampers, such as the giant steel ball near the top of Taipei 101, Taiwan. _(R5)_
- **Reducing earthquake damage: infrastructure and preparedness** — Automatic shut-off valves on gas mains and flexible pipe joints; retrofitting older buildings; strict building codes and planning that avoids soft ground and fault lines; drills, such as Japan's Disaster Prevention Day each 1 September. _(R5, R6)_

### 3. Landforms from volcanic and plutonic structures (17)

- **Plutonic features: formed by endogenic or exogenic processes?** — Endogenic: magma intrudes and cools slowly underground. Exogenic erosion only reveals them later, when the rock above is removed, so the statement that plutonic features are formed by exogenic processes is false. _(I1)_
- **Joints in igneous rock: two ways they form and why they matter** — By contraction as the rock cools; in granite also by expansion when the overlying rock is removed and pressure is released. Weathering and erosion work along joints, so the joint pattern shapes the landform. _(I2)_
- **Lava plateau: formation in four stages** — First, runny basic lava pours out of long fissures rather than a single vent; each flow spreads widely before it cools; repeated flows build up one on top of another; finally a high, flat-topped plateau with steep edges forms, such as the Antrim plateau. _(E1)_
- **Antrim plateau: age, cause and the basalt-over-chalk coast** — Layers of basalt from fissure eruptions about 60 million years ago, linked to the opening of the North Atlantic. Along the Antrim coast dark basalt lies on top of white chalk, which the basalt protected from erosion. _(S1, E1)_
- **Giant's Causeway: how the basalt columns formed** — A thick lava flow cooled slowly and evenly and contracted; cracks formed at about 120° to each other, giving mainly six-sided columns at right angles to the cooling surface; sea erosion exposed about 40,000 such columns on the north Antrim coast. _(E2, S1)_
- **Shield volcano: formation and Hawaiian examples** — Runny basic lava flows a long way before cooling, and mostly effusive eruptions spread successive flows from the central vent, building a broad cone with gentle slopes. Examples: Mauna Loa, the largest active volcano on Earth by volume, and Kilauea. _(E3, S3)_
- **Composite volcano: formation and four examples** — Viscous acidic or intermediate magma cannot flow far; explosive eruptions alternate with lava flows, laying down layers of ash and lava that build a steep cone with a crater, main vent and secondary vents. Examples: Fuji, Vesuvius, Etna, Mount St Helens. _(E4)_
- **Caldera: how it forms and a named example** — A huge eruption empties the magma chamber and the summit collapses into it, leaving a basin much larger than a crater, which may fill with water. Crater Lake, Oregon, formed when Mount Mazama collapsed about 7,700 years ago; others: Santorini, Krakatoa. _(E6)_
- **Volcanic plug: formation in three stages and two examples** — First, magma solidifies in the vent of a volcano; then the softer cone around it is eroded away; finally the resistant plug is left as a steep-sided hill. Examples: Slemish, Co. Antrim; Castle Rock, Edinburgh, later shaped by ice into a crag-and-tail. _(E7)_
- **Iceland: four kinds of volcanic feature, with named examples** — The Laki fissure eruption of 1783 poured out huge lava flows, and its gases caused crop failure and famine; Geysir and Strokkur are geysers; Surtsey, a new island, formed off southern Iceland from 1963; Hekla and Eyjafjallajökull are volcanoes. _(S4, E8)_
- **Batholith: formation and effect on the landscape** — Magma cools slowly deep underground into granite, and its heat alters the surrounding rock into a metamorphic aureole. Granite is usually more resistant than the rock around it, so when the overlying rock is eroded it is left standing as upland. _(P1, P7)_
- **Leinster batholith: age, extent and landforms** — Granite intruded about 400 million years ago in the Caledonian period: Ireland's largest granite mass, from south Co. Dublin through Wicklow and Carlow. Erosion of the overlying slate exposed the Wicklow Mountains, ringed by mica schist; Lugnaquilla is 925 m. _(S2, P1)_
- **Mourne Mountains: type of structure and age** — Granite intrusions in Co. Down from the same period as the Antrim basalts, much younger than the Leinster granite, so they are not Caledonian. Slieve Donard is the highest peak. _(S1)_
- **Sill: landscape effects and two examples** — A horizontal sheet along the rock layers; when exposed, a resistant sill forms cliffs, ledges and waterfalls. Fair Head, Co. Antrim, is a dolerite sill with columnar jointing; the Great Whin Sill, northern England, carries part of Hadrian's Wall on its crest. _(P4)_
- **Dyke: two possible landscape effects and an example** — A dyke harder than the surrounding rock stands up as a wall-like ridge; a softer dyke is eroded into a trench. The Isle of Arran, Scotland, has a dyke swarm. _(P5)_
- **Plutonic features on a labelled diagram: name and tell apart** — Batholith (huge granite dome); laccolith (bulges layers up, as in the Henry Mountains, Utah); lopolith (sags down in the centre); sill (along the layers); dyke (across the layers); with a volcanic plug in the old vent. _(P6, P2, P3)_
- **Devon and Cornwall: granite landscape facts** — Dartmoor and Bodmin Moor are granite uplands, parts of one large batholith exposed by erosion; Haytor, Dartmoor, is a tor; at St Austell, Cornwall, china clay (kaolin) is quarried from decomposed granite. _(S5)_

### 4. Landforms from sedimentary structures (12)

- **Joints in sedimentary rock: how they form and what they do** — They form as sediment dries and shrinks, and by stress and the removal of overlying rock. They run at right angles to the bedding planes, so together the two divide the rock into blocks. _(T3, T2)_
- **How bedding planes and joints guide erosion of layered rock** — Water, ice and weathering exploit bedding planes and joints, so rock breaks into blocks and erosion is concentrated there. Where resistant sandstone or limestone alternates with weak shale or clay, the weak layers wear faster, leaving cliffs and caps. _(W1, W2)_
- **Plateau on horizontal strata: formation in three stages** — First, a resistant cap rock lies above weaker horizontal layers; then erosion strips the weaker layers beneath the cap and wears back the edges; finally, the cap protects the layers beneath, so the top stays flat and the edges stay steep. _(L1)_
- **Stepped slopes: how alternating layers produce them** — Hard and soft horizontal layers erode at different rates: hard layers form cliffs and soft layers form gentler slopes, often covered by scree, so the cliff-and-slope profile repeats for each pair of layers. _(L2)_
- **Benbulben: height, structure and slopes** — Benbulben, Co. Sligo (526 m), in the Dartry Mountains: near-horizontal strata with a resistant limestone cap forming vertical cliffs over weaker shale, which forms gentler lower slopes partly covered in scree. Ice later steepened its sides. _(S1)_
- **Cuilcagh: height, structure and features at its foot** — At 665 m it is the highest point in Co. Cavan and Co. Fermanagh: a resistant sandstone cap over shale over limestone gives a stepped profile and a flat summit ridge. The Shannon Pot lies at its foot and the Marble Arch Caves in the limestone beneath. _(S1)_
- **Mesa and butte: formation and a named example** — Weathering and erosion work back the edges of a plateau, breaking it into flat-topped remnants capped by resistant rock: a mesa is wider than it is high; a butte is a smaller, narrower remnant. Example: Monument Valley, on the Arizona–Utah border, USA. _(L3)_
- **Escarpment (cuesta): formation in four stages** — First, a resistant layer is exposed on gently dipping strata; then erosion cuts a steep scarp slope across the edges of the layers; the layer continues along the tilt as a long, gentle dip slope; finally the weaker rock between cuestas is eroded into a vale. _(L4)_
- **Name an example of a cuesta and of a hogback** — Cuesta: the Cotswolds, England, a limestone ridge with a steep west-facing scarp and a long dip slope to the east. Hogback: the Hog's Back, Surrey, England, a narrow chalk ridge on steeply dipping strata. _(L4, L5)_
- **Paris Basin: structure and landforms** — A saucer-shaped basin of limestone, chalk and clay layers dipping gently in towards Paris, near its centre. On the eastern side resistant layers form escarpments facing outwards (east), with dip slopes facing in towards Paris and clay vales between them. _(S2)_
- **Brecon Beacons: rock, landforms and highest peak** — Gently dipping Old Red Sandstone forms flat-topped summits with steep north-facing escarpments and gentle south-facing dip slopes. Pen y Fan (886 m) is the highest peak in southern Britain. The area was renamed Bannau Brycheiniog National Park in 2023. _(S3)_
- **Bedding planes and joints: four smaller landforms they produce** — Limestone pavement, with clints separated by grikes; caves along bedding planes in limestone; waterfalls over horizontal cap rock; sea caves and arches along joints on the coast. _(L6)_

### 5. Landforms from structures of deformation (17)

- **Stress types: the plate movement behind each and the structure it makes** — Converging plates cause compression, giving folds and reverse or thrust faults; diverging plates cause tension, giving normal faults; plates sliding past cause shearing, giving tear (strike-slip) faults. _(D1)_
- **When rock folds and when it faults** — Rock bends into folds when compressed slowly, especially when deep, hot and under pressure; it breaks into faults when the stress is sudden or the rock is brittle. _(D2)_
- **Five types of fold, in order of increasing compression** — Simple (symmetrical): limbs dip equally; asymmetrical: one limb steeper; overfold: one limb pushed over the other; recumbent: layers lie almost flat; overthrust fold or nappe: broken along a thrust fault and pushed far forward over other rocks. _(F3)_
- **How fold mountains form: five stages** — Sediments are deposited in a sea between two plates; the plates converge and compress them; they fold into anticlines and synclines; continued pressure makes overfolds, recumbent folds and nappes; the crust thickens and is uplifted into long, high ranges. _(F4)_
- **Young vs old fold mountains: appearance and named ranges** — Young ranges are high and steep: the Alps, Himalayas, Andes, Rockies and Atlas Mountains. Old ranges, worn down by hundreds of millions of years of weathering and erosion, are lower and rounded, like Macgillycuddy's Reeks and the Appalachians. _(F5)_
- **Munster ridge-and-valley: why ridges and valleys alternate** — Armorican folding made east–west anticlines and synclines. Erosion left resistant Old Red Sandstone on anticlines as ridges (Macgillycuddy's Reeks, Galtee, Knockmealdown and Caha Mountains) and weaker limestone in synclines as valleys (Blackwater, Lee). _(F6, S1)_
- **River Blackwater: how folding shaped its course** — It flows east along a limestone valley, then turns sharply south at Cappoquin, Co. Waterford, cutting across the ridges to reach the sea at Youghal. Rivers following synclines and breaking through ridges give trellised drainage with right-angled bends. _(F7, S1)_
- **Armorican front: what it is and the contrast either side** — The northern limit of strong Armorican folding, running east–west across Munster. South of it rocks were intensely folded and thrust northwards; north of it folding is gentle. _(S2)_
- **The Alps: cause, typical folds and highest peak** — Young fold mountains from Alpine folding, as the African plate collided with the Eurasian plate. Recumbent folds and nappes are characteristic, and Mont Blanc is the highest peak. _(S3)_
- **The Appalachians: why they are linked to Ireland's mountains** — Old fold mountains of eastern North America, folded in the same mountain-building episodes as Ireland's older mountains when the two continents were joined. The Ridge-and-Valley Appalachians show long, parallel folded ridges and valleys. _(S4)_
- **Eroded dome: three resulting features and an example** — Erosion removes the top first, exposing older rocks in the centre; resistant layers form rings of inward-facing escarpments; drainage flows outwards in a radial pattern, as in the Lake District, England. _(M2)_
- **The Weald: structure and landforms** — Rocks in Kent, Surrey and Sussex were pushed up into a dome (an elongated anticline). Erosion removed the top chalk, exposing older sandstones and clays; the chalk forms the inward-facing North and South Downs, with clay vales and the High Weald inside. _(M3)_
- **Normal vs reverse fault: stress, movement and effect on the crust** — Normal: tension; one block slides down the fault plane relative to the other, so the crust is lengthened. Reverse: compression; one block is pushed up over the other, so the crust is shortened. A thrust fault is a low-angle reverse fault, linked to nappes. _(T2, T3)_
- **Horst and graben: formation in three stages** — First, tension stretches the crust, producing parallel normal faults either side of a block; then the central block sinks, forming a graben (rift valley) with a flat floor and steep walls, often holding lakes; the blocks either side stand higher as horsts. _(T7, T6)_
- **Horst and graben: three named examples** — The Rhine rift valley, a graben between the Vosges (France) and Black Forest (Germany) horsts; the East African Rift Valley, with Lake Tanganyika in a graben; the Central Lowlands of Scotland, between the Highland Boundary and Southern Uplands Faults. _(T6, T7)_
- **Tear faults: two landforms and a named example** — The shattered rock of the fault zone is eroded into a straight valley, and ridges and rivers are offset sideways across the fault. The Great Glen Fault, Scotland, is a tear fault; Loch Ness lies in the straight valley eroded along it. _(T8)_
- **Donegal tear faults: trend, names and landforms** — They run north-east to south-west, and rivers and glaciers have eroded long, straight valleys along them. Examples: the Gweebarra Fault, followed by the Gweebarra River valley, and the Leannan Fault. _(T9)_

## Weather climate

### Weather maps and weather data (23)

- **Name five Met Éireann weather stations** — Valentia Observatory, Co. Kerry; Malin Head, Co. Donegal; Belmullet, Co. Mayo; Dublin Airport; Shannon Airport. Met Éireann combines their observations with satellite and radar data to produce Irish weather maps. _(M3)_
- **Weather map units, and average sea-level pressure** — Pressure in hectopascals (1 hPa = 1 mb, so never convert); temperature in °C; rainfall in mm; wind speed in km/h or knots; sunshine in hours; cloud cover in oktas. Average sea-level pressure is about 1013 hPa. _(M4, P1)_
- **Reading the pressure between two isobars: method** — Find the isobars either side of the point, note their values and the interval (read the labels; commonly 4 hPa), then estimate in proportion to distance. Illustrative: midway between the 988 and 992 hPa isobars is about 990 hPa. _(I6, I1)_
- **Reading a shaded rainfall, sunshine or UV map with a colour key** — Read the class from the key and give the range it states, such as 50–74 mm, when the map shows a band rather than a single value. UV and other isoline maps are read the same way. _(I7, R6)_
- **Depression vs anticyclone: why one brings rain and the other doesn't** — In a depression air rises, cools and condenses, giving cloud, rain and strong winds. In an anticyclone air sinks and warms, so clouds rarely form: warm, sunny days in summer; cold, clear nights with frost and fog in winter. _(P2, P3)_
- **Trough, ridge and col: the weather each brings** — Trough: an elongated low, often unsettled, sometimes drawn as a line. Ridge: an elongated extension of high pressure, bringing a short settled spell. Col: a slack area between two highs and two lows, with light winds and possible fog or thunderstorms. _(P4)_
- **Wind direction around lows and highs in the northern hemisphere** — Anticlockwise around a depression and clockwise around an anticyclone, nearly parallel to the isobars but angled slightly in towards a low and out from a high. Buys Ballot's law: with your back to the wind, low pressure is on your left. _(V3)_
- **Wind: how direction is named and how force is rated** — Direction is named from where the wind blows from, so a westerly blows from the west; Ireland's prevailing wind is south-westerly. Force is rated on the Beaufort scale from 0 (calm) to 12 (hurricane force), based on observed effects. _(V1, V5)_
- **Front symbols: how to draw the four types** — Warm: a line with semicircles (red). Cold: a line with triangles (blue). Occluded: triangles and semicircles alternating on the same side (purple). Stationary: alternating on opposite sides. The symbols sit on the side the front is moving towards. _(F1, F2, F3, F4, F5)_
- **Warm front vs cold front: the weather each brings** — Warm: warm air rises gently over cold air, giving thickening cloud and long steady rain, then rising temperature. Cold: cold air undercuts warm air, giving short heavy showers, possible thunder and falling temperature, then brighter showery weather. _(F2, F3)_
- **Depression on a synoptic chart: where the fronts lie** — The warm front lies ahead, the warm sector between the fronts and the cold front behind; the occluded front lies near the centre of a mature depression; isobars kink where they cross the fronts. _(F4, F6)_
- **Station model: reading the wind arrow** — The shaft points from the station towards where the wind comes from. Half barb 5 knots, full barb 10, pennant 50: add them; a circle with no shaft means calm. Illustrative: shaft to the south-west, two full barbs and a half = south-westerly 25 knots. _(S2)_
- **Rainfall forecast sequence: how to find the direction of movement** — Compare the rain band's position on successive timed maps; heavier rain shows in darker or warmer colours. Illustrative: Munster and Connacht at 06:00, the midlands at 12:00, Leinster at 18:00, so the rain moves west to east and reaches Leinster last. _(R5, S4)_
- **Met Éireann weather warnings: colours and types** — Yellow, Orange and Red, by expected impact; Red is the most severe, when people should act to protect themselves and property. Types: wind, rain, snow-ice, low temperature, high temperature, fog and thunderstorm. _(R3)_
- **Storm naming: who names storms, since when, and a recent example** — Met Éireann and the UK Met Office since 2015, joined by the Dutch service KNMI in 2019; names make warnings easier to communicate. Storm Éowyn crossed Ireland on 24 January 2025, with Red wind warnings. _(R4)_
- **Ex-hurricane Ophelia, 2017: key facts** — It reached Ireland on 16 October 2017 as a post-tropical storm, after being a major hurricane south of the Azores, unusually far east for an Atlantic hurricane. Met Éireann issued Red warnings; three people died in Ireland. _(R4)_
- **UV index: the five World Health Organization categories** — Low 1–2; Moderate 3–5; High 6–7; Very high 8–10; Extreme 11 and above. The index measures the strength of the sun's ultraviolet radiation. _(R6)_
- **Weather instruments: name the instrument for each element** — Rain gauge: rainfall. Thermometer: temperature. Barometer: pressure (barograph: continuous record). Anemometer: wind speed. Wind vane: wind direction. Hygrometer: humidity. Sunshine recorder: hours of sunshine. _(D1)_
- **Weather data calculations: total, mean, mean daily temperature, range** — Total: add the values. Mean: total ÷ number of values. Mean daily temperature = (maximum + minimum) ÷ 2. Range = highest − lowest. Illustrative: maximum 14 °C, minimum 6 °C gives a mean of 10 °C and a range of 8 °C. Always give units. _(D2)_
- **Reading hourly graphs and Irish climate graphs** — Hourly: read times carefully (00:00 is midnight), read the value at the given hour and count the hours with rain. An Irish climate graph shows mild winters, cool summers and rain all year, wettest usually in autumn and winter. _(D3, D4)_
- **Why the west of Ireland is wetter than the east** — Moist south-westerly winds from the Atlantic are forced to rise over the western mountains, where the air cools and condenses, giving relief (orographic) rain. _(E1)_
- **Why the east and south-east of Ireland are sunnier and drier** — They lie in the rain shadow of the western and central uplands: the descending air warms and its cloud evaporates, so there is less cloud and more sunshine. The south-east is the sunniest part of Ireland. _(E2)_
- **Annual rainfall in Ireland: east, west and mountains** — Met Éireann long-term averages: most of the east gets about 750–1,000 mm a year, the west about 1,000–1,250 mm, and many mountain areas over 2,000 mm. The lowest totals are around Dublin and the east. _(E4)_

### Satellite imagery (19)

- **Satellite image vs aerial photograph: the differences** — A satellite image is taken from far higher, in space, covers a much larger area, is repeated on a schedule and can record wavebands the eye cannot see. An aerial photograph shows more detail of a small area and is vertical or oblique. _(B6)_
- **Meteosat: type of satellite, operator and Ireland's link** — Meteosat weather satellites are geostationary, about 36,000 km above the equator, viewing a whole hemisphere continuously in low detail. They are operated by EUMETSAT, of which Ireland is a member state. _(B3)_
- **Landsat and Sentinel: type of satellite and main uses** — Both are polar-orbiting: low orbits near both poles cover the earth strip by strip in high detail, for land use, mapping and environmental monitoring. Landsat (USA) has imaged the earth since 1972; Sentinel belongs to the EU's Copernicus programme. _(B4)_
- **Four types of satellite image and what each shows** — Visible: reflected sunlight, daytime only, thick cloud bright white. Infrared: heat, day and night, cold high cloud tops white. False-colour: near-infrared shown red, so healthy vegetation is red. Radar: microwave echoes that see through cloud; maps floods. _(B5)_
- **Reading a satellite image: the five method steps** — Read the caption, date, scale and image type (true colour, false colour or infrared); find north and a coastline or river to orient yourself; identify features by colour, texture, shape, size, pattern and association; describe the pattern; explain it. _(R1)_
- **Satellite image in true colour: what each colour usually shows** — Water dark blue to black (muddy or shallow water brown or turquoise); forest dark green, crops and grass lighter; bare ground and desert yellow, brown, orange or red; urban areas grey; snow, ice, cloud and salt flats white. _(R2)_
- **Satellite image: how to tell cloud from snow and ice** — Cloud is fluffy, streaky or swirling, casts shadows and changes position between images. Snow and ice follow the shape of the land, filling valleys and lying along mountain ridges and the coastline. _(R3)_
- **Recognition clues: depression, hurricane and anticyclone** — Depression: a large comma-shaped or spiral band of white cloud with a long trailing cloud band (the front). Hurricane: a huge circular spiral of thick white cloud with a clear, dark eye, over the ocean. Anticyclone: a large cloud-free area. _(C1, C2, C3)_
- **Recognition clues: glacier and ice sheet** — Glacier: a long white or grey-blue river of ice in a valley between dark mountains, with dark stripes of moraine and a cracked (crevassed) texture; it may end in the sea or a lake with icebergs. Ice sheet: a vast, smooth white surface with no land visible. _(C4)_
- **Recognition clues: erupting volcano and wildfire** — Volcano: a circular cone with a summit crater and radial gullies; a grey or white ash plume blown one way, and dark lava flows downslope. Wildfire: grey or brown smoke drifting from red-orange fire fronts; afterwards a dark burn scar among green vegetation. _(C5, C6)_
