- **Recognition clues: flood and hot desert** — Flood: brown, muddy water spread over fields and roads outside the river channel; compare with a before image. Hot desert: a yellow, orange or red-brown surface with no vegetation, sand-dune ripples or ridges, and dry river beds. _(C7, C8)_
- **Recognition clues: irrigated farming and temperate crop farming** — Irrigated farming: bright green centre-pivot circles (Saudi Arabia, the Great Plains) or rectangles against brown desert, or a green strip like the Nile valley. Crop farming: a chequerboard of differently coloured rectangular fields with straight grid roads. _(C9, C10)_
- **Recognition clues: forest, deforestation, urban area and airport** — Forest: uniform dark green. Deforestation: light-brown clearings in a fishbone pattern along new roads, as in Rondônia, Brazil. Urban area: grey or purple-grey, fine texture, street grid, converging roads. Airport: long straight runways, often crossing. _(C11, C12)_
- **Recognition clues: coastal features, canyons and salt pans** — Spits, tombolos and sandbars: narrow pale strips of sand from the coast. Deltas: fan-shaped land with branching channels and sediment plumes. Canyons: deep, branching, steep-sided valleys in bare rock. Salt pans: dazzling white geometric patches in deserts. _(C13, C14)_
- **Uses of satellite images: weather forecasting and hazards** — Tracking depressions, fronts and hurricanes to issue storm warnings and evacuate coasts (ex-hurricane Ophelia was tracked as it neared Ireland); tracking volcanic ash plumes; mapping earthquake and flood damage to direct relief; monitoring wildfires. _(U1, U5)_
- **Uses of satellite images: mapping, farming, pollution and cities** — Mapping remote areas and updating maps with new roads and housing; monitoring crop health, yields and irrigation, and checking farm area claims; detecting oil spills, smog and algal blooms; measuring urban sprawl over time. _(U2, U3, U6, U7)_
- **Satellites and environmental change: what they track, with an example** — Deforestation, desertification, shrinking lakes, glacier and ice-sheet retreat, sea ice and sea temperature. Images from different decades show the Aral Sea shrinking since the 1960s, as its rivers, the Amu Darya and Syr Darya, were diverted for irrigation. _(U4)_
- **Satellite imagery: five advantages** — One image covers a very large area; repeat coverage shows change over time, with archives going back decades; remote and dangerous areas are reached without a visit; infrared and radar are recorded; images are produced fast in emergencies. _(A1)_
- **Satellite imagery: its main limitations** — Cloud blocks visible and infrared images; many images have limited resolution; interpretation needs skill and software; images carry no names or labels, and relief is hard to judge unless shadows show it; high-resolution imagery can be costly. _(A2)_

### Geographical information systems (GIS) (24)

- **GIS analysis: five operations and what each does** — Overlay combines layers to show where things coincide; buffering draws a zone of set distance around a feature; querying selects features meeting a condition; change detection compares a layer at different dates; network analysis finds the shortest route. _(G5)_
- **Steps in a GIS study: the six stages** — Define the problem and area; collect the data layers; tie them by coordinates to one base map; overlay and analyse; map and present the results; draw conclusions and recommend planning action. _(G6)_
- **GIS: three advantages and three limitations** — Advantages: handles very large data sets from many sources; fast analysis and easy updating; models 'what if' scenarios. Limitations: costly software and data needing training; results only as good as the data; layers at different scales may not match. _(G8)_
- **Irish GIS data sources: who supplies maps and census data** — Tailte Éireann, set up in 2023 from Ordnance Survey Ireland, the Property Registration Authority and the Valuation Office, supplies maps and aerial photography. The CSO supplies census data, published for Small Areas of generally 50–200 dwellings. _(G9, U3)_
- **Urban sprawl study: what each data source shows** — Aerial photographs of different dates show farmland replaced by estates, roads and business parks. Census data by small area show where population grew and how people commute. Old and new maps show the built-up area spreading and land zoned for housing. _(U2, U3, U4)_
- **Urban sprawl study: the GIS analysis** — Overlay the built-up area at each date to measure how much land was urbanised; overlay census population growth on it; buffer motorways and rail stations to test whether growth followed transport; link commuting data to show dependence on the car. _(U5)_
- **Dublin sprawl: findings and named commuter towns** — The city and suburbs spread into the surrounding counties of the Greater Dublin Area (Dublin, Meath, Kildare, Wicklow); commuter towns such as Navan, Drogheda, Naas, Newbridge and Portlaoise grew rapidly, and many residents commute long distances. _(U6)_
- **Problems of urban sprawl shown by a GIS study** — Loss of farmland and green space; car dependence, long commutes and congestion; services lagging behind new housing; the high cost of extending water, sewerage and public transport to low-density areas; weakened community life in dormitory settlements. _(U7)_
- **Planning responses to urban sprawl, with examples** — Compact growth and higher density near public transport, promoted by the National Planning Framework (2018, part of Project Ireland 2040); planned communities with services built alongside housing, such as the Adamstown SDZ, Co. Dublin; greenbelts. _(U8)_
- **Agricultural land-use study: data sources and what they show** — Satellite images from different years show changes in crops, grassland and field size; CORINE land cover maps Ireland for 1990, 2000, 2006, 2012 and 2018; the CSO Census of Agriculture (about 135,000 farms in 2020) gives farm and crop figures by county. _(A1, A2, A3)_
- **Agricultural land-use study: the GIS analysis** — Overlay satellite-derived land use from two dates to map where tillage, grassland, forestry and built land changed; join county statistics; relate change to soil, relief and rainfall layers, e.g. tillage on the drier, flatter land of the east and south-east. _(A4)_
- **Changing Irish farmland: four findings** — Most farmland is grassland for cattle and sheep, with tillage mainly in the east and south-east; dairy farming expanded after EU milk quotas ended in 2015; some marginal western land was planted with forestry; city-edge farmland was lost to urban growth. _(A5)_
- **Forestry study: what aerial photographs and maps show** — Photos of different dates show new plantations as dark, uniform, straight-edged blocks with firebreaks and clear-felled areas as bare brown patches. OS maps of different editions show forest by symbol; soil and peat maps show planting on marginal land. _(F1, F2)_
- **Forestry study: the GIS analysis** — Overlay forest cover at successive dates to map expansion; overlay soils, slope and altitude to explain where planting happened; buffer protected sites, rivers and archaeological sites to find conflicts; query which counties had the largest increase. _(F4)_
- **Irish forestry: key figures and the state company** — Forest cover was about 1% of Ireland at the start of the 20th century and is about 11% today (National Forest Inventory, 2022). Sitka spruce makes up about half of all forest area. Coillte, the state forestry company set up in 1988, manages most public forest. _(F5)_
- **Forestry development: environmental problems** — Dense conifer blocks change the landscape and reduce biodiversity; acidification and sediment in streams harm fish and the freshwater pearl mussel; open upland habitat for the hen harrier is lost; planting on peat releases carbon; clear-felling causes erosion. _(F6)_
- **Forestry development: benefits, local issues and management** — Benefits: timber and jobs, farm income, carbon storage, recreation. Issues: local opposition in Co. Leitrim; ash dieback, first found in Ireland in 2012. Management: broadleaf planting, river buffer zones, environmental assessment, continuous-cover forestry. _(F6)_
- **Record of Monuments and Places: the Act, the format and the database** — Set up under section 12 of the National Monuments (Amendment) Act 1994: a written county list with OS maps on which each site is circled. Its database, the Sites and Monuments Record, is in the National Monuments Service's online Historic Environment Viewer. _(R1)_
- **Archaeological sites: how old maps reveal destruction** — First edition OS six-inch maps of the 1830s–1840s record sites such as ringforts, drawn as circles; comparing them with modern maps and the RMP shows which have disappeared. Ringforts, about 45,000 in Ireland, are the most common field monument. _(R3, R2)_
- **Aerial photographs: marks that reveal buried archaeological sites** — Cropmarks (crops taller and greener over buried ditches, stunted over walls); parchmarks in dry summers; soil marks in ploughed fields; shadow marks in low sun. In the July 2018 drought a henge at Brú na Bóinne, Co. Meath, was spotted from the air by drone. _(R4, R7)_
- **Archaeology study: the GIS analysis** — Overlay the RMP point layer on old and new maps and aerial photographs to record which sites survive, are damaged or destroyed; overlay development layers (roads, housing, quarries, forestry), buffer each monument to predict threats; map destruction by area. _(R5)_
- **Causes of destruction of archaeological sites** — Agricultural improvement (reclamation, drainage, ploughing, removing field boundaries); road building and infrastructure; urban and housing development; quarrying; afforestation (ploughing, drains, tree roots); coastal erosion of promontory forts and middens. _(R6)_
- **Archaeological destruction: three Irish case studies** — Wood Quay, Dublin: a Viking-age site largely removed for civic offices after protests in the late 1970s. M3 through the Tara–Skryne valley, Co. Meath: opened 2010; Lismullin henge found in 2007. Carrickmines Castle, M50 route: a dispute in the early 2000s. _(R7)_
- **Archaeological sites: protection measures a GIS study supports** — The RMP and its notification system; archaeological assessment and excavation before development (preservation by record or in situ); planning conditions; rerouting roads; public awareness; farm schemes that protect monuments. _(R8)_
